# Exterior Binding and Spine

The image shows the front cover and spine of a <term>leather-bound manuscript<note>A book where the pages are sewn together and protected by a cover made of animal skin.</note></term>. The cover is crafted from a **dark reddish-brown leather**, which bears the marks of its long history through various scratches and scuffs across the surface. A strip of lighter, tan-colored leather or parchment runs vertically along the edge near the spine, likely a reinforcement or a later repair.

The <term>spine<note>The edge of the book where the pages are joined together and which is visible when the book is on a shelf.</note></term> is structurally defined by **six raised bands** <note>These horizontal ridges are created by the cords used to sew the gatherings of pages together.</note>. In the spaces between these bands, one can see the faint, shimmering remnants of **gold-stamped titles**. Unfortunately, these inscriptions have been worn away by time and handling, making the original lettering illegible. 

At the very base of the spine, there is a small, modern library <term>shelf mark<note>A label used by libraries to identify a book's specific location on their shelves.</note></term> featuring a blue border, marking its transition from a private or monastic collection into a modern institutional archive.

<keywords>binding, leather, spine, raised bands, shelf mark, manuscript, gold stamping</keywords>

Concerning the word **postponement** <note>original: "Iffifionis"—a transcription of *diffissionis*, referring to the legal postponement of a case to another day</note>: and likewise regarding the phrase commonly used in court, **to split the day** <note>Latin: *diffindi diem*</note>, which has now been almost entirely removed from use. Through a certain wicked ignorance—both troublesome and destructive to the liberal arts—it has been nearly obliterated; we shall endeavor to restore it to the best of our ability and renew it through our own diligence.

Whether that figure of speech [was used], even the jurist Ulpian <note>An influential Roman jurist of the 2nd and 3rd centuries AD whose writings formed a large part of later Roman law codes</note> in the 73rd book of his commentary *On the Edict*, uses these very words, which are found in the first book of the *Digest* <note>A vast collection of Roman legal opinions compiled by order of Emperor Justinian in the 6th century</note> under the title: *If anyone has not complied with the securities made for the purpose of appearing in court.* "If anyone," he says, "has promised to appear in court, and was prevented by health, or a storm, or the force of a river, and could not appear, he is assisted by an **exception** <note>original: *exceptione*; a legal defense or excuse allowing a defendant to stop an action even if the plaintiff's claim is valid</note>."

And not without merit. For since personal presence is necessary in such a promise, how could one appear who is hindered by adverse health? And for that reason, even the Law of the Twelve Tables <note>The ancient centerpiece of Roman law, dating to roughly 450 BC, which formed the foundation of the Republican constitution</note> states: "If the judge or either of the litigants is hindered by a **serious illness** <note>original: *morbo fontico*, referring to a condition that provides a valid legal excuse for failing to perform a duty</note>, the day of the trial shall be **postponed** <note>original Latin fragment: *dies diffissus esto*, meaning "let the day be split" or "adjourned"</note>..."

<summary>This page features the spine of a 1489 edition of Poliziano's Miscellanea and a scholarly analysis of Roman legal terms for trial postponement, citing the jurist Ulpian and the Law of the Twelve Tables.</summary>
<keywords>Angelo Poliziano, Roman Law, Ulpian, Digest of Justinian, Law of the Twelve Tables, legal etymology, Antonio Miscomini, incunabula, Latin philology</keywords>

<meta>Although the previous page discussed the myth of Melampus and the daughters of Proetus, this page marks a significant shift as it is the very beginning of the formal printed volume: the Preface to the "Miscellanies." This "Century" (a collection of 100 philological essays) is Poliziano’s most famous work of scholarship.</meta>

# PREFACE OF ANGELO POLIZIANO TO THE FIRST
# CENTURY OF THE MISCELLANIES, ADDRESSED TO LORENZO DE' MEDICI

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap "C" in gold is set within a square frame. The letter is decorated with a vertical band containing three dots. The background of the frame is a faded pink with delicate patterns, and it is surrounded by a decorative red-ink border consisting of stylized scrolling vines and leaves.</image-desc>

WHEN I was recently reciting these *Miscellanies* <note>The title *Miscellanea* refers to a collection of diverse topics, a popular genre for Renaissance humanists to showcase wide-ranging knowledge</note> of ours to you, Lorenzo de' Medici, while we were out riding, I believe you were delighted by the very novelty of the subjects and the pleasant variety of the reading. You began to urge me to publish at least one "century" from them—for each individual book explains a hundred chapters. I am doing this now, moved partly by your authority—than which no greater exists for me on this earth—and partly by reason. For some people are already "sweeping up" <note>original: "cum puluiſculo cõuerrũt," a Latin idiom meaning to take everything, including the scraps or dust</note> these works of ours, so much so that they please others, and themselves, far too much under the title of our own labors.

Although I know these books will face great envy and undergo much talk, since they pronounce freely regarding authors of great name. But that is not my own pattern alone. For almost all writers defend their own positions in such a way that they attack those of others, and they frequently seek an opportunity for praise from the errors of others. Furthermore, those "nobles" (so to speak) and the leading figures of literature are often insulted by the humbler and common folk—those of the lowest mark, such as myself or Cluvienus <note>A reference to a famously bad poet mentioned by the Roman satirist Juvenal; Poliziano is using "modesty" to deflect criticism</note>. 

However, there is an old saying: *Conscience is a thousand witnesses.* My conscience knows that absolutely nothing here was given to hatred, nothing to spite. Everything is presented candidly and simply. We have brought forth nothing with a spirit of harassment or malevolence; nothing from a desire to "bark" <note>original: "oblatrandi," a common metaphor for cynical or dog-like criticism</note> at others, and nothing from a wish to show off. Our mind has always been more inclined toward approving (if it were permitted) than toward disapproving. Clearly, we have conducted this business not with tricks or "hidden tunnels" <note>original: "cuniculis," referring to underground military mines or deceptive tactics</note>, but with free examination and the open face of truth. 

Indeed, we did not seek this in order to sprinkle any kind of stain, as it were, upon learned men; rather, we took care lest the faith of students be endangered while under the authority of those men. Finally, it is against those people specifically that this "wedge" <note>a triangular military formation used to break enemy lines</note> is professedly directed; against them this "battering ram" is aimed by design:

<image-desc>At the bottom of the page, there are two ink stamps. On the left is a circular, somewhat faded official seal. On the right is an oval library stamp from the "BIBLIOTECA NAZIONALE CEN. FIRENZE", featuring a crown and the Roman numerals "XV".</image-desc>

<summary>In this preface, Angelo Poliziano dedicates his scholarly work to his patron, Lorenzo de' Medici, explaining that he is publishing these essays to prevent others from plagiarizing his unfinished notes.</summary>
<keywords>Angelo Poliziano, Lorenzo de' Medici, Miscellanea, humanism, Renaissance scholarship, philology, plagiarism, literary criticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s defense of his work, shifting from his address to Lorenzo de' Medici into a justification for the "scattered" style of the Miscellanies. He invokes classical precedents to explain why the book is organized as a collection of diverse observations rather than a single narrative.</meta>

<margin>
L
B
S
</margin>

Those who harden their faces <note>original: "frontem durant," an idiom for being stubborn or shameless</note> against the truth for the sake of a home-grown but imaginary sense: we prick them most sharply with the point of our pen. These are men who have never learned to "turn the pen" <note>original: "stilum uertere," a Roman expression meaning to erase or correct one’s work using the flat end of a stylus</note>—that is, they have not learned to strike out their own errors. 

As for this disordered and confused collection, which might be called a forest or a mixed fodder <note>original: "farraginem," referring to a mash of different grains given to cattle</note> because we write not in a continuous flow but by leaps and snatches: far from grieving over this, we have specifically sought no other title than *Miscellanies*. In this, we follow the Greek Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175 – c. 235 AD), author of *Varia Historia*</note> and the Latin Gellius <note>Aulus Gellius (c. 125 – after 180 AD), author of *Attic Nights*</note>; the books of both are more charming for their variety than for their order. Indeed, even the commentaries of Clement <note>Clement of Alexandria (c. 150 – c. 215 AD)</note>, that Roman pontiff and apostolic man, were titled *Stromata* <note>meaning "Patchwork" or "Tapestries"</note>—which you might call "painted coverlets"—and they surely suggested to us no other title but one of this same variety. Furthermore, I shall stay silent regarding the commentaries of Aristoxenus, which Porphyry cites with a nearly identical title in the volume he composed on Ptolemy’s *Harmonics*. 

Finally, if this very variety—the expeller of boredom and the provoker of reading—is blamed in these *Miscellanies*, then with the same effort, the very nature of things could be criticized; I profess myself to be a disciple of Nature through such diversity.

But if they stoop to every small detail: this is permitted among the Latins by the examples of Caesar, Varro, Messalla, Cicero, Pliny, Quintilian, and others like them. For those who emulate such men, it is more honorable even to err. Therefore, what was fitting for those "patricians" <note>The high-ranking noble class of ancient Rome</note> should not be unfitting for me, as if I were one of the "lesser families" <note>original: "minorū gentiū," referring to the families added to the Roman Senate at a later date</note>. Such famous names equally insert Greek phrases or interpret them for convenience; sometimes for the sense, sometimes word-for-word, both of which I do here. 

Now, if any parts seem insufficiently explained to someone, or perhaps even too harsh and obscure, surely that reader lacks a sufficiently lively wit or solid, faithful learning. If they find certain "barbarous" and obsolete terms, we have scattered them intentionally, so that we might even please the unlearned and make the book more marketable as a collection of "scraps of grace" gathered from all sides. If some say that words are found here too remote to be considered common, I suspect they will change their minds once they have advanced further into the heart of literature. For there are those to whom even (as Marcus Tullius <note>Cicero</note> says) an "alabaster jar of ointment..."

<summary>Poliziano justifies the "miscellaneous" and fragmented structure of his work by citing classical and patristic precedents like Aulus Gellius and Clement of Alexandria. He argues that variety prevents boredom and defends his use of obscure or "barbarous" vocabulary as a deliberate scholarly choice.</summary>

<keywords>Angelo Poliziano, Miscellanea, Aulus Gellius, Aelian, Clement of Alexandria, philology, classical precedents, literary style, variety, Latin scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded Chapter III’s discussion on the rituals of Diana at Ephesus, specifically mocking the castration of her priests. This page serves as a stylistic preface or defense of the author's writing choices, moving away from the specific historical commentary toward a discussion on the art of writing itself.</meta>

...might seem full of stench. In this kind of writing <note>original: "scriptionibus"</note>, which does not sell itself to the masses but prepares itself only for a few, the use of such "hidden furniture" <note>recondite or specialized vocabulary</note>—especially if used modestly—is by no means disapproved of by good men. For it is no disgrace to renew things that have almost faded away, provided that these things do not merely decay with age, but grow old with a new vigor <note>The author distinguishes between "vetustescere" (to grow old/decay) and "veterascere" (to become established or inveterate)</note>.

If some of these stylistic detours occasionally offend anyone—perhaps a man less familiar with such things, who has read only ten pages of Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and orator considered the gold standard for Latin prose</note> and nothing else—I certainly appeal from him to the learned. I believe they will especially praise what he disapproves of. Nothing is perhaps more intolerable than for an unlearned person to pass judgment on a matter, yet one who seems most learned to himself. 

If anyone, when something is refuted, desires a mass of many authorities or reasons, let him know that victory is sought by us there, not through a glut of victory <note>suggesting that a concise, elegant argument is better than an overwhelming, repetitive one</note>. If some chapters are thought a bit long, and others again a bit short, let us believe this too is the law of a new work: that it should have nothing equal, never be the same as itself, always limp with dissimilarity, and uphold this one rule: that nothing should be brought back to a strict rule, or to the plumb-line and level <note>metaphors for strict geometric or stylistic uniformity</note>. 

If a freer style is judged to be of an unusual nature, perhaps a kinder interpreter will attribute it instead to simplicity, which is not unbecoming to well-born men. Furthermore, if custom should resist anywhere, then truth—which does not deal in trifles—or a better custom acting as a proxy for truth, will defend it. For it is said that something is "taken up by custom" when it is accepted against authority, and the "error of custom" is often criticized by the learned. Then someone said beautifully: "Custom without truth is the antiquity of error" <note>This is a famous quote from Saint Cyprian of Carthage</note>. 

Finally, if we have occasionally sprinkled in a few things that are meticulous and anxious—or more truly, a bit sharp—drawn either from philosophy (of which we have long been candidates) or from that circle of disciplines <note>the "encyclia" or liberal arts</note> which serve the study of wisdom <note>original: "sapientiæ"</note>, those things will perhaps refresh and sharpen the reader's stomach, which is fading from too many sweets. For so that all these things might harmonize in their own pleasing turn and be joined as if by a certain antithesis <note>original: "antitheto" — a contrast or opposition of ideas</note>, we have run almost "from the lime-kiln into the coal-house," as they say <note>a Latin proverb meaning moving from one intense thing to another, or from one extreme to another</note>, and we have taken care that opposite things should also follow one another in turn.

a ii

**Vocabulary:** writing, Cicero, authority, custom, truth, philosophy, wisdom, antithesis

<summary>In this prefatory text, the author defends his idiosyncratic writing style, arguing that a varied and "aged" vocabulary is appropriate for a learned audience and prioritizing truth over common custom.</summary>
<keywords>literary style, Cicero, aesthetics of writing, custom vs. truth, St. Cyprian, antithesis, philosophical prose</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his literary style. He has just finished arguing that learned readers will appreciate the nuances of his work even if the unlearned find it inconsistent in length or complexity.</meta>

...should follow, which would free us from complaints of this sort. For we do not allow ourselves to lack things that are more pleasant and delightful—not to say alluring—than they are useful or necessary. After all, as Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BCE), a prolific Roman scholar and writer</note> says, "one thing suffices for a man, another for humanity" <note>humanitas: here meaning the refinement, culture, and education that distinguishes humans from animals</note>. At times, beautiful things are sought even without financial gain—and by Hercules, no less so than a profit without beauty.

Therefore, just as those rustic and "goat-like" <note>hircosos: a common Roman metaphor for the unwashed or unrefined</note> men will be delighted by things that are unpolished and rough—hewn with an axe rather than finely carved, having felt neither the file, nor the plane, nor the rasp—so, on the other hand, a "mosaic-like" <note>original: "vermiculata", referring to intricate, wave-like mosaic patterns</note> style, variegated with multi-colored tiles, will captivate these more delicate readers—the ones who are plucked and smoothed with pumice stone. This is done so that the voices gathered from both sides—resulting in either a steady applause or a hiss—might neither be raised to the heavens nor cast down to the ground. 

However, a middle path between these extremes—marked by neither—will satisfy, acting as a kind of "Cinnus" <note>original: "cinnus", a term used by Cicero for a mixed drink or a confused medley</note> of different styles: not excelling in any single one, yet sharing in all of them. For not everyone has the same taste! Rather, each person has their own palate. Then, I suppose, some will spit things out at a banquet as being too fastidious or meager, only to lick up greedily in the kitchen what they find sweet and rich.

Certainly, we have prepared these things not for the forum or the senate-house, but for the bedroom and the schoolroom. For that reason, we have strived—though I do not know if we have succeeded—that whatever is found here should be entirely easy and characterized by a simple elegance <note>original: "simplices munditiae", a phrase famously used by the poet Horace to describe unadorned beauty</note>. We avoided laborious and painted allurements; we sought a true and natural color and brightness, not something adopted or from a cosmetic jar. We aimed for a suitable and unimpeded style, not one that is burdensome or overly wordy. 

Finally, in whatever way these works turn out, or however they are received, we certainly do not refuse—though I don't think we will escape—the common lot of writers: to be poorly spoken of. Let it be so, if that is what the readers desire. On the contrary, we expose our head to that very gamble <note>original: "aleae", literally a die-roll or game of chance</note>, and we do not look for protection, which we have voluntarily stripped from ourselves through our own judgment. Let judgment be passed against us; let us be mocked and harassed, rather than allow the public's trust in literature—which we honor more than our own interests—to be defrauded. May that honor be redeemed even at the cost of our reputation or our leisure. For I ask you, what is the point of this profession of "common studies" <note>original: "studiorum communium", referring to the liberal arts or the shared pursuit of learning</note> if we have reached the point where the duty we owe to universal learning should cease...

->II 6<-

<summary>The author advocates for a balanced "middle" style of writing that satisfies both the unrefined and the delicate, arguing that true scholarship (humanitas) values elegance and public intellectual integrity over personal profit or reputation.</summary>
<keywords>literary style, Varro, Cicero, humanitas, aesthetics, writing ethics, Cinnus, scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>The previous page established the author’s stylistic intent: to provide a "middle path" or a "mixture" of styles that might appeal to both refined and unrefined readers, comparing his work to a multi-colored mosaic or a blended drink.</meta>

...yet shall we suffer everything from base suspicion? By what means can the integrity of honorable letters continue to exist, or how can it endure such hardship, if criticism is entirely cast aside while we all equally dread the sting of envy? And yet—as far as I am personally concerned—I only wish that my labor were met with contempt rather than envy. For it is almost my prayer, if only what Livy <note>Titus Livius (64 BCE–17 CE), the famous Roman historian</note> says is true: *Envy, like fire, seeks the high places.* <note>original: "Inuidia, uelut ignis alta petit"</note>

Come now, you excellent cultivators of studies, each of you eager for your part: tear out this invincible weed by which the good fruits of humanity <term>humanitas: the classical ideal of a refined education in the liberal arts</term> are being suffocated. These things grow beneath the sacred stems like destructive ivy, sucking out all their lifeblood with their wandering coils; break them off entirely and cut them down! 

There is no lack of those who corrupt the truth; let there not be a lack of those who restore it. I ask that some champion stand forth as a defender and a patron for these endangered disciplines. For I, as one of the common crowd, will certainly test what freedom is permitted to me. Whether or not I make it worth the effort, I shall see later. However things flow, or wherever the matter falls, I will take it in good part. I will not only swallow the poison of all malice and detraction, but—given my constitution—I will even digest it. I am certain that this is the very nature of truth: as soon as it appears in public, it is hateful and hostile to the common crowd.

Therefore, though others may turn a blind eye and mutter about telling the truth, I alone surely—whatever happens—will not disguise my judgment. I will not suppress what I feel; I will no longer indulge such things with my patience! 

Instead, I will whisper to this little book <note>original: "libello," a diminutive often used by authors to show humility toward their work</note> of mine, at least with some small degree of freedom: 

I have seen, I have seen, my little book—and I see it every day—that many capital crimes are committed in literature. Foreign works are plundered by deceit; things are fabricated at whim for whoever’s convenience; writings are attributed to reputable authors which they would not even recognize; authors are cited who do not even exist; even manuscripts that appear nowhere are cited as ancient. All books are filled with the most laborious vanities. Falsehoods are substituted for truths, the adopted for the native, and the recent for the home-grown. Everything is polluted, adulterated, smeared over, encrusted, distorted, confounded, thrown headlong, and overturned—with no faith, no shame, and no judgment. And what is more ruinous than all of these: even by the occasion of this recent art <note>The author is referring to the invention of the printing press, which allowed for the rapid spread of both knowledge and errors</note>, which...

<summary>The author issues a passionate call to defend the "integrity of letters" against corrupt scholars and fraudulent publishers. He laments the state of contemporary book production, where forgery, plagiarism, and the misuse of the new printing technology have led to the decay of truthful scholarship.</summary>
<keywords>envy, scholarship, plagiarism, printing press, corruption of truth, Livy, humanitas, literary criticism</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>The previous page established the author’s stylist intent: to provide a "middle path" or a "mixture" of styles that might appeal to both refined and unrefined readers. He concluded by expressing his willingness to endure the "poison" of critics, noting that truth is often hateful to the common crowd.</meta>

It seems people are content to let the most foolish opinions spread in an instant through a thousand volumes. In short—to say it once and for all—even now, many people still possess the ears of a donkey <note>A classical reference to King Midas, who was given donkey ears by Apollo for his poor taste in music; the author suggests many modern readers lack discernment.</note>. 

Someone might ask: "And what about you?" I confess that I can indeed be mistaken—I am, after all, only human! But I certainly do not mislead anyone intentionally; and while I might perhaps speak a falsehood, I am certainly not a liar <note>The author distinguishes between an objective error made in good faith and the willful intent to deceive.</note>. As the saying goes, "I forgive myself," as Mevius <note>Mevius was a notoriously poor Roman poet, often cited by Virgil and Horace as a symbol of bad taste.</note> said. But joking aside, I do not even forgive myself; if I have put forward anything unlearned or careless, I desire to be refuted by anyone at all. I will be the first to refute myself if my errors are pointed out. 

For I do not love myself so perversely that I would rather others fall into error than have my own mistakes revealed! I am one of those who write to learn, and learn by writing; as for the success of that effort, let fortune decide. This much is true: had I kept these commentaries <term>commentarios: informal scholarly notes or observations</term> at home in my hands a little longer, and had I been able to revisit them after some time, these instructions would have been far richer and the supporting evidence more plentiful. 

Clearly, this is the nature of this kind of study—it grows through long hours of night-work. Just as a river swells from its course, so this work grows more abundant, bit by bit, through constant reading. Even now, much remains unpolished, unstrained, and unexamined—not yet smoothed or rounded on the lathe <note>A common classical metaphor for refining a literary work.</note>. As for the things I have omitted entirely, they were thought out in haste and off-hand rather than with care—though the pen runs almost with a surplus of energy and shapes its speech for one who is primarily concerned with ancient truth and good faith. 

Indeed, lest idle men think that I have drawn these observations from the dregs or overstepped the bounds of the grammarians, I have followed the example of Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 CE), whose *Natural History* famously included a list of sources for every book.</note> and listed my authors at the outset. I have included only honorable and ancient writers, from whom these matters derive their authority and from whom I have "borrowed." I have not included those who are merely cited by others but whose own works have been lost to time. 

Instead, I list those whose treasures I have handled myself and through whose writings I have traveled. Furthermore, the age of the manuscripts, the reliability of coins, and the antiquities carved in bronze or marble—which you, Lorenzo <note>Lorenzo de' Medici (1449–1492), the author's patron and a famous collector of antiquities.</note>, have provided for us—offer great support to my commentaries, in addition to the variety of books. 

<summary>The author humbles himself as a student who learns through writing and explains his scholarly methodology. He emphasizes his reliance on primary sources, ancient authors, and the physical artifacts—coins and marbles—provided by his patron, Lorenzo de' Medici.</summary>
<keywords>humanism, Lorenzo de' Medici, Pliny the Elder, scholarly method, antiquities, manuscripts, intellectual humility</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the author further explains his scholarly integrity, stating that although he is human and prone to error, he never intends to deceive. He invites correction and expresses regret that he could not spend more time refining these commentaries before publication.</meta>

...we might teach by example that the tricks of charlatans and all impostures must be abolished, and that the wantonness of certain people must be restrained—a wantonness which now creeps far and wide like a cancer—of departing idly and licentiously from the established rule. At no point have we cited the testimony of ancient writing <note>original: *ueteris ſcripturæ*</note> in such a way that anything added-on or superimposed might prevail over the truth; rather, we have represented each thing in its own true form. We have also applied those notes from which the authority of the text may be thoroughly sought out, even from its very root.

And so that no additions to this work of ours might be missed, we have even grouped the main subjects themselves into "centuries" <note>original: *concenturiauimus*; this refers to the common Renaissance practice of organizing scholarly observations or adages into groups of one hundred.</note>, enclosed in very brief titles. You might say their utility is greater than their number; for, in the manner of accountants, we have collected vast sums upon brief wax tablets, so that we ourselves might best accommodate the negligence of the fastidious reader. 

Therefore, just as the Homeric Teucer <note>In the *Iliad*, the archer Teucer used the massive shield of his half-brother Ajax for protection while he fired his arrows.</note> hid under the shield of Ajax, so we, lurking under the shadow of your name, shall launch an attack upon the barbarians, group by group. Thus, through this edition, the whims of both the fair and the unfair will be satisfied from time to time; and by a certain compensating turn, just as we provide for the former something to kiss, so for the latter we shall supply an abundance of something to bite. 

For I think certain rabid fellows will bite our work—those whom Cato elegantly calls "vicious litigants" <note>original: *uitilitigatores*; a portmanteau used by the Roman statesman Cato the Elder to describe those who find fault for the sake of arguing.</note>, suffering especially from that vice of quarreling as if from a disease. They are a hot-headed and irritable race who, without cause, without discernment, and with (as they say) "closed eyes," bark at whatever name comes their way. 

Or those, again, who through mercenary advocacy and venal opinion, once they have bound and indentured themselves to anyone—becoming, as it were, a surrendered slave—have resolved to defend that same person combatively and stubbornly, whether by right or by wrong. 

Or those boastful little men and sellers of trifles, full of pompous but also foolish conjecture, who love only themselves and their own works—even without rivals!—favoring themselves while disparaging others. 

Or finally, those certain shunners of the light of truth and—to speak more plainly—moth-like <note>original: *blattarii*; derived from the Latin word for cockroach or moth, implying these critics hide in the dark and consume the work of others.</note> petty grammarians; who, with bleary and truly owl-like eyes, cannot bear even a clear day, nor even a glimmering shadow. For no one doubts those whom the moth of malice consumes, whom the rust of envy gnaws, who consider another's glory to be their own punishment, and another's happiness...

<summary>In this preface, the author defends his scholarly method, explaining that he has organized his notes into "centuries" for the reader's convenience and used ancient authorities to root out impostures. He anticipates harsh criticism from various types of "vicious" and envious critics but claims the protection of his patron's reputation.</summary>

<keywords>philology, scholarly method, Teucer and Ajax, vitilitigatores, envy in academia, ancient authority, Renaissance commentaries</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the author completes his thought regarding his critics, contrasting those who are consumed by envy with the integrity he strives for in his own scholarly work.</meta>

...others make their own place of torture; they waste away at the success of others: those whom malevolence, sickened by the prosperity of others, constantly tortures like an internal dread! But also those censors from the pulpit <note>original: *censores item pulpitarii*; referring to preachers or lecturers who attacked humanist scholarship from their positions of authority.</note> (though I am not speaking now of the learned and good) are often accustomed to prod us and shout down these studies of ours. For, full of barrenness, they defame the *humanities* <term>litterae humaniores: the study of classical literature, grammar, rhetoric, and history, seen by humanists as essential for a civilized life</term> before the ignorant masses—I might almost say solemnly—with puffing cheeks; and, fierce in their coarse boorishness—which is the only thing they take for holiness (as Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the Bible translator who famously struggled to reconcile his love for classical literature with his Christian faith.</note> says)—they rage against those studies and their students with bombastic proclamations, gnashing their teeth and foaming at the mouth. 

They do this so that they clearly reveal themselves to disapprove of and almost hate even those Greek authors—truly holy souls—such as Basil, Chrysostom, and the Gregorys; and also the Latins: Cyprian, Ambrose, Augustine, and Jerome himself, and other leaders of our religion of that kind, who were packed with the disciplines of all nations and languages, just like the "wealth of the Egyptians." <note>This is a common Renaissance reference to a metaphor by St. Augustine: just as the Israelites took Egyptian gold to build the Tabernacle, Christians should use "pagan" classical learning to support Christian truth.</note>

For this reason, your favorite, this **Mariano da Genazzano** <note>Mariano da Genazzano (1450–1498) was a celebrated Augustinian preacher in Florence and a rival of Savonarola, admired for combining deep theology with polished humanist oratory.</note>, seems all the more admirable to me. He is second to none in theology, and of all those we have heard preaching in the church, he is not only the wisest but also the most eloquent. His eloquence is not viewed with suspicion by the people striving for a virtuous life, because it is recommended by the incredible severity of his lifestyle; nor, on the other hand, is his austerity gloomy or formidable, since he does not despise the charms of poetry and that beautiful variety, brilliance, and delight of literature. 

Therefore, just as wool that is to be truly dyed purple is first stained with certain preparations, so I believe it is proper for minds to be cultivated by literature and such teachings—as Cicero says in the *Hortensius* <note>A lost philosophical dialogue by Cicero that was famously credited by St. Augustine for turning his heart toward God.</note>—and to be steeped and prepared for the receiving of wisdom. And yet, I will not be bothered by those men, nor will I cease to refine with my pen whatever results from these midnight labors of ours, nor will I stop testing what is permitted against those who conceive of capital frauds in the business of literature. For I, too, forgive **Philoxenus** <note>Philoxenus of Cythera (c. 435–380 BC) was a poet who was sent to the quarries by the tyrant Dionysius for criticizing the ruler's bad poetry; when released and asked again, he simply said, "Take me back to the quarries," rather than lie.</note>—just as Cicero did when writing to Atticus—for preferring to return to prison rather than surrender his judgment; especially if not...

<summary>The author defends the study of classical literature (the humanities) against narrow-minded religious critics, arguing that even the Church Fathers used such learning. He holds up the preacher Mariano da Genazzano as an ideal model of one who combines strict holiness with the beauty of eloquent scholarship.</summary>
<keywords>humanities, Mariano da Genazzano, Church Fathers, classical learning, Cicero, St. Jerome, religious vs. secular studies, eloquence</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the author shifts from criticizing his detractors to defining his own purpose as a scholar, asserting that true writing is intended for future generations rather than the fleeting praise of the present. He concludes by dedicating the work to his patron, Lorenzo de' Medici.</meta>

...the present age! But it is fitting for writers to serve eternity (just as was said by Thucydides <note>The Greek historian Thucrydides (c. 460–400 BC) famously described his work as a "possession for all time" (*ktēma es aiei*) rather than a temporary showpiece.</note>). For this reason, I believe books themselves are called "monuments": because they ought to look toward the memory of posterity, which will judge more incorruptibly, rather than toward the foolish little favors of the present time. 

Therefore, so that this wickedness of those who corrupt everything—if it be permitted—might not only be extinguished, but even suppressed! And so that this worn-out and shameless <note>original: *prostituta frons*; literally a "prostituted brow," a Roman idiom for someone who has lost the ability to blush or feel shame.</note> face, this great hardness of mouth, does not become an example to others! Even now, I promise the following centuries <note>The author is referring to his *Centuriae* (Centuries), a scholarly work organized into sets of 100 observations or "miscellanies."</note> of this healthy effort I have undertaken, so that you are not left only with a taste of this first one. 

For those whom we now treat with a soft touch, those whom we touch with a light and gentle arm, we shall, I think, later press and urge more strongly. Being content for a short while with this preliminary skirmish <note>original: *uelitariq; pugna*; referring to the *velites*, the light-armed skirmishers of the Roman army who fought before the main battle.</note>, we will soon collect our strength and fight with the full force of the army. 

Indeed—since "the tunic is closer than the cloak" <note>A common Latin proverb (*tunica propior pallio*) meaning that one’s own interests or family must come first.</note>—we will boldly and diligently reclaim what is ours from plagiarists and petty thieves; we will lay a hand upon them in a "liberal cause" <term>liberali causa manum: a legal procedure in Roman law used to assert a person's status as a free man rather than a slave</term>. However, we only want those things to be accepted as ours which we ourselves have produced as leaders through great labor and industry. We will never allow them to be wheedled or extorted from us as long as we are able; these are, I say, not our cast-offs, but our pledges. 

For just as we leave these present writings in the open to be snatched up and trampled like missiles thrown to a crowd, we keep and defend those others; we set these forth, but those we both recognize and take up! Yet we do so in such a way that we do not wish to imitate monkeys, who are said to admire their own offspring, however deformed, as if they were the most beautiful. 

Nor will these works of ours—though they are studies of lighter effort or, more truly, "trifles"—be a disgrace to you, **Lorenzo de' Medici**, to whom they are now dedicated. They are dedicated to you, however, not so much to prove me grateful for your benefits or to repay a favor (though they have had you as a helper and a counselor), but rather so that they may proceed under good auspices and that your memory may be frequented within them! Thus, a book that tastes of authority may be commended by the fame of a great name. Finally, for the one to whom the entire sum of my studies is owed, it is only right that the work itself should begin from him.

<margin>Ammianus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus (c. 330–395 AD), the last major Roman historian, likely cited here for his views on history and fame.</note></margin>

<summary>Poliziano defends the longevity of his scholarship against contemporary critics and transitions into a formal dedication to his patron, Lorenzo de' Medici, framing his work as a lasting monument for future generations.</summary>
<keywords>Thucydides, Lorenzo de' Medici, Ammianus Marcellinus, posterity, plagiarism, Centuriae, literary skirmish, patronage</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the author completes his dedication to Lorenzo de' Medici, comparing his own miscellaneous scholarly notes to a light dessert following the substantial "main course" of philosophy provided by other great scholars in Lorenzo's circle. He concludes with a list of the ancient authorities—grammarians and poets—who serve as the foundation for his work.</meta>

And by Hercules, let these observations of mine be like desserts <note>original: *bellaria*; sweetmeats or treats served at the end of a meal.</note>, welcomed at the second course. For those weightier matters occupy the place and appearance of a proper dinner—such as the many things that either Marsilio Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499) was a celebrated philosopher and priest who led the Platonic Academy in Florence under the Medici.</note> (on Plato) or the Byzantine John Argyropoulos <note>John Argyropoulos (c. 1415–1487) was a Greek scholar who played a central role in the revival of Greek learning in 15th-century Italy.</note>, the interpreter of Aristotle, have gathered for you from the larder of philosophy. Indeed, concerning that man to be admired above all others—not just Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), the brilliant polymath and philosopher.</note> now, but (as I myself am accustomed to call him) a "phoenix" <note>The author puns on Pico's name and the mythical bird, suggesting he is unique and reborn from ancient wisdom.</note>—who now makes his nest in your laurel <note>A pun on Lorenzo de' Medici’s name (Latin: *Laurentius*), which is derived from *laurus* (laurel).</note>: my expectation is indeed so great that I would dare to chime in with Propertius: *Yield, you Roman writers; yield, you Greeks!* <note>original: *Cedite romani scriptores / cedite grai*; a famous line from the poet Propertius signaling the arrival of a work of genius (originally Virgil's *Aeneid*).</note> Farewell to you, most delightful patron; and (as you are already doing) continue to favor the learned; continue to rouse literature; continue to rescue the Latin language from neglect and the Greek from ruin, so that the memory of your name may be utterly defended from the injustice of oblivion.

# SACRED AUTHORS

There are also other ancient, learned experts cited whose authority is obscure, such as the writer of the *Poemenicon* <note>original: "Poemenicon scriptor"; likely referring to *The Shepherd of Hermas*, an early Christian visionary text.</note> and the commentators on Aristophanes, Xenophon, Aristides, Euripides, Callimachus, and Synesius! Finally, even the foreign Hebrew and Chaldean <note>Refers to ancient Near Eastern sources, often associated with astronomical or mystical knowledge.</note> authors are not without their own contributions <note>original: "asymboli"; a Greek-derived term meaning "not contributing one's share." Here it means these authors provide valuable evidence for their own origins.</note> regarding their origins.

# CHAPTERS OF THE FIRST CENTURY

## Chapter i.

Cicero defended from slander regarding the meaning of a new term in Aristotle: which is *Entelechy* <note>original: "Endelechia"; refers to the philosophical concept of "actualization." There was a famous scholarly debate over whether the word should be *entelecheia* (actuality) or *endelecheia* (continuity).</note>.

## Chapter ii.

What the "Carpathian sandals" <note>original: "crepidæ Carpatiæ"</note> are in the works of Catullus: a term correctly written that is being wrongly altered.

## Chapter iii.

In what words Horace indicated the giraffe <note>original: "chamelopardalin"; literally "camel-leopard."</note>: what the appearance of that animal is, by what names it is known, and at what time it was first seen in Italy.

## Chapter iv.

How many things must be read by those who interpret the poets: and how Persius fashioned his satire after Plato’s *Alcibiades*.

## Chapter v.

What the "Wooden Horse" <note>original: "Durateus equus"; a Greek term used by the Roman poet Lucretius to refer to the Trojan Horse.</note> is in Lucretius: and what ought to be read in...

<meta>This page continues the table of contents for the "First Century" of scholarly observations. The previous page ended with Chapter V; this page covers Chapters VI through XV, focusing largely on classical literary criticism, philology (the study of language in historical texts), and ancient customs.</meta>

In the second book of the *Argonautica* <note>An epic poem by the Roman author Valerius Flaccus (1st century AD) regarding Jason and the Golden Fleece.</note>, Valerius Flaccus wrote not indeed "Doric Night," but rather "Duric" or "Durian." <note>original: "Nox dorica... durica uel duria." The author is arguing for a specific textual correction based on a geographic location rather than the more common term "Doric."</note>

->## Chapter vi.<-
In what sense the **sparrow** of Catullus <note>Catullus, a Roman lyric poet, wrote famous poems to his lover’s pet bird; scholars have long debated if the "sparrow" was a literal pet or an erotic metaphor.</note> is to be understood; and a passage in Martial indicated as well.

->## Chapter vii.<-
That Juvenal <note>A Roman satirical poet known for his biting critiques of Roman society.</note> hints at *pathic* men <note>original: "Pathicos homines"; refers to the passive partner in a male-male relationship, often used as a derogatory label in Roman satire for those considered "unmanly."</note> as those who scratch their head with a single finger; and concerning the verses of the poet **Calvus**.

->## Chapter viii.<-
Why the names of the wandering stars <note>The "wandering stars" are the planets known to the ancients: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, along with the Sun and Moon.</note> were assigned to the days; and the delightful reasons for their order, both to know and to recount.

->## Chapter viiii.<-
What should be believed regarding the writings of **Domitius** <note>Likely Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century Renaissance scholar often criticized by his rivals for errors.</note> and to what extent; and concerning his evident and shameful error regarding the word *Arregias*.

->## Chapter x.<-
Concerning the *Baptae* <note>Priests of the goddess Cotytto whose rituals were famously secretive and perceived as scandalous.</note> and **Corytto**; and concerning the Coryttians; also, the correct reading and explanation indicated in the works of Juvenal and Horace.

->## Chapter xi.<-
An opinion set forth regarding **Claudian** <note>A late Roman court poet (c. 370–404 AD).</note>, with the retelling of a very charming little tale; then another story, no less pleasant, translated from the Greek regarding **Venus and the rose**. <note>Commonly the story of how the white rose turned red when Venus pricked her foot on a thorn while running to her dying lover, Adonis.</note>

->## Chapter xii.<-
How **purple dye** was discovered; at the same time, a passage by the Greek poet **Nonnus** <note>Author of the *Dionysiaca*, the longest surviving poem from antiquity.</note> is explained.

->## Chapter xiii.<-
What the "Augury of Safety" <note>original: "Salutis augurium"; a Roman ritual held annually to determine if the gods would permit a prayer for the Roman people's well-being.</note> may be, found in the works of **Suetonius**.

->## Chapter xiiii.<-
What the *Naulia* <note>A type of ancient stringed instrument, likely of Phoenician or Hebrew origin (the *nevel*).</note> are in Ovid’s *The Art of Love*; and therein regarding the foreign names of musical instruments used by both the Greeks and our own people; and how even the **Chaldeans** <note>Refers here to speakers of Aramaic or Syriac, or more broadly to ancient Near Eastern scholars.</note> do not occasionally disdain our Greek and Latin words of that kind.

->## Chapter xv.<-
Who was the author of the *Sybaritis* <note>A lost collection of erotic or "obscene" stories.</note> mentioned by Ovid; and concerning the Sybaritic books in **Martial**; and likewise regarding the other luxuries of the people of **Sybaris**. <note>An ancient Greek city in Italy famous for its extreme wealth and hedonism, giving us the word "sybarite."</note>

<summary>This page lists the table of contents for chapters six through fifteen of a scholarly work, focusing on the interpretation of Roman poets like Catullus and Juvenal, the origin of the days of the week, and ancient cultural curiosities like purple dye and musical instruments.</summary>
<keywords>Catullus, Juvenal, Valerius Flaccus, planetary days, purple dye, Ovid, Sybaris, classical philology, Roman satire, ancient music</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents for the scholarly work. The previous page covered chapters six through nine, dealing with poetic metaphors, social critiques in satire, and the naming of the planets. This page continues with chapters sixteen through twenty-six, focusing on textual corrections of classical authors like Seneca, Suetonius, and Cicero.</meta>

## Chapter xvi.
Concerning **Aristides of Miletus** <note>A Greek author from the 2nd century BC known for writing "Milesian Tales," which were often bawdy or erotic short stories.</note> and what is said of him in **Ovid**; and regarding the "Milesian delights" <note>original: "milesiorū deliciis"; a reference to the luxury and licentiousness associated with the city of Miletus.</note>.

## Chapter xvii.
A passage in the tragedy of **Seneca** corrected; and explained by means of a more obscure Greek fable concerning the **Nemean lion** <note>The Nemean lion was a legendary monster in Greek mythology, killed by Heracles as the first of his twelve labors.</note>.

## Chapter xviii.
Who the "**Little brother of a giant**" <note>original: "Fraterculus gigantis." In his Satires, Juvenal uses this ironic phrase to describe someone of obscure or humble origin who pretends to be important.</note> is in **Juvenal**; and who the "**Son of Earth**" <note>original: "Terrę filius"; a common classical idiom for a person of unknown or low-born parentage.</note> is in other authors; and in the same place, concerning the name of **Manes**.

## Chapter xviiii.
Certain matters cited regarding **aspiration** <note>The pronunciation of the "h" sound. Roman scholars often debated which words should be "aspirated" based on their Greek or Latin origins.</note>; together with an explanation of the noble epigram of **Catullus**.

## Chapter xx.
The word "**Ascopera**" <note>A Greek-derived term for a traveler’s leather bag or knapsack.</note> corrected and explained in the works of **Suetonius**.

## Chapter xxi.
What either the "**Minerval**" or the "**Minerval gift**" is; and from that, the opinion of **Juvenal** and **Varro** <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, an immensely prolific Roman scholar and writer of the 1st century BC.</note> explained.

## Chapter xxii.
What kind of verses those are in which **Martial** says a "**Little Greek Echo**" <note>original: "Echo gręcula." This refers to a specific type of wordplay or poetic device where the end of a line mimics the sound of an echo.</note> sings back.

## Chapter xxiii.
That it should be read in **Martial** "With a companion mule," whereas many read "With a companion Humpback"; together with the reasoning and the truer sense explained. <note>original: "Cum cōpare mulo" vs "Cū cōpare Gibbo." The author is arguing for a textual correction based on a more logical reading of the poem's context.</note>

## Chapter xxiv.
Certain things recorded to memory regarding the old woman **Hecale** <note>A poor old woman who hospitably received the hero Theseus; she was the subject of a famous but fragmentary poem by Callimachus.</note>; and on the poetry of **Callimachus**; then a passage explained from the *Priapic Epigrams*; and likewise another in **Statius**; and that which is erroneously read concerning it in the manuscripts of **Apuleius**.

## Chapter xxv.
How many things are misplaced in the *Letters to Friends* <note>original: "epistolis familiaribus." These are the personal letters of Marcus Tullius Cicero, which are a primary source for Roman history.</note> of **Cicero** which we now possess; then, into what order they should be restored.

## Chapter xxvi.
Verses in the *Fasti* <note>Ovid's "Fasti" is a poetic treatment of the Roman religious calendar.</note> from the Greek regarding the vine and the goat; then a certain parody.

...more obscure in **Suetonius**.

## Chapter xxvii.
Words in a letter of **Cicero** taken from the *Medea* of **Ennius** <note>Quintus Ennius was an early Roman poet; his *Medea* was a Latin adaptation of the famous Greek tragedy.</note>, which was itself derived from the Greek of **Euripides**.

## Chapter xxviii.
What are called "**Panic terrors**" <note>original: "Panici terrores"; sudden, groundless fears attributed to the god Pan.</note>; and the most reliable witnesses cited concerning them.

## Chapter xxviiii.
The origin in **Ovid** of the phrase "**Teuthrantian crowd**" <note>original: "Teuthrātia turba"; a reference to the fifty daughters of Thespius, often associated with the region of Teuthrania, who were all said to have been impregnated by Hercules.</note>, who are called the daughters of **Thespius**.

## Chapter xxx.
Words that are rare to find, yet not unique: **Cucuma** <note>A thin-necked kettle or cooking pot.</note>, **Proseucha** <note>A Greek term for a place of prayer or a chapel, often used by Romans to refer to Jewish houses of prayer.</note>, and **Scruta** <note>A term for old, broken garments or junk; second-hand frippery.</note>.

## Chapter xxxi.
The meaning of that passage in **Juvenal**, "**As if you had three names**" <note>original: "Tanq̃ habeas tria nomina." This refers to the *tria nomina* (praenomen, nomen, and cognomen) which were the mark of a full Roman citizen.</note>; and why those released from slavery <note>term: "manu emissi" (manumitted slaves)</note> took the names of their patrons for themselves; and concerning adoption into a name by means of a will.

## Chapter xxxii.
A word long lost that has been restored to the manuscripts of **Pliny**; what the terms **poppysmos** or **poppysma** <note>A clicking or smacking sound made with the lips, used by the Romans both to soothe animals and as a ritualistic gesture during prayer.</note> signify; and the custom of using these sounds when worshipping lightning bolts.

## Chapter xxxiii.
What the "**Crambe**" <note>original: "Crambe"; literally "cabbage." Juvenal uses the phrase *crambe repetita* (reheated cabbage) to describe something tediously repeated.</note> is in **Juvenal**; and the Greek proverb concerning it.

## Chapter xxxiiii.
Certain errors in a letter of **Cicero**; and concerning the word **Sciamachia** <note>A Greek-derived term meaning "shadow-fighting" or mock combat, often used to describe rhetorical exercises that lack real-world stakes.</note>.

## Chapter xxxv.
A passage in **Marcus Varro** <note>A prolific Roman scholar known as "the most learned of the Romans."</note> and likewise another in the books on agriculture by **Columella** <note>A Roman writer who produced a comprehensive manual on farming and animal husbandry.</note> defended against error; and certain matters therein regarding **Euhemerus** <note>A Greek mythographer who argued that the gods were originally great kings or heroes deified after death.</note>, the **Tomb of Jove**, and additions regarding the **Cretans**; and a passage in **Statius** explained.

## Chapter xxxvi.
Solved and untangled here: the riddle from **Varro's** book that **Gellius** <note>Aulus Gellius, author of *Attic Nights*, who often preserved fragments of earlier, lost works.</note> cites.

## Chapter xxxvii.
That **Servius** <note>A 4th-century grammarian famous for his extensive commentary on Virgil.</note> fell into a foul error regarding the river **Oaxes**, and other matters.

<meta>This page continues the table of contents for this scholarly work, transitioning from discussions of rare vocabulary and Roman naming conventions into philological corrections of legal and poetic texts. The previous page ended mid-sentence regarding a term in Pliny.</meta>

### Chapter xxxviii.
An explanation of a poem by **Juvenal**, which goes against all previous opinions regarding the **Laconicum** <note>original: "Laconico"; likely referring to a Spartan-style dry-sweat room in a bath-house or a specific type of Spartan garment.</note> and the **pitylisma** <note>original: "pityliſmate"; from the Greek *pytilisma*, referring to the act of spitting out or spraying wine during a tasting.</note>.

### Chapter xxxviiii.
A **riddle** <term>original: "Scirpus"; literally a bulrush, used metaphorically by the Romans to mean a knotty problem or a riddle</term> by **Ausonius** <note>A 4th-century Roman poet and teacher.</note> solved in various ways: concerning the daughters of **Cadmus**; the **Melon**; the **Cuttlefish**; and **Cnidian knots**.

### Chapter xxxx.
An epigram by the same poet <note>Ausonius</note>, which is similar to a riddle, corrected and explained.

### Chapter xxxxi.
That in the **Digests of Emperor Justinian**, the phrase "**deferred day**" <note>original: "diem diffuſum"</note> is incorrectly read instead of "**adjourned day**" <note>original: "diffiſum"; a legal term meaning to split or postpone a day of trial</note>; just as in **Gellius**, "to be spread" is read for "to be split," as well as "definitions" for "adjournments." Also, some remarks regarding the **Florentine Pandects** <note>A famous 6th-century manuscript of Roman law held in Florence, essential for reconstructing the original text of Justinian's legal code.</note>.

### Chapter xxxxii.
That **thumbs** are pressed to show favor, just as they are turned to deny favor; and from this, the meanings in **Horace**, **Juvenal**, and **Prudentius** are clarified. <note>This refers to the Roman custom of *pollice presso* (thumb pressed) to signal mercy for a gladiator, vs. *pollice verso* (thumb turned) to signal death.</note>

### Chapter xxxxiii.
How many **diphthongs** the ancients possessed.

### Chapter xxxxiiii.
That one should read "**Pegasean nectar**" in the works of **Persius**, not "**Pegasean melody**" <note>original: "melos"</note>.

### Chapter xxxxv.
That **Achilles** was younger than **Patroclus**, contrary to what **Aeschylus** recorded or what is commonly believed.

### Chapter xxxxvi.
The word **Cacoethes** <term>original: "Cacoethes"; a Greek term used by Juvenal to describe an "insatiable itch" or a malignant habit, specifically of writing.</term> in **Juvenal** has four syllables, not three. Also, one should read "**Lyde with the box stored away**" <note>original: "cōdita pyxide lyde"</note> rather than "un-stored." Simultaneously, a passage in **Martianus Capella** is explained, I believe, with no little care.

### Chapter xxxxvii.
What **Pliny** recorded concerning the "hanging" titles of ancient artists, and how these same things are recognized on ancient monuments in **Rome**; and likewise the appearance of the **Vergilian aegis** <note>The shield or breastplate associated with Zeus and Athena, described in the works of Virgil.</note>.

->Chapter xxxxviii.<-

That others have written about **Megabyzus** <note>A Persian nobleman; the anecdote usually involves him visiting a painter's studio.</note>: what **Pliny** says of **Alexander**: that he was accustomed to frequent the workshop of **Apelles** <note>The most famous painter of ancient Greece, and the only one Alexander the Great would allow to paint his portrait.</note>.

->Chapter xxxxviiii.<-

A competition of epigrams between the Greek **Posidippus** and the Latin **Ausonius** regarding the image of **Opportunity** <note>Represented as the personification *Occasio* or *Kairos*, usually depicted with hair in front but bald behind to show she must be grasped as she approaches.</note>: then a most beautiful **description** <term>original: "ecphrasis"; a literary device providing a vivid, dramatic description of a work of visual art</term> by the Greek **Callistratus**.

->Chapter l.<-

A passage in the copies of **Pliny** corrected regarding the name of the **Proetides** <note>The daughters of King Proetus, who were famously punished with madness for their impiety.</note>: and the history of their madness related therein.

->Chapter li.<-

Why "soft honors" <note>original: "molles... honores"</note> are so named by **Martial** in the temple of **Ephesian Diana**.

->Chapter lii.<-

What the "altar crowded with horns" <note>original: "Cornibus ara frequens"</note> refers to in **Martial**.

->Chapter liii.<-

That the name of **Agamemnon** placed in **Marcus Tullius's** <note>Cicero's</note> *On Divination* stands for **Ulysses**: then, in passing, a quite elegant passage in the *Letters to Atticus* is corrected and explained regarding **vermilion wax** <note>original: "miniatula cera"; red wax used for marking or sealing letters.</note>.

->Chapter liiii.<-

Who the **Horned ones** <term>original: "Ceratinae"; a type of logical paradox or sophism where one is trapped by their own answers</term> are in **Quintilian**.

->Chapter lv.<-

Who the **Crocodiles** <term>original: "Crocodilitę"; another logical puzzle involving a crocodile returning a child only if the parent guesses correctly what the crocodile will do</term> are in the same author.

->Chapter lvi.<-

Why **Martial** spoke of a "**double horn**" in the spectacles: where he discusses the **Rhinoceros** and **Ethiopian bulls**.

->Chapter lvii.<-

What the ***Terraones*** <note>A rare term in Suetonius, likely referring to some form of earth-born creature or spirit.</note> are in **Suetonius**: and an error corrected in the copies of **Pliny**.

->Chapter lviii.<-

The origin and ritual of the **Secular Games** <note>The *Ludi saeculares*, a major Roman religious celebration occurring roughly once a century.</note>: and other things pertaining to it: the oracle of the **Sibyl** cited therein: then some things explained in passing, and others refuted to good effect.

### Chapter lix. )

That Ovid, not Virgil, appears to be the author of the *Priapeia* <note>A collection of poems dedicated to the fertility god Priapus, often debated by scholars regarding its true authorship</note>. That the little verse found in Martial—"Laugh, if you are wise, O girl, laugh"—is undoubtedly by Naso <note>original: "Nasonis"; the family name of the poet Ovid</note>; but it seems to be quoted from his epigrams rather than from either his *Art of Love* or his tragedy *Medea*; and shortly after, something new and wonderful regarding Naso's *Medea* is added.

### Chapter lx. )

The word *Agon* <term>original: "Agon"; a sacrificial cry or ritual question asked by the priest's assistant</term>: which the *Victimarius* <note>The attendant at a Roman sacrifice who struck the animal</note> was accustomed to use: regarding which Ovid also writes in the *Fasti*.

### Chapter lxi. )

An error in the manuscripts of Pliny concerning the terms "tame" <note>original: "cicura"</note> and "wine": and that "Aconite" <note>a poisonous plant, wolfsbane</note> is incorrectly written instead of "Hemlock" <note>original: "conio"</note> in the works of Macrobius.

### Chapter lxii. )

What "ram's testicles" are: and what the "middle finger" refers to in the works of Tullius <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero</note>.

### Chapter lxiii. )

That the words of Terence are cited by Varro differently than they are found in the copies or acknowledged by Donatus <note>A famous 4th-century grammarian and commentator on the playwright Terence</note>.

### Chapter lxiv. )

Verses of Ausonius explained: and also Ovid’s verses regarding the *Choliambic* or *Scazon* <term>original: "Choliābo aut Scazōte"; a "limping" poetic meter where the final rhythmic foot is reversed for a jarring effect</term>.

### Chapter lxv. )

What the posture of a "peacemaker" is in statues: and that Statius refers to it in the first book of his *Silvae*.

### Chapter lxvi. )

A passage in Plautus's *Mostellaria* <note>The Haunted House</note>, and likewise another in his *Miles Gloriosus* <note>The Braggart Soldier</note>, cleared of errors.

### Chapter lxvii. )

That the crow appears to be a symbol of concord among the ancients: not the stork, as everyone believes regarding a passage in Juvenal.

### Chapter lxviii. )

Verses from Callimachus regarding the hair of Queen Berenice <note>Refers to the "Lock of Berenice," a famous poem about a queen who sacrificed her hair to the gods</note>: and from there, an error removed from a little verse by Catullus.

### Chapter lxix. )

That *Oarion* <note>A Greek-influenced spelling of the constellation Orion</note> is the correct reading in Catullus: which those others read as *Aorion*.

### Chapter LXXXII.
That in the *Digest* <note>original: "digestis"; the massive collection of Roman legal opinions compiled under Emperor Justinian</note>, under the title "On Disclosure," where it is read "to another," it is more appropriate to read "to the album" <note>original: "ad album"; referring to the white board where the Roman Praetor’s edicts were posted for public view</note>.

### Chapter LXXXIII.
Concerning Harpocrates <note>The Greek adaptation of the Egyptian god Horus, often depicted with a finger to his lips as a symbol of silence</note>.

### Chapter LXXXIV.
Concerning freedmen who are called "dediticii" <note>A legal class of former slaves who had surrendered unconditionally; they were granted freedom but denied the rights of Roman citizenship</note>.

### Chapter LXXXV.
How the Games of Ceres <note>The Cerialia, a major Roman festival</note> are to be understood in the works of Juvenal; and concerning Memmius, the founder of the Cereal games.

### Chapter LXXXVI.
Concerning the "tenth wave" or "decuman wave" <note>original: "fluctu decimo seu decumano"; a classical belief that every tenth wave was larger and more dangerous than the others</note>, which very many poets have mentioned.

### Chapter LXXXVII.
That the word "Philorheon" in a letter of Cicero was later wickedly corrupted into the name "Philoctetes."

### Chapter LXXXVIII.
From where that phrase is derived: "We were Trojans" <note>original: "Fuimus troes"; a famous line from Virgil's Aeneid meaning 'Troy is no more'</note>; and what records exist concerning the Spartan choir and song.

### Chapter LXXXIX.
That Servius the grammarian <note>A famous 4th-century commentator on Virgil</note> reads the *Bucolic* verses of Virgil <note>original: "Maronis"</note> as faultily as he explains them incorrectly. Also noted and related there are certain matters regarding the initiation of boys, and concerning various gods of the ancients, especially the *Genii* <note>guardian spirits of men</note> and *Junones* <note>guardian spirits of women</note>.

### Chapter LXXXX.
What sort of man Theodore Gaza <note>A 15th-century Greek scholar who translated many works into Latin</note> was; and yet how much he slipped in his translation of Aristotle's *Problems*.

### Chapter LXXXXI.
In what way the same man translated into Greek that phrase which appears in Cicero as "the marrow of Persuasion" <note>original: "Suadae medulla"; a metaphor for the quintessence of oratorical power</note>, and the polished verses of Eupolis concerning Pericles; then it is shown by what Latin names the goddess Pitho <note>The Greek personification of persuasion</note> is designated.

### Chapter LXXXII.
Who the *Causarii* <note>original: "Causarii"; soldiers discharged from military service due to ill health, injury, or other specific reasons</note> are in the works of Livy.

A certain preface by the Emperor Justinian <note>the 6th-century Byzantine ruler famous for codifying Roman Law</note> defended against several faults and errors.

The charming commentary of Severianus <note>likely Severianus of Gabala, a bishop known for his oratorical works on creation</note> regarding the creation of the moon and sun, and concerning monthly and annual days.

An elegant Greek proverb restored to the copies of the *Digest* <note>the *Digest* or *Pandects*, a massive compendium of Roman legal jurists' writings</note> regarding *xenia* <note>original: "xeniis"; gifts given to guests or, in this legal context, small tokens given to public officials</note>.

### Chapter LXXXVI.
How much deceit there is in mice; and an interpretation of the words of Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus, a writer who cataloged the nature and behavior of animals</note>; and therein a passage in Martial <note>the Roman epigrammatist</note> corrected and explained.

### Chapter LXXXVII.
That "automaton" should be read in the works of Suetonius <note>the Roman biographer of the Caesars</note> where it is now commonly read as *ornatum* <note>meaning "adorned"</note>; and what is signified by that word.

### Chapter LXXXVIII.
A passage of Persius and likewise of Seneca clarified; and concerning the manumission <note>the formal legal process of freeing a slave</note> of slaves by the rod and by spinning them around <note>original: "festuca" and "circumactu"; refers to the ritual where a slave was turned in a circle by their master to symbolize their new status as a free person</note>.

### Chapter LXXXVIIII.
Why those who won the sacred games <note>original: "hieronicae"; from the Greek "hieronikes," meaning victors in the prestigious Panhellenic games</note> were accustomed to tear down part of a wall, as Suetonius says.

### Chapter C.
<note>The chapter numbering in the original source jumps from 89 to 100.</note>
What the "silences of the moon" are in the works of Maro <note>Virgil</note>, or the "silent maiden" in Horace; and the opinions of Servius and Acron <note>influential ancient commentators on Virgil and Horace</note> on these matters refuted.
