### Chapter One.

#### Cicero defended against calumny: concerning the explanation of the power of the new term in Aristotle: which is Endelechia.

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap "A" is surrounded by a wreath of dark leaves and red flowers, tied at the top with a red ribbon.</image-desc>

John Argyropoulos <note>A prominent Greek scholar and refugee from Constantinople who became a central figure in the revival of Greek learning in Renaissance Italy</note> the Byzantine, formerly our teacher in philosophy, was considered most learned not only in Latin letters (to which he was by no means indifferent) but also in all the decrees of wisdom and the disciplines which are called "cyclic" <note>the seven liberal arts: grammar, rhetoric, logic, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy</note> by Martianus <note>Martianus Capella, a 5th-century author whose work defined the liberal arts for the Middle Ages</note>. This man enjoyed great authority and favor both with Cosimo de' Medici—who was named Father of the Fatherland by public decree—and then with Cosimo’s son Piero, and even with his grandson Lorenzo, our current glory, who is the heir to his father's and grandfather's virtue and the pillar of his Republic. To them he dedicated books of Aristotle that he translated into Latin; he also instructed the barely pubescent Lorenzo himself in logic and that part of philosophy which teaches about morals <note>Ethics</note>.

However, being a Greek, he bore with an unfair spirit that noble and (as Theodore Gaza <note>Another famous Greek scholar who, unlike Argyropoulos, generally defended Latin culture</note> thinks) not inappropriate exclamation of Marcus Tullius Cicero, in which he proclaimed—perhaps no more eloquently than truly—that Greece was sometimes lacking in words, while he thought his own language <note>Latin</note> abounded in them. Therefore, somewhat angered by this father and prince of Latin eloquence, the Greek master even dared to keep saying (something our ears can still hardly bear): that Cicero was ignorant not only of philosophy, but even—God help us—of Greek literature. For it can hardly be said how reluctantly that nation <note>the Greeks</note> sometimes admits us, that is, Latin people, to a shared interest in their language and learning. For they believe that we possess the scraps of literature, while they have the fruit; we the trimmings, they the body; we the husks, they the kernel!

But to return to Argyropoulos, who in this way shut his eyes to our evident, living, and enduring fame: he used this argument above all: that in the first book of the *Tusculan Disputations*, Cicero writes that Aristotle believes there is a fifth nature, apart from the elements, from which the mind comes. Finally, his <note>Cicero's</note> words are read as follows: "He adds a fifth kind that lacks a name, and thus he calls the soul itself *entelecheia* <note>original: "entelechian"; Poliziano is addressing a famous philological dispute where Cicero was accused of confusing *entelecheia* (actuality) with *endelecheia* (continuity)</note> by a new name: as if it were a certain continuous and perennial motion!" But...

<summary>This page begins a defense of Cicero against the Greek scholar John Argyropoulos, who claimed Cicero misunderstood Aristotle's terminology regarding the soul. Poliziano frames this as part of a broader cultural rivalry between Greek and Latin scholars in Renaissance Italy.</summary>

<keywords>Cicero, John Argyropoulos, Aristotle, Entelechia, Endelechia, Medici family, Greek vs Latin scholarship, Tusculan Disputations</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced John Argyropoulos as a prestigious Greek scholar in the Medici circle who, despite his learning, held a biased view against Cicero’s command of Greek philosophy. This page continues with Argyropoulos’s specific linguistic critique regarding the term "entelecheia."</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>

There is no one, he used to say, who is even slightly frequent in the reading of Aristotle who does not know that *entelecheia* <note>original: "entelechian"; a technical term Aristotle coined to describe "actuality" or "perfection"</note> is rather an Aristotelian word—not at all signifying what Cicero thinks: "continuous and perennial motion," but rather perfection, or some kind of consummation. For since Aristotle wished to create, as it were, a nomenclature for his concepts, he himself conceived certain new words in his mind and then, as it were, gave birth to them. Among these was this word also, by which he indicates the form and species to which each thing owes its own perfection. For whatever a thing is, it is chiefly due to this *entelecheia*. 

Cicero, however—misled by the trickery of similarity—explained not this Aristotelian term, but another entirely contiguous and neighboring one. For *endeleches* <note>original: "Endelechos"</note> means "continually moving" or "continuous mobility"; it is from *endelechismos* that Cicero thought this *entelecheia* of Aristotle was derived—though the latter does not have the letter *d*, but rather a *t* <note>The OCR reads "r," but the context of the Greek *entelecheia* (εντελέχεια) versus *endelecheia* (ενδελέχεια) confirms the author is distinguishing between the letters Tau (t) and Delta (d)</note> in the second syllable. 

He used to add that any of the great Peripatetics <note>the followers of Aristotle’s school of philosophy</note> stood against Cicero. And as he was a man (as it seemed then) most sharp in disputation, and possessed of an ear "washed with biting vinegar" (as Persius <note>a Roman satirist; the phrase suggests a keen, critical, and sophisticated judgment</note> says), and moreover a great hurler of our own Latin words, he easily persuaded either us or his other followers at that time. He did so to such an extent that (it is almost a sacrilege even to say it) it was held as a granted fact among us that Marcus Tullius Cicero knew neither philosophy nor Greek letters. 

But truly, once the whole matter was more diligently investigated, I judged it to be my duty—and likewise the duty of every professor of Latin—to defend with all effort the glory of Cicero (through whom we especially stand our ground against the Greeks), even at the risk of my life <note>original: "vice capitis"; literally "in place of my head," a legal metaphor for a capital trial or a defense of one's very existence</note>, although not only his antiquity but a certain near-divinity has placed that glory beyond reach and above injury. 

First, therefore, the authority of this Argyropoulos—which carries no small weight even with me—must be refuted by other and, I believe, much stronger authorities. Then it must be shown that, from the very thing he is accused of, Cicero's renown is increased rather than diminished. And there will come forth into the public square (I believe) sufficiently suitable praisers of Cicero regarding philosophy (to satisfy ourselves with as few as possible): Boethius, Macrobius, and Augustine. For who is sharper than Boethius in logic? Or more subtle in mathematics? Or more enriched in philosophy?

<summary>The author describes how John Argyropoulos criticized Cicero for confusing the Aristotelian term "entelecheia" (perfection) with "endelecheia" (continuity), and announces his intention to defend Cicero’s reputation using the authority of later scholars like Boethius.</summary>

<keywords>Cicero, Aristotle, entelecheia, endelecheia, John Argyropoulos, linguistics, Peripatetics, Boethius, Macrobius, Saint Augustine, Greek vs Latin learning</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Cicero against the criticisms of John Argyropoulos mentioned on the previous page. While the previous page focused on a specific linguistic error regarding the word "entelecheia," this page appeals to the immense authority of historical scholars who revered Cicero’s philosophical contributions.</meta>

...or more sublime in theology? Whom even the later philosophers—by far the most perceptive of all, I think—held in such high regard: that he deserved to have that divine man, Thomas Aquinas <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274) was a premier philosopher and theologian who integrated Aristotelian thought with Christian principles</note>, and that great teacher of Thomas <note>Albertus Magnus, or Albert the Great (c. 1200–1280)</note> (Good God, what men!) as his commentators; and from his <note>Cicero's</note> opinion, there is now almost no appeal. Furthermore, regarding Macrobius <note>a 5th-century Roman provincial and writer famous for his commentary on Cicero’s "Dream of Scipio"</note>, who, I ask, is more to be believed than the especially praised Boethius <note>Boethius (c. 477–524) was a philosopher whose works became a bridge between classical antiquity and the Middle Ages</note>? Finally, the authority of Augustine himself is so deeply rooted in almost all disciplines that it is placed beyond all risk <note>original: "extra omnem sit aleam posita"; an idiom meaning "beyond all doubt" or "not subject to the roll of the dice"</note>; so much so that from it now, it is considered unlawful to depart even a "fingernail's breadth" <note>original: "transversum unguem"; a common Latin idiom for a tiny distance or the slightest degree</note> (as the saying goes). 

For it is well known that he flourished with such swiftness of intellect that whatever is taught about the art of speaking and debating, whatever about the dimensions of figures <note>geometry</note>, about music, or about numbers, he grasped without great difficulty and with no man to teach him. Then he showed himself with such sharpness in disputation that it seems the defense of the whole truth relies most especially on this one man. Let us consider, therefore, what each of these men thought of Cicero, and how much they attributed to him in philosophy; then let us believe boldly: it is not the place of just anyone to stir up slander against a man who has been so uniquely praised by the most highly praised men. 

Boethius, therefore, not only uses the testimonies of Marcus Tullius <note>Cicero</note> repeatedly, casting them as the firmest and most inviolable arguments, but he does not even blush to undertake the interpretation of his *Topics* <note>original: "topicorum"; Cicero’s work on how to find arguments for logic and rhetoric</note> (which is the greatest honor). Macrobius, moreover, dwells so much on even a small part of that one and the same book <note>Cicero’s "The Dream of Scipio"</note> that he did not hesitate to make him an equal to Plato, nor to distinguish him with the titles of all the disciplines. 

Finally, Augustine, although in his *Academics* <note>Augustine’s "Contra Academicos," a philosophical dialogue</note>—though speaking through the persona of another—calls him now "his Cicero," now "the wise man," at last says that philosophy was both begun and perfected in the Latin language by him. Then in his *Confessions*, where he is not wearing a mask at all, he condemns those who admire the tongue of Cicero but not his heart; indeed, [he describes] how his own affection was changed! How he turned his prayers and vows to God! How he no longer had the same desires he had before! How all vain hope grew cheap to him! How he began to thirst for immortality and wisdom with an incredible heat of the heart! Finally, how he already began to rise up, so that...

<summary>The author defends Cicero by citing the high regard in which he was held by intellectual giants like Thomas Aquinas, Boethius, Macrobius, and Augustine. He specifically highlights how Augustine credited Cicero’s "Hortensius" with sparking his spiritual and philosophical conversion.</summary>
<keywords>Cicero, Thomas Aquinas, Boethius, Macrobius, St. Augustine, Latin philosophy, Scholasticism, Academics, Confessions, defense of Cicero</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Cicero's intellectual and spiritual legacy. Having previously cited the authority of Thomas Aquinas, Albertus Magnus, and Boethius, the author now focuses on the testimony of Saint Augustine and Saint Jerome, before transitioning to a defense of Cicero’s mastery of the Greek language.</meta>

...might return to God! He [Augustine] confesses that all this was the gift of Cicero; and he credited the entire occasion of his salvation to that one book called *Hortensius* <note>A now-lost dialogue by Cicero that famously inspired Saint Augustine to pursue philosophy and eventually convert to Christianity</note>. For he says that the book was not offered to "sharpen his tongue"—that is, for the sake of mere rhetoric—but rather that its message had persuaded him. If only those people would read this, who cast in our teeth that dream of the sick Jerome being lashed before the judgment seat <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420) famously recounted a dream where he was brought before the divine tribunal and scourged for being a "Ciceronian" rather than a "Christian"</note>! Nor do they even consider that Jerome himself, when he was later accused of perjury by Rufinus <note>Tyrannius Rufinus (c. 345–410), a friend-turned-rival of Jerome who accused him of hypocrisy for continuing to read classical authors</note> for immediately returning to the reading of Cicero, complained most bitterly that his own dreams were being thrown back at him by that man. 

But (to return to the point), let Argyropoulos <note>John Argyropoulos (1415–1487), a Greek scholar in Italy who was known for his critical views on Cicero’s grasp of philosophy</note> or any other "Scourge of Cicero" <note>original: "Ciceromastix"; a term modeled on 'Homeromastix,' used for those who vitriolically attack a famous author</note> certainly prevail! But I ask: let him first dare to climb over these masses of authorities that we have set in his way. 

Indeed, regarding Greek literature, the judgment is even easier and more favorable. For who has such a rough ear, or who is so hostile to the truth, that if he should catch even the scent of a few—and almost haphazard—Greek words scattered here and there in Cicero's letters, he would not say that he has never found anything more elegant or more "Attic" <note>referring to the pure, refined style associated with Athens</note> in all of Greece? Thus, he rightly deterred that most learned man Posidonius <note>a Stoic philosopher and polymath</note> from writing; and he rightly threw the Greek nation into confusion with his Greek "Commentary on His Own Consulship," in which he says he exhausted the entire "perfume-cabinet" <note>original: "myrhothecion"; a Greek-derived metaphor for a collection of rhetorical ornaments or "scents"</note> of Isocrates <note>an influential Greek rhetorician</note>, all the jewelry boxes of his disciples, and even some of the "pigments" of Aristotle. 

Because of this confidence, it seems to me that he at times extended the "boundary" <note>original: "pomerium"; the sacred boundary of a city, used here as a metaphor for the limits of a language</note> of the Greek language no less than the Latin. Let even this be an argument (so that we may be content with one example for now) that he denies the word for "innocence" <margin>.</margin> has a common name among the Greeks, but says they could nevertheless use *Eulabia* <note>original: "Εὐλάβεια" (Eulabeia); meaning caution, discretion, or piety</note>. Then he also sometimes chastises a certain "laziness" in some of their words, as when he says they wish to make a distinction but lack the strength of the specific word; they prove this in the terms that exist, such as "melancholy" and "madness," or likewise in the fact that they use the word *ponon* <note>original: "πόνον" (ponon)</note> for "toil" too confusingly, taking it for both "labor" and "pain" alike. Then he indicates that we [Latins] say some things better than they do, such as *convivium* <note>literally a "living together," whereas the Greek "symposion" means a "drinking together"</note> and others; and that certain things are noted much better with Latin words than Greek—which he signifies is found in many places, but also that the Greeks themselves lack a name for these things.

<summary>The author cites Saint Augustine’s conversion and Saint Jerome’s struggles to defend Cicero’s moral value, before arguing that Cicero possessed a mastery of the Greek language that rivaled and sometimes surpassed the Greeks themselves.</summary>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Cicero's intellectual reputation. Having previously discussed the respect shown to him by the Church Fathers (Augustine and Jerome), the author now addresses accusations regarding Cicero's mastery of the Greek language and his specific translation of Aristotelian philosophical terms.</meta>

...he does not hide the fact that he is most familiar with whatever matter is at hand. None of those [critics], however much they tortured themselves in seeking, have been able—in their ineptitude—to find or even devise a name for it yet.

Was it not also that famous Apollonius Molon <note>A renowned Greek teacher of rhetoric from Rhodes who taught both Cicero and Julius Caesar</note>, the most celebrated of all the rhetoricians of his time, who, having heard Marcus Tullius [Cicero] deliver a Greek speech just once—and an extemporaneous one at that—is said to have stood transfixed for a long time? Finally, in a state of wonder, he is reported to have pronounced: "Indeed, Cicero, I both praise and admire you! But I pity the fate of the Greeks, and I am weary; for the beauties of humanity and eloquence, which were the only things we had left for ourselves, are now—as far as I can see—being claimed by the Romans through you."

Truly, this judgment was recorded not by some Latin, nor by some inept person, but by Plutarch himself <note>The famous Greek-Roman biographer and philosopher (c. 46–120 AD)</note>, a man of Greek spirit but of Roman gravity in literature. Therefore, in this very manner, the ancients have left us testimony concerning his knowledge and his mastery of the Greek language.

Come now, let us also weigh that term *Endelechia* <note>original: "Endelechian"; a term Cicero used to describe the "perpetual motion" or continuity of the soul, which some critics argued was a misspelling of Aristotle's "Entelecheia"</note>, the late slanderer of Cicero. Is he criticized here because he said Aristotle employed a certain "fifth nature" <note>The *quintessence*, a substance distinct from the four earthly elements (earth, air, fire, water), which Aristotle associated with the heavens and the soul</note> beyond the elements, and thus named the soul itself with a new name?

That this is the case is shown by Alexander of Aphrodisias <note>A celebrated commentator on Aristotle (c. 200 AD)</note>, the most praised of all the Peripatetics even by Averroes <note>The 12th-century Islamic philosopher known as "The Commentator" on Aristotle</note>, in those exceptionally subtle books of *Natural Questions*. Saint Jerome also boasts that these were read by him. Furthermore, Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 AD)</note> in the commentary he published on Aristotle's *Ten Categories* through questions and answers; and Themistius <note>A 4th-century rhetorician and philosopher</note> after them in his paraphrase of the second book *On the Soul*; and Simplicius <note>A 6th-century commentator</note>, where they interpret Aristotle’s volume of the same argument; and Quintilianus Aristides <note>A 3rd-century author of a treatise on music</note> in his third book *On Music*; and finally, Saint Ambrose in a certain letter of his (to pass over others for now), have almost unanimously signified this. Regarding this point, there is certainly no doubt even in the very words of Aristotle himself.

Or do they rather blame him because he did not write the word *Endelechia* in its own [Greek] letters, and applied a different meaning than Aristotle intended (as we said above)? First, therefore, I ask of those men: by what argument exactly do they gather that *entelechia* <note>meaning "actuality" or "completion"</note> rather than *endelechia* <note>meaning "continuity" or "duration"</note> was written by Aristotle? For this word is just as new as that one, and signifies no less than the other.

<summary>The author defends Cicero's Greek proficiency by citing the praise of Apollonius Molon and addresses the philological debate over whether the soul is defined by "actuality" (entelecheia) or "perpetual motion" (endelecheia).</summary>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Cicero’s use of the Aristotelian term "Endelechia." The author argues that Cicero’s version of the word might actually be more authentic than the one used by later Greek commentators, citing the famous historical account of how Aristotle’s original manuscripts were lost, damaged by dampness, and poorly edited centuries later.</meta>

...[of the] soul, than the other can; nor did Aristotle himself pronounce by that new word <note>Referring to *Entelecheia*</note> "perfection" rather than that "continuous motion" <note>Cicero’s *Endelechia*</note> being indicated. Indeed, Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 AD)</note>, Themistius, Simplicius, or any similar commentators might have believed so. But what, finally, is this envy? Should it matter that Cicero thought differently than those who came later, if he himself can stand his ground and defend his position well and bravely? 

For whether the truer reading is the one Cicero recognizes, or the one that prevailed among later thinkers, is still uncertain. For all the Aristotelian books—which now carry such great authority—were originally published interpolated and full of many errors, if we believe the testimony of Strabo <note>The Greek geographer and historian (c. 64 BC–24 AD)</note>, who is not a bad authority. 

It is said that Neleus of Scepsis, the son of Coriscus, was not only a student of Aristotle and Theophrastus but also the successor and heir to their library; he is reported to have carried the books of both philosophers to Scepsis (that is, his own homeland) in their entirety. Afterward, his descendants—fearing the Attalid kings, who were very zealous collectors of such books—buried them in the ground, where they were neglected for a long time. 

Finally, they are said to have been bought for a great price by Apellicon of Teos. But, as was inevitable, they were eaten away by rot and dampness in many places and weakened. However, Apellicon himself, being more a lover of books than of philosophy, dared to fill in the gaps rather unwisely and amend them according to his own judgment (or so he thought), defiling them with many errors. 

These books, however, lay hidden in Athens until the time of Sulla <note>The Roman general and dictator who sacked Athens in 86 BC</note> (as Plutarch records). For the old Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle’s school</note>, lacking these standard works on which all philosophy is built, had only a few which they called "exoteric" <term>Exoteric (outward) works were intended for the general public, whereas "esoteric" works were for advanced students</term>. Therefore, later thinkers philosophized more exquisitely because Aristotle's volumes, though contaminated in places, were widely read. 

Indeed, Lucius Sulla was the first to transfer this library of Apellicon, seized in Athens, to Rome. Then the grammarian Tyrannion and his near-equal Andronicus of Rhodes—the one who even denies that the books *On Interpretation* <note>original: "perihermenias"</note> are by Aristotle—made the books of that philosopher public in great quantity, with some sort of lists published. Wherefore there is nothing that we should now doubt, since concerning the primeval purity of that reading—especially with such a tiny difference—suspicion is either free to go either way, or (if one must side with one)...

He cites the history of the library's journey from Scepsis to Rome via Sulla to undermine the authority of Cicero's critics.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the defense of Cicero’s use of the term *Endelechia* (continuous motion) versus the later standard *Entelecheia* (perfection). The previous page ended with the history of Aristotle’s damaged manuscripts, which were poorly restored by Apellicon of Teos, suggesting that later Greek versions of Aristotle's text might be less reliable than the one Cicero accessed.</meta>

...[it is better] that Cicero be preferred as far earlier than Alexander <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias (fl. c. 200 AD), a famous commentator on Aristotle</note>, who was the first from that group we mentioned to be a commentator on Aristotle. Alexander flourished in the times of Emperor Severus, as his own work *On Fate* indicates; he was almost a contemporary of—or perhaps, I believe, slightly older than—those greatest academics, Origen <note>The Christian theologian and Neoplatonist</note> and Plotinus <note>The founder of Neoplatonism</note>. 

What, then, prevents Cicero himself from having been able to see the primary source <note>original: "matricem"</note> of Aristotle's books which were published in his own age? Even if they were not uncorrupted, they were certainly (as we have said) already edited and scribbled upon. Furthermore, I would believe it was done deliberately by such a most learned man: that, protected by the free wall of his own heart, he dared to rightly adapt that interpretation <note>Cicero’s definition of the soul as "continuous motion"</note> to this new term of Aristotle's. For this interpretation agreed with Plato’s opinion in the *Phaedrus* regarding the eternal motion of the soul, and with the views of Dio, as Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), a prolific Roman scholar</note> says. Cicero himself mentions this either in his *Tusculan Disputations* or in the sixth volume of his *Republic*.

Indeed, Philoponus in his *Life of Aristotle*, Simplicius in his *Commentaries on the Soul*, and Boethius himself in the final book of his commentary on *Perihermenias* <note>Aristotle's "On Interpretation"</note>, all asserted that the opinions of both philosophers <note>Plato and Aristotle</note> are kindred and comparable. It is also handed down that Porphyry composed seven books in which this very argument was gathered: that the school of thought <note>original: "heresin"</note> of Plato and Aristotle is precisely the same. 

This is a topic that my friend Pico della Mirandola <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous Renaissance humanist and philosopher</note> has also treated in a certain preface to his disputations. Truly, he will prevail with a most extensive and (I believe) most beautiful work—unless my own taste fails me—regarding this very harmony <note>original: "concordia"</note> of Plato and Aristotle, which he labors over and forges night and day. 

Thus, it will be understood that our Cicero not only did not err in using that term, but perhaps acted even more eruditely than the rest, or at least more suitably for his own school of thought and writings. Just as the authors we cited above—who are quite suitable witnesses—guaranteed the learning of this most famous man, he himself has provided most generously and abundantly, even in that particular point where he most often suffers slander. 

Nor is it a new or unusual thing for Cicero to suffer slander from the Greeks, since even Didymus <note>Didymus Chalcenterus (c. 63 BC–10 AD), a Greek scholar in Alexandria</note> composed six books against him, imitating the *Sillographoi* <note>A term for writers of satirical or scurrilous poems</note> and malicious writers; yet those books were sharply condemned by the judgment of the learned. So...

He cites numerous ancient and contemporary authorities, including Pico della Mirandola, who advocate for the fundamental unity of Platonic and Aristotelian philosophy.</summary>

...even certain dogs, who usually bark by the lamp <note>original: "lucernarii canes"; a metaphor for petty critics who work late at night</note>, often bark from a distance with despicable voices against the most noble lion.

## Chapter II

### What the "Carpatine" sandals in Catullus are: a correctly written word that has been wrongly changed.

Valerius Catullus <note>A famous Roman lyric poet of the 1st century BC</note> says the following in one of his epigrams:
> If that tongue of yours were ever put to use, you could
> Lick backsides and Carpatine sandals. <note>original: "Culos: & crepidas lingere carpatinas." Catullus 98.</note>

Many have asked, yet no one has so far explained, what these "Carpatine"—or "Carbatine"—sandals are (for both spellings are correct). Some even say they are "carbasine" <note>meaning made of "carbasus" or fine Spanish flax</note>. However, certain tricksters and "dwellers in the shadows" of literature <note>original: "tenebriones in litteris"</note> have erased the ancient word! They substitute I know not what—either "cercopythas" or "coprotinas"—words brought forth from the pigsty rather than the school; naked words, hollow names, and utterances of no value. I shall bring forth authorities from the Greek storehouse—as if from a rich pantry—authorities that are not to be despised nor whose credit is to be denied. By these, the reading may be preserved unharmed and the cloud of misunderstanding may be cleared away.

First, therefore, Julius Pollux <note>A Greek grammarian and sophist of the 2nd century AD</note> himself, in the ninth book of his work dedicated to Commodus Caesar, says that *carbatinas* are a rustic footwear named after the Carians <note>A people of Asia Minor</note>. Aristotle, moreover, in the second book of his *History of Animals*, writes that camels are shod with *carbatinas* so that they do not break down from exhaustion on longer journeys during a military campaign. 

Furthermore, there exist four very elegant Greek books titled *Pastoral* <note>original: "poemenicon"; likely referring to Longus's *Daphnis and Chloe*</note>; in the second of these, an old man is introduced with a leather satchel and *carbatine* shoes. Lucian, too, in the dialogue titled either *Alexander* or *The False Prophet*, says that certain orators from Paphlagonia were shod in *carbatinas*. 

Finally, even Xenophon the Socratic, in the third book of his *Expedition Up-Country* <note>original: "Anabaseos"</note>, says: "When their old shoes had failed, they had *carbatinas* made from fresh ox-hides." This is the same passage that Suidas <note>The author of a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedic lexicon</note> cites, though he conceals the name of the original author. Indeed, a certain commentator on that same Xenophon adds that *carbatinas* are a "barbaric footwear."

<vocab>Catullus, carpatinae, carbatinæ, Julius Pollux, Aristotle, Xenophon, Lucian, Suidas, footwear, sandals</vocab>

<summary>The author investigates the meaning of "carpatine" (or carbatinæ) sandals mentioned in Catullus’s 98th epigram, defending the original term against scribal corruptions. He provides extensive evidence from Greek authors—including Aristotle, Xenophon, and Pollux—to prove they are rustic shoes made of raw leather.</summary>

# Chapter IV

->Regarding the words by which Horace signaled the "camelopard" (giraffe): what the appearance of that animal is, by what names it is known, and when it was first seen in Italy.<-

Horace, in his letter to Augustus, says: "A diverse race, the panther mixed with the camel" <note>original: "Diversum confusa genus panthera chamelo"; Horace, *Epistles* 2.1.196</note>. We long ago declared in a public lecture that he seems to be thinking of the <term>camelopardalis</term> <note>a "camel-leopard," the ancient name for a giraffe, based on its camel-like neck and leopard-like spots</note>, which is commonly called a "Giraffa." For what we call a "panther," the Greeks call a *pardalis*. 

And we saw this animal ourselves, sent among other gifts by the King of Egypt himself (who is called the Sultan), as a present to Lorenzo de' Medici—a patron not so much of mine as of all talents and virtues <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" de' Medici received a famous giraffe in Florence in 1487, which caused a sensation in Renaissance Italy</note>. We were greatly amazed that it had little horns, especially since it was a male, for we had read nothing of these in ancient records until now. 

Furthermore, the Greek writer Heliodorus—an author of no small authority—mentions it in the tenth book of his *Ethiopian Story* <note>original: "Aethiopica"; a popular ancient Greek novel about the adventures of Chariclea and Theagenes</note> in almost this way, when he relates that it was given as a gift to Hydaspes, the King of the Ethiopians, by the ambassadors of the <term>Auxomites</term> <note>People from the Kingdom of Aksum, located in modern-day Ethiopia and Eritrea</note>. 

"Finally," he says, "there arrived also the ambassadors of the Auxomites, not as taxpayers but as friends and allies. Showing their goodwill for successful deeds, they offered other gifts and, most notably, a certain animal of unusual appearance and nature, whose height reached the size of a camel. The color of its skin was variegated with spots, like those of a panther, as if blooming. The hind parts beneath the belly sloped toward the ground, just as we see in lions. Its shoulders, front feet, and chest were tall beyond the proportion of its other limbs. The neck was slender, extending from the rest of the body's mass into a swan-like neck. The head was similar to a camel. It was almost double the size of a Libyan ostrich <note>original: "libyssae struthi"</note>, and it blinked its eyes, moving them grimly. Its gait was far different from all other land or water animals; for it did not move its legs alternately, but moved both right legs together, and then both left legs. And with these it carried its sides suspended, with a movement and pull so easy that it could be led wherever one wished by a thin..."

<summary>This page identifies the "camelopard" mentioned by Horace as the giraffe and provides a detailed physical description based on both a contemporary encounter in the Medici court and ancient Greek literature.</summary>
<keywords>giraffe, camelopardalis, Horace, Lorenzo de' Medici, Heliodorus, Aksum, ancient biology, Renaissance Florence</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the author's firsthand observation of a giraffe sent to Lorenzo de' Medici and began citing Heliodorus’s ancient description of the animal.</meta>

with a halter bound to the top of its head, as if by a very strong bond, the handler led it. When this animal was seen, it immediately astounded the entire multitude: and its appearance found a name based on the most prominent features of its body, so that from that time the people called it a <term>chamelopardalis</term> <note>original: "chamelopardalin"; from the Greek words for camel and leopard, describing its shape and spots</note>. Thus far Heliodorus.

But Dion <note>Cassius Dio, a Roman statesman and historian of Greek origin</note>, also a Greek author, writing about Caesar in the forty-third book of his *Roman History*, writes almost thus: "He [Julius Caesar] was the first to bring the camelopard to Rome and show it to the people. That animal is like a camel in other respects, but it has unequal legs, the back ones being shorter than the front, so that from the haunches it seems to rise gradually, as if beginning an ascent. The rest of the body is supported very high by the front legs, but the neck raises itself to an almost peculiar height. It is spotted in color, like a panther, which is why it is known by a name blended from both."

But Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the famous *Natural History*</note>, dealing with camels, adds this also: "There is some similarity to these [camels] transferred into two other animals. The Ethiopians call it a *Nabun* <note>the ancient Ethiopic name for the giraffe, possibly related to the modern word "zarafa"</note>: with a neck similar to a horse, feet and legs like an ox, and a head like a camel; distinguished by a reddish color with white spots. From this it was called a Camelopardalis. It was first seen in Rome at the circus games of the Dictator Caesar. From that time on, it is seen occasionally, more conspicuous for its appearance than for any ferocity; for which reason it even earned the name 'wild sheep' <note>original: "ouis ferę" (ovis fera); the Romans called it this because of its gentle nature despite its size</note>."

Solinus <note>a 3rd-century Roman compiler known for his "Wonders of the World"</note> handed down almost identical details to these in the section where he speaks of the Ethiopians. But also Marcus Varro, in his book *On the Latin Language* addressed to Cicero, says: "The word 'Camel' came into Latin by its own Syrian name; just as from Alexandria, the 'Camelopardalis' was recently brought over, which was in shape like a camel and in spots like a panther." 

I also laughed recently because some grammarian or other—I know not who—had entered a halved word from Varro, just *pardalis* <note>leopard</note>, into his own lexicon, which is circulated arranged in alphabetical order. However, we have learned from the author Capitolinus <note>one of the traditional authors of the *Historia Augusta*</note> that one hundred of these "wild sheep" were exhibited at the games of the Prince Gordian <note>Roman Emperor Gordian III, who ruled in the 3rd century AD</note>.

->### Chapter IV.<-

How many things must be read by interpreters of poets: and how Persius fashioned his satire from Plato’s *Alcibiades*.

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical description of the giraffe (the camelopard) to a broader discussion on the necessity of wide-ranging classical and philosophical knowledge for any scholar attempting to interpret poetry. It specifically focuses on how the Roman satirist Persius drew upon Platonic philosophy.</meta>

¶ Anyone who undertakes the interpretation of poets must have labored late—as the saying goes—not only by the **lamp of Aristophanes** <note>Aristophanes was a Greek comic playwright; his "lamp" symbolizes the meticulous work of a grammarian or literary critic</note> but also by that of **Cleanthes** <note>Cleanthes was a Stoic philosopher; his "lamp" symbolizes rigorous philosophical study and moral discipline</note>. Furthermore, a scholar must examine not only the schools of the philosophers, but also those of the lawyers, the physicians, the logicians, and whatever branches of learning make up that "circle of knowledge" which we call the <term>encyclia</term> <note>original: "encyclia"; the "encyclopedic" or "all-round" education required of a free citizen in antiquity</note>, as well as the works of all the philologists. These subjects must not merely be glimpsed from afar, but looked into deeply; they should not just be greeted "from the threshold or the vestibule" <note>a proverbial expression meaning to have only a superficial acquaintance with a subject</note>, but rather summoned into the inner rooms and into the most intimate familiarity. This is essential if we strive to assist the cause of Latin letters and to put to flight the ignorance that grows stronger every day; otherwise, a "half-learned" diligence, coupled with great self-confidence, brings harm rather than benefit.

Nevertheless, one may find many things, especially in the great works of the philosophers, which have been adopted into the books of our own poets. I pointed this out several years ago while lecturing publicly on the poet **Persius** <note>Aulus Persius Flaccus, a Roman satirist known for his difficult, Stoic-influenced poetry</note>. His fourth Satire—the one beginning with the words, *Are you managing the people's business?* <note>original: "Rem populi tractas"</note>—is sketched out after the model of the Platonic dialogue called the *First Alcibiades*. It is clear that Persius did not merely borrow modestly from what **Socrates** discusses there with **Alcibiades** regarding the just and the unjust, or regarding self-knowledge; he also drew from that same source certain passages which, even if they are not of great weight, are certainly full of charm and wit.

An example of this is the line: *Tell me this, pupil of great Pericles* <note>original: "Dic hoc magni pupille Pericli"</note>, since Socrates also points out that the greatest advantage Alcibiades appeared to have was that **Pericles**, the son of Xanthippus, had been left to him by his father as a guardian. So too is the line: *I am the son of Dinomache* <note>original: "Dinomaches ego ſum"</note>. This is taken from what is written in **Plato**: *O dear son of Cleinias and Dinomache* <note>original: "ὦ φίλε παῖ κλεινίου καὶ Δεινομα΄χησ"</note>. Similarly, when Persius says in the same poem, *Live with yourself* <note>original: "Tecum habita"</note>, does he not seem to have foreseen the intent of that same dialogue? For indeed (as the commentator **Proclus** affirms), Plato here regarded nothing else but the Delphic inscription which warns that *each person should know himself*.

**Pico della Mirandola**, our dear friend, also touched upon this beautifully in his *Heptaplus* <note>A work by the famous Renaissance philosopher Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, providing a sevenfold interpretation of the creation story in Genesis</note>, which he is soon to publish, concerning the sevenfold narration of the six days of Genesis. Therefore, to have finally discovered these connections, or even to have compared them with one another, will produce not only profit for students but also a pleasure that is by no means common.

<summary>The author argues that a true literary scholar must master all fields of knowledge, particularly philosophy, illustrating this by showing how the poet Persius modeled his fourth Satire on Plato's First Alcibiades.</summary>
<keywords>philology, Persius, Plato, Alcibiades, Socrates, Pico della Mirandola, Heptaplus, encyclia, Stoicism, classical education</keywords>

This page shifts from general theory to a technical analysis of the term "wooden horse" in Lucretius and Valerius Flaccus.</meta>

<sig>e ii</sig>

### Chapter 5

On the identity of the **Duratean horse** <note>original: *Durateus equus*; the Wooden Horse of Troy</note> in Lucretius; and what ought to be read in Valerius Flaccus: not indeed "Dorian night," but "Durican," or rather "Durian," in the second book of the *Argonautica*.

Lucretius writes in the first book of *On the Nature of Things*: "Nor when the **wooden horse** <note>original: *durateus equus*</note> with its Trojan offspring had set the citadel of Troy ablaze in the night-time birth of the Greeks." He called that Trojan horse—which was built by **Epeius** <note>The legendary Greek architect who, instructed by Athena, built the wooden horse used to infiltrate Troy</note>—"Durateus" both skillfully and elegantly. This is a Greek poetic word that signifies "wooden." Indeed, Homer also calls this the *Durateon* horse in the eighth book of the *Odyssey*, saying: "But come now, change your theme and sing of the construction of the **wooden horse** <note>original: *δουρατέου* (*dourateou*)</note>, which Epeius made with the help of Athena." And likewise a little later: "the great **wooden horse** <note>original: *δουράτεον* (*dourateon*)</note> wherein all the best <note>original: *πάντες ἄριστοι*; referring to the elite Greek warriors hidden inside</note> sat." It is commonly called not *Durateon* but *durion* in the more common tongue, as the poet **Aristophanes** <note>A famous Greek comic playwright; the author notes that he used the more colloquial form of the word</note> also does.

Yet, **ignorant men** <note>original: *opici homines*; literally "Oscan men," a term used by ancient Romans and later humanists to describe people who were uncultured or lacked knowledge of Greek</note>, being unfamiliar with a word unknown to them, have begun to strike it out and replace *Durateus* with various bits of nonsense like *duratuens* or *duratenens*. To ensure they do not dare to attempt this further, I felt it would be worth the effort to include something about this in these miscellanies.

Furthermore, **Taddeo Ugoleto** of Parma <note>Taddeo Ugoleto (c. 1448–1513) was a famous Italian humanist and librarian to the Hungarian King</note>—a man of great learning and a courtier to **Matthias** <note>Matthias Corvinus (r. 1458–1490), King of Hungary and one of the greatest patrons of book-collecting in the Renaissance</note>, the most wise and invincible King of the Hungarians—showed me a very ancient manuscript of the *Argonautica* by Valerius Flaccus. I believe all other copies currently in use were derived from this one. In the second book, it reads: "The **Durican night** <note>original: *nox durica*</note> remains by the immovable fates," rather than "Dorian night" <note>original: *dorica*</note> as it is commonly read. This is certainly either the true and corrected reading, or (as I suspect more strongly) very close to the truth. As both usage and the sound of the word suggest, it should likely be "Durian night" [*nox duria*] in that passage—the night, that is, when that **wooden horse** <note>original: *equus Durius*</note> (which is the same as *Durateus*) was opened and released the Greeks hidden in its belly into the air. The diphthong should not be an obstacle, for the word is as much *Dourios* as it is *Doureios* in Greek.

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the correct Greek-derived terminology for the Trojan Horse in the works of Lucretius and Valerius Flaccus, the author now transitions into an interpretation of erotic metaphors in Catullus and behavioral signals of effeminacy in Roman satire and history.</meta>

## In what sense Catullus’s sparrow is to be understood: and a passage in Martial also indicated.

That **sparrow** <note>original: *passer*; a famous reference to the pet bird of Catullus’s lover, Lesbia</note> of Catullus, in my opinion, hides an allegorical and somewhat more obscene meaning which, for the sake of modesty, we cannot state openly. I am persuaded to believe this by that epigram of **Martial** <note>A Roman poet known for his witty and often ribald epigrams</note>, of which these are the final verses:

> Give me kisses, but such as Catullus gave;
> If they be as many as he said:
> I will give to you the sparrow of Catullus.

For the poet would be far too foolish (which it is a sin to believe) if he were saying he would give the boy a literal sparrow and not something else—which I suspect more—after the kisses. What this thing may be, I leave, out of respect for the modesty of my pen, to each reader’s own conjecture regarding the natural lewdness of the sparrow.

---

### Chapter 7

### That Juvenal alludes to "pathic" men: those who scratch their head with one finger; and this from the verses of the poet Calvus.

"Those who scratch their head with one finger": To be sure, no one doubts that in **Juvenal**'s <note>A Roman satirical poet</note> ninth satire, a "soft" <note>effeminate or sexually submissive</note> man is signified by this gesture. **Plutarch** also indicates in his *Life of Pompey* that **Pompey** <note>Pompey the Great, a leading Roman general and statesman</note> was thus marked by **Clodius** <note>Publius Clodius Pulcher, a populist politician and enemy of Pompey</note>. I will omit for now that he also touched upon this trait of Pompey in that little book titled *How one may profit even from enemies*. 

Moreover, **Ammianus Marcellinus** <note>A 4th-century Roman soldier and historian</note> says in the seventeenth book of his *History* that Pompey’s detractors observed this about him as something ridiculous and ineffective: that by a certain natural habit, he used to scratch his head with one finger. However, it is not merely a "soft" man that is designated by these words, but clearly a **pathic** <term>pathicus; the passive partner in male-male sexual acts</term> and a **draucus** <term>a term used in Roman satire for a virile or sexually active male partner, sometimes used derogatorily</term>. Juvenal attacks this kind of man throughout his entire satire. 

Otherwise, the author would be a weak and pimp-like attacker, or rather (if I may say so) a **Ficulnean poet** <note>original: *Ficulneus poeta*; likely a reference to the brittle wood of the fig tree, implying someone weak or worthless</note>, if he were now attacking merely the effeminate after having inveighed so far against the pathics. For he alludes, as it seems to me, to the verses of **Calvus** <note>Gaius Licinius Macer Calvus, an orator and poet of the late Roman Republic</note>...

<summary>The author interprets Catullus’s "sparrow" as an erotic allegory and explains the Roman cultural perception of scratching one's head with a single finger as a sign of sexual passivity or effeminacy, citing examples from Pompey the Great's life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter 7 regarding the poet Calvus’s insults toward Pompey the Great, specifically focusing on the gesture of scratching the head with one finger as a sign of effeminacy. It then transitions into Chapter 8, which explores the Egyptian origins of naming the days of the week after the seven planetary bodies.</meta>

...the poet's little verses against **Pompey** <note>Gnaeus Pompeius Magnus, the famous Roman general and rival of Julius Caesar</note>: concerning which it is written in **Seneca** <note>Seneca the Elder, in his work on rhetoric</note>, in his books of orators and rhetoricians. His poems also, if any survive, although they are playful, are full of great spirit. He says of Pompey: "He scratches his head with one finger: by which you might believe this person wants a man." <note>original: *Digito caput uno, scalpit: quo credas hunc velle virum.* In Roman culture, scratching the head with a single finger was a stereotypical gesture used to mock men perceived as effeminate or overly concerned with their appearance.</note> Thus far Seneca concerning **Calvus** <note>Gaius Licinius Macer Calvus, an orator and poet</note>. Therefore, when the poet **Juvenal** attempted to indicate that all the "pathics" <note>original: *pathicos*; a derogatory term for men perceived as sexually submissive or effeminate</note> were flocking to Rome—that is, those who desire a man for themselves—he wittily derived this from a fragment of Calvus’s verses. These were then (as is probable) very well known, through which the implications would be recognized. I believe that **Porcius Latro** <note>A celebrated Roman declaimer and teacher of rhetoric</note>, in a certain declamation recorded in the same Seneca, was referring to these aforementioned verses of Calvus. He says: "Against Gnaeus Pompey, victor on land and sea, there was one who composed a poem describing him scratching his head with (as he says) one finger; there was someone who, by the license of poetry, looked down upon his three golden chariots <note>referring to Pompey's three Triumphs, the highest military honors in Rome</note>." Thus far Porcius Latro. Since, however, this book of Seneca which we cite is still very rare to find, we have therefore deemed this passage worthy of our commentaries as if it were new, lest anyone think we have reported things that are perhaps too common, trite, or lying out in the open for anyone to see.

### Chapter 8

### Why the names of the wandering stars are assigned to the days; and the reasons for their order therein, pleasant to know and to relate.

These names of the days, called after the names of the **wandering stars** <note>The seven "planets" of antiquity: the Sun, the Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn</note>: what origin or reason they have, I truly remember reading nowhere else but in the historian **Dio** <note>Cassius Dio, a Roman historian writing in Greek</note>. From his thirty-seventh book, we can interpret his words as follows: "For the fact that days are assigned to these seven stars which are called **planets** <note>original: *planetes*; literally 'wanderers' in Greek</note> is a custom accepted and thought to be an invention of the Egyptians. However, it only began to spread among all people relatively recently (so to speak). Furthermore, the ancient Greeks (as far as I know) had no knowledge of this practice previously. Since, however, this custom is so widespread among other nations, and especially among the Romans, that it now seems almost ancestral, [I will say] a few things about them..."

<sig>c iii</sig>

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on Roman political insults involving gestures of effeminacy and begins an investigation into the Egyptian origins of the seven-day week named after the planets, citing Cassius Dio.</summary>
<keywords>Pompey the Great, Seneca the Elder, Calvus, Juvenal, Porcius Latro, effeminacy, Roman satire, astrology, planets, days of the week, Cassius Dio, Egyptian influence</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the naming of the days of the week, specifically moving from the general Egyptian origin mentioned at the end of the previous page to two specific theories—one musical and one mathematical—explaining how the seven planets came to be assigned to the seven days.</meta>

...I shall explain! And in what way or for what reason they are so ordered, I will set forth.

Therefore, I have heard of a twofold reasoning; both are surely not difficult to understand, yet both nevertheless warrant inspection. For if anyone should relate the harmony called the **fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*; a musical interval comprising four notes of the scale, which the author notes was considered the fundamental building block of music</note>—by which alone the entire power of all music is believed to be contained—to these stars <note>the seven classical planets: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury, and the Moon</note> by which all the adornment of the heavens is distinguished, in the same order in which they are carried: 

And beginning from that highest sphere which is attributed to **Saturn**, and having skipped the two following, he should name the fourth the master; and then, likewise jumping over two others, he should add the seventh; and proceeding in the same way, he should successively review the gods presiding over them in a cycle, and thus count the days; he will discover that all of them, by a certain musical reasoning, agree with that celestial division and arrangement. 

However, this is one reasoning that is reported. The other is of this kind: If you begin to count the hours of the day and night immediately from the first! So that you assign it to **Saturn**; then the following to **Jupiter**; the third to **Mars**; the fourth to the **Sun**; the fifth to **Venus**; the sixth to **Mercury**; and the seventh to the **Moon**; following, of course, that sequence of the spheres which is handed down by the **Egyptians**! 

And you do the same again until you pass through twenty-four hours, you will find the first hour of the following day is linked to the **Sun**. Then, if you do this same thing for those twenty-four hours by the same reasoning as before, you will assign the first hour of the third day to the **Moon**. If you observe this very thing in the remaining days, each day will have clearly adopted the corresponding god for itself. 

These things **Dio** <note>Cassius Dio, the Roman historian who wrote about these origins in his Roman History</note> says: quite festive and clever (unless I am mistaken). But since commonly now the **Sabbath** and the **Lord’s Day** <note>original: *dies dominicus*; Sunday</note> have lost their ancient names <note>In the Romance languages influenced by the Church, the planetary names for Saturday and Sunday were replaced by "Sabbath" and "The Lord's Day," while Monday through Friday often retained their planetary roots</note>, while the others still retain them, it is fitting for scholars to know that the former was called the day of **Saturn** and the latter the day of the **Sun**. 

For concerning the day of the Sun (as far as I now recall), even the Greek historian **Sozomen** and also the divine **Jerome** <note>The influential Church Father and translator of the Vulgate Bible</note> make mention; concerning the day of Saturn, either this same **Dio** in the same book, or **Frontinus** <note>A Roman general and author</note> in his *Stratagems*, or likewise the elegiac poet **Tibullus**, or finally even **Tertullian** in his *Apology*, and **Gaudentius** in his *Sermons* have mentioned it. 

But also **Faustus of Milevis**, that great champion of the **Manichaeans** <note>A dualistic religious movement that was a major rival to Christianity</note>, spoke thus: "It is pleasing to take up the leisure of the Sabbaths, and to join hands in the Saturnian fasts." <note>original: *Placet suscipere sabbatorū otiū: & saturniacis manus isertare carenis.* Faustus is acknowledging how the Manichaeans adopted certain outward Jewish or Pagan customs, such as Saturday observances.</note> Nor should I omit that: the [names] of the days having been changed...

<sig>m 3</sig>

<summary>This page explains two theories for the naming of the days of the week: a musical theory based on the "diatessaron" (fourth) interval and a mathematical theory based on assigning gods to each hour of a 24-hour cycle. It also lists several historical and religious authorities who reference the planetary names of the days, such as Saturn's day and the Sun's day.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the naming of the days of the week to a sharp philological critique of the scholar Domitius Calderinus. It connects the previous discussion of Christian naming practices (influenced by Constantine) to a specific debate over a corrupted word in the Roman satirist Juvenal.</meta>

...these names, if any are now used by Christians: at the instigation of Prince Constantine <note>Emperor Constantine the Great, who officially sanctioned the seven-day week and the Christian day of rest</note>: just as Nicephorus <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopolus, a 14th-century Byzantine church historian</note> mentions in his Greek eulogy of him.

### Chapter VIII.

What is found in the writings of Domitius: and how far he is to be believed: and concerning his evident and shameful error regarding the word *Atregias*.

"Destroy the *atregiae* of the Moors." <note>original: *Irue maurorū atregias*; a corrupted reading of a line from Juvenal's Satires</note> Let us now (if you have the time) examine how Domitius explains this half-line from the fourteenth satire of **Juvenal**—whether he does so learnedly, faithfully, or even with insight. But first, a few words about the man, in case anyone is perhaps interested in such things. **Domitius Calderinus** of Verona, then, who died ten years ago, was a man of sharp intellect and great industry in literature; he was most diligent in the Latin language and not indifferent to the Greek. He was not at all unskilled in composing prose, nor a bad poet. Yet he was (as his own works testify) an excessive admirer of himself, and stubborn and rebellious against the truth for the sake of an opinion he had once embraced. 

Furthermore, because of this, he was quite boastful in his writing, a scorner of all others, a mocker of everyone at the slightest opportunity, and a critic without discrimination. Thus, he faced far too many rivalries with very learned men, and he labored under a quite obvious hatred among them. Yet among the common people, his authority was so great that while still a young man, he claimed the highest fame for himself among the professors in Rome. To maintain and keep this fame, we believe it happened many times that he "rubbed his forehead" <note>a Latin idiom, *frontem perfricuerit*, meaning to act with brazen shamelessness</note> in his works; and to catch fame by fair means or foul, he reported things in bad faith. Some things he even handled with a malicious cleverness—being deceptive with appearances that have a surface-level truth—to the great cost of those who believed him. He entangled them in obscure opinions and "threw dust in their eyes" <note>a metaphor for intentional deception</note> (as they say). Or, whenever there was a greater difficulty, he neither withdrew nor engaged with it, but cheated the reader's hope. 

So, while he strove to prove he was ignorant of nothing, he was sometimes found to be more lying than the **Parthians** or the **Cretans** <note>ancient proverbial groups known for being untrustworthy or deceitful</note>. Following his example, this bloody gangrene of a disease has spread into all parts of literature (to use **Varro’s** words); it must be cut away by all learned men with the erasing part of the stylus <note>the flat end of the Roman writing tool used to smooth over wax, effectively deleting mistakes</note>, lest it creep all the way to the vital organs. There are, however, several grammatical commentaries published by him, such as on **Martial**...

<meta>The previous page introduced Domitius Calderinus as a brilliant but arrogant scholar. This page continues the list of his works, the criticisms leveled against him by his contemporary Giorgio Merula, and the author's own motives for correcting Domitius’s philological errors.</meta>

...[Juven]al and other poets: these he dedicated to my **Medici** patrons, the brothers **Lorenzo** and **Giuliano** <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" and Giuliano de' Medici, the de facto rulers of Florence and great patrons of the arts</note>, by whom he was also honored with most ample rewards. But he also published certain things on the *Silvae* of **Statius** <note>original: *Statii siluulas*; a collection of occasional poems by the Roman poet Statius</note>, on the *Epistle of Sappho*, and also on the *Ibis* of **Ovid** <note>original: *Ibin quoq; Nasonis*; Ovid's full name was Publius Ovidius Naso</note>; these are certainly full of good substance where he does not apply "dye" <note>original: *fucum facit*; a metaphor for deceiving or disguising the truth</note> and play upon the reader's credulity. 

On the other hand, **Giorgio Merula** <note>An Italian humanist and rival scholar known for his meticulousness</note>, a man clearly learned and diligent, and far more cautious in his writing than Domitius, and now of the very first reputation, attacked him—not out of rivalry (as they say), but with "full sails" <note>original: *uelificatione plena*; a maritime metaphor for a vigorous or forceful approach</note>. By publishing many notes on Domitius's commentaries on **Martial**, Merula was the first to restrain the man’s license, which was then striding along with great steps and already becoming quite ferocious. 

We also, in the flower of our early youth—not indeed to strike his books entirely out of people’s hands, but to rouse the "sleepy youth," as if they were sleeping on both ears—did likewise. Even then, in the very apprenticeship of our profession, we used to strike him gently from time to time, as if with a sparing hand. We profess that we will do the same now, however the matter goes, so that our good faith and the liberal arts may be maintained. For we will not endure, so far as our strength allows, that credulous youth be mocked with impunity and that simple minds be undermined. 

Since, however (as it says in **Plautus**) <note>Titus Maccius Plautus, the Roman comic playwright</note>, he is happily wise who is wise through the danger of another: we ourselves will take care, to the best of our ability, not to record anything in these books that cannot be defended by authorities. As for what we feel about him in total, if we weigh his vices against his virtues and estimate where his talent most stood, let our own epigram testify. This poem was carved into stone nearly nine years ago on the shore of **Lake Garda** <note>original: *Benaci lacus*; the largest lake in Italy, near Domitius's birthplace</note> (Domitius's own native soil) by **Baccio Ugolini** of Florence and **Angelo Maffeo** of Verona, who were devoted to his memory; I will transcribe it here:

> Stop, traveler: you see sacred dust,
> Which the wave of swirling Lake Garda disturbs:
> For this place the Muse often exchanges Libethron,
> The fountain of Sisyphus, and the groves of Permessus <note>These are all mythical locations associated with the Muses and poetic inspiration</note>:
> For on this soil, Domitius first wailed <note>i.e., was born</note>.

<meta>This page continues the critique of Domitius Calderinus. After mentioning the challenges posed by Giorgio Merula, the author now focuses on a specific philological error made by Domitius regarding a rare word and a corrupted poetic fragment.</meta>

He—that learned man—he whom you know well,
Giving lessons to the Roman youth,
Unearthing wondrous meanings from the storehouse of the poets.
Go your way, traveler; you have seen enough with your own eyes.

But perhaps I have said too much about the man, especially since I promised only a little. Yet I have done this with the specific intent of throwing a morsel, as it were, to those "two-penny" <term>diobolaribus: literally "worth two obols," a Greek coin of low value, used here to mean cheap or third-rate</term> grammarians who see everything as if through a mist. These are men who think nothing at all is correct unless they have finally extracted it—long worn and trampled—from those paltry little commentaries of theirs. 

As for the word <term>attegias: a rare Latin word of foreign origin, likely referring to a small hut or cottage</term>, these are the words of **Domitius** himself: "Others," he says, "take *attegias* in the language of the Moors to mean huts. I, however, understand it to mean Mauritania at the furthest part of **Libya** <note>In ancient geography, 'Libya' often referred to the entire continent of Africa</note>." He then cites **Dionysius** <note>Dionysius Periegetes, a 2nd-century author of a popular geographical poem in Greek</note>:

->At the edge of Libya they inhabit the *attegias* waters,<-
->Where the statues of Hercules stand, the Moorish people.<-

So says Domitius. You see how he follows the worse path while seeing the better. I certainly wanted to believe those claims, but rumor denies they are true. Domitius cites Dionysius as his authority; however, if I ever find that Dionysius made any mention of that word, I will yield and throw up my hands even now, and I will not refuse to be considered the most deceptive of all men. 

But to speak plainly and without "puffery" <note>original: *uessica*; literally 'bladder,' a metaphor for empty, inflated speech</note>, let us consider the matter itself: these are not the verses of Dionysius—they are not. Rather, they belong to **Priscian** <note>A famous 6th-century Latin grammarian who translated Dionysius's Greek poem into Latin hexameters</note>, in the little book where he translates the poet Dionysius from the Greek. But Domitius has corrupted and distorted them. For where the text in Priscian read "at the waves of **Tethys**" <note>original: *tethyos undas*; Tethys was a sea goddess, and her name was commonly used by poets to represent the ocean</note>, Domitius erased the original and interpolated the text, substituting "the *attegias* waves." 

I believe he surely would not have done this if he had consulted the logic of the poem, or if he had considered the lengths and pauses of the syllables <note>The "meter" or rhythm of the verse</note>. For he placed the word *attegias* with a long antepenultimate syllable, contrary to the testimony of the very author he is translating; then, he made the final syllable short, contrary to the authority of everyone. It is fortunate that the original Dionysius survives; I believe these are his actual Greek verses on that subject:

->But truly they inhabit the very furthest corner,<-
<note>original Greek: ἀλλ' ἤτοι πύματὴν μὲν ἐπὶ γλωχῖνα νέμονΤαι</note>
->Near the pillars [of Hercules], the tribes of the Moorish land.<-
<note>original Greek: ἀγχοῦ στηλάων μαυρουσίδος ἔθνεα γαίης.</note>

<summary>The author accuses the scholar Domitius Calderinus of falsifying a Latin translation by Priscian to invent a geographical definition for the rare word "attegias," proving the fraud by citing the original Greek of Dionysius Periegetes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological critique of the scholar Domitius Calderinus. The previous page established that Domitius incorrectly cited the Greek geographer Dionysius Periegetes to support his definition of the rare word "attegias" (huts). The author is now demonstrating that the word does not appear in Dionysius at all and is offering the correct historical and linguistic origins of the term.</meta>

Although you might read through the whole work now, you will see that not even the slightest hint of **attegiae** <term>attegiarum: the genitive plural form of a word for small huts or cottages</term> exists in **Dionysius**! You will see another word cited instead of the garment <note>original: "palio" (pallium), referring to a cloak or cover</note>; the true reading is erased, a false one substituted, and no account is taken of the meter or the syllables of the verse. And will anyone hesitate to disagree most freely with him now and refute these trifles? Or does the long-held opinion of the man’s talent and learning make this challenge more controversial? <note>original: "invidiosiorem," here implying that criticizing a famous scholar might bring public ill-will upon the critic</note>.

Furthermore, we understand **Attegiae** to be either tents, or shelters, or (if you prefer) huts—the kind the Moors inhabit. These people were called Numidians by the Romans, as the historian **Herodian** confirms. **Rufinus** uses this word in the seventh book of his translation of **Josephus’s** *The Jewish War*. He says that when they came to the festival day, it was the custom for men to set up *attegiae* in honor of God. For where the Latin Rufinus says "to set up *attegiae*," the original author Josephus had used the Greek word *skenopoieisthai* <note>original: "σκηνοποιεισθαι," meaning to build or pitch tents, specifically in the context of the Festival of Tabernacles</note>.

->Caput x.<-
->Concerning the *Baptae* and Cotytto: and the *Coryttia*; then the correct reading and explanation provided for Juvenal and Horace.<-

> The Baptae, accustomed to wearying the Athenian Cocytus. <note>A line from Juvenal's Satires (2.92), referring to the priests of the goddess Cotytto and their secretive rituals.</note>

When **Domitius** set out to explain this verse by **Juvenal**, he unbuckled the strap on his "erratic suspicions" (if I may call them that) and began to hallucinate absurdities and pure nonsense. He claimed that the **Baptae** were the priests of **Ceres** and that **Cocyton** was Ceres herself. I do not think he would have ever said this if there were even a trace—I won't say of shame—but of any common sense in the man! For we shall only believe this when someone produces a sufficiently certain author on whom we can rely. But this behavior is typical of him (for I shall speak quite freely now): when he is unable to explain anything, he complicates it; he seizes upon whatever is at hand and is carried away by a wavering conscience to wherever the impulse of his wandering opinion finally drives him. 

We, however, suspect this refers to the "Old Comedy" of the poet **Eupolis**, as the excellent authority of the ancients has persuaded us. Indeed, the Greek author **Platonius** speaks of it thus when he deals with the differences between comedies.

<meta>This page continues the author's philological investigation, moving from the definition of "attegiae" to a discussion of the lost play "Baptae" by the Greek comic poet Eupolis. The author is refuting previous scholarly misconceptions by citing classical authorities to reconstruct the play's themes of foreign religious corruption and social decadence.</meta>

We know, therefore, that Eupolis, for having produced the play *Baptae*, was drowned in the sea <note>original: "Ἴσμεν οὖν τὸν Εὔπολιν... ἀποπνιγέντα εἰς τὴν θάλατταν"</note> by those against whom he had directed the *Baptae*. He says we know Eupolis—because he produced the play *Baptae*—was submerged in the sea by those very people against whom he had sent out the *Baptae*. **Lucian** also, in that speech titled *To the Ignorant Book-Collector* <note>original: "ad ineruditum multos emittentem libros"</note>, wrote roughly to this effect:

> Tell me, he says, do you even read the speech of **Aeschines** against **Timarchus**? Or do you indeed know all those things and are familiar with the details? Have you encountered **Aristophanes** and **Eupolis**? Have you read the entire play *Baptae*? Then did you think none of these things pertained to you? Did you not blush even when you recognized them?

But even **Hephaestion**, in his manual dealing with acatalectic verses <term>acatalectic: a line of poetry that has the full number of syllables in its final foot</term>, cites two lines from **Eupolis’s** *Baptae*. And **Suidas** mentions one from the same under the entry for the word *Atrypheros* <term>Atrypheros: a Greek term meaning delicate, luxurious, or effeminate</term>. Now, first of all, I think it is established that the *Baptae* was a play of the "Old Comedy" <note>a style of ancient Greek theater known for high-spirited satire and bold personal attacks</note>, sharp and biting, by the poet **Eupolis**. As to what the plot of the comedy was and what its subject matter was, this will be shrewdly investigated through the very traces which now remain.

The **Athenians**, therefore, at that time had adopted certain foreign rites and religions, and new gods, for whom nocturnal vigils had been established—later the cause of great outrages, just like the **Bacchanalia** at Rome. But among the other rites, there were also those called *Cotyttia* <term>Cotyttia: secret, often licentious nocturnal festivals held in honor of the Thracian goddess Cotytto</term>. We believe **Eupolis** wrote the *Baptae* against these nocturnal *Cotyttia*; but we also see other comic poets, and among them **Aristophanes** as well, attacked this whole class of nocturnal vigils and foreign influences. So it is that **Juvenal** says the things which occurred at Rome contrary to the ritual of ceremonies were not unlike those which the *Baptae* perpetrated in the nocturnal *Cotyttia* at Athens. **Cicero**, in his work *On the Laws*, says this:

> But what displeases me in nocturnal rites, the comic poets indicate; if such license were granted at Rome, what would that man have done <note>referring to Clodius Pulcher, who famously disguised himself as a woman to infiltrate the women-only rites of the Bona Dea</note>—he who brought premeditated lust into a sacrifice, where it was not even lawful to cast the impudence of the eyes?

And soon after the same author says:

> And all nocturnal rites (lest we should perhaps seem too harsh), **Diagondas the Theban** abolished by a perpetual law in the heart of Greece;

As for the new gods, and the nocturnal vigils in worshiping them, so **Aristophanes**, that most witty poet of the Old Comedy, harasses them, as follows:
