<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the play *Baptae* by Eupolis. Having established that the play was a biting satire, the author now explores the specific foreign religious influences—particularly the Thracian goddess Cotytto—that the play mocked. He corrects a textual error in the works of Synesius, arguing that the name of this goddess has been misread by previous scholars like Domitius.</meta>

s 2

In his work, **Sabazius** <note>original: "Sabadius"; a nomadic horseman and sky father god of the Phrygians and Thracians</note> and certain other foreign gods were judged and expelled from the city. It is certainly clear from the testimony of **Cicero** that **Aristophanes**, as well as other poets of the "Old Comedy," sharpened the edge of their pens against new gods and against nocturnal vigils <note>all-night religious festivals, often associated with mystery cults and debauchery</note>. Since it is clear from the above that the *Baptae* was a comedy in which shameful and obscene things were criticized, and since mention is made here of the *Baptae* and the nocturnal rites of the Athenians—which we shall declare were called the *Coryttia*—who would not now understand that the *Baptae* was the very play in which such things were primarily attacked?

**Strabo**, however, in the tenth book of his *Geography*, relates that the *Coryttian* and *Bendidian* rites <note>Bendidia: a festival in honor of the Thracian moon goddess Bendis</note> existed among the Thracians, from whom the Orphic rites took their origin. He also names the goddess **Cotys**, of whom **Aeschylus** makes mention. Then, a little later, he says the Athenians accepted many things from foreign rites, "so that they were even deafened <note>original: "ὥστε καί ἐκκωφωθῆσαν"; suggesting they were overwhelmed or stunned by the noise and variety of foreign cults</note>," he says. But among the other foreign rites he records as having been received by them, he first enumerates the Thracian ones, which we showed above were called the *Coryttia* and *Bendidia*.

It is clear, therefore, that the *Coryttia* were foreign rites customarily practiced by the Athenians; it is also clear they were attacked by the comic poets. We are led by conjecture, however, since mention is made here of the *Baptae*—which we know was a comedy by **Eupolis**—and of the nocturnal rites of the Athenians, that it was in that play specifically that these kinds of rites were driven out. Furthermore, we believe the goddess who presided over such rituals and vigils was called **Cotytto**, from whose name the designation "Coryttia" is derived by a correct grammatical declension. Because this goddess was most infamous in the old comedies, **Synesius**, in a certain letter whose beginning is:

> He who is a slave both in name and in disposition <note>original: "ὁ καί τοὔνομα δοῦλοσ καί τήν προαίρεσιν"</note>

describes the manner of it quite clearly:

> He is not at all suited to **Hermes** and **Hercules**, the overseers of the wrestling school, but rather serves as a temple-warden for **Cotytto** and the other Athenian *Conisali* <term>Conisali: phallic deities or demons associated with the dust of the wrestling ground, often depicted as lewd figures</term>. <note>original: "ἐρμῇ μέν καί ἡρακλεῖ τοῖσ παλαίσ7ρασ ἐφόροισ οὐδέ κατά μικρόν πρέπει κοτυτ7οῖ δέ καί τοῖσ ἄλλοισ ἀτ7ικοῖσ κονισάλοισ νεωκορεῖ"</note>

By these words, he signifies that the person he is discussing in the letter is not compatible with **Hermes** or **Hercules**, the presiding deities of the gymnasium, but is instead a temple-warden <note>original: "editimū"</note> of the *Coryttia* and other most despicable things among the Athenians. Therefore, one should read "**Cotytto**," not "Cocytus" <note>the river of wailing in the underworld; the author is correcting a scholar who mistook the goddess for the river</note>, especially since the quantity of the syllable is inconsistent; for it is not short even if you remove the second 'c', as **Domitius** prescribes—and he does so quite ignorantly (be that...

He uses passages from Strabo and Synesius to prove that these Thracian-influenced "Coryttian" rituals were a source of social and religious controversy in ancient Athens.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name and nature of the goddess Cotytto. After arguing that the name has been misread by previous scholars like Domitius, the author now provides evidence from classical poets and lexicographers to support his correction of the text, before pivoting to a new chapter on the mythological origins of the rose.</meta>

...truly (if you will forgive me) too unskillfully and negligently: he who did not consider that the first syllable of the name is not only short by position <note>The author is discussing poetic meter; "short by position" means a vowel's length is determined by the consonants following it</note>—which is the only thing he noticed—but also long by nature. Some, however, preferred this goddess to be called **Cotys** <note>original: "Cotyn"</note> rather than **Cotytto**; as **Strabo** does above, and as **Synesius** does elsewhere in these words: <note>original Greek: "θαρρῶν ἀποφαίνου τόν ἄνδρα ἡμίγυνον ἀντόχρημα θιασώτην τῆς κότυος"</note> "Boldly declare the man to be an absolute half-woman, a fellow-worshipper of Cotys." **Suidas** <note>A 10th-century Greek encyclopedist</note>, explaining this passage, says that Cotys is a goddess worshipped as the overseer of shameful things. From this, he says, come men like **Clisthenes** and **Timarchus** <note>Figures often mocked in Greek comedy for their effeminacy or perceived lack of virtue</note>, and whoever else cultivates their beauty for the sake of profit, toward unspeakable lust, such as the effeminate and those who style their hair.

But I believe learned men will find other sources elsewhere to assist them in these matters, if they apply a zeal for learning. Indeed, in **Horace** too, in the *Epodes*, we believe it should be read thus:

> Lest you laugh unpunished at the **Cotyttian** rites <note>original: "Inultus ut tu riseris Cotyttia"</note>

and not as most manuscripts have it, "Cocytian" <note>Referring to Cocytus, the river of wailing in the underworld</note>. And in a very old book belonging to **Giorgio Antonio Vespucci** of Florence <note>The uncle of the explorer Amerigo Vespucci and a prominent humanist scholar</note>—a man learned in both Greek and Latin—that word retains the letter "r" in the third-to-last syllable, not a "c"; I believe this is a single trace, still intact, of the true and original reading.

### Chapter XI.

->An opinion on **Claudian** explained: by the telling of a most delightful little story: Then<-
->another story, no less sweet, likewise translated from the Greek, concerning **Venus** and the rose.<-

**Claudian** <note>A Latin poet of the late 4th century</note>, in the second book of *The Rape of Proserpina*, speaking of the goddess **Venus** gathering purple roses, says:

> Thus having spoken, she plucks the signs of her own blood.

The passage could certainly appear obscure, were it not for a story explained in **Aphthonius’s** *Rhetorical Exercises* <note>original: "progymnasmatis rhetoricis"; a standard textbook for teaching rhetoric in the Renaissance</note>, which goes almost as follows: He who admires the beauty of the rose should consider the wound of Venus. The goddess loved **Adonis**; **Mars** loved her. What the goddess was to Adonis, Mars was to Venus. A god loved a goddess, and a goddess followed a man. Their passion was equal, even if their status was unequal. Mars, out of jealousy, sought to kill Adonis...

<meta>This page continues the transition from the discussion of the goddess Cotytto to the mythological origins of the rose. The author is mid-sentence, describing the tragic aftermath of the death of Adonis and how it relates to the goddess Venus.</meta>

i b

...was mourning: <note>The Latin fragment "rendebat" likely concludes "moerebat," meaning he or she was mourning</note> thinking that the death of Adonis was the end of love. Mars strikes Adonis. Venus runs to his aid: hurrying, she falls into the roses and becomes entangled in the thorns. Thus she pierces her foot: the blood that flowed from the wound colored the rose from her own person. Thus the rose, which was previously white, became what is now seen. I myself also touched upon this little fable in my *Rusticus* <note>A poem by the author, Angelo Poliziano, celebrating country life</note> in that little verse:

->The modest rose stains its Idalian <note>Idalium was a city in Cyprus sacred to Venus</note> breast with blood.<-

But also the Maecenas <note>A famous Roman patron of the arts; here used as a title for a great patron</note> of our age, **Lorenzo de' Medici**, has most beautifully embraced this whole story in his native verses <note>Referencing Lorenzo’s poetry in Italian (the vernacular)</note>: for as he is most eminent in every virtue, so he is also in the poetic art. Furthermore, that wonderful poet **Nonnus**, in the forty-first book of his *Dionysiaca* <note>An epic poem about the god Dionysus</note>, tells the story that Mars himself, after Adonis had been killed by him, turned himself into a boar, writing thus of Venus:

*She alone did not wish to look upon the pleasure of boars, the untamable one. For in the likeness of a boar, Mars, sending forth a death-bearing poison from his jagged teeth, intended in his mad jealousy to weave a fate for Adonis.* <note>original Greek: "μούνην δὲ συῶν οὐκ ἤθελε λεύσσειν τερπωλὴν ἀτεμάντιο. ἐπεὶ συὸς εἰκόνι μορφὰς ἄρης καρχαρόδων θανάτηφόρον ἰὸν ἰάλλων ζηλομανὴς ἤμελλεν ἀδώνιδι πότμον ὑφαίνειν."</note>

**Nonnus** denies in these verses that Venus was delighted by the boar, because she had learned that Mars would one day take the form of a boar and, out of jealousy, kill Adonis. However, that the rose is otherwise pleasing to Venus is not kept silent even by that most charming poet of epigrams <note>Referring to the Roman poet Martial</note>:

> Go, happy rose (he says), and with soft garlands
> Bind the hair of our Apollinaris,
> Which you should weave while they are white! But remember,
> May Venus always love you so hereafter.
> <note>original: "I felix rosa... Nostri cinge comas Apollinaris..." from Martial, Epigrams 7.89</note>

But also **Virgil**, in a certain little elegy of his (for why should we not believe it to be his, since it is the most elegant of all?), speaks thus:

->Venus is the one mistress of both the star and the flower.<-
-><note>original: "Sideris & floris est domina una Venus."</note><-

Furthermore, **Libanius**, that Greek sophist who is second to none of the masters of rhetoric, plays with this argument about the rose of Venus in a certain corner [of his work]. At the time when the story gave the shepherd <note>Paris, the Trojan prince who judged the beauty of the goddesses</note> as judge over the goddesses, it went badly for **Juno** and badly for **Minerva** as well, because of that **Cestus** <note>A magical girdle or belt</note> of Venus, in which was loveliness, and in which was also **Cupid**. Thus they refused to enter into the contest until Venus had removed her charm from herself. So...

<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s retelling of a story by the Greek rhetorician Libanius regarding the beauty contest between the goddesses Juno, Minerva, and Venus. The previous page ended with Venus defending her beauty; here she prepares to find an ornament—the rose—to secure her victory.</meta>

...for she called it that girdle. <note>The "cestus" of Venus, a magical belt that made the wearer irresistibly desirable.</note> "But not even you," Venus said, "are free from adornments. You wear a golden crown in your hair, Juno; and you, Minerva, wear a similarly golden helmet upon your head. But let it be permitted by me for you to keep those deceptions. <note>original Latin: *fucos*, meaning dyes, cosmetics, or superficial tricks.</note> Only let it be permitted by you for me to adopt another neat ornament for myself in place of my girdle." Thus complying with the goddesses’ wishes, she proceeded to go to the Scamander <note>A river near the city of Troy.</note> to gather flowers where a grassy meadow spread out beneath the bank. At the same time, the gentle and clear waters were close by, enticing her to bathe. Having bathed there, the goddess immediately—for this was the reason she had come—sought out an ornament for herself. Then, a wonderfully sweet scent seemed to waft toward her. The goddess had already gathered lilies and violets, yet that scent meanwhile enticed her more and more. She proceeded to follow the traces of the fragrant breeze. She saw a rose, and having seen it, she brought it to her nose; she recognized this as the source of that sweet fragrance. There, scorning the other flowers, she threw them on the ground. Crowned only with roses, she returned again to Mount Ida. <note>The mountain where the Judgment of Paris took place.</note> And it seemed that the flower did not bring more grace to Venus than Venus did to the flower. Juno and Minerva were so immediately defeated that they did not even wait for the verdict <note>original Latin: *calculum*, literally a pebble used for voting.</note> of the shepherd. <note>Paris, the Trojan prince tasked with judging which goddess was the most beautiful.</note> Instead, both running forward, they pulled the iron pins from their hair, kissed the flower itself, and placed it back upon Venus's head. Thus far Libanius speaks of the rose; yet I do not know if our hurried Latin has represented to perfection <note>original Latin: *ad unguem*, literally "to the fingernail," a phrase used by sculptors to denote a flawless finish.</note> the tongue of that most brilliant of rhetoricians, painted as it is with Greek colors.

## Chapter XII.
### How purple dye was discovered; together with an explanation of a passage from Nonnus, the Greek poet.

It would not be out of place to relate here how purple dye was discovered, both so that the passage from the Greek poet Nonnus might be understood, and so that a very charming little story, untouched by Latin writers, might become known. It is found in Pollux, <note>Julius Pollux, a 2nd-century Greek scholar who wrote a dictionary-like work for the Emperor Commodus.</note> in the first book of his *On Terms* addressed to Commodus, to this effect: The Tyrians say, he writes, that Hercules was seized with love for a certain local nymph named Tyro. A dog followed him, he says, in the ancient fashion, by which dogs would accompany their masters even to a public assembly. Because of this, this dog...

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the story of the rose and the judgment of Paris to another mythological origin story: the discovery of Tyrian purple by Hercules’s dog. Poliziano then moves into a philological explanation of a Roman religious rite mentioned by Suetonius, citing the historian Cassius Dio for clarification.</meta>

Hercules, having caught sight of a <term>murex</term> <note>original Latin: *purpuram*. The murex is the sea snail from which the famous Tyrian purple dye was extracted.</note> crawling among the rocks, which his little dog had bitten into, stained his own lips with the Phoenician crimson blood. When Hercules then approached the girl <note>The nymph Tyros, whom Hercules was courting in this legend.</note>, she was delighted by that unusual dye which she saw blooming on the dog's lips. She declared that she would have nothing more to do with him unless he brought her a garment even more brilliant than the lips of that dog. Therefore, having found the creature and collected its blood, Hercules brought the gift to the girl; he was the first author of the Phoenician dye, as the Tyrians tell. Therefore, in our *Rusticus* <note>A poem written by Poliziano himself in 1483.</note>, for this very reason, we eventually used the term "Herculean shell" when we meant purple. Furthermore, the verses of the poet Nonnus <note>Nonnus of Panopolis, a Greek epic poet of the 5th century AD.</note> regarding this very fable are found in the fortieth book of the *Dionysiaca* <note>An epic poem about the life of Dionysus.</note> as follows:

> And he gazed upon the Tyrian robes loosened by the shell, <note>original Greek: *κοχλω* (kochlo), referring to the murex shell.</note>
> darting forth purple sparks of the sea,
> where a sea-roving dog, crawling along the shores,
> [found] a deep-dwelling fish with its grey jaws;
> it stained the snowy cheeks inside the shell,
> reddening its lips with moist fire;
> by this alone was the radiant sea-cloak of kings once reddened.

## Chapter xiii.
## What the "augury of health" is in Suetonius.

Suetonius Tranquillus, in his *Life of Augustus*, says: "He also restored several ancient ceremonies that had gradually been abolished, such as the **augury of health** <note>original Latin: *salutis augurium*.</note> and others." However, as to what the "augury of health" might be, I see that even some learned men are ignorant. We found the matter in the thirty-seventh book of Dio’s *Histories* <note>Cassius Dio, a Roman historian who wrote in Greek.</note> to this effect: "Then they also performed the so-called 'augury of health' after a long interval. It is a certain type of augury where, if the god permits, they ask for health for the people; as if it were not even lawful to seek health from the gods unless the gods themselves first granted permission for this very request. One day was observed every year on which no army would set out for war, no one would prepare against another, and no one would fight." Because of this

<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s discussion of Roman religious rites as recorded by the historian Cassius Dio. The previous page explained that certain rituals, specifically the Augury of Health, were neglected during times of civil strife. Poliziano is now explaining the theological and practical reasons for this abandonment before transitioning to a philological study of ancient musical instruments.</meta>

in constant dangers, and especially in civil ones, it <note>The "Augury of Health," a ritual to determine if the gods would permit a prayer for the Roman state's well-being.</note> was by no means performed. For it was especially difficult to keep a day free <note>original Latin: *puram*, meaning a day ritually pure or free from the "stain" of civil conflict or ill omens.</note> from all these matters. Indeed, it could seem utterly absurd for men to willfully bring incredible losses upon themselves through seditions—since such a state of affairs would be ruinous for the conquered and likewise destructive for the victors—and then immediately seek health from the immortal gods. Furthermore, could such an augury even be held at such a time? Certainly not; even that attempt did not proceed purely, because certain adverse birds flew down to the place where the augury was being taken, and other ill-omened signs appeared. The same author <note>The historian Cassius Dio.</note> also writes in his fiftieth book that after Augustus had secured peace on land and sea, the gates of Janus were closed <note>The Temple of Janus in Rome had doors that stayed open during war and were closed only in times of universal peace.</note>; and the Augury of Health was also performed, which previously had been omitted for many reasons.

### Chapter 14.

What the <term>naulia</term> <note>A stringed instrument of Phoenician or Hebrew origin, similar to a harp or lute.</note> are in Ovid’s *Art of Love*; and therein concerning the foreign names of instruments which the Greeks and our own people use; and how even the Chaldeans <note>Poliziano uses "Chaldeans" here to refer to speakers of Aramaic or Syriac, or more broadly to Eastern scholars.</note> do not reject Greek and Roman words of that kind.

> Learn also to sweep the festive naulia with both palms;
> they are suited to sweet melodies.
<note>Quoting Ovid’s *Ars Amatoria* 3.327–328.</note>

I believe *naulia* is a diminutive form, or perhaps *nablia*, derived from those things we call either *naula* or *nabla*. Indeed, we have often noted in ancient monuments that the letters "b" and "u" <note>The Latin 'v' and 'u' were written identically; Poliziano is noting the phonetic shift between 'b' and 'v'.</note> are frequently used interchangeably for nearly the same sound. Suidas <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia.</note> also says that the *nabla* is a species of instrument; and in another entry, he says the <term>Psaltery</term> <note>A stringed instrument played by plucking.</note> is a musical instrument which is also called a *naula*. Moreover, Plutarch cites a verse from Sophocles:

> Neither the naula nor the lyre are dear to lamentations.
<note>original Greek: *οὐ ναῦλα κωκυτοῖσιν οὐ λύρα φίλα*.</note>

This is found in the book Plutarch wrote concerning the "E" at Delphi <note>A philosophical essay about a mysterious inscription at the Temple of Apollo.</note>. But Theodoret <note>A 5th-century Christian bishop and theologian.</note> also mentions it in his commentary on the Psalms, as does Plutarch in his *Table Talk* <note>original Latin: *Symposiacis*</note>. From these sources, we wrote in our *Nutricia* <note>A poem by Poliziano himself, acting as an introduction to his lectures on poetry.</note>:

> Not those festive naulia which ring out,
> struck by both hands.

There are, however, many foreign names for instruments, just as Strabo <note>A Greek geographer and historian of the 1st century AD.</note> relates...

...where <term>nabla</term> <note>A stringed instrument, often identified with the harp or psaltery.</note> are also enumerated: that is, psalteries. But I also recently observed this in the prophet Daniel, with <note>original Latin: *ducēte me scilicet... Ioanne Pico Mirādula*</note> Giovanni Pico della Mirandola leading me, of course, into those recesses and the sacred inner chambers of his studies: that even those names of such instruments which seem Greek and even Latin are used by <term>Chaldean</term> <note>Referring to the Aramaic language used in parts of the Book of Daniel.</note> authors in a slightly more corrupt form: such as *qarna* for horn, *qathros* for <term>kithara</term> <note>An ancient Greek stringed instrument similar to a lyre.</note>, *sabka* for <term>sambuca</term> <note>A triangular stringed instrument, often associated with luxury or decadence.</note>, *psanterin* for psaltery, and *symphonia* for symphony.

Moreover, <term>Solomon the Frenchman</term> <note>original: *Gallus Salomon*. This refers to the famous Jewish commentator Rashi (Rabbi Shlomo itzhaki, 1040–1105), who lived in France.</note>, the standard commentator on sacred letters among the Hebrews, says that *Nevel* is the Hebrew word for what is read there as *psanterin*, that is, the psaltery. It is established by these three letters: <insert>נבל</insert> <note>Hebrew: N-V-L, the root for *nevel*.</note> from which *Navel* is read in Isaiah and Amos; and then the Greek form *Naulon*, from which the Ovidian diminutive *Naulion* is derived. 

Since those marks which the Hebrews now use for vowels are recent additions—as my friend Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, one of all men truly most blessed in every part, found and showed in his singular and admirable work—in which he teaches that the Psalms translated by the <term>Seventy</term> <note>The Septuagint, the early Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> correspond to the Hebrew truth, chiefly by this argument regarding the [vowel] marks. This ensures that it can no longer be insulted by Jews or objected that we sing things in the temple every hour which we ourselves do not consider to have been translated faithfully enough. 

Finally, why Ovid called them "festive" <note>original Latin: *genialia*.</note> you will easily know by reading the words from the speech of Scipio Africanus Aemilianus against the judicial law of Cornelius Gracchus, which are found in Macrobius’s *Saturnalia*; their beginning is as follows: 

> "They teach dishonest tricks; with dancers and the *sambuca* and the psaltery they go to the school of actors; they learn to sing things which our ancestors wished to be considered a disgrace for free-born people. They go," he says, "into the dancing school among effeminate men—maidens and free-born boys." 

Thus says Scipio. Then Ambrose also, in his third book *On the Utility of Fasting*, teaches that psalteries are used at banquets to incite lusts. But indeed, in comedies too there is frequent mention of female psaltery players. 

Furthermore, the word itself is Hebrew; for in Greek it is called *psalterium*, and in Latin *laudatorium* <note>A "praise-giver" or instrument used for praise.</note>. As Saint Jerome says in his letter to Dardanus, teaching that it is shaped like a square shield with ten strings, although the Roman Emanuel <note>Likely Immanuel the Roman (c. 1261–1335), a Jewish poet and scholar.</note> in his Hebrew commentary...

## Chapter 15.

->Who the author of the *Sybaritis* was, whom Ovid mentions; and concerning the "Sybaritic books" in Martial, and likewise the other luxury of the Sybarites.<-

Ovid says the following in his letter to Augustus, in the second book of the *Tristia*:
> Nor has he who recently composed the *Sybaritis* escaped.

There is a question among students of literature as to who exactly this man was who composed the *Sybaritis*, or what the subject matter of that work was. We seem to have discovered both in Lucian, in the speech addressed to an uneducated man who was buying many books. In that work, it is written to roughly this effect: "Tell me this as well," he says. "If that Bassus, your <term>sophist</term> <note>A teacher of rhetoric and philosophy, often used pejoratively here to mean a clever but deceptive speaker.</note>, or the flute-player Batralus, or the <term>lewd</term> <note>original: *cinædus*, referring to a man who was considered effeminate or sexually passive in a way that was stigmatized in antiquity.</note> Hemitheon of <term>Sybaris</term> <note>An ancient Greek city in Italy famous for its extreme luxury and decadence, from which we get the word 'sybarite.'</note>—who wrote those wonderful laws for you on how one ought to be mad, and to be plucked <note>Refers to the ancient practice of removing body hair, often associated with effeminacy.</note>, and to submit to and perform those acts!—if any of these were now to walk about wrapped in a lion’s skin and holding a club, do you think he would appear to be Hercules to the spectators?"

But also Philo the Jew, in his *Life of Moses*, mentions those "composing comedies and Sybaritic wickednesses." Martial also, in the twelfth book of his *Epigrams*, says this regarding the Sybaritic books:
> You who compete with Sybaritic books,
> Read the most shameful books of Mussetus
> And the pages stained with itching salt;
> Read them, Rufus, but let your girl
> Be with you, lest you signal "Talassio" <note>A traditional Roman wedding cry; Martial implies that without a woman present, the reader will resort to masturbation.</note>
> With the hands of a lustful man,
> And become a husband without a woman.

It appears, therefore, that it was Hemitheon—not, as Domitius <note>Likely referring to the scholar Domitio Calderini.</note> conjectures, "Sybariti"...

<keywords>Sybaris, Hemitheon, Ovid, Martial, Lucian, Sybaritis, ancient luxury, decadent literature, philology</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter 15 regarding the identity of Hemitheon of Sybaris and the legendary decadence of the Sybarite people. It transitions from identifying the author of the "Sybaritis" mentioned by Ovid to a compilation of classical accounts of Sybaritic luxury.</meta>

...by whom either the *Sybaritis* or the "Sybaritic books" <note>original: *libelli Sybaritici*</note> were composed with a most obscene theme. Moreover, the Sybarites are especially notable for their luxury and delights, so much so that they honored the Ionians and the Etruscans <note>original: *Tyrrhenos*</note> above all others; since the former were the most luxurious among the Greeks, and the latter among the barbarians. From them also come the "Sybaritic fables," which are much like those of Aesop. And a proverb was derived: "A Sybarite through the street," directed against those who walk too haughtily. 

Suidas <note>A 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia and biographical dictionary.</note> records these things and other similar details about the Sybarites. Plutarch, however, in his *Banquet of the Seven Sages*, writes that it was the custom for the Sybarites to invite women to feasts a full year in advance, so that they might spend their leisure time preparing their clothing and gold ornaments and be fully adorned. 

But Maximus Tyrius also, in his third dissertation on pleasure, lists the Sybarites first among those peoples he wishes to show as the most soft and delightful; he also mentions "Sybaritic dances." Indeed, the same author, in another dissertation titled *What is the End of Philosophy?*, speaks roughly to this effect: "The inhabitant of Croton," he says, "loves the wild olive; the Spartan, armor; the Cretan, hunting; the Sybarite, luxury; the Ionian, dances." Hence Juvenal says:

> No crime is absent, no deed of lust, since 
> Roman poverty perished; from here it flowed to the Istrians 
> And the hills of Sybaris.

Strabo also agrees: he narrates that the Sybarites, despite all their prosperity, were driven out by the people of Croton in only seventy days because of their delights and luxury. Furthermore, Plutarch records a saying of a Sybarite in his *Life of Pelopidas*, who said it was no wonder the Spartans threw themselves into death in wars, just so they might escape such great labors and the miserable diet they were forced to endure. 

Aristotle, in the first book of the *Eudemian Ethics*, named Smyndirides the Sybarite—a peer in luxury to Sardanapalus <note>An Assyrian king legendary for his decadence.</note>—among those most devoted to pleasure. That detail is certainly witty which [Julius] Africanus relates in his *Cesti*—for that is how his Greek book on military matters is titled. For he relates that the Sybarites reached such a level of decadence that they introduced horses into their banquets; these horses were so trained that, upon hearing the sound of the flute, they would immediately rear up and perform certain gestures of <term>pantomime</term> <note>original: *chironomiæ*, referring to a rhythmic art of hand and arm movements.</note> and dance motions in rhythm with their front hooves. But, as it happened, a flute-player...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter 15 regarding the legendary decadence of the Sybarites, specifically recounting a famous anecdote about how their luxury led to their military defeat. It then transitions into Chapter 16, which investigates the identity of Aristides and his "Milesian Tales," as referenced in Ovid’s poetry.</meta>

...he says that someone there, having been treated with insult, fled to the enemy Crotoniates <note>Inhabitants of Croton, a Greek city in southern Italy.</note>, who had been defeated in battle by the Sybarites only a little while before. He promised that it would be to their state's advantage (if only they would trust him) that by his efforts all the Sybarite cavalry would fall into their power. The matter was believed, and having been made the leader of the war by the Crotoniates, he summoned all the flute-players <note>original: *tibicines*</note> who practiced that craft in the city. He taught them a melody and, when the time seemed right, ordered them to advance against the enemy. The Sybarites, sitting swollen with pride because they were superior in cavalry, came out against them and joined battle. At this point, a signal was suddenly given and all the flute-players played together. Behold, immediately upon recognizing the melody and that local song, the Sybarite horses reared up on their feet; they threw off their riders and performed the dance in the battle line that they had learned at home. Thus all the riders were captured while lying on the ground, and all the horses while performing dances. So much concerning the Sybarites from Africanus <note>Likely referring to Sextus Julius Africanus, a 3rd-century traveler and historian.</note>.

### Chapter xvi.
### Concerning the *Milesian Tales* of Aristides mentioned in Ovid, and the luxuries of the Milesians.

The same Ovid, in that same letter to Augustus, says:

->Sisenna translated Aristides, nor did it harm him<-
->To have inserted shameful jokes into his history.<-

Who this Aristides was, or what material he used for his books, is a matter of great hesitation; however, we refer the studious youth to the author Plutarch, whose words in the *Life of Marcus Crassus* are roughly to this effect: Having gathered the senate of the Seleucians, he <note>Surena, the Parthian general.</note> displayed the books of the *Milesian Tales* <note>original: *Aristidae Milesiacon*</note> by Aristides, which were quite shameless. He told no lie in this, for they had indeed been found among the baggage of Rustius <note>A Roman officer under Crassus.</note>. This gave Surena an opportunity for upright mockery against the Romans, since even in the midst of war they were unable to restrain themselves from matters and writings of this kind. So says Plutarch. Moreover, "Surena" is the name of a magistrate among the Persians, as if you were to say "praetor" <note>A high-ranking Roman official or general.</note>; Zosimus also shows this.

<summary>This page recounts a humorous legend where the Sybarites' war horses were defeated because they had been trained to dance to flute music, and begins an inquiry into Aristides' "Milesian Tales," a work of ancient erotic literature.</summary>
<keywords>Sybarites, Crotoniates, flute-players, military strategy, Ovid, Aristides, Milesian Tales, Plutarch, Marcus Crassus, Surena, Sisenna</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 16 regarding the literary and cultural reputation of Miletus. The previous page established Aristides as the author of the "Milesian Tales" (Milesiacae), which were known for their scandalous and erotic content. This page expands on the historical reputation of the Milesians for extreme luxury, fine textiles, and moral decadence, citing various classical authors.</meta>

And indeed, you will find this passage from Plutarch transcribed almost exactly as it is in many other parts of Appian’s history. Lucian likewise, in his *Affairs of the Heart*, writes of Aristides in nearly the same sense. He says: "The charming and sweet persuasion of your little stories, though quite immodest, has greatly delighted me; so much so that I almost believe myself to be Aristides, and that I am being soothed by Milesian tales." For this reason, even Apuleius begins the very epigram <note>A short, pithy poem or introductory verse.</note> that he placed at the front of his *The Golden Ass* in this way: "But I shall weave together various stories for you in that Milesian style, and soothe your well-disposed ears with a charming whisper." From this, Marcianus <note>Likely referring to the 5th-century author Marcianus Capella.</note> says: "For certainly the myth: even the Milesian delights of poetic diversity," and so on.

However, the Milesians were also notable for their delights and luxury, and from this a Greek proverb is told: "Keep your Milesian ways at home, not here" <note>original: οἴκοι τὰ μιλήσια μὴ ἐνθάδε</note>. That is: "At home, not here, for Milesian things." This was said against those who celebrate their domestic luxury in a place where it is less approved of. For thus a Milesian guest heard it in Lacedaemon <note>Sparta, a city-state famous for its austerity and rejection of luxury.</note> when he was praising the luxuries of his homeland, as is also written in the collection of proverbs by Apostolius of Byzantium. But Maximus Tyrius also signifies the same; he calls the Milesians "the most elegantly dressed" <note>original: εὐειμονοτάτους</note> because of their softer clothing. From this, Virgil says in his *Georgics*:

> The nymphs were carding Milesian fleeces.

And from this also comes that line of Horace:

> The other will avoid a cloak woven at Miletus worse than a dog or a snake.

Moreover, all the other Ionians were generally considered to be wallowing in luxury, as is found everywhere in ancient records. Furthermore, we shall not pass over this in silence: that Milesian women were accustomed to use an artificial phallus <note>original: veretro</note> made of leather, as is also written by the author Suidas <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world.</note>. Indeed, we also read in the same author that four books *On the Dissolution of Love* were composed by Cadmus of Miletus.

## Chapter xvii.
### A passage in a tragedy by Seneca is corrected, and explained by a more remote Greek myth concerning the Nemean lion:

It concludes by transitioning to a philological correction of a passage in Seneca's tragedies.</summary>

<keywords>Miletus, Milesian Tales, Aristides, Apuleius, Virgil, Horace, luxury, ancient fashion, Suidas, Seneca, Nemean lion</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on the reputation of the Milesians for luxury and their influence on literature, specifically citing the "Milesian Tales." This page shifts to a new series of philological observations, beginning with a textual correction in Seneca’s "Hercules Furens" regarding the origins of the Nemean lion, followed by an interpretation of a phrase in Juvenal’s fourth satire.</meta>

In Seneca’s tragedy, which is titled *Hercules Furens* <note>The Mad Hercules</note>, this short verse <note>original: "senariolus" – a line of six iambic feet</note> is read:

->Let the moon high above conceive other wild beasts.<- <note>original: "Sublimis alias luna concipiat feras."</note>

For an ancient manuscript from the public library of the Medici family has "other" <note>original: "alias"</note> and not "lofty" <note>original: "altas"</note> as is found in the common copies. One should not rashly seek the understanding of this passage from anywhere other than from Achilles <note>Referring to Achilles Tatius, an author of an astronomical treatise</note>, an author whom Julius Firmicus also cites and praises in his books on *The Mathematics* <note>original: *Matheseon* – a 4th-century work on astrology</note>. He, therefore, in his commentaries on Aratus <note>A Greek poet who wrote on stars and weather</note>, when speaking of the moon, writes exactly as follows: "They say that there is another habitation in it, with rivers and all such things as are on earth, and the myth says the Nemean lion fell from there." <note>original Greek: εἶναι δὲ ἐς᾿ αὐτὴν οἰκησιν αλληνω πο τα μονος τε καὶ ὅσα ἐστὶ γῇ σιν καὶ τὸν λέοντα τὸν νεμαῖον ἐκεῖθεν πεσεῖν μυθολογοῦσιν.</note> That is to say: In it there is also a habitation, and there are rivers, and other things, just as on earth. Indeed, they even tell the story that the Nemean lion fell from there. From this, we wrote in our poem *The Nurse* <note>original: *Nutrix*</note>:

->And the Nemean matter...<-
->The moon-born beast we falsely claimed.<-

### Chapter 18.

What the "little brother of a giant" means in Juvenal, and what the "Son of the Earth" means in other authors; and likewise concerning the name of Mani.

> Why it is that I would rather be the little brother of a giant <note>original: "Vnde sit ut malim fraterculus esse gigantis" – from Juvenal's Satires, 4.98</note>

Even if all those who have interpreted this passage from the fourth satire of the poet Juvenal have, until now, been stuck in the mud <note>original: "herent in salebra" – a metaphor for being stuck in a difficult or rugged place</note> (as far as I can see), they do not seem to me to explain it, but rather to hunt for a laugh with old wives' tales, or perhaps to invite mockery. Indeed, some invent stories of giants being turned into monkeys; others claim that after the giants, Earth brought forth a monkey in contempt of the gods. Other explanations are even colder <note>A classical idiom for something dull or witless</note> than these, which, like those of the rhetorician mentioned by Martial, would "chill the Baths of Nero" <note>Martial used this phrase to describe a writer so boring he could lower the temperature of a heated bath</note>. For, as far as I can grasp, they speak these trifles with no real sense and (as far as I know) citing no supporting author. To me, "little brother of a giant" seems to signify a man born of no ancestors, of an unknown and obscure family. Since their parents were unknown, the ancients were accustomed to calling such a person, in common speech, a "Son of the Earth" <note>original: "terrae filium"</note>. From this, Persius...

<meta>Continuing the previous page's focus on philological corrections and the interpretation of classical texts, this page transitions from the celestial origins of the Nemean lion to a discussion on the idiomatic expression "son of the earth" (terrae filius) and the etymology of the name "Manius."</meta>

"Manius is my heir,
A son of the earth: ask of me, I pray, who my fourth
Father might be / I would not say it readily. Yet add even one,
Even one more! He is already a child of the earth." <note>original Latin: "presto est mihi Manius heres / Progenies terræ..." — Quoting Persius, *Satire 6*, lines 56–59. The poet argues that even a noble lineage, if traced back far enough, ends in an unknown "son of the earth."</note>

You see, therefore, on the authority of Persius alone, that a **son of the earth** <term>terrae filius: a Latin idiom for a person of unknown or low-born origin, similar to "a nobody"</term> is one whose parents are unknown; nor should you think it was for no reason that Manius was named there by Persius. For, to omit what Pedianus <note>Asconius Pedianus, a 1st-century commentator on Cicero</note> writes concerning Manius of Aricia; even the Greek author Zosimus <note>A 5th-century historian; the text spells it "Sozimus"</note>, in the third book of his *Roman History*, says that the name Manius itself is not because he was born in the morning <note>original: "mane"</note>—as others are!—but was named from the *Manes* <note>The spirits of the dead</note>. Furthermore, a book by Marcus Varro <note>A prolific Roman scholar</note> is cited many times by Marcellus under the title *Manius*; it even touches on something lacking elsewhere, so that I might predict this also favors our argument. Cicero likewise, in the first volume of his letters to Atticus, says: "But I will write to you of these things more precisely elsewhere; for they are not yet sufficiently known to me, and I do not dare entrust a letter concerning such great matters to this **son of the earth**, whoever he may be." <note>Cicero, *Ad Atticum* 1.13.4. Cicero uses the term to describe a common messenger or person of no account.</note> But the same author also speaks thus in a letter to Trebatius: "Cnaeus Octavius, or rather Cnaeus Cornelius—your friend indeed, born of the highest family—is a **son of the earth**." This passage is corrupted in certain manuscripts, and in its place is substituted "son of Zera" <note>original: "Zerae filius"</note>; however, the truer reading remains to this day in a very old book, formerly belonging to the most learned man Filelfo <note>Francesco Filelfo (1398–1481), a prominent humanist</note>, now belonging to Lorenzo de' Medici <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" de' Medici (1449–1492), the author's patron</note>, a patron of letters. It is likewise found in another book copied (as it appears) from that old one, which is now in the public library of the Medici family. You see that by Cicero, no less, a completely unknown man is called a **son of the earth**. Lactantius <note>Early Christian author, c. 250–325 AD</note> also, in the first book of his *Divine Institutes*, uses these words: "Minucius Felix, in that book titled *Octavius*, argued thus: That Saturn, when he had been put to flight by his son and had come to Italy, was called a 'son of the sky' <note>original: "caeli filium"</note> because we are accustomed to say that those whose virtue we admire, or who arrive suddenly among people, have 'fallen from the sky.' But we call those born of unknown parents **sons of the earth**." Thus far Lactantius. But Tertullian <note>Early Christian author from Carthage, c. 155–220 AD</note> also writes thus of the same Saturn in his *Apology*: "He was called the son of earth and sky because those who are unknown, or who appear unexpectedly, are said to have come down from the sky, just as the common people call those of uncertain lineage **sons of the earth**."

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the term "son of the earth" (terrae filius) as a metaphor for low-born or obscure origins, then transitions into Chapter 18, which examines the history of "aspiration"—the pronunciation of the letter 'h'—in Latin, using a famous poem by Catullus as a primary example.</meta>

Therefore, since he preferred to be an obscure man rather than the noble **Umbricius**! <note>Umbricius was a character in Juvenal's Satires who left Rome in disgust; the author here is likely referencing Juvenal's Satire 4 regarding the dangers of being a noble under tyrant emperors.</note> Because once, old age among the nobility was equal to a miracle, seeing as the Roman princes were so cruel toward it! He says he would rather be the **little brother of a giant**—that is, a "son of the earth" <term>terrae filius: a classical idiom for someone of unknown or humble origin, as if they sprung directly from the soil like the mythological giants</term> (which, as we have shown, signifies someone obscure)—rather than a noble patrician. For the **Giants**, after all, were born of the earth. He uses the word "little brother" (*fraterculus*) rather than "brother" (*frater*) more pleasantly, so that he might not seem to desire that vastness and giant mass of the body, but only the lowliness of birth.

## Chapter xviii.

Certain things cited concerning **aspiration** <term>aspiration: the act of pronouncing a breathy 'h' sound before a vowel or consonant</term>; along with an explanation of Catullus’s noble epigram.

As **Quintilian**, in the first book of his *Institutes of Oratory*, says while discussing aspiration: "The ancients used it very sparingly even with vowels, when they said *edos* [goats] and *ircos* [bucks] <note>original: "edos / ircosq̄"; these words later became "haedos" and "hircos"</note>; it was then long observed that consonants should not be aspirated, as in the names *Gracci* and *triumpis* [triumphs]. In a short time, an excessive usage broke out, so that *choronae* [crowns], *chenturiones* [centurions], and *praechones* [heralds] still remain in certain inscriptions." Regarding this matter, there is Catullus’s noble epigram. These are his [Quintilian's] words. We believe this epigram is the one most notably cited:

> Arrius used to say "Chommoda" whenever he meant to say "commoda" <note>advantages</note>
> And "hinfidias" when he meant "insidias" <note>ambushes</note>;
> And then he hoped he had spoken marvelously,
> When he had said "hinfidias" with as much breath as he could.
> I believe his mother, his free uncle,
> His maternal grandfather, and his grandmother all spoke this way.
> When this man was sent to Syria, everyone’s ears had a rest;
> They heard these same words softly and lightly,
> And thereafter did not fear such words for themselves.
> When suddenly a horrible message is brought:
> That the Ionian waves, after Arrius had gone there,
> Were no longer "Ionian" but "Hionian." <note>This poem (Catullus 84) mocks a man named Arrius for "hypercorrection"—adding 'h' sounds to words where they didn't belong to sound more sophisticated or Greek, effectively ruining the names of geographical features like the Ionian Sea.</note>

It must indeed be observed, that even in pronouncing, just as in

...whether you should add aspiration in writing or in speech. Currently, the Greeks do this only with consonants, while the Romans? Not even with consonants is it retained. On the contrary, the ancient Romans used aspiration even when speaking with vowels. Regarding this, **Cicero** writes as follows in his *Orator* addressed to Brutus: "Indeed, I myself, though I knew our ancestors spoke in such a way that they used no aspiration except on a vowel, used to speak in a manner where I would say *pulcros* [beautiful], *cetegos* [Cethegi], *triumphos* [triumphs], and *carthaginem* [Carthage]. <note>original: "pulcros: cetegos: triumphos: carthaginē"; Cicero is explaining that he originally followed the old-fashioned rule of not adding 'h' to consonants (e.g., 'p' instead of 'ph'), but eventually gave in to the modern "aspirated" pronunciation.</note> At last, and quite late, due to the protest of my ears, once the truth had been wrung from me, I conceded the custom of speaking to the people, but reserved the knowledge for myself." So says Cicero.

But I myself recently saw a small gold coin at the house of **Lorenzo de' Medici** <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" (1449–1492), the famous Florentine statesman and patron of the arts.</note> with the inscription **TRIVMPVS**. And likewise another silver one with the more common spelling, **TRIVMPHVS**. Indeed, **Aulus Gellius** also, in the second book of his *Attic Nights*, testifies that the ancients inserted the letter 'h'—or the breath—into many words to strengthen and reinforce them (as he says), so that their sound might be more vigorous and lively. But **Priscian** <note>A late Roman grammarian whose works were the standard for Latin study in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> also says: Aspiration is added to vowels from the outside, so it sounds very little; but to consonants from the inside, so it sounds the most.

Therefore, if you wish the charm of **Catullus’s** epigram <note>Referencing Catullus 84, which mocks a man named Arrius for misplacing his 'h' sounds to sound more sophisticated.</note> to be clear to you, you will pronounce that aspiration in its proper place as much as you can. But I see these things are now so well known that they might perhaps seem common or redundant to someone. And yet, when I was still almost a youth, in the presence of several learned men (whom I think remember this), I spoke on this very passage to **Domitius** in Florence. He applauded it immediately, frankly admitting that he had learned more that day from a single student than in many years from any of the professors.

**Aurelius of Rimini** knows this (to omit others), who now lives in Pavia, a young man of outstanding talent and learning. He knows how many years ago he received this explanation from us in Florence while he was then living there, and how it seemed (as he himself later told in Pavia) entirely new and unknown even then to everyone to whom he spoke of it. Or indeed, the whole of Catullus, we explained in Verona (as **Baccio Ugolini**, a man of most free intellect, knows) inside a certain workshop where the rain had driven us, before several learned men who were almost compelling us. At that time, **Giovanni Battista** (if I am not mistaken in the name), a relative of **Guarino**, was present there, along with a certain **Dante**

Alighieri—the fifth in descent, as he claimed, from the Florentine poet **Dante**—and likewise two other men, by no means unlearned, were then teaching in Verona. There was also a great band of students who received those lectures of ours, such as they were, with such eager ears that they repeatedly cried out that an **Angel** had been sent down to them from heaven (for so they said) to interpret their fellow countryman, the poet. Yet I do not mention these things for the sake of demanding that my discoveries be attributed to me alone, with no share given to others (for I do not claim so much for myself), but rather lest someone perhaps think that I feed on the labors of others like a crow or a vulture feeding on carcasses left behind by an eagle.

### Chapter XX.

### The word *Ascopera* corrected in Suetonius and explained.

The manuscripts of **Suetonius** are found to be corrupted in the *Life of Nero*. For they all read as follows: "To the neck of the other, a broom [*scopa*] was tied, along with a placard: 'I did what I could, but you have deserved the sack [*culleum*].'" <note>original: "Alterius collo: & scopa deligata: simulq; titulus. Ego quod potui: sed tu culleum meruisti." This refers to a joke played on a statue of Nero, mocking him for murdering his mother.</note> However, the word *scopa* [broom] is not used in the singular in Latin; and even if it were, a broom has nothing to do with a sack [*culleo*]. 

But in the ancient copies, a trace remains, I believe, of the unharmed and true reading, in this manner: "To the neck of the other an *Ascopa* was tied." Therefore, if you write out the second to last letter, it becomes **Ascopera**, which I think is most correct. An *ascopera* is a small leather bag; the name is a compound in Greek, derived from "leather bottle" [*utre*] and "pouch" [*sacco*]. Regarding this word, **Suidas** <note>A 10th-century Byzantine Greek historical encyclopedia.</note> writes as follows: *askopera: the little purse or the pouch-bag*. <note>original: "ἀσκοπήρα τὸ μαρσίππιον ἤτοι τὸ σακκοπήριον"</note>

Suidas thus teaches that *Ascopera* signifies a small purse. This *Ascopera* was therefore tied to the neck of Nero's statue as a symbol of the *culleus* [sack], because he was a matricide <note>A person who kills their own mother.</note>. Since matricides are sewn into a *culleus* made of ox-hide, the person who tied the *Ascopera* to the statue represented a kind of miniature version of that sack. **Juvenal** <note>A Roman satirist of the late 1st century.</note> writes of the parricide and the sack thus:
> "And to be lowered into the sea in an ox-hide: with which
> is shut in, by an adverse fate, the harmless monkey."
> <note>original: "Et deducendum corio bouis in mare: cum quo / Clauditur aduersis innoxia simia fatis." Under Roman law, a parricide was sewn into a sack with a dog, a cock, a viper, and a monkey before being thrown into the sea.</note>

<vocab>Aligherius, Dante, Suetonius, Nero, Ascopera, Suidas, Iuuenalis, culleus, matricida</vocab>

<summary>Poliziano defends his original scholarship on Dante and provides a philological correction of Suetonius's "Life of Nero," replacing the nonsensical word 'scopa' (broom) with 'ascopera' (a leather bag representing the sack used to punish those who kill their parents).</summary>

# Chapter XXI.
## What either a *Minerval* or a *Minerval gift* is; and an explanation of the opinions of Juvenal and Varro on the matter.

**Whoever** still worships a small Minerva with a single copper coin: <note>original: "Quisq; adhuc uno parrā colit axe Mineruā" — this is a quotation from Juvenal’s *Satire 10*. The words "parrā" and "axe" in the transcription are likely errors for *parvam* (small) and *asse* (a small coin).</note> Domitius explains this line from the tenth satire of the poet **Juvenal** in such a way that he seems not to have fully grasped its meaning. He suggests a "small Minerva" refers to one whose games during the <term>Quinquatria</term> <note>A five-day Roman festival held in March in honor of Minerva, the patron goddess of wisdom, weaving, and school-learning.</note> he gained the ability to compete in. What could be more absurd or awkward than that? Does anyone, I ask, achieve the ability to compete publicly in eloquence for the price of a single copper coin? 

Furthermore, it seems Juvenal was referring to the <term>Minerval gift</term>—which is also simply called a <term>Minerval</term>—namely, the small fee for teaching that was customarily given by students to their masters. I believe this was paid primarily before the Quinquatria, during which days the boys were on holiday; hence **Horace** says at the end of an Epistle:

> And rather, like a boy once in the festive Quinquatria,
> Enjoy your brief and welcome time in haste.

The same custom applied to the <term>Saturnalia</term> <note>A mid-winter festival of gift-giving and role-reversal.</note> and the Kalends <note>The first day of the month.</note>. Therefore, writing about the Quinquatria in his *Fasti*, **Ovid** says:

> Nor you, the crowd of students, though defrauded of the fierce teacher's fee,
> Despise him; that fee will attract new pupils. <note>original: "Nec uos turba feri censu fraudata magistri. Spernite discipulos attrahet illa nouos." This suggests that paying the fee, even if it feels like a loss, ensures the continuation of the school.</note>

Saint **Jerome** also mentions this *Minerval gift* in his commentary on the Epistle of Paul to the Ephesians, which you may also find in the canon law decrees. He says: "Let bishops and priests read, who educate their sons in secular letters and make them read comedies and sing the shameful writings of mimes. They are perhaps educated at the expense of the churches; and that which a virgin, or a widow, or some poor person—pouring out their whole substance—had offered into the <term>Corban</term> <note>The treasury of the temple or church.</note> for their sins, the grammarian or rhetorician converts into a New Year's gift, or a <term>sportula</term> <note>Literally a "little basket"; a small gift or tip given to a social superior or professional.</note> for the Saturnalia, or a *Minerval gift*—spending it on domestic expenses, temple offerings, or sordid profit."

What Jerome calls "Saturnalian sportulas," **Tertullian** calls simply "Saturnalia-gifts" [*saturnalicia*]. **Varro** also, in the third book of *On Agriculture*, speaks of the *Minerval* in this way:

<vocab>Minerval, Quinquatria, Juvenal, Varro, Jerome, Saturnalia, grammarian, rhetorician</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the "Minerval," a traditional fee paid by Roman students to their teachers during the festival of Minerva. The author uses classical and patristic sources to correct previous misinterpretations and criticize the use of church funds for secular education.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Roman "Minerval" fee, concluding a critique of a commentary on Juvenal before moving to a new chapter on "Echo" poetry, where the author cites his own musical compositions and ancient Greek examples.</meta>

...of Juvenal. **Axius** says: "Merula, receive me as a disciple of villa-pasturing" <note>This refers to Marcus Terentius Varro’s *De Re Rustica*, a foundational Roman text on agriculture. Axius and Merula are characters in the dialogue.</note>. **Merula** replies: "As soon as you have promised a **Minerval** beforehand, I will begin—that is, a dinner."

**Merula** is joking here in **Varro**'s work; because Axius had said he wanted to be received as a disciple, Merula demands a <term>Minerval</term> <note>A fee paid to a teacher, named after Minerva, goddess of wisdom.</note> be promised to him, just as if he were a schoolmaster. He jokingly called the dinner a "Minerval" because it is the "price of mastery." Perhaps, then, the poet's meaning here is that even one who has once offered a Minerval to a teacher—represented by a single copper coin <note>original: "asse", the smallest Roman unit of currency</note>—meaning one who has given his attention to a rhetorician or grammarian for only a few days—prays during the <term>Quinquatria</term> <note>A five-day festival of Minerva held in March, often a time for school fees and holidays.</note> (the festival of Minerva, the patron of genius) not for something modest, but for the eloquence and fame of a **Demosthenes** or a **Cicero**.

### Chapter XXII.

->Of what kind those verses are, in which (as Martial says) a little Greek<-
->Echo sings back.<-

**Marcus Martial**, in the second book of his *Epigrams*, while discussing certain lazy and superfluous poems in which there is much labor but very little praise for the effort, writes this among other things:
> Nowhere does a little Greek Echo sing back.

**Domitius** <note>likely Domizio Calderini, a 15th-century commentator</note> interprets "little Greek Echo" as meaning a Greek word inserted into the text, as if Martial is claiming he never uses Greek words in his epigrams—a claim that is clearly found to be the contrary. Therefore, either our conjecture deceives us, or he is hinting at certain verses composed in such a way that responses are placed at the ends of the lines in the character of **Echo**, completing the sense and following her habit of repeating the last sounds. 

We ourselves have even made some such verses in the vernacular, which are now celebrated by musicians, having been enhanced by the melodies of **Henry** <note>This likely refers to a contemporary composer like Heinrich Isaac or another renowned musician of the period.</note>, and which we also gave about ten years ago to **Pietro Contarini**, a Venetian patrician and a man of no inelegant wit (who desired them greatly at the time), and to several other students of literature. But there also exist Greek examples by a certain ancient poet, **Gauradas**, which I shall also provide:

Echo, dear to me, grant me your assent <margin>something.</margin> <note>original Greek: "ἀχὼ φίλα μοι. συγκαταίνε σόν τι."</note>
I love a girl. She loves another. <margin>She loves.</margin> <note>original Greek: "ἐρῶ κορίσκας. ἂλέμον φιλεῖ. φιλεῖ." In Echo poems, the repeated word often changes the meaning or adds a witty confirmation.</note>

<summary>This page concludes an explanation of the "Minerval" school fee as a pun in Varro's writing and introduces "Echo poetry," where the final syllables of a line are repeated to create a dialogue.</summary>

To act, the time brings no time. <note>original: "πρᾶξαι δ’ ὁ καῖρος καιρὸν οὐ φέρει"</note> **It brings.**
You then tell her that I love. <note>original: "σύ Τοίνυν αὐτᾷ λέξον ὡς ἐρῶ"</note> **I love.**
And give her a pledge of coins. You give. <note>original: "καὶ πίστιν αὐτᾷ κερμάτων σύ Δοσ"</note> **You give.**

## Chapter XXIII.

A passage in Martial should be read as "With a mule as a companion," whereas most read it as "With Gibbus as a companion": at the same time, the reasoning for this is explained, and the truer sense is revealed.

**Martial**, in the sixth book of his *Epigrams*:
->Atlas is no otherwise laughed at than with **Gibbus** as a companion.<-
-><note>original: "Non aliter ridetur Atlas cum compare Gibbo."</note><-

This is how everyone commonly reads it now, and those who are more famous in the study of letters explain it as being most correct. We, on the contrary, in many old manuscripts of less wavering authority and reliability, have found it written thus:
->Atlas is no otherwise laughed at than with a **mule** as a companion.<-
-><note>original: "Non aliter ridetur Atlas cum compare mulo."</note><-

I say "mule" <note>original: "mulo"</note>, not "Gibbus" <note>The author is debating a single word change that alters the meaning from a specific person/hunchback to a beast of burden.</note>. Lest we deal with "deaf witnesses" (as the saying goes), in this very public library of the **Medici** family, there is a very ancient manuscript written in Lombardic script <note>term: "Lombardic script"—Renaissance scholars often used this term to describe early medieval handwriting styles, which they considered more reliable than the later "Gothic" scripts.</note> which **Domitius** <note>likely Domizio Calderini, a prominent 15th-century scholar</note> once read thoroughly here in Florence. Furthermore, in Verona, a certain noble youth named **Bernardinus**, a relative of Domitius, lent me some pages from another most ancient volume. At that time, **Baccio Ugolini**—a man of the most perfected talent and integrity—was with me. 

In Rome as well, **Bernardino Valla**—a man to be celebrated for his devotion to poetry, his knowledge of the law, the nobility of his family, and his wealth, as well as a certain uncommon kindness—showed us a volume of Martial likewise in Lombardic characters and allowed us to read it. Moreover, in Florence, besides these, **Pandolfo**, our chancellor and a citizen of the first nobility, gave us a semi-ancient manuscript to use. In every one of these, you will find the reading we are proposing. 

However, I would not deny that I have also seen that other reading <note>meaning the version with "Gibbus"</note> in one or two examples that were not entirely new; for instance, in the one in the **Palatine Library** in Rome, which is of middle antiquity, and likewise in another when we were reading the collection of **Francesco Sassetti**, the Florentine merchant, but now...

<summary>The author argues for a textual correction in Martial's Epigrams, changing "Gibbus" to "mule" based on his research across several prestigious 15th-century libraries. He cites ancient "Lombardic" manuscripts as superior evidence compared to more common contemporary versions.</summary>
<keywords>Martial, philology, textual criticism, Medici Library, Palatine Library, Lombardic script, Domizio Calderini, Francesco Sassetti, Baccio Ugolini</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into Martial’s Epigrams. Having established the "mule" reading using manuscripts from the Medici library, the author now cites the testimony of Taddeo Ugoleto, the librarian of the famous Corvinian Library, and provides classical context for the joke involving the name "Atlas."</meta>

Taddeo Ugoleto of Parma—a humane and learned man who managed books and other ornaments for the King of the Pannonians <note>term: "Pannonians"—A reference to the Kingdom of Hungary.</note>, Matthias <note>Matthias Corvinus, one of the most famous book collectors of the Renaissance.</note>, a prince of a truly royal spirit—provided these things to us in Florence. In a book that the learned scholar Bernardo Michelotto (versed in both languages <note>meaning Greek and Latin</note>) recently gave me to inspect—which he had borrowed from some Rufinus—I noted that the text was written more corruptly as **Cippo** <note>original: "Cippo." This is a further variation of the corrupted "Gibbo" mentioned on the previous page.</note>. Therefore, since we find the other reading <note>referring to "mulo" or mule</note> to be consistent and solid in the better manuscripts, we must ask: what does "Atlas with a companion mule" mean? And for what reason is it laughed at, just as "a Moor riding an elephant"? Furthermore, we gather from the words of **Juvenal** that a certain **Atlas** was well known to be a dwarf or a pygmy in those times. For he says:

> We call some dwarf "Atlas." <note>original: "Nanum cuiusdam Atlanta uocamus."</note>

So that, conversely, through mockery, he would be called "Atlas" who was of tiny stature; just as:

> To lazy dogs, foul with old mange
> And licking the edges of a dry lamp:
> The name will be Tiger, Leopard, Lion, or whatever else
> Roars more violently on earth.
> <note>Juvenal, Satire 8. The poet explains the Roman habit of ironically naming pathetic pets after fierce beasts.</note>

But also small mules, or she-mules, were then held in high price and kept as pets. Concerning these, the same **Martial** says in his distichs <note>term: "distichs"—A poem or verse of two lines.</note>:

> From these mules of yours, you need not fear a fall;
> You are accustomed to sit almost on the ground.
> <note>original: "His tibi de mulis / non est metuenda ruina / Altius in terra / pene sedere soles."</note>

If, therefore, this dwarf Atlas ever used a mule similarly dwarfish and a match for himself, it is likely that he was commonly laughed at; just as a black Moor <note>original: "maurus niger"</note> was a joke to the common people whenever he mounted an elephant of the same color as himself. Whence the same poet says in the first book:

> And to perform the ordered soft dances,
> The beast refuses nothing to its black master.
> <note>original: "Et molles dare iussa quod choreas / Nigro bellua nil negat magistro"</note>

This fits the purpose and argument of the poet against a man who was young, strong, and poor, yet was repeatedly carried in a litter by six men—who were themselves young, strong, and poor. And just like those mentioned above, he gave everyone an occasion to laugh at him.

<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on the ironic use of the name "Atlas" for dwarves and mentioned the corruption of classical texts. ### Some notes on the old woman Hecale given for memory: on the poetry of Callimachus, and an explanation of a passage from the *Priapeia*; as well as another in Statius, and what is erroneously read concerning it in the manuscripts of Apuleius.

->Equal to you, whom Theseus, returning home,<-
->found lying on a funeral pyre.<-

These little verses are from the *Priapeia* <note>A collection of poems dedicated to the god Priapus.</note>, through which—unless I am mistaken—the little old woman **Hecale** is identified. She was the one by whom **Theseus**, when he was a young man, was once kindly received as a guest. From this, the sacred festival called the **Hecalesion** <note>A rural festival in Attica.</note> became celebrated throughout the villages in honor of **Jupiter Hecalos**, including honors for Hecale herself. They also used to call her **Hecalene** in a diminutive form, because she, in the manner of an old woman, used to greet Theseus—who was still a youth—and welcome him with such "little" endearments and nicknames. Since she had vowed a sacrifice to Jupiter for Theseus’s return when he set out for battle, but died on the day before he returned, Theseus ordered that she receive these honors in exchange for her hospitality, as **Philochorus** <note>An ancient Greek historian and expert on Athenian customs.</note> has recorded. **Plutarch** also mentions this matter in his *Life of Theseus*. Furthermore, **Callimachus** <note>A famous scholar-poet of the Library of Alexandria.</note> wrote a proper poem about her titled *Hecale*, as suggested by a verse from a very well-known epigram:

> I sing of the ways of that hospitable old woman. <note>original Greek: "μέλπω δὲ γραὸς τῆς φιλοξένου τρόπους"</note>

Many authors are found here and there citing Callimachus’s *Hecale*. For even **Pliny** sometimes lists edible herbs that the rustic Hecale serves in Callimachus's work; such as **samphire** <note>original: "Crethmon"</note> in his twenty-sixth book, and also **sow-thistle** <note>original: "Sonchon"</note> in the twenty-second. Although in modern manuscripts "Hecate" is written there instead of "Hecale," the trace of the true reading still remains in the ancient ones kept in the public library of the **Medici family** <note>The Laurentian Library in Florence, founded by the Medici.</note>. Indeed, where Callimachus says in his *Hymn to Apollo*:

> Envy spoke secretly into the ears of Apollo: 
> "I do not admire the singer who does not sing as much as the sea."
> <note>original Greek: "ὁ φθόνος ἀπόλλωνος ἐπ’ οὔατα λάθριος εἶπεν / οὐκ ἄγαμαι τὸν ἀοιδὸν ὃς οὐδ’ ὅσα πόντος ἀείδει." This is a famous defense of short, polished poetry over long, "muddy" epics.</note>

On that passage, the commentator says roughly this: by these words, Callimachus accuses those who mocked him, claiming he was unable to produce a "great" poem <note>meaning a long epic poem.</note>. Because of this, he was compelled to write the *Hecale*. The words of the commentator are: "He accuses [them] because..." <note>original Greek: "εγκαλεῖ διὰ"</note>.

<summary>This page explores the legend of Hecale, the hospitable old woman who hosted Theseus, and defends the literary reputation of Callimachus by explaining the origins of his poem "Hecale." The author uses ancient manuscripts from the Medici library to correct textual corruptions where Hecale's name was mistaken for Hecate.</summary>

<keywords>Hecale, Callimachus, Theseus, Priapeia, Plutarch, Pliny the Elder, Medici Library, philology, Greek poetry, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>The previous page established the identity of Hecale as an elderly woman who hosted Theseus, citing traditions found in the Priapeia, Plutarch, and Philochorus. It introduced the Hellenistic poet Callimachus and his lost epic poem named after her. This page continues to gather classical citations to prove Hecale's significance and then shifts to a critical philological discovery regarding a famous manuscript of Cicero’s letters.</meta>

Because of those mocking him <note>original: "Τούτων τους σκώπτοντας αὐτὸν..."</note> for not being able to write a great [long] poem, he was forced to write the *Hecale*. Here he refers to the nurse.
**The son of Battus** <note>A name for Callimachus, who claimed descent from Battus, founder of Cyrene.</note> **sings of Hecale and the deeds at Marathon, rising loftier than is his custom!**
But **Statius** also speaks of this same woman at the end of his *Thebaid*:
*Nor did the aged hostess pour out vain tears.* <note>original: "Nec fudit uanos anus hospita fletus"</note>
And the **Suda** <note>A 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> says that **Hecale** is a certain heroine found in the works of Callimachus.
Finally, **Apuleius**, in the first book of the *Metamorphoses*, says clearly of her: "If," he says, "content in your little hideaway, you should emulate the virtues of that **Theseus**, who shared your father's name: he did not scorn the humble hospitality of the old woman Hecale." Nor am I unaware that the text is corrupted almost everywhere; for you will frequently find "the old woman **Ales**" written instead of "the old woman **Hecale**," with several letters having been cut off. Thus, in passing, let us also purge the manuscripts of Apuleius of their stains, and dismiss the reader burdened, as it were, with a little extra weight <note>superpondio - a bonus or surplus of information</note>.

->### Chapter 25.<-

### How many things in the *Letters to Friends* of Cicero which are now available are out of order; and into what order they ought to be restored.

I have obtained a most ancient volume of **Cicero’s** *Letters to Friends*, of which I have already spoken above; and from that very same volume, another copy was transcribed, as some believe, by the hand of **Francesco Petrarch**. It is clear from many arguments, which I shall omit for now, that it was indeed copied from that source. But this later manuscript I mentioned has been so poorly bound by a careless bookseller <note>bibliopola - here referring to the craftsman responsible for binding the pages</note> that a set of ten pages has been transposed—as can be discovered by checking the numbers noted against them. This book is kept in the public library of the **Medici family** <note>The Laurentian Library in Florence</note>. From this single volume, as far as I can gather, all the manuscripts of these letters that currently exist have flowed, as if from a spring and source. In all of them, the order of reading is jumbled and perverted; it must now be restored to its proper place by me, and, as it were, renewed. Therefore, I pray, give me your attention, whoever you are that embraces these liberal studies. Therefore, in the eighth book, the letter from **Caelius** to Cicero begins thus: *Cer*...

<summary>This page concludes a philological defense of Callimachus's Hecale by correcting a corruption in Apuleius's text, then identifies a major structural error in the manuscript tradition of Cicero's letters housed in the Medici library.</summary>
<keywords>Hecale, Callimachus, Apuleius, textual corruption, Cicero, Letters to Friends, Francesco Petrarch, Medici Library, codicology, philology</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on the Greek poet Callimachus and his poem *Hecale*, while starting a new section on correcting the sequence of Cicero's *Letters to Friends*. The translator is now following the author's detailed instructions on how to physically reorder the text of these letters, which had been scrambled by negligent copyists.</meta>

"he was acquitted," I tell you, "and it was announced that I would represent him: and indeed by all the orders <note>The three classes of judges in Roman trials: senators, equestrians, and treasury tribunes.</note>, but also by the individual votes in every class." Look now, you say; up to this point the order has not yet been interrupted. But the words that follow immediately—"you will have shown by letters," and so on—you will find to be entirely different from those preceding them and belonging to another letter altogether.

Proceed further: from that very letter, count the subsequent ones in order until you reach the twenty-fourth, which begins like this: "By Hercules, not I. For nothing ever." This letter actually continues from the previous one. Thus you should read: "Certainly," he says, "he was acquitted, and it was announced that I would represent him: and indeed by all the orders, but also by the individual votes in every class. Look now, you say. By Hercules, not I. For nothing ever," and so connect the rest. Continue until from this letter (which we said belongs elsewhere) you reach the tenth following one, which begins: "So you ask about **Hirtius** <note>Aulus Hirtius, a Roman general and historian associated with Julius Caesar.</note>." Read that until you hit these words: "If any hope." Once you encounter those, you will delete all of this.

Or, "on the right side of the flaw" <note>original: "in recto uitii"</note>, "the rest will be approved by me." All of this, I say, you will delete or strike out, for they are clearly alien to this location. Soon you will return again to those passages placed incorrectly in that letter of **Caelius** <note>Marcus Caelius Rufus, a close friend and correspondent of Cicero.</note> mentioned at the start; and thus you will continue the reading: "If you have shown any hope by letters." Thus you will read continuously until you reach the twenty-third letter from here, which begins: "I will respond to two of his letters," and ends with this conclusion: "For if there is nothing in the walls, or on the right side of the flaw, the rest will be approved by me." Immediately after that, the letter which now begins as the eleventh should follow, starting: "Your letters have delighted me."

This order which we have set out is confirmed not only by both manuscripts—the ancient one and this one immediately derived from it, which makes it clear that all other copies were corrupted by the bookseller's error—but also by the logic and sense itself, which is clearer than daylight. Thus, for anyone looking closely, whatever was previously obscure and ambiguous is entirely removed.
