--- Page 78 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a technical discussion on the proper arrangement of Cicero’s *Letters to Friends*, which had been corrupted by copyists. This page shifts to a literary commentary, comparing Latin translations of Greek epigrams, specifically focusing on a witty verse about a vine and its predator.</meta>

Ovid in the first book of the *Fasti* <note>original: "Vidius" (likely a typo for Ovidius). The *Fasti* is Ovid’s poetic calendar of Roman festivals and mythology.</note>.
> The pig had paid the penalty; terrified by this example,
> You, O goat, should have kept away from the vine-shoot.
> Someone, seeing him pressing his teeth into the vine,
> Uttered these words with no silent grief:
> "Gnaw the vine, goat; yet when you stand at the altar,
> There will be enough to be sprinkled upon your horns."

Truly, these two verses were fashioned from a very famous and witty couplet by a Greek poet; it is widely celebrated and, as they say, "flies through the mouths of learned men" <note>A reference to the poet Ennius’s famous line about fame surviving death.</note>. When the author recorded those words, he pointed his finger at the writer of the poem, **Evenus** <note>Evenus of Ascalon, a Greek epigrammatist whose work often featured such wordplay.</note>. In the Greek verses, however, the vine itself speaks, like this:

->Even if you eat me to the root, I will still bear fruit<-
->enough to pour as a libation over you, goat, when you are sacrificed.<-
<note>original Greek: "κἤν με φάγῃσ᾽ ἐπὶ ῥίζαν ὅμωσ᾽ ἔτι καρποφορήσω / ὅσον ἐπισπεῖσαί σοι τράγε θυομένῳ."</note>

Our most ingenious poet [Ovid] translated these as precisely as he could; yet there are certain things in the Greek that our author rendered somewhat vaguely. Indeed, if we must concede the point, our poet did not even touch upon that "overseas Venus"—some specific charm of the original—which is perhaps a fault of a language that is less playful rather than one that lacks vocabulary <note>The author suggests Latin is a "sturdier" but less "graceful" language than Greek for this kind of wit.</note>. But since we have run into the mention of this Greek couplet, let us also bring forward what appears in **Suetonius**’s life of **Domitian**, so that we may shed light on that well-known passage in passing; let us provide it as a sort of bonus with the main goods, so as not to be stingy. The words are these: When the edict for cutting down the vineyards was proposed, it is believed he [the Emperor] was swayed by nothing so much as the fact that pamphlets were scattered about containing these verses:

->Even if you eat me to the root, I will still bear fruit<-
->enough to pour as a libation over Caesar when he is sacrificed.<-
<note>original Greek: "κἤν με φάγῃσ᾽ ἐπὶ ῥίζαν ὅμωσ᾽ ἔτι καρποφορήσω / ὅσον ἐπισπεῖσαι καίσαρι θυομένῳ."</note>

For what the vine previously threatened against the goat, it now threatens against **Caesar** <note>The Emperor Domitian. He had ordered the destruction of vineyards to increase grain production, a highly unpopular move.</note>—a most beautiful parody, by Hercules! "Even if you eat me to the root," it says, "I will still produce enough wine to be poured over Caesar while he is being sacrificed." For it was the custom of the ancients to pour wine on the heads of the victims as they stood beneath the blow and the knife. This is seen, for instance, in the works of **Maro** <note>The poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro).</note>:

<summary>This page analyzes a famous Greek epigram by Evenus of Ascalon about a vine threatening a goat, comparing Ovid’s Latin translation and a daring political parody used against the Emperor Domitian. It highlights the cultural practice of pouring wine libations on sacrificial victims.</summary>

<keywords>Ovid, Evenus, Suetonius, Domitian, Virgil, Latin translation, Greek epigrams, political parody, Roman sacrifice, viticulture</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed a Greek epigram about a goat eating a vine, which Ovid translated into Latin. It concluded by noting the subtle charms of the Greek language that even the best Roman poets sometimes struggle to capture.</meta>

> The most beautiful Dido herself, holding the bowl in her right hand,
> Pours [the wine] between the horns of a white cow.
<note>original: "Ipſa tenens dextra pateram pulcherrima Dido / Candentis uaccę media / inter cornua fundit." This is a quote from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (4.60-61), illustrating the ritual of a libation, continuing the theme of pouring wine over a sacrifice.</note>

Therefore, they used these little verses in pamphlets to mark the Emperor Domitian for death. As the same Suetonius <note>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus, a Roman biographer known for his *Lives of the Caesars*.</note> writes: because of a great abundance of wine but a shortage of grain, and thinking that the fields were being neglected due to an excessive devotion to vineyards, Domitian issued an edict that no one in Italy should plant new vines, and that in the provinces the vineyards should be cut down, leaving at most half of them standing; however, he did not persist in carrying out the matter. Let the studious reader remember that in this discovery we have looked out for the common good in more than one way. In most manuscripts of Suetonius until now—even some of the oldest—you would not only fail to find these Greek verses, but you would find not a trace nor even a space where they might be inserted. <margin>o</margin> But because we have possessed them for a long time—for they are most charming—we have tracked them down through the obsolete and faulty copies, weighing each letter one by one, gradually recognizing and restoring them.

## Chapter XXVII.
### Words in an epistle of Cicero taken from Ennius’s *Medea*, which was based on the Greek of Euripides.

Cicero, in a letter to Trebatius <note>Gaius Trebatius Testa, a prominent Roman jurist and friend of Cicero.</note>, says: "We friends will forgive you for this as much as the wealthy, noble matrons who lived in the high citadel of Corinth forgave Medea; she, with her most whitened hands <note>original: "manibus gypsatissimis." This likely refers to the "whitened" hands of the noblewomen, or perhaps a corruption in the text the author is correcting.</note>, persuaded them not to hold it against her as a fault that she was absent from her fatherland. For many have managed their own affairs and the public business well while far from their country. Many who spent their lives at home have for that very reason been disparaged."

Until now, no one among those whose opinions have been published has suspected that these are not entirely Cicero’s own words, but rather those of a poet who was permitted to use such expressions and figures of speech. Examples include: "who lived in the high citadel of Corinth" and "wealthy, noble matrons." Nor have they seen this: that two entire iambic verses still stand intact in these lines, and the mangled limbs <note>A metaphor for fragments of poetry embedded in prose.</note> of others can, as it were, be recognized. We have gathered that these words come from the *Medea* of Ennius <note>Quintus Ennius, the "father of Roman poetry." His *Medea Exul* was a Latin adaptation of Euripides’ Greek tragedy.</note>, though not in their exact order. But—

<summary>The author explains how a Greek epigram was used as a political threat against Emperor Domitian and identifies hidden poetic fragments from Ennius's "Medea" within Cicero's personal letters.</summary>
<keywords>Suetonius, Domitian, vineyards, textual criticism, Cicero, Trebatius, Ennius, Medea, Euripides, philology</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced Chapter XXVII, discussing how Cicero used lines from Ennius’s Latin version of the play *Medea* in a letter to the lawyer Trebatius. It set the stage for a comparison between the Roman adaptation and the Greek original by Euripides.</meta>

...which was most convenient for Cicero to quote. Since we have learned that the Latin *Medea* by Ennius was translated from the Greek of Euripides, and because we have suffered the loss of that Ennian work <note>Quintus Ennius was an early Roman poet; most of his tragedies survive only as fragments quoted by later authors like Cicero.</note>, I decided to seek the meaning of these words in Euripides' tragedy itself. 

Thus, in that play, Medea is introduced speaking with the Corinthian matrons so that she might persuade them that her absence from her fatherland ought not to be turned into a fault against her. For she says that many honorable people live far from their country, while many others, on the contrary, have lived at home without glory. Indeed, foreign women often had a bad reputation; hence that line from a comedy: "Is he so mad as to marry a foreigner?" <note>This reflects a common Roman and Greek social prejudice where foreign women were often viewed with suspicion or seen as lower in status than citizen-wives.</note> Similarly, the character Parmeno <note>A slave character from Terence’s play *The Eunuch*.</note> says it could be kept quiet that Thais's mother was from Samos <note>An island in the Aegean Sea.</note> while they were then living in Athens. 

Lest anyone should perhaps lack them, we shall write down here the little verses of Euripides himself, spoken in the persona of Medea:

> Women of Corinth, I have come forth from the house,
> Lest you find some fault with me. For I know many mortals
> Are perceived as proud: some from what is seen of them,
> Others in their private lives; while some, through a quiet nature,
> Have gained a reputation for laziness and ill-repute.
<note>original: "κορίνθιαι γυναῖκες ἐξῆλθον Δόμων / μή μοί τι μέμψησθ᾽ οἶδα γὰρ πολλοὺς βροτῶν / σεμνοὺς γεγῶτας . τοὺς μὲν ὀμμάτων ἄπο / Τοὺς δὲ θυραίους . οἱ δ᾽ ἀφ᾽ ἡσύχου ποδὸς / Δύσκλειαν ἐκτήσαντο καὶ ῥᾳθυμίαν." — These are lines 214–218 of Euripides’ *Medea*.</note>

Regarding my statement that the poet Ennius translated his tragedy *Medea* from the Greek of Euripides, we gather this partly from the words of Cicero, who says in the first book of *On the Ends of Good and Evil*: <note>original: "de bonorū malorūq; finibus" — Cicero's *De Finibus*, a philosophical work on ethics.</note> "For who is so hostile to the Roman name that he would despise or reject Ennius’s *Medea* or Pacuvius’s <note>Marcus Pacuvius, another early Roman tragic playwright.</note> *Antiopa*? Who, while claiming to delight in those same plays by Euripides, would hate Latin literature?" 

We also know this because the beginning of Ennius’s tragedy is cited in many places: such as in Quintilian’s <note>A famous Roman teacher of rhetoric.</note> fifth volume; in Cicero’s book *On Fate*; <note>original: "de fato"</note> and likewise by other authors repeatedly. However, it is nowhere cited in a longer passage than in the second book on rhetoric by the same Cicero, *To Herennius*. <note>The *Rhetorica ad Herennium*, long attributed to Cicero, though now usually considered the work of an unknown author.</note> We provide a version of this here, corrected from various manuscripts, so that the obscurity arising in several places in the Latin language may be illuminated—specifically those instances where one or two verses from the start of that tragedy are quoted without the author's name, leaving the reader's understanding in suspense. The passage in Cicero itself is as follows: "Let us not," he says, "imitate Ennius and the other poets, to whom it is permitted to speak in this manner."

<sig>f i</sig>

<summary>The author examines the relationship between Euripides’ Greek tragedy *Medea* and Ennius’s lost Latin translation, providing the original Greek verses and citing Cicero to defend the value of Latin adaptations.</summary>
<keywords>Cicero, Euripides, Ennius, Medea, Latin literature, Greek tragedy, translation, philology, Ad Herennium, De Finibus</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed how the Roman poet Ennius translated Euripides’ *Medea* into Latin, and how Cicero used those lines to illustrate points in his own letters. This page provides the actual text of Ennius's opening lines, compares them to the Greek original, and begins a new discussion on the origins of "Panic terrors."</meta>

Would that the fir timbers felled by axes in the Pelian grove <note>Mount Pelion in Greece, where the wood for the Argo was cut.</note> had never fallen to the ground, nor that the beginning of building the ship had commenced there—the ship which is now called by the name *Argo*—in which the chosen Argive men, carried across the sea, sought that golden fleece of the ram from the Colchians <note>Colchis was a kingdom on the eastern coast of the Black Sea, in modern-day Georgia.</note> through the guile of King Pelias. For then my wandering mistress, Medea, her mind aching and wounded by cruel love, would never have set foot from her home.

In the second verse, the word *trabes* <note>meaning a beam or timber</note> is used in the singular number, just as Priscian <note>A famous Roman grammarian; his work was the standard for Latin grammar throughout the Middle Ages.</note> reads it in his seventh book. He notes that the most ancient writers (as he says himself) used the form *trabes* instead of the standard singular *trabs*. If you compare the opening of the tragedy by Euripides <note>The Athenian playwright whose *Medea* (431 BC) is the source for Ennius’s version.</note> with these lines by Ennius, I believe you will find it worth the effort; therefore, I have added the Greek below:

Would that the hull of the *Argo* had never flown through the dark-blue Symplegades <note>original: "κυανέας συμπληγάδας" (kyaneas symplēgadas); these were the "Clashing Rocks" that guarded the entrance to the Black Sea.</note> to the land of the Colchians, nor that the hewn pine had ever fallen in the glens of Pelion; and would that it had never equipped the hands of the noble men with oars, who went to fetch the all-golden fleece for Pelias. For then my mistress Medea would not have sailed to the towers of the land of Iolcus, her heart struck with love for Jason.

### Chapter XXVIII:

->What are called "Panic terrors," and the many distinguished witnesses cited regarding them.<-

In Cicero’s letter to Tiro <note>Marcus Tullius Tiro was Cicero’s beloved former slave and secretary who edited his letters.</note>, which is found at the end of his collected *Letters to Friends*, it is written as follows: "Even if our friend Atticus <note>Titus Pomponius Atticus, Cicero's closest friend.</note>, who once noticed that I was stirred by panic terrors <note>original: "πανικοῖς" (panikois)</note>, always thinks the same, he does not see with what defenses of philosophy I am now protected. By Hercules, because he is timid himself, he makes a fuss <note>original: "θορυβοποιεῖ" (thorubopoiei); literally meaning to cause an uproar or agitation.</note>."

These are called "Panic" terrors (as I believe) by those following the Greek tradition; they are certain sudden fears and consternations, such as those that seize people driven mad <note>The text mentions "lympha," likely referring to *lymphatici*, people whom the ancients believed were driven to a state of panic or frenzy by water nymphs or forest spirits.</note>...

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of how Ennius translated Euripides’ Medea and how Latin authors used Greek concepts. This page continues that investigation by exploring the specific origins and literary uses of the term "panic," tracing it from Cicero back to Greek mythology and the god Pan.</meta>

<note>original: "[pani]tici"</note> ...fears are so irrevocable that they lack not only reason, but even mind and intellect. The same Cicero uses this word in his *Letters to Atticus*, book five: "For you know," he says, "that certain things are called **panic**." <note>original: "πανικὰ" (panika). Cicero is referencing sudden, irrational fears.</note> Likewise, Hyginus <note>Gaius Julius Hyginus, a Roman mythographer and librarian to Emperor Augustus.</note> mentions **panic terror** in his passage concerning the constellation **Capricorn** with these words: "For he is said, when Jupiter fought the Titans, to have been the first to cast a terror against the enemies which is called **Panic**; as Eratosthenes <note>A Greek polymath and librarian at Alexandria who wrote on the stars.</note> says." This story is also alluded to by Germanicus in his commentary on Aratus. <note>Aratus was a Greek poet whose work on astronomy was famously translated into Latin by Germanicus, the Roman general.</note> Euripides says the following in his *Medea*:

->And some aged servant, thinking perhaps that<-
->the wrath of Pan or some other god had come,<-
->raised a ritual cry.<-
<note>original: "καὶ Τίς γεραιὰ προσπόλων δόξασά που / ἢ πανὸς ὀργὰς ἢ Τινός θεῶν μολεῖν / ἀνολόλυξε." This describes a servant seeing Medea's distress and assuming she is possessed or under a divine spell.</note>

Euripides calls them the "wraths of Pan," just as his most ancient commentator calls them **panic terrors** <note>original: "τὰ πανικὰ δείματα" (ta panika deimata)</note>; that is, "because they attribute the cause of sudden fears and disturbances to Pan." Since, he says, they attribute the cause of sudden terrors and consternation to the god Pan. And in Synesius, in his book *On Providence*, I find roughly this: "And **panic tumults** seized the army during the day." <note>original: "καὶ πανικοὶ θόρυβοι μεθ’ ἡμέραν τὸ στράτευμα κατελάμβανον." Synesius was a 4th-century philosopher-bishop.</note> In that passage, he also explains at length what kind of terrors these finally were.

Indeed, an unknown commentator explains these words of Synesius such that he says they are called **panic terrors** when men and horses in an army are suddenly thrown into confusion with no apparent cause. For women, he says, stirred by divine power, are accustomed to celebrate the **rites of Pan** <note>original: "panos orgia"</note> with shouts; by which shouts, being sudden, the listeners are struck with fear.

But also Nicetas Choniates, <note>A Byzantine Greek historian and writer of the 12th century.</note> an author by no means to be despised among the Greeks, mentions "**panic phantasms**" in one of his orations. Theon, a commentator on the poet Aratus, also says that the god Pan fought against the Titans; and it seems he was the first to invent that twisted and spiraled shell which they use for a trumpet, which is called in Greek a **Cochlos** <note>term: Cochlos (κόχλος). A conch or spiral shell used as a signaling horn in antiquity.</note>. And by Pan's effort, his allies were given time to arm themselves while the Titans were driven into flight by that sound—whatever it was—which is called "**panic**." But also the poet Nonnus in the tenth book of the *Dionysiaca*, <note>An epic poem about the god Dionysus, written in the 5th century AD.</note> describing the raging Athamas...

### Chapter XXVIII.

Why the "Teuthrantian crowd" in Ovid are called the daughters of Thespius.
Ovid, in the letter from Deianira to Hercules <note>Heroides IX, a fictional letter from Hercules's wife complaining of his many infidelities.</note>:
> Nor shall the sisters of the Teuthrantian crowd be a crime for you,
> Of whose people none was left untouched by you.
<note>original: "Nec tibi crimen erunt teuthrantia turba ſorores. / Quarum de populo nulla relicta tibi eſt." Ovid refers to the myth where Hercules slept with all fifty daughters of King Thespius in a single night.</note>

This is a very well-known story, also recorded by Diodorus <note>Diodorus Siculus, a 1st-century BC Greek historian.</note>, regarding the daughters of Thespius who were bedded by Hercules. However, why they are called the "Teuthrantian crowd" is so unknown and obscure to many that certain grammarians have dared to substitute the word *Thespeia* for *Teuthrantia*. This is done with great wickedness and ignorance. We have found the explanation for this in Eustathius <note>A 12th-century Byzantine scholar and archbishop who wrote extensive commentaries on Homer.</note>, the interpreter of Homer, where he is explaining the "Catalogue of Ships": 
> The writer of the *Ethnica* <note>Stephanus of Byzantium, an 6th-century author of a geographical dictionary.</note> says that Thespeia is a foundation of Thespiades; but according to some, of Thespius, the son of Teuthras, the son of Pandion.
<note>original: "φησὶ δὲ ὁ τῶν ἐθνικῶν συγγραφεὺς· ὅτι κτίσμα ἐστιν ἡ θέσπεια θεσπιάδου. κατὰ δὲ τινάς θεσπίου· τοῦ Τεύθραντος τοῦ πανδίονος."</note> 
Thus, based on the authority of someone who wrote about various peoples, he records that the city of Thespia was founded by Thespiades; but as others write, it was by Thespius, the son of Teuthras and grandson of Pandion <note>An ancient legendary King of Athens.</note>. Therefore, Ovid calls the daughters of Thespius the "Teuthrantian crowd" after their grandfather, Teuthras. Finally, even Aeschylus, in his tragedy *Agamemnon*, called the city of Thespia by the name of Teuthras.

### Chapter XXX.

Words rare to find, yet not unique: *Cucuma*, *Proseucha*, *Scruta*.
Certain words are so rare to find that if we happen upon them a second time, we feel we have done something worthwhile, since they were previously believed to be unique (appearing only once). Such is the case with *Cucuma* <note>A cooking pot or kettle.</note> in Martial, in this half-line of verse:
<note>The Roman poet Martial mentions the "cucuma" in his *Epigrams* (14.114), describing a vessel used to heat water.</note>

<meta>The previous page discussed the myth of Hercules and the daughters of King Thespius, explaining why they were referred to by Ovid as the "Teuthrantian crowd." It focused on the etymology and historical geography linking the region of Teuthrania to the family of Thespius.</meta>

Oracilius made a kettle:
For so we read in the satirical work of Petronius Arbiter <note>A Roman courtier and novelist under Nero, traditionally identified as the author of the *Satyricon*.</note>: "She tasted just a tiny bit of that meat; and while she began to put back the portion meant for her birthday offerings into the meat-rack with a fork, the rotten stool—which she had used to add to her height—broke. It sent the old woman, falling by her own weight, onto the hearth. Thus the neck of the **Kettle** <term>cucuma: a metal cooking vessel or water-heater</term> was broken, extinguishing the growing fire and covering her whole face with the stirred-up ash." From this noun comes the diminutive *cucumion*, which I find in Arrian’s books on Epictetus <note>Arrian was a 2nd-century historian who recorded the Stoic teachings of Epictetus.</note>, specifically in the chapter titled "On Cynicism" <note>original: "περι κυνισμου" (peri kynismou)</note>; I will even provide Arrian's words: "He must have a little kettle <note>original: "κουκούμιον" (koukoumion)</note> where he may heat water for the child, so that he may wash it." He said *cucumion* in the Greek fashion, which we call a *cucumula* [little kettle], quite elegantly.

Furthermore, the word *proseucha* <term>proseucha: a Greek term for a place of prayer, often used by Romans to refer specifically to Jewish meeting houses</term> is thought to be found only in Juvenal <note>A Roman satirical poet active in the late 1st and early 2nd centuries AD.</note> as a term for a place of medicinal remedies. However, Cleomedes <note>A Stoic astronomer and philosopher.</note>, in the second book of his *Circular Inspection*, when arguing against Epicurus, speaks in almost this way: "Some of these ideas are taken from brothels; some are very similar to those things said by women during the festivals of Ceres; others indeed are taken from the middle of the **house of prayer** [*proseucha*] and from those very people who beg there—plainly Jewish, worn-out, and even lower than reptiles."

Additionally, Horace used the word *scruta* <term>scruta: odds and ends, junk, or second-hand goods</term> once in this verse:
->Selling cheap **junk** to the tunic-clad common folk<-
<note>original: "Vilia uendentem tunicato scruta popello." From Horace, *Epistles* 1.7.65. The "tunic-clad" refers to the poor who did not wear togas.</note>
As far as I can see, Sidonius Apollinaris <note>A 5th-century Gallo-Roman poet and bishop.</note> used it again in the seventh book of his letters with these words: "Now he gave away trifles, now **junk** [*scruta*] fit for a young girl's game."

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on the term "proseucha," used by Juvenal and Cleomedes to describe places of prayer or assembly, often associated with the Jewish community or medicinal contexts. This page shifts to another passage in Juvenal regarding social status and the "three names" of a Roman citizen.</meta>

"To open your mouth: as if you have three names." <note>original: "Hiscere: tanquam habeas tria nomina." This is a reference to Juvenal’s *Satire 5*, line 127.</note>

Indeed, Domitius says the same (for I omit other interpreters, as I clash with him as the more stubborn one). Domitius, then, explained this passage from Juvenal’s fifth satire thus: "If you wished to speak," he says, "as if you also were one of the nobles; as if it pleases you that only those who are noble are allowed to speak." For nobility was designated by the surname, the additional name, and the personal name <term>tria nomina: the traditional three-part name of a Roman citizen (praenomen, nomen, and cognomen)</term>. For example: Gaius Iulius Caesar. Thus far, Domitius's words are literal. 

However, neither did the nobles alone possess three names, nor did all of them. Rather, I believe it refers to those who were set free from slavery <term>manumissio: the formal act of freeing a slave</term>. Therefore, the sense is even sharper and more elegant: that the host does not even consider the guest a free man, nor does he think it right for him to speak as a free man, even though he has invited him as a guest. If authors do not prove this—though it is hardly a slippery foundation for belief—I will admit that nothing is more carefully weighed than Domitius's opinion. 

Artemidorus of Ephesus, therefore—or if he prefers to be called Daldianus—in the first book of his *On the Interpretation of Dreams* <note>A 2nd-century AD Greek manual on dream analysis.</note>, wrote in Greek, but to this effect: "I knew," he says, "a certain slave who dreamt that he had three male organs. Later he was indeed made free, and in that way had three names instead of one <note>original: "tria pro uno habuit nomina"</note>, having assumed two names from his patron." Thus far Artemidorus. 

Septimius Florens Tertullian <note>An early Christian author from Carthage, active around 200 AD.</note> also writes literally as follows in his book *On the Resurrection of the Flesh*: "I ask you," he says, "if you change your servant's status through freedom, because the same flesh and soul will remain which were previously subject to lashes, shackles, and brands, will it therefore be necessary for them to suffer the same things? I think not. Yet he is honored both by the brightness of a white garment, and the honor of a gold ring, and the name and tribe of his patron." 

From this, I believe, comes that line of Persius: "He exits as Marcus Dama." <note>Persius, *Satire 5.78-79*. Dama was a common slave name; by adding the Roman name "Marcus," he signifies his new status as a citizen.</note> Cicero also proves this custom in the fourth volume of his letters to Atticus, writing thus: "Regarding Eutychides, it is pleasing: who, with his old personal name and a new name, will be T. Caecilius; just as Dionysius is M. Pomponius, joined from both me and you." <note>Cicero's slaves Eutychides and Dionysius took the names of Cicero’s friend, Titus Pomponius Atticus, upon being freed.</note> 

But to interpret this passage of Cicero in passing—which is not obscure—one must know that it was also the custom for heirs to be adopted into a name and family by a will. Just as Gaius Octavius was adopted by Caesar, as Suetonius also writes in...

<meta>The previous page discussed Roman naming conventions (the tria nomina) and how they applied to both the nobility and freed slaves, specifically referencing a passage from Juvenal. Julius: For this reason, after that day he was always called "Caesar," as he is also addressed in the letters of Brutus to Cicero. Similarly, Quintus Scipio, Pompey’s colleague in the consulship: although he was the son of Nasica, he was adopted by will into the family of Metellus Pius and was also called by his name. As Dio <note>Dio Cassius, a Roman historian who wrote in Greek.</note> writes in the fortieth book of his *Histories*. For this reason also, Titus Pomponius Atticus, left as heir to three-quarters of the estate <term>ex dodrante: an inheritance of nine-twelfths or three-quarters of an estate</term> of Quintus Caecilius—as Cornelius Nepos writes in his *Life of Atticus*—was afterwards called Quintus Caecilius Atticus instead of Titus Pomponius Atticus, according to Varro in the second book of *On Farming*. Therefore, he called the manumitted <term>manumissio: the formal act of freeing a slave</term> Eutychides "Titus Caecilius Eutychides," using his own old personal name <term>praenomen: the first name of a Roman citizen, such as Titus or Quintus</term>. For he used to be called "Titus Pomponius" in the past; but by his new name, he had recently begun to be called "Quintus Caecilius." Thus the slave Dionysius was called Marcus Pomponius Dionysius, having adopted Cicero’s personal name and Atticus’s family name. But also Alexander of Miletus, who had the surname "Polyhistor," was also called "Cornelius" after Cornelius Lentulus, by whom he had clearly been set free from slavery; as the collections called *The Suda* <note>original: "Suidae" — a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world.</note> testify.

### Chapter xxxii.

->A word long abolished is restored to the manuscripts of Pliny; what a "poppysmos" or "poppysma" signifies; and the custom of adoring lightning-flashes with poppysms.<-

A passage in Pliny must be corrected from the twenty-eighth book of his *Natural History*, in the second chapter where he treats whether there is any power in words for healing. For one word has been removed by which a true custom is indicated, a fact also confirmed by an older testimony. Most manuscripts have it thus: "It is the consensus of nations to adore lightning-flashes." But in two ancient copies from the noble library of the Medici family <note>The *Bibliotheca Medicea Laurenziana* in Florence, one of the most significant collections of classical manuscripts.</note>, it is written thus: "It is the consensus of nations to adore lightning-flashes with **poppysms**." <term>poppysmus: a rhythmic smacking or clucking sound made with the lips, used in antiquity as a ritualistic or soothing gesture</term>. This reading is confirmed even by the very witty poet Aristophanes in the comedy titled *The Wasps* <note>original Greek: "σφηκεσ" (Sphekes)</note>. For in his work, a certain old man named Philocleo speaks in such a way that...

<keywords>Roman naming conventions, adoption, manumission, Titus Pomponius Atticus, Pliny the Elder, Natural History, poppysmus, ritual, lightning, Aristophanes, philology</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on Roman naming conventions and the use of the tria nomina (three names) for citizens and freedmen. This page continues with a philological investigation into specific Greek and Latin terms used by classical authors, beginning with an analysis of the word "poppysmus" (a clucking or popping sound) and moving into a commentary on a famous proverb from the satirist Juvenal regarding "crambe" (cabbage).</meta>

He says that he will strike with lightning those who "cluck" <note>original: "poppyssantibus"; referring to a specific sound made by the lips, often used to calm animals or as a superstitious gesture</note> (if I may be permitted to use such a word). Then, a most ancient interpreter says it is the custom to cluck at flashes of lightning. These are the words of Aristophanes: *And I will flash lightning at those who cluck.* <note>original: "κἀναστράψω ποππύζουσιν"</note> Then the interpreter says this: *It is the custom to cluck at lightning.* <note>original: "ἔθοσ ταισ ἀστραπαισ ποππύζειν"</note> Therefore, just as the word *poppysma* <note>a clicking or smacking sound</note> and *poppysmata* are read in the poets Juvenal and Martial, so in Pliny we find *poppysmis* from the nominative case which is *poppysmus*. It is, moreover, an onomatopoeic word <term>facticia de sono vox: a word created to imitate a natural sound</term> signifying that sound with which we especially soothe horses not yet fully tamed, as Maximus says in his lexicon, and Nicas, as well as Suidas. <note>Refers to the *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedic lexicon.</note> Sophocles also uses the word: *the woman driver clucks,* <note>original: "ποππύζεται ζευγηλάτρις"</note> just as Pollux mentions in the seventh book of his *Onomasticon* <note>A Greek thesaurus and dictionary from the 2nd century</note>. Indeed, the same author also teaches in his first book that a horse is stopped by clucking, employing this very word, which Pliny also uses; however, in the thirty-fifth book of his *Natural History*, Pliny says: *A similar success followed Nealces in the matter of horse-foam when a sponge was thrown at the painting; he was depicting a man clucking* <note>original: "poppyzonta"</note> *while holding back a horse.* Dexippus the Platonist philosopher, in the dialogue he published on Aristotle’s *Categories*, also writes: *or if there is some inarticulate sound, as in clucking.* <note>original: "ἡ εἴτις ἄναρθροσ ψόφοσ ώσ ἐπὶ ποππυσμάτων"</note>

### Chapter XXXIII.
->The "Crambe" <note>Cabbage</note> that appears in Juvenal; and furthermore, the Greek proverb concerning it.<-

> Repeated cabbage kills the wretched teachers. <note>original: "Occidit miseros Crambe repetita magistros" — a famous line from Juvenal’s Satires (7.154) suggesting that repeating the same material is deadly dull.</note>

Domitius Calderinus explains this verse of Juvenal in these words: Cornelius writes thus concerning declamations <term>declamationes: formal rhetorical exercises or practice speeches used in Roman education</term>. Declamations were either drawn from histories—as some certainly are even to this day—or from truth and actual events, if something had perhaps recently happened. Therefore, they were usually presented with the names of places added; and certainly, those that have been collected and published are of this nature. "Crambe," therefore, is the title of a declamation named after a place. For Crambe is a town in Teuthrania in the Troad, as Pliny writes; and in the nearest region is Lesbos, where tyrants occupied Mitylene, as Strabo attests; these were Myrsilus and Megarogilus; the poet Alcaeus [attacked] them in song...

<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on the word "poppysmus" (a clucking sound) used by classical authors like Pliny and Juvenal. ...criticized: whence he is said to have possessed a golden plectrum <note>original: "aureum plectrum"; a small tool used to pluck the strings of a lyre, here symbolizing divine or supreme poetic skill</note> according to the authority of Fabius <note>referring to the Roman rhetorician Quintilian</note>.

Teachers always had to repeat a "Cambrian" declamation <term>declamatio: a formal speech or rhetorical exercise practiced in schools</term> regarding the expulsion of tyrants. This interpretation is pleasing to some, so that it might agree with what the poet said:

->**When a numerous fleet destroys cruel tyrants.**<-

However, the fact that others derive this "Cambrian" declamation from Cambrus, a king of the Scythians mentioned by Diodorus <note>Diodorus Siculus, a 1st-century BCE Greek historian</note>, is in no way appropriate.

Thus far Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century scholar known for his controversial commentaries</note> has argued with a thoroughly winding and evasive speech. From his words, this confused and forced opinion is finally gathered: that the "Cambrian" declamation is named after a town in Teuthrania <note>A region in Asia Minor</note>. He argues this because names are often given to declamations based on locations, and because tyrants once occupied Mitylene, a town in Lesbos; he believes the poet signifies this in that specific verse:

->**When a numerous fleet destroys cruel tyrants.**<-

Therefore, we do not labor over whether or not controversies <term>controversiae: a specific type of legal-themed rhetorical exercise</term> were proposed by the ancients with the names of places added. It was not Cornelius (as he himself claims), but Suetonius Tranquillus who wrote this in the little book he composed *On Famous Grammarians and Rhetoricians*. Domitius himself previously recognized that book not as the work of Cornelius, but of Suetonius, and thus he entangles himself in his own snare (as the saying goes) while explaining—as it seems to him, at least—a verse from that same satire:

->**In another part, put down the lonely countryside of the tattered one.**<- <note>original: "Parte alia solum rus sati pone lacerre"; this likely refers to a line in Juvenal's Seventh Satire regarding the poor rewards of teachers.</note>

But now (to deal with the matter currently at hand), we plainly deny that "Cambren" is a title. In fact, we boldly declare that no such thing is found among any suitable writers. Even if it were found everywhere, it should not be referred to those tyrants who occupied Mitylene, since the town of Cambre is not only not in Lesbos, but is not even located on any island at all.

Therefore (if I may say so with his permission), nothing could be said or imagined that is more discordant, awkward, or ridiculous than this fabricated opinion of Domitius—if one examines it carefully—so that it collapses on its own without anyone even pushing it. Furthermore, there was no reason for Alcaeus <note>A Greek lyric poet from Lesbos</note> to flee to Mitylene so that a "numerous fleet might destroy tyrants," since among the first controversies...

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the Renaissance scholar Domitius Calderinus. The previous page discussed how Domitius confused the word for cabbage (crambe) with a proper noun (Cambre) when interpreting the Roman satirist Juvenal. The author is now explaining the true origin of the metaphor for "boring repetition."</meta>

...topics: Cornelius lists the rewards for those who kill tyrants in that dialogue which he published about famous orators <note>original: "de claris oratoribus"; referring to Tacitus’s *Dialogue on Orators*</note>. Domitius pours out such works of darkness and gloom upon himself whenever he cannot disentangle himself—just like a little fish caught in a wicker trap. As the philosopher Atticus, a quite elegant defender of Plato, teaches that Aristotle used to do: just as a cuttlefish escapes from the eyes and the very hand of a fisherman by releasing its own ink <note>original: "suo sibi suco"; literally "by its own juice," referring to the cuttlefish's defensive mechanism</note>, so Domitius, fearing to be caught, intentionally darkens his opinions with obscurities. This description fits this man perfectly: never more so than when he was almost caught, veiling himself and clouding things with evasions, vomiting forth a kind of ink of complexity.

However, concerning what a certain otherwise learned man—who has served literature well through his own hard work—brings forward in a recently published commentary about "Gambra" and "Anathus" <note>garbled names resulting from a misreading of "Crambe" and "thanatos"</note> from Probus (as he says), the interpreter of Juvenal: I confess that I neither know who this Probus is, nor do I think he is the one whom Gellius and other ancient writers mention. Therefore, let the credit for that claim remain with its author. Nonetheless, I will briefly set forth what seems right to me; for (as that tragic poet, whoever he was, says):

->**The speech of truth is simple.**<-

Therefore, I believe Juvenal expressed this Greek proverb: **Twice-served cabbage is death** <note>original: "Δις κράμβη θάνατος" (Dis krambe thanatos)</note>, which signifies that cabbage served twice is fatal. Now, *Crambe* is a type of cabbage <term>brassica: the general Latin name for the genus of leafy vegetables including kale and cabbage</term>. It was used to denote the boredom of a controversy repeated too often, since even among the common people, "warmed-up cabbage" has already passed into a proverb for disgust. The poet preferred to use the Greek word rather than the Latin one, either because the proverb is more common among the Greeks than the Latins, or perhaps because a certain species of cabbage is specifically called *Crambe*. According to Pliny, it has thinner, simple, and very dense leaves; it is more bitter, but very effective as a remedy. Indeed, Suidas <note>a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> mentions the Greek proverb itself and teaches that cabbage was even served at banquets because it acts against drunkenness, as Cato testifies in his book on rural affairs <note>Cato the Elder's *De Agri Cultura*</note>.

### Chapter 34.

## Some errors in a letter of Cicero; and regarding the word
## Sciamachia.

<summary>The author explains that Juvenal's phrase "crambe repetita" refers to a Greek proverb about "warmed-up cabbage" being tiresome, correcting contemporary scholars who mistook the word for a geographical name. He notes that cabbage was anciently used both as a metaphor for boring repetition and a medicinal cure for drunkenness.</summary>

<keywords>Juvenal, cabbage, proverbs, Domitius Calderinus, philology, Pliny the Elder, Cato the Elder, Sciamachia, Greek language, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s philological corrections of ancient texts, moving from a critique of the scholar Domitius Calderinus to a specific corruption in the letters of Cicero. The previous page ended with a discussion on the simplicity of truth, which the author now applies to restoring a botched passage in Cicero’s correspondence with Brutus.</meta>

An excessively rustic, unpolished, and plainly filthy word has occupied a letter of Cicero’s: specifically, the one addressed to Brutus that begins with the line, "I am wonderfully glad, my Brutus." This is how we currently read it: *And those vehement contentions of mine seem to have been annihilated as if by knowledge.* <note>original: "Meęq; illę uehementes contētiones tanq̃ scientia adnihilatę esse uideantur."</note> Some people have tried to correct this so far by reading: "as if they seem to have been *already* annihilated." I ask that the learned, those skilled in the proper use of words and the reading of the ancients, consult their own judgment and sense. Only then might they dare to believe that Cicero wrote that his contentions had been "annihilated" <note>original: "adnihilatas"; a word the author considers too "rustic" or non-classical for Cicero’s refined style</note>. In two manuscripts from the Medici family <note>The Medicean codices are famous manuscript collections in Florence</note>—whose reliability we have cited above—we found the text written this way (erroneously, yet in a way that the traces of the original error have not been completely wiped away): *as if they were shadow-boxings.* <note>The manuscript corruption was "tanq̃ sciamachalaę," which the author deciphers as "sciamachiae."</note>

Therefore, we suspect the correct reading is **Shadow-boxings** <note>original: "Sciamachię"</note>. For "Shadow-boxings" are nothing other than "mock fights in the shade." <note>term: <term>Sciamachia</term> (from the Greek *skia* 'shadow' and *mache* 'battle'); a term for practice-fighting or metaphorical boxing against an invisible opponent</note> Cicero certainly felt that he was now "growing cold" in the Senate, as Brutus’s affairs were not succeeding, and thus those once-vehement speeches of his now seemed like mere shadow-boxing. The word itself is quite elegant among the Greeks and very frequently used. Indeed, Galen the physician, in the second book of his work *On Health*, says the following:

*It is the right time to transition to quick exercises without intensity or force; these include running and shadow-boxing.* <note>original Greek: "μεταβαίνειν δὲ καιροσ έπιτα ταχέα χωρισ έντονίασ και βίασ Δρόμοι δε εισι ταυτα και σκιαμαχίαι"</note>

Among those quick exercises which are not overly strained or violent, he numbers both running and "shadow-boxing." Finally, even Eustathius <note>A 12th-century archbishop and scholar known for his commentaries on Homer</note>, in his commentary on the third book of the *Odyssey*, mentions the word:

*Similarly, the phrase ‘we fight under a shadow’ [as separate words] indicates nothing bad; however, the compound verb ‘to shadow-box’ does.* <note>original Greek: "ὁμοίωσ δὲ καὶ τὸ μὲν ὑπὸ σκιὰ μαχούμεθα φαῦλον δηλοῖ οὐδὲν. οὐ μέντοι καὶ τὸ σύνθετον σκιαμαχεῖν."</note>

Furthermore, Marcus Varro entitled one of his Menippean Satires *The Shadow-Boxer* <note>original: "Sciamachiā"</note>. According to Aulus Gellius <note>An author of the 2nd century AD who preserved fragments of earlier Latin works</note>, this is the true reading from the ancient manuscripts, and not "The Ram-Fight" <note>original: "Criomachia"</note> as some others write. Indeed, a little earlier in the same letter of Cicero, another word—the Greek **instrument** <note>original: "ὄργανον" (organon)</note>—has migrated into Latin in a corrupted form. For instead of "instrument," it had been turned into the nonsensical "optanon." To this day, no one has noticed this either. Therefore, you should read it as follows: *Clearly, Brutus, I am now growing cold. For the Senate was my instrument.* <note>original: "Organōn enī erat meū senatus"</note> Cicero meant that he used the Senate as his tool or instrument of influence.

<summary>The author corrects two corrupted words in Cicero's letters to Brutus, arguing that the nonsensical "annihilated" and "optanon" should actually be the Greek-derived terms for "shadow-boxing" and "instrument."</summary>

### Chapter XXXV.

**A passage in Marcus Varro, and another in the books of Columella on rural affairs, defended from error; some additions regarding Euhemerus and the tomb of Jupiter; and a passage in Statius explained.**

In the first book of Marcus Varro’s *On Agriculture* <note>original: "rerũ rusticariũ"</note>, this passage is read incorrectly: **"The ear of corn and the grain are known to almost everyone; the husk to few."** Therefore, I know that this was written only by Ennius in the verses of his *Ephemeris*. For regarding the phrase found even in what are thought to be the most corrected manuscripts—"in the verses of the *Ephemeris*"—I find it instead in a manuscript of venerable antiquity from the public library of the Medici family as follows: **"In the translated books of Euhemerus."** <note>The author is correcting a common scribal error where "Euhemeri" (of Euhemerus) was mistaken for "Ephemeris" (a diary or calendar). He argues Varro was actually citing Ennius’s Latin translation of the Greek writer Euhemerus.</note> Furthermore, there is no doubt that the *Sacred History* <note>original: "sacra historia"</note> of Ennius is what Varro refers to, which was translated into Latin from Euhemerus; this is the same work that Lactantius, Eusebius, and Augustine cite repeatedly. The words of Lactantius from the first book of his *Divine Institutes* are as follows:

> The ancient author Euhemerus, who was from the city of Messina, collected the deeds of Jupiter and the others who are thought to be gods; he wove together a history from the titles and sacred inscriptions which were held in the most ancient temples, and especially in the shrine of Triphylian Jupiter, where a golden column was placed by Jupiter himself, as the inscription indicated. On this column, he recorded his deeds so that it might be a monument of his affairs for posterity. This history was translated and followed by Ennius, whose words are these: *Where Jupiter gives the command of the sea to Neptune, so that he might reign over all islands and all places along the sea.*

Then the same Lactantius cites several passages from Ennius concerning this very history again and again, which I, striving for brevity, shall pass over. Indeed, Sextus Empiricus also alleges the words of Euhemerus himself, especially in the fourth volume of his *Skeptical Treatises* <note>original: "pyrhoniorũ"; referring to the Pyrrhonian Outlines</note>, and says he was called an <term>Atheist</term> <note>original Greek: "Atheon," meaning one who denies the gods</note>, just as were Diagoras of Melos, Prodicus of Ceos, Theodorus of Cyrene, and many others; and Timon of Phlius also speaks of him thus, I believe:

->Euhemerus, an old braggart, who writes wicked books in his soul<-
<note>original Greek: ἐυήμερος γέρων ἀλαζὼν ἀδικα βιβλία ψυχων</note>

But leaving these things aside, that point especially must be inspected more keenly in Columella as well, in book ten, chapter three: where he treats bees <note>The author likely refers to Book IX, as Book X of Columella is traditionally about gardens, but Renaissance chapter numbering often varied</note>, Homer is cited twice instead of **Euhemerus** in the common manuscripts. This is also indicated by a very ancient book from the private library of the Medici family, written in Longobardic script...

engraved on tablets; and likewise another from the public records of that same people, which Nicolaus Niccoli <note>Nicolaus Niccoli (1364–1437) was a famous Florentine humanist and book collector whose library formed the core of the Medici collection.</note>, a learned and diligent man, transcribed with his own hand from an ancient exemplar. Moreover, reason agrees, against which no barriers can stand; for indeed in Homer no fable of this kind regarding bees is found. It is likely that it was written instead by Euhemerus, who composed the history of Jupiter, and who declares that those bees were Jupiter's nurses <note>According to myth, the infant Zeus was hidden in a cave on Crete and fed honey by bees to protect him from his father, Saturn.</note>. Furthermore, what the poet Callimachus found written in this same Euhemerus (as I believe), of whom we spoke above, is that in that same *Sacred History* Jupiter had finished his life in Crete, and that his tomb was there in the town of Cnossus. In that tomb, his name was inscribed in ancient Greek letters as *Zeus, son of Kronos* <note>original: "Ζευσι κρόνου"</note>, which in Latin is *Jupiter, son of Saturn*. Since this most learned poet [Callimachus] knew of this writing and recognized it as a fact held and maintained by the Cretans, he says (for this reason, I believe) in his *Hymn to Jupiter*: "Cretans are always liars, for they have even fabricated a tomb for you, O King! But you did not die, for you exist forever." These are the verses:

> Cretans are always liars; for they even built a tomb for you, O King!
> But you did not die, for you exist forever. <note>original: Callimachus, Hymn to Zeus 8–9.</note>

Finally, because of this very thing, I think Statius also says in the first book of the *Thebaid*: "And Crete, which lied about your ghost" <note>original: "Mentitaque manis Creta tuos."</note>. Even the martyr Cyprian, a most eloquent man, in his book *On the Origin and Kinds of Idols*, says: "Jupiter’s cave in Crete is visited, and his tomb is shown"—although these words are found written with little correction in modern manuscripts. Furthermore, the Cretans were always held to be such liars that in the Greek proverb, which is "to play the Cretan against a Cretan" <note>original: "πρὸς κρητα κρητιζειν" (pros kreta kretizein). It means to try and out-lie a known liar.</note>, it signifies nothing other than using a lie against a liar! But even that little verse by the Cretan Epimenides was cited by Paul the Apostle, and for that reason even sanctified:

> Cretans are always liars, evil beasts, lazy bellies. <note>original: "Κρῆτες ἀεὶ ψευ̃σται κακὰ θηρία γαστέρες ἀργοί". This is quoted in the New Testament, Titus 1:12.</note>

It means this: "Cretans are always liars, evil beasts, slow bellies." For Callimachus took the first half-line of his verse from Epimenides. Wherefore Ovid also elegantly says this in his *Amores*:

> The Cretans will be witnesses; nor do the Cretans invent everything.

And elsewhere:

> I speak of known things; not even she whom a hundred cities support—
> ->Though Crete is prone to lie, she is not accustomed to deny this.<-

<meta>This page continues the author’s philological detective work regarding the island of Crete and the history of Jupiter. ### Chapter 36.
Here is solved and explained the riddle from Varro’s book which 
Gellius cites.

<margin>a</margin> That riddle from Varro which Gellius <note>Aulus Gellius (c. 125 – 180 AD), author of the "Attic Nights," a collection of notes on grammar, philosophy, and history.</note> sets forth and leaves uninterpreted, in order to sharpen the conjectures of his readers, I myself profess that I shall solve, even at my own risk. It consists of these three iambic verses:
> Whether it be "once minus" or "twice minus," I do not quite know,
> Or perhaps both of these—as I once heard it said—
> He refused to yield to King Jupiter himself.
It appears to signify **Terminus** <note>The Roman god of boundary markers. "Terminus" is a wordplay: "ter" (three) + "minus." The riddle asks if it is "once (1) minus" or "twice (2) minus," or both (1+2=3), which equals "thrice (ter) minus."</note>, the god who refused to yield to Jupiter when the Capitol was being cleared of other shrines through augury. Ovid writes in the second book of the *Fasti*:
> Indeed, when the new Capitols were being built, the whole
>    crowd of gods yielded to Jupiter and gave up their place.
> Terminus, as the ancients recall, remained in the shrine 
>    where he was, and holds the temple along with great Jupiter.
Therefore, the author of this word-puzzle doubts whether it was "once minus" or "twice minus" or "both of these"; for from "both" (that is, from "once" and "twice") the result is "thrice" <note>original: "ter"</note>. Therefore, it was not "once minus" nor "twice minus," but it was **Terminus** who refused to yield to King Jupiter himself.

### Chapter 37.
On the error of Servius regarding the river Oaxes and "creta."

<margin>e</margin> *We shall come to the rapid Oaxes of Crete.* <note>A famous and debated line from Virgil's first "Eclogue." The word "cretae" can mean either the island of Crete or the substance "chalk."</note>
Servius <note>Maurus Servius Honoratus (late 4th century AD), the most famous ancient commentator on the works of Virgil.</note> explains this passage from the first Eclogue of the *Bucolics* word-for-word as follows: he says "rapid" means "muddy," because it "seizes the chalk" <note>original: "rapit cretam"</note>. Chalk is said to be a white earth. For the Oaxes is a river of Mesopotamia which, by its own speed, seizes the white earth and is thus made turbulent. So says Servius. But I would instead believe that the Oaxes is understood to be a river of the island of Crete. For Vibius Sequester <note>A 4th or 5th-century geographer who compiled lists of names found in Roman poets.</note> also says the Oaxes is a river of Crete, from which the city of Oaxia takes its name. Wherefore Apollonius <note>Apollonius of Rhodes, author of the "Argonautica."</note> also called the island of Crete "the Oaxian land" in his first book.

<summary>The author solves a linguistic riddle from Varro regarding the god Terminus and corrects Servius's geographical claim that the river Oaxes is in Mesopotamia, arguing instead that it is in Crete.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the analysis of Varro’s translations regarding Cretan mythology and transitions into a new philological investigation of Juvenal’s satires. The author is moving from a discussion of the "Idaean Dactyls" (mythical craftsmen) to a correction of previous scholars' interpretations of Roman poetry.</meta>

...the *Argonautica* <note>The *Argonautica* is an epic poem by Apollonius of Rhodes about Jason and the Argonauts.</note> in these words:
<note>original: "Δάκτυλοι ἰδαῖοι κρηταέες οὕς ποτε νύμφη ἀγχιάλη Δικταῖον ἀνὰ σπέος ἀμφοτέρῃσι Δραξαμένη γαίης οἰαξίδος ἐβλάστησε."</note>

Varro—I believe the one from Atax, who was a translator of other men's works—translated this last part as follows:
**"And with both palms, she grasped the Oaxian earth."**
Nor indeed is it inconsistent with reason that a poet, wishing to show that his countrymen would be dispersed throughout the whole world, should remember an island situated in the middle (as if in the Mediterranean Sea) and also another part of the world entirely divided from the whole.

### Chapter 38.

->An explanation of a poem by Juvenal, contrary to all opinions held until now, regarding the "Laconian" and the "pitylism."<-

"Who makes the Lacedaemonian orb slippery with pitylism." <note>original: "Qui lacedemonium pitylismate lubricat orbem." This is a line from Juvenal’s Satire 11, which discusses luxury and Greek influences on Roman behavior.</note>
This verse from the eleventh satire of Juvenal has been explained in various ways until now, and, as I believe, quite carelessly. For Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus, a renowned 15th-century humanist commentator.</note> says that *pitylma* is a spraying of oil with spit, from the verb *pityo* [to spit]. He takes the "Lacedaemonian orb" to mean a ring, because, he says, the use of rings originated with the Lacedaemonians [Spartans]; he suggests that senators and knights are therefore signified by the mention of a ring. Furthermore, another most learned man—whose name I will refrain from mentioning out of respect, despite our disagreement—thinks that "Lacedaemonian" refers to a dance. But he candidly admits that he has read nothing of it so far, except for a brief mention in Pollux <note>Julius Pollux, a Greek grammarian whose "Onomasticon" is a thesaurus of Greek terms and concepts.</note> regarding a "Lacedaemonian dance." Finally, a third man, also very learned, understands it to be a <term>trochus</term> [a playing hoop] made from a pine tree, because a pine is called *pitys* in Greek, which is where *pitysma* would come from. Of these three opinions, with all due respect to the authors, I approve of none. To begin with, I deny that the "Lacedaemonian orb" can signify a ring, since it is nowhere recorded that the origin of the ring came from the Lacedaemonians. Moreover, Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman encyclopedist.</note>, whom Domitius...

<summary>The author concludes a discussion on Varro's translation of Greek poetry about Cretan origins before challenging various scholarly interpretations of a difficult line in Juvenal's eleventh Satire.</summary>
<keywords>Varro Atacinus, Apollonius of Rhodes, Idaean Dactyls, Juvenal, Satire 11, pitylism, Lacedaemonian orb, philology, Domitius Calderinus, Julius Pollux</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into Juvenal's eleventh satire, specifically focusing on a correction of the term "pitylism" and its connection to Spartan (Lacedaemonian) customs.</meta>

...he brings forward: he reports that even then at Lacedaemon <note>Sparta</note>, they used only an iron ring. But if it is established that the "Lacedaemonian orb" cannot be taken to mean a ring—as is certainly the case—then that fictitious "spraying" <note>The previous page mentioned a theory that *pitylma* referred to spraying oil with one's mouth.</note> will definitely be worth nothing. Nor is it derived from *pityo* <note>original: "pityo", to spit</note>, for you would never find such a word. Rather, *pitysma* <note>A term for something spit out, often used in wine-tasting.</note> is deduced from *pityzo*, as is declared in the works of Nica the grammarian. 

Moreover, those who think that *pitysma* is a Lacedaemonian dance—since nothing can be found about it—will easily (as is the habit of a candid mind) yield to a better opinion. I believe the same will happen for those who think *pitysma* is a hoop <note>term: *trochus* — a metal hoop rolled with a stick, a popular Greek and Roman exercise.</note> made of pine <note>original: *pitys*</note>. This has never been read anywhere, nor does it fit the context; furthermore, the rules of poetic meter conflict with it, since *pitys* has a short first syllable, as is clear from even the first verse of Theocritus <note>Theocritus was a 3rd-century BC Greek poet known for his pastoral idylls.</note>. 

We, therefore, believe that we should read *pitylisma*; it is likely that due to the ignorance of scribes, a syllable from the middle was dropped. This is hardly surprising in such great darkness <note>The author refers to the "darkness" of historical distance and corrupted manuscripts.</note>. By *pitylisma*, we understand a physical exercise about which Galen, the excellent physician, writes in the second book of his *On Hygiene* <note>original: "υγιεινων" [Hygieinon]; Galen was the most famous physician of the Roman Empire.</note>. Although in his work, the verb *pitylizein* <note>original: "pitylizin"</note> is used rather than the noun *pitylisma*. 

But it is correctly formed: *pitylisma* follows from it by a very common linguistic proportion. It is not inappropriate to first write down Galen's Greek words, and then interpret them to the best of our ability. Since he knows various types of exercises and even teaches about them—specifically those that are fast but without violent strain, among which is this very one we are now discussing—he writes as follows, word for word: 

<note>Translation of the Greek text from Galen:</note>
"To 'pitylize' is when someone, having stood on the tips of their feet and stretched up their hands, moves them very quickly, bringing one backward and the other forward; they perform this gymnastic exercise mostly while standing before a wall, so that if they should ever stumble, by touching the wall they may easily be set upright; and thus, through those exercising, the stumbles go unnoticed and the exercise becomes less strenuous."

He says it is *pitylizin*, or if you prefer, *pitylissare*, when someone, moving on the tips of their feet, extends their hands and moves them most swiftly, one backward and the other forward; this type of gymnastics is practiced mostly against walls, so that if they slip anywhere, having caught the wall they might easily rise again, and thus the slips of the exercisers are hidden, and the exercise itself becomes lighter. Furthermore, the Lacedaemonian orb...

<summary>The author argues that a corrupted word in Juvenal's poetry should be read as "pitylisma," referring to a specific Spartan aerobic exercise described by the physician Galen. He refutes earlier interpretations that the word related to spitting oil or wooden hoops.</summary>

<keywords>pitylisma, Juvenal, Galen, Lacedaemonian, gymnastics, philology, textual criticism, Sparta, physical exercise</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into Juvenal's eleventh satire, transitioning from a discussion of "pitylisma" (exercise) to the "Lacedaemonian orb," which the author identifies as the "laconicum"—a circular Spartan-style steam bath.</meta>

I would take the "laconicum" <note>original: *laconicum*; a dry sweat room, similar to a modern sauna, named after the Spartans/Lacedaemonians</note> itself to be the place in which they were accustomed to exercise in that manner. 
Dion <note>Cassius Dio, a Roman historian of the 2nd–3rd century AD</note>, in the fifty-third book of his histories, reports that a "laconicum" in the city—a steam-room or sweating-room (for so we seem able to interpret what he calls a *pyriaterium* <note>original: *pyriaterium*; from the Greek for "fire-heater"</note>)—was built by Agrippa. Then he says it was called a "laconicum" because the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> at that time exercised naked and anointed with oil before all others. Martial also hints at this "laconicum" when he says:

> If the rites of the Spartans please you, 
> you can be content with dry steam 
> and be plunged into the raw Virgin and the Martial waters.
> <note>original: Martial, *Epigrams* 6.42. The "Virgin" refers to the Aqua Virgo aqueduct, and "Martial" to the baths in the Campus Martius.</note>

But also Columella <note>A 1st-century AD writer on agriculture</note>, in the first book of *On Agriculture*, says: "And we marvel, astonished, at the gestures of effeminate men, who mimic with womanly movement the sex denied to men by nature, and deceive the eyes of the spectators." Soon after: "So that we may come to the gluttonous feasts ready, we boil away our daily indigestion in the *laconica*; and having burned off our sweat, we seek out a thirst." 

Now, therefore, it can be clear to anyone even from these words of Columella which people the poet <note>The author is likely referring to the satirist Juvenal.</note> specifically signifies: namely, those who without a doubt spent the whole day in those *laconica* and gymnasiums, anointed and naked. This is why he says "it may also be pleasing" <note>original: *lubeat*</note>, on account of the oil and ointment with which they were rubbed during exercise. Seneca also, in the seventh book of his *Epistles to Lucilius*, introduces nothing else before the *laconica*, saying: "What have I to do with those hot pools? What with the sweating-rooms, in which a dry steam is enclosed to exhaust the body, so that all sweat may exit through effort?" 

It remains now, since he has mentioned a "circle" <note>referring to the "Lacedaemonian orb" mentioned on the previous page</note>, that we also show that *laconica* were constructed in a circle. This is evident from the fifth book of Vitruvius's <term>Architecture</term> <note>Vitruvius was the most famous Roman author on architecture.</note>, in these words: 

"The *laconica* and sweating-rooms, which are to be joined to the *tepidarium* <note>warm room</note>, should be as high as they are wide, up to the bottom of the curve of the hemisphere. A central light should be left in the hemisphere, and from it a bronze shield <note>original: *clypeum*</note> should hang by chains. By raising and lowering this shield, the temperature of the sweating-room will be managed. It seems it should be made according to a compass <note>that is, perfectly circular</note> so that the force of the flame and steam may travel equally from the center through the roundness of the curves."

<summary>The author identifies the "Lacedaemonian orb" as the *laconicum*, a circular Roman steam bath, supporting this with evidence from historians, poets, and architects like Vitruvius.</summary>
<keywords>laconicum, steam bath, Agrippa, Vitruvius, Columella, Seneca, Roman architecture, Spartan customs, hygiene, physical exercise</keywords>

# Chapter 38.

### The Riddle of the poet Ausonius solved: concerning the daughters of Cadmus, the Melon, the Cuttlefish, and the Cnidian knots.

The riddle <note>original: *scirpus*; literally a "bulrush," but used by Romans to mean a complex knot or a difficult riddle</note> which the ingenious and quite learned poet Ausonius <note>Decimus Magnus Ausonius (c. 310–395 AD), a Roman poet and teacher from Bordeaux</note> proposed to the grammarian Theon in iambic dimeters, I shall now attempt to explain. But first, let us write down the verses themselves:

->Or let your interpreter be present,<-
->the one who was the judge<-
->of my enigmas, when he revealed to you<-
->the little black daughters of Cadmus,<-
->the white page of the Melon,<-
->and the marks of the dark cuttlefish,<-
->and the Cnidian knots.<-

Now, among these, the last verse is read incorrectly in the more common books; you will find "measures" <note>original: *modos*</note> or "mine" <note>original: *meos*</note> in place of what I have restored as "knots" <note>original: *nodos*</note>. I found this reading not only in several other sources but especially in a book written by the hand of Giovanni Boccaccio <note>The famous 14th-century Italian author of the *Decameron*, who was also a dedicated scholar of Latin manuscripts</note>, which is now preserved in the library of Santo Spirito in Florence. Furthermore, the meter rarely allows for the other options, whether "measures" or "mine," because the second foot ought to be an iamb <note>a metrical foot consisting of a short syllable followed by a long one</note> rather than a pyrrhic <note>two short syllables</note>.

Therefore, it seems to me that under these veils Ausonius has hidden the alphabet <note>original: *litteras*; "the gift of Cadmus the Phoenician"</note>, papyrus from the Nile, writing ink, and the scribe's reed pen—the close companion of the man of letters. For Cadmus was the first to bring letters into Greece from Phoenicia. Herodotus <note>The 5th-century BC Greek historian</note> testifies to this in his book *Terpsichore* <note>Book 5 of Herodotus's *Histories*</note>, stating that those Phoenicians who came to Greece under the leadership of Cadmus first showed the Greeks their native and ancestral letters, which were Phoenician. But as time passed, he says, the shapes of the characters gradually changed along with the language. The Ionians were the first to use them, which is why they were called "Phoenician things" <note>original: *φοινικήια* (phoinikēia)</note>. Herodotus also writes that he himself saw "Cadmean letters" in the temple of Ismenian Apollo at Thebes in Boeotia, engraved upon certain tripods; he cites three inscriptions from them, which were (as he himself says) very similar to Ionian letters. For this reason, if the ancient Greek characters were none other than Phoenician, and the Latin characters were none other than Greek (as Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of *Natural History*</note> also indicates, citing the evidence of the Delphic characters), then the connection is clear.

<summary>This page deciphers a riddle by the poet Ausonius about the materials of writing, using a manuscript hand-copied by Boccaccio to correct a textual error. It explains how "Cadmus's daughters" and "Cnidian knots" are metaphors for the alphabet and the reed pen.</summary>
<keywords>Ausonius, Cadmus, Giovanni Boccaccio, history of the alphabet, Phoenician letters, Herodotus, philology, reed pen, papyrus</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of Ausonius's riddle, specifically identifying the "daughters of Cadmus" as the alphabet and the "Melon" as the River Nile.</meta>

(For that "Table" is a recent invention and added by the unlearned.) It is clear that the Latin letters are none other than those we learned from Cadmus <note>In Greek mythology, Cadmus was the Phoenician prince credited with bringing the alphabet to Greece; the Romans adopted their letters from the Greeks</note>. For this reason, Ausonius calls them the **daughters of Cadmus**; and because they are printed small in black ink, he sweetly calls them *Nigellas* <note>original: *nigellas*; a diminutive of the Latin *niger* (black), meaning "little black ones"</note>. Regarding this, Zeno wrote thus in an epigram:

> His homeland knows Phoenicia; why the envy? Is not that man Cadmus the one from whom Greece possesses the written page? <note>original Greek: οἶδε πάτρα φοίνικια... This epigram defends the foreign origin of the Greek alphabet</note>

Timon the Pyrrhonist also, as we read in Sextus <note>Sextus Empiricus, a physician and philosopher who recorded the works of the skeptic Timon of Phlius</note> in the fourth book of his *Pyrrhonian Sketches*, wrote thus:

> Grammar: of which there is no contemplation or clear examination for a man being taught the Phoenician signs of Cadmus. <note>original Greek: γραμματικὴ· Τῆσ ὄντισ... Here Timon mocks the study of grammar as merely learning "Phoenician signs"</note>

He calls the Phoenician signs of Cadmus the marks of the letters. I pass over the fact that Suidas, or rather Zopyrion <note>Ancient Greek lexicographers and grammarians</note>, says the letters were called "Phoenician." I also pass over Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the *Natural History*</note> and many others who say they were brought into Greece by Cadmus. Since various readers will recall what they have read scattered throughout Herodotus <note>The 5th-century BC historian who described the arrival of "Cadmean letters" in Greece</note>, we seem to have done enough by restoring these things to their proper authority; for the testimonies of the ancients are not to be counted as much as they are to be weighed.

Furthermore, he speaks of the "**white page of the Melon**" for this reason: because the Nile produces the papyrus from which white pages are made. Among the ancient Latins, who were not yet accustomed to the Greek language, the Nile was called the **Melon**, just as Pompeius <note>Festus Pompeius, a 2nd-century Roman grammarian</note> declares under the word "nourishment." Although many manuscripts have not *Melo* but *Milo*, it is an ignorant error. Therefore, we shall support this wavering authority (if it so seems) with the help of Servius <note>The famous 4th-century commentator on Virgil</note>, whose words in the first book of the *Aeneid* are as follows: "Certainly 'Atlas' is Greek, just like 'Nilus' (the Nile). For Ennius <note>The "father" of Roman poetry (c. 239–169 BC)</note> says the Nile is called *Melo*, while Atlas is called *Telamon*." So he says.

Finally, we take **Sepia** <note>The cuttlefish</note> for the ink smeared into the shapes of letters, as seen also in Persius <note>A Roman satirist known for his dense and difficult style</note>:

> Because the black cuttlefish-ink vanishes when water is poured in <note>original: *Nigra quod infusa uanescat sepia lymphai*; Persius is describing a student making excuses for not writing because his ink is too thick or too thin</note>

This is, of course, due to the nature of that fish, which is said to pour out a black liquid. Perhaps ink was once made from this. From this, Marcianus <note>likely Martianus Capella, the late antique author of an encyclopedia on the liberal arts</note> says that afterwards, with a "little black powder" (which was thought to be made from ash or cuttlefish ink) introduced through small reeds, the same things were repaired. Furthermore, "furvum" means black, about which also...

<summary>The author explains the literary metaphors in Ausonius's riddle, identifying "daughters of Cadmus" as the alphabet, "Melon" as an archaic Latin name for the Nile (source of papyrus), and "Sepia" as cuttlefish ink.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the poet Ausonius, moving from his use of riddles and metaphors for writing materials to specific textual corrections in both poetry and legal codes.</meta>

Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, a prolific Roman scholar of the 1st century BC</note> thinks the word for thief <term>furem</term> is named for this! But Horace also says of it, "the dark <note>original: *furuæ*; an archaic word for dark or black</note> kingdoms of Proserpina." 

Valerius Maximus <note>A 1st-century AD Roman author known for his collection of historical anecdotes</note> also speaks of "dark victims" offered to Father Dis <note>Dis Pater, the Roman god of the underworld, equivalent to the Greek Hades</note>. Even before him, Varro interpreted these as "black." Next, take the "Cnidian nodes," since the quality of reeds <note>The <term>calamus</term> was a reed pen used for writing on papyrus</note> is tested at Cnidus <note>An ancient Greek city in modern-day Turkey</note>, as Pliny says in the sixteenth book of his *Natural History*. However, the poet Persius was aiming for an imitation of this when he spoke of "nodes," for he had also written:

> Into his hands come the paper and the reed with its nodes. <note>original: *Inque manus chartæ, nodosque uenit harundo.* From the Satires of Persius</note>

# Chapter 40.
->An epigram of the same poet, similar to a riddle, cleansed of errors and<-
->explained.<-

The riddle <note>original: *scirpum*; literally a "bulrush," but used by the Romans to mean a complex knot or a riddle</note> of the poet Ausonius which we recently "unknotted" brought to our mind a four-line poem by the same author. It is perhaps easier to understand, provided that one has a text cleansed of errors and is not ignorant of Greek literature; for if someone lacks either of these, it will surely require no less effort than the riddle mentioned above. These are the verses:

> Lais, Eros, and Itys; Chiron, and Eros, and another Itys.
> If you write these names, take away the first letters.
> To make the word for what you do, Teacher Eunus;
> For it is not fitting for me to speak the Latin word for this disgrace.

Indeed, from the first letters of each of these names joined together in order, there jumps out this Greek word <note>original: λείχει; pronounced *leichei*</note>, which in Latin is "he licks" <note>original: *lingit*</note>. Thus, by that word—if I may speak with respect for your ears—Eunus is identified as a "licker of groins," or if something similar might be suggested to the divine. <note>The epigram is a crude insult directed at a teacher named Eunus, accusing him of performing cunnilingus. The first letters of **L**ais, **E**ros, **I**tys, **CH**iron, **E**ros, and **I**tys spell out the Greek verb for "to lick."</note>
