--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page transitions from a discussion of Ausonius’s poetry and philological riddles to a technical correction of Roman legal terminology. It focuses on the distinction between the terms "diffissum" and "diffusum" in the context of legal adjournments, citing the famous Florentine manuscript of Justinian's Digest.</meta>

The term **adjournment** <note>original: *diffisio*</note>, and likewise the phrase common in legal proceedings, **"the day is split"** <note>original: *diffindi diem*; a legal term meaning to postpone or adjourn a court date</note>—which has been almost entirely removed from common use and obliterated by a certain wicked ignorance that is ruinous to the liberal arts—we shall endeavor to restore and renew to the best of our ability through our own diligence. 

The jurist Ulpian <note>Domitius Ulpianus, a major Roman jurist of the 3rd century AD whose works comprise a large part of the Digest</note> also uses this figure of speech in the seventy-fourth book of his commentary *On the Edict*, specifically in these words, which are recorded in the first book of the *Digest* under this title: *If anyone should fail to obey the guarantees made for appearing in court.* Ulpian says: "If anyone has promised to appear in court and was prevented by health, weather, or the force of a river, and thus could not appear, he is assisted by an exception." And not without reason. Since presence is necessary in such a promise, how could one appear if hindered by ill health? Therefore, the Law of the Twelve Tables <note>The earliest code of Roman law, dating to the mid-5th century BC</note> also orders that if the judge or either of the litigants is hindered by a **serious illness** <note>original: *morbo sontico*; an illness significant enough to excuse one from legal duties</note>, the day of the trial is to be **split** <note>original: *diffissum*; meaning adjourned to another day</note>. Thus far Ulpian. 

However, one must read it as "split" <note>original: *diffissum*</note> rather than "diffused" <note>original: *diffusum*</note>, which is the reading found in almost all the books currently in circulation. Yet, the volume of Justinian's *Digest* or *Pandects* <note>The *Pandectae* or *Digest* is a massive compilation of Roman legal opinions commissioned by Emperor Justinian I in 530 AD</note>—which is beyond doubt the original archetype—is publicly preserved by the highest magistrate in the Florentine court itself. It is held in great veneration, and is shown only rarely, and even then by torchlight. This book is no small portion of the Pisan spoils <note>The manuscript, known as the *Littera Florentina*, was taken by Florence from Pisa in 1406; it is the most authoritative source for the Digest</note> and is frequently cited by legal experts. It is written in larger characters, with no spaces between words and no shorthand abbreviations. Some parts, at least in the preface, appear to have been marked out, erased, or written over by the author himself as he was thinking and creating, rather than by a mere scribe or copyist. It features a Greek letter and this most beautiful Greek epigram on its front page:

->King Justinian fashioned this book,<-
->Which Tribonian <note>Justinian's chief legal advisor and the primary compiler of the Digest</note> labored on for the great All-King;<-
->As if he were forging for Achilles a shield of many colors,<-
->Upon which shine all the images of the Laws;<-
->Asia, and Libya won by the spear,<-
->And Europe are governed by this, the signifier of the whole world.<-

I have had the opportunity to read and turn over this volume at my leisure...

<summary>The author argues for the restoration of the legal term "diffisio" (adjournment), correcting a common textual error in Ulpian’s writings by citing the ancient and authoritative Florentine manuscript of Justinian’s Digest.</summary>
<keywords>Ulpian, Justinian, Digest, Pandects, Littera Florentina, diffisio, legal terminology, Tribonian, Twelve Tables, Roman law</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological argument regarding legal terminology. It concludes the discussion on the term "split" (diffissum) vs "spread" (diffusum) in the Florentine Pandects and begins a new chapter on the Roman custom of using thumbs to signal favor or disapproval in the arena.</meta>

...available only to one person, through the work and cause of **Lorenzo de' Medici** <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" (1449–1492), the de facto ruler of the Florentine Republic and a massive patron of Renaissance humanism</note>: who, as the leading citizen in his Republic, while favoring scholars, even descends to these specific services. Therefore, in these *Pandects* <note>The *Digest* or *Pandects* of Justinian, a massive compilation of Roman law</note>—no longer called the Pisan ones as they once were, but the Florentine ones—in which the words are pure and not, as in others, full of blemishes and decay, I find the word **"split"** <note>original: *diffissum*</note>, not **"spread"** <note>original: *diffusum*</note>.

I see that this same word is corrupted in **Aulus Gellius** <note>A 2nd-century Roman author of the *Attic Nights*, a collection of notes on grammar, philosophy, and history</note> as well, in the book commonly called the thirteenth, though it is the fifteenth in the corrected manuscripts. For where we read these words: "And indeed in the definitions and adjournments of matters": here, in the manuscript of Gellius from the public library of the Medici family—which **Niccolò Niccoli** <note>A famous Florentine humanist (1364–1437) known for his beautiful handwriting and for collecting ancient manuscripts</note>, a man not unlearned (as the times then went) but above all diligent, faithfully transcribed from a very ancient exemplar according to his custom—I find it written thus: "And indeed in the **adjournments** <note>original: *diffisionibus*</note> and postponements of matters." 

If I am allowed some room for suspicion, I would rather believe that the text originally and correctly read "of days" rather than "of matters." Indeed, in the same chapter, Gellius says: "I ordered, therefore," he said, "the day to be **split** <note>original: *diffindi*; a legal term for postponing a trial</note>." For so it is in that same manuscript, whereas in these more common ones it says: "the day to be **spread** <note>original: *diffundi*</note>."

### Chapter 42.

->That thumbs are usually pressed down in favor, just as they are turned in denying favor; and from this, the meanings of Horace, Juvenal, and Prudentius are clarified.<-

**Horace** <note>The leading Roman lyric poet during the time of Augustus</note> says in the first book of his *Epistles*:
> He who believes you agree with his interests, 
> as a supporter, will praise your play with **both thumbs**. <note>original: *utroque pollice*</note>

**Porphyrio** <note>Pomponius Porphyrio, a 3rd-century commentator on the works of Horace</note> says "with both thumbs" means "with both hands," so that the figure of speech is a **synecdoche** <note>a literary device where a part is used to represent the whole</note>, where a part stands for the whole. But lest the authority of an ancient writer perhaps mislead anyone, we shall also give his ear a gentle tug: for it is written in **Pliny's** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the encyclopedic *Natural History*</note> *Natural History*, book twenty-eight, in these words: "We are even commanded by a proverb to press down our thumbs when we show favor." From which **Juvenal** <note>A Roman satirist of the late 1st and early 2nd centuries AD</note> most skillfully wrote:
> And with the **thumb turned** <note>original: *verso pollice*</note> by the crowd, 
> they kill whomever they please to win popularity.

<summary>The author corrects a common legal misspelling using the Florentine Pandects and then explains the Roman ritual of using thumbs (pressed down or turned) to signal life or death in the arena.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Pandects, Aulus Gellius, Niccolò Niccoli, Horace, Juvenal, Pliny the Elder, Roman Law, philology, thumbs up, gladiatorial combat</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of Roman customs and philological evidence. Having previously discussed the term for legal adjournments (diffisio), the author now concludes a brief point on gladiatorial hand signals before beginning a new chapter on the history of Latin diphthongs, citing evidence from the archaeological collections of Lorenzo de' Medici and personal observations atop the Pantheon.</meta>

Just as if favor is withdrawn by a turned thumb. Finally, even **Prudentius** <note>Aurelius Prudentius Clemens (c. 348–413 AD), a Roman Christian poet</note> in his heroic poem against **Symmachus** <note>Quintus Aurelius Symmachus, a 4th-century pagan statesman who sought to preserve Roman traditions</note> declaimed with anger against the Vestal Virgins who were present at the gladiatorial games:

> And as often as the victor plunges the blade into the throat, she 
> says these are her delights; and the modest Virgin 
> commands that the breast of the fallen man be pierced 
> with a turned thumb. <note>original: *Et quoties uictor ferrum iugulo inſerit/ illa / Delicias ait eſſe ſuas/ pectuſq; iacentis / Virgo modeſta iubet conuerſo pollice rumpi*</note>

For just as those who pressed down their thumb were thought to show favor, so I believe those who turned it were thought to deny favor to the gladiators.

### Chapter XXXIV.
### How many diphthongs the ancients possessed.

Among the Latins, it is maintained that there are no more than four **diphthongs** <term>diphthong: a sound formed by the combination of two vowels in a single syllable</term>, whereas we find at least seven of them in ancient marbles or coins, of which **Lorenzo de' Medici** <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent," the author's patron and collector of antiquities</note> supplies us an immense abundance. For besides those very common ones—which are *ae*, *oe*, *au*, and *eu*—one may also find *ai* and *ei*, and likewise *ou* in place of *ae*, *i*, and *u* in ancient monuments. 

And indeed, some always pronounced *ai* instead of *ae* with the letter *i*, not *e*, after the manner of the Greeks, as **Quintilian** <note>A 1st-century Roman rhetorician and teacher</note> shows. Some did this only in the singular, when they occurred in the genitive or dative case. From this, we also noted the name **Aimilius** instead of what should be **Aemilius** somewhere in the ancient sights of Rome. Furthermore, when we had climbed atop the dome of the **Pantheon** <note>The famous Roman temple; the author describes physically ascending the structure to inspect its inscriptions</note>, we came across a marble fragment there with traces of writing of this sort. From this we also see the sacred **diaeresis** <term>diaeresis: the separation of two vowels into distinct syllables, often for poetic meter</term> in those words found in **Virgil** <note>Publius Vergilius Maro (70–19 BC), author of the *Aeneid*</note>: *Aulaï* [of the hall], *pictaï* [of the painted]; and what is now erroneously read as *Aquaï* [of the water]. 

Moreover, the diphthong *ei* is found more frequently, and it is almost common knowledge, as both many coins and very ancient marbles declare, as well as the very plots of the comedies of **Plautus** <note>Titus Maccius Plautus (c. 254–184 BC), a Roman playwright</note> which are titled *The Captives* and *The Menaechmi*. For if you connect the first letters of each individual verse <note>Referring to acrostics, where the first letter of each line spells a word</note>, it results in *Captiuei* and *Menaechmei*, such that the *ei* diphthong is everywhere. Furthermore, we read *Fouri*, not *Furi*, on another coin; as...

<summary>This page discusses the Roman "turned thumb" gesture as a sign of death in the arena and argues for the existence of seven ancient Latin diphthongs based on physical evidence from coins and inscriptions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's examination of Latin diphthongs and orthography, moving from the use of 'ai' and 'ei' to the practice of doubling vowels for length. the first syllable is lengthened by a diphthong. This is also the case among the Greeks. 
What, then, shall I say of that which **Quintilian** <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus (c. 35–100 AD), a Roman rhetorician</note> indicates, and which we ourselves have occasionally observed: that they would double a vowel that was to be lengthened? For instance, on a gold coin, the name **Feelix** <note>original: "Feelix"</note> appears with a double /e/; and on a bronze coin of the Emperor **Galba**, the word **uirtuus** <note>original: "uirtus" (virtue), noted here as having a double 'u'</note> is found with a double /u/. Furthermore, is that **irregular** <note>original: "ἄκυρον" (akyron) - a technical term for an improper or unusual linguistic form</note> diphthong not also found among us, at least in Greek names such as **Harpyia**, **Orithyia**, and **Thyiade**? Thus, it may seem that our ancestors possessed not just seven diphthongs, but ten.

# Chapter XLIV.

->That "Pegasean nectar" ought to be read in Persius, not "melody" [melos].<-

**Persius** <note>A Roman satirist (34–62 AD) known for his difficult and dense style</note> says in his epigram, which serves as a prologue:
> You would believe that crow poets and magpie poetesses,
> Sing a Pegasean melody. <note>original: "Coruos poetas, inquit & poetridas picas, / Cantare credas pegaſeium melos." The author is quoting this to argue against the word "melos" (melody).</note>

In a very ancient commentary, written in the letters they call **Lombardic** <note>A script used in Italy from the 6th to 11th centuries, often difficult for later Renaissance scholars to read</note>—which we have even displayed publicly to our students—we found it written word-for-word as follows: "Pegasean nectar," though in others it is "melody" [*melos*]. From this, we believe the ancient and uncorrupted text had "nectar." The "melody" [*melos*] reading is a newer and faulty one. Moreover, **Pomponius Laetus** <note>Julius Pomponius Laetus (1428–1498), a prominent Italian humanist and founder of the Roman Academy</note>, the head of the Roman Academy and a most diligent man of antiquity, affirmed to many listeners that he possessed an ancient manuscript of Persius that supported this reading of ours. 

Even the requirements of the poem's meter prove this. For in a **choliambic** <term>choliambic: also called a 'limping iambic,' a meter where the final foot is a spondee or trochee instead of the expected iamb</term> verse, the final foot must be a spondee or a trochee. However, "melody" [*melos*] is never accepted—neither among the Greeks nor among us—unless the first syllable is short. It would certainly be a defect in the verse if you admitted "melody" [*melos*]. 

Now, some claim that the letter <gloss>l</gloss> [λ] has the power to lengthen a syllable among the Greeks—a point which **Hephaestion** <note>A 2nd-century Greek grammarian who wrote the definitive manual on meters</note>, that singular authority on meter, was entirely silent about. I know this is sometimes found, but only in certain obscure and ignoble sheets. They bring forward an example from **Homer**, where we read **Heloria** with the first syllable lengthened. But how frivolous, how slight, and how trifling that is! This is most clearly shown by the fact that it is now accepted by the usage and authority of every learned man that this word in Homer should also be marked with a double <gloss>l</gloss> [λ]. Furthermore, even if we were to grant that this occasionally happens among the Greeks, one should not immediately believe that such a license...

<meta>This page continues the author's philological argument regarding a specific word in the prologue of the satirist Persius. On the previous page, the author began critiquing the use of the word "melos" (melody) on metrical grounds, suggesting it is a corruption of the original text.</meta>

license <note>original: "licentia"</note> for Persius—a single Greek designation which the Greeks themselves are found to use hardly once in such a great forest of writings. Especially since neither among the Greeks—as far as I have observed—nor likewise among the Latins, is the word **melody** <note>original: "melos"</note> found with anything other than a short first syllable. For the ancient Latins avoided this license (which was granted only to the Greeks), as the poet **Martial** <note>Marcus Valerius Martialis (c. 38–103 AD), a Roman poet famous for his epigrams</note> declares when he tormented himself so much over the name **Earinus**. Nor did he allow himself to say *eiarinon* <note>original: "εἰαρινον" (eiarinon) — a Greek form of "vernal" or "of spring"</note> for Earinus, despite the common Greek license of inserting a letter, as in **Homer**: "upon the flowers of spring" <note>original: "ἐπ᾽ ἀνθεσιν εἰαρινοῖσιν" (ep' anthesin eiarinoisin)</note>. Indeed, listen to Martial himself:

->Poets sing of Earinus,<-
->But the Greeks, to whom nothing is denied,<-
->And who are allowed to sound *Ares, Ares* <note>original: "Ares Ares" — Martial is referring to how Greek poets could change the vowel length of the god Ares' name to fit the meter</note>,<-
->It is not permitted for us to be so eloquent,<-
->We who cultivate more severe Muses.<-

Therefore, there should be no doubt that the word "melody" <note>original: "melos"</note> in that verse of Persius must be cut away like a hideous wart, and **Nectar** must be restored, as both reason and ancient authority have fully supported it.

### Chapter XLV.

### That Achilles was younger than Patroclus: contrary to what either Aeschylus recorded or what is commonly believed.

An opinion has long been spread and accepted among everyone that **Patroclus** was younger than **Achilles**, and was loved by him much like **Hylas** was by **Hercules**. Martial is believed to imply this very thing, saying:

> The light-hearted friend was closer to the descendant of Aeacus. <note>original: "Aeacidæ propior leuis amicus erat." The descendant of Aeacus is Achilles.</note>

For this reason, applause was even commonly given to a certain most wicked verse from the books of the *Hermaphrodite* <note>Likely referring to the *Hermaphroditus*, a collection of erotic epigrams by the Renaissance poet Antonio Beccadelli</note>. However, **Statius** in the *Achilleid* <note>An unfinished epic poem by Publius Papinius Statius (c. 45–96 AD) about the life of Achilles</note> declares their age to be finally equal, saying:

> Patroclus follows, already then bound by a great love, 
> and he exerts himself as a rival in such great deeds, 
> equal in pursuits and in the measure of his age, but far behind in strength. 
> And yet, he was destined to see Troy with an equal fate.

But **Plato**, in the *Symposium*, contends something far different. For he says that Achilles was much younger...

->### Chapter XXXVI.<-

The word *Cacoethes* <note>original: "Cacoethes" — a term derived from Greek meaning a persistent bad habit or a malignant disease</note> in the poet Juvenal is four syllables, not *cacethos*. And one should read "stored Lyde in a jar," not "unstored." At the same time, I believe a passage in Martianus Capella is explained with some care.

IN the seventh Satire of the poet Juvenal <note>Decimus Iunius Iuvenalis (1st–2nd century AD), a Roman poet known for his biting social critiques</note>, it is written thus:
> An incurable vice of writing holds many,
> And grows old in the sick heart.
<note>original: "Tenet insanabile multos / Scribendi cacethes: & egro in corde senescit"</note>

However, the word "cacethos" is used neither by the Greeks nor the Latins anywhere. Nor does the meter of the verse permit it. Therefore, we suspect it should be read as *cacoethes*, so that it matches what the Greeks call "the bad habit" <note>original: "Το κακόηθεσ" (to kakoethes) — meaning a malicious disposition or an ill-natured character</note>. For by that very common and frequent word, a "bad habit" is signified. I likewise found this in an ancient manuscript written in Lombardic script <note>A style of medieval handwriting used in Italy</note>, which Francesco Gaddi of Florence—a secretary to the highest magistrate, a prudent and kind man, and not unlearned in literature—allowed me to read. And that verse appears there in the same way:
> Nor does the swollen Lyde, stored in a jar, provide any help.
<note>original: "Turgida nec prodest condita pyxide lyde"</note>

Which elsewhere is incorrectly written as: "Lyde stored in a jar" <note>original: "In pyxide condita lyde"</note>; so that you might understand the old woman Lyde to be selling medicines of that kind, fat and (as Plautus <note>Titus Maccius Plautus (c. 254–184 BC), a Roman comedic playwright</note> says) "barrel-shaped" <note>original: "doliarem"</note>. Perhaps Martianus Capella <note>A 5th-century author famous for his encyclopedia of the liberal arts</note>, observing this and joking very wittily about the drunken Silenus, says: "And while he desires to move his step, Lyde wants to call him away." For just as if the names of Canthara or Pyrthia or Baucis...

### Chapter XLVII.
#### What Pliny recorded regarding the "pending" titles of ancient artists, which have also been recognized on ancient monuments in Rome; as well as the appearance of the Virgilian aegis.

Recently in Rome, in the atrium of the Mellini house, we saw a certain marble base on which it was written in Greek: *King Seleucus; Lysippus was making [this]* <note>original: "σέλευκοσ βασιλευσ λύσιπποσ ἐποίει" — Seleukos Basileus Lysippos epoiei</note>. In Latin, this means: "King Seleucus: Lysippus was making [this]." There was with us then a man most learned in both Greek and Latin, and most diligent in such refinements: Giovanni Lorenzo of Venice, secretary to the Supreme Pontiff; a man most learned in both languages and most devoted to all such elegant things. He, therefore, pointed out to us—which we immediately grasped and recognized—that it was not by chance that *was making* <note>original: "ἐποίει" — epoiei</note> was used there rather than *made* <note>original: "ἐποίησεν" — epoiesen</note>; that is, "was making" rather than "made."

Indeed, as Pliny says in the preface to his books of *Natural History*: those greatest artists, even on finished works, inscribed a <term>pending title</term> <note>original: "pendenti titulo" — a signature implying the work is still in progress</note>, such as "Apelles was making [this]" and "Polyclitus [was making this]," as if the art were always just begun and incomplete. This was done so that, against the variations of public judgment, there might remain for the artist a recourse to forgiveness, as if he were about to correct whatever was lacking, had he not been interrupted by death or fate. Therefore, he says, it is full of modesty that they inscribed all their works as if they were the very latest, and as if something had been snatched away from each individual work by fate.

However, only three works (not more, as some suppose) are recorded as being inscribed "absolutely" <note>original: "adsolute" — in the completed past tense</note>, stating "He made it" <note>original: "Ille fecit"</note>, which Pliny lists in their proper places. From this, it appeared that a supreme confidence in his art pleased those specific authors. And for that reason, all those works were objects of great envy. Furthermore, the same Giovanni Lorenzo of Venice asserted that similar titles are to be found elsewhere in Rome. We also saw—which we thought should be inserted into these *Miscellanea* of ours—a certain marble [object] in the same city...

### Chapter XLVIII.
#### That others have written about Megabyzus what Pliny wrote about Alexander: who was accustomed to visit the workshop of Apelles.

Pliny Secundus <note>Pliny the Elder</note>, in the thirty-fifth volume of his *Natural History*, says that Alexander of Macedon was accustomed to frequent the workshop of the most excellent painter Apelles, delighted not only by his skill but also by the man's courtesy. While in the workshop, Pliny says, Alexander would discuss many things unskillfully, and Apelles would politely urge him to be silent, saying that the boys who were grinding the colors were laughing at him. What Pliny writes about Alexander, Plutarch attributed to Megabyzus <note>a high-ranking Persian official or priest of Artemis at Ephesus</note> in that volume where he discusses the differences between a flatterer and a friend. If we translate his words into English, they are roughly these: Apelles the painter, as Megabyzus sat beside him and attempted to say something regarding line and shadow, said: "Do you see how the boys grinding the melinum <note>original: "melida"; a white earth pigment from the island of Melos</note>—who were just now paying attention to you while you were silent and marveling at your purple and gold—now mock you when you begin to speak about things you have not learned?" Moreover, Aelian also, in his books of *Various History*, attributed this to Megabyzus himself, and not to Alexander.

# Chapter XLIX.

## A comparison of the epigrams of the Greek Posidippus and the Latin Ausonius regarding a statue of "Opportunity": then a most beautiful Greek description by Callistratus.

The epigram of the poet **Ausonius** is famous, in which he describes a statue of **Opportunity** <note>In Latin, *Occasio*</note>: for in this way he interpreted the god whom the Greeks call *Kairos* <note>original: καιρόν; the personification of the "opportune moment"</note>. But truly, I wonder greatly why he attributed the work to **Phidias** <note>one of the most famous Greek sculptors, known for the Statue of Zeus at Olympia</note>, when it actually belonged to **Lysippus**. For there still exists a Greek epigram by **Posidippus** on a similar subject, from which Ausonius fashioned his own: though in the Greek, **Lysippus of Sicyon** is named as the craftsman, while in the Latin, it is claimed to be Phidias. 

Furthermore, the Greek author has the figure walking on the tips of his toes, while the Latin author says he stands upon a small wheel. Both attach winged sandals to the ankles; both cover the face with hair; both leave the back of the head bare. However, that Greek *Kairos* carries a razor in his right hand, and **Penitence** <note>original: *penitentia*; representing the regret felt when an opportunity is missed</note> is joined to the Latin **Opportunity** as a companion. The figures are similar in both poets, yet the Greek version is somewhat more pleasing; for in all these "embellishments" (if I may call them that), I know not how it is, but the Greeks are more elegant than our Romans; and so the copy inevitably falls short of the original.

But we also read the wonderfully festive *ecphrasis* <term>ecphrasis: a vivid, dramatic literary description of a work of visual art</term> by **Callistratus** of the same statue: by which it is confirmed that the work was made by Lysippus, and was a public sight for the people of Sicyon. Furthermore, Callistratus says this god is made of bronze: a boy still in the first flower of youth from his head to his feet. He describes him as beautiful, with hair scattered by the west wind <note>original: *fauonio*, referring to Favonius, the Roman name for Zephyrus</note>, with great grace in his forehead and cheeks, and in the beauty of his whole body, looking very much like **Dionysus** <note>the god of wine and theater, often depicted as a beautiful, youthful man</note>: indeed, he stands upon a ball with a suspended step and winged feet! 

The same Callistratus, while bringing the hair down over the boy's eyes and cheeks, leaves the back of the head free of hair. Finally, the other "seasonings" and allurements—the delights, the festivities, and the overseas wit of this writer—I believe should be sought from the author himself. It happens, I know not how, that our Roman "gravity" <note>original: *gravitas*; the Roman ideal of seriousness and dignity</note> shrinks from these little playful bits and stylistic flourishes—as if they were a finely woven cloak from Miletus—fearing them more than a dog or a snake. I should by no means pass over this: that **Nicephorus**, also a not inelegant Greek writer, began in a similar way when writing about the Virgin Mother of God...

<summary>This page compares how different ancient poets, specifically Ausonius and Posidippus, described the allegorical statue of "Opportunity" (Kairos) created by Lysippus. It highlights the symbolic features of the statue—such as the hair over the eyes and the bald back of the head—which represent how opportunity must be seized as it approaches but cannot be caught once it has passed.</summary>

## Chapter
### A passage in the copies of Pliny amended concerning the name of the Daughters of Proetus; and therein the related history of their madness.

In the twenty-fifth book of the *Natural History*, in the chapter where **Pliny the Elder** <note>original: *Plini Secundi*</note> treats hellebore, his words are found thus in the most common manuscripts:

"The fame of **Melampus** is known for his arts of divination; from him, a type of hellebore is called **Melampodion**. Some report that a shepherd of the same name discovered it, noticing that his goats were purged by it; and having given their milk, he cured the raging **ear-tumors** <note>original: *parotidas*; swellings of the parotid glands near the ear</note>."

However, we found in one most ancient manuscript from that most noble library of the **Medici** family, not "by a shepherd" but "by feeding" <note>original: *pasto* instead of *pastore*</note>; and not "ear-tumors" but "**Daughters of Proetus**" <note>original: *proetidas* instead of *parotidas*</note>! 

The distinction between "shepherd" and "feeding" is a simple matter; likewise, there is clear evidence that one should read "Daughters of Proetus" rather than "ear-tumors." This is the most indubitable and true reading, as reason itself teaches most effectively. (For what, after all, would "raging ear-tumors" be? Or what do ear-tumors—an affliction of the ears—have to do with hellebore?) 

Furthermore, **Dioscorides** <note>a Greek physician and botanist</note>, a fine authority, declares this most clearly, writing as follows: 

"Black **hellebore** <note>original: *Veratrum nigrum*; a plant used in antiquity to treat madness</note>: some call it *melampodion*, others *ectomon*, and some *polyrrhizon*. It is called *melampodion* because a certain Melampus, a shepherd, seems to have purged and cured the raging daughters of **Proetus** with it." 

Thus he wrote. But **Apollodorus of Athens**, in his *Library* (for that is the title of his book), attributes the purging of Proetus's daughters to the seer Melampus rather than to a shepherd. He says that the daughters born to Proetus and **Sthenoebia**—**Lysippe**, **Iphinoë**, and **Iphianassa**—were afflicted with madness as soon as they reached adulthood; either because (as **Hesiod** writes) they did not accept the rites of **Bacchus** <note>original: *Liberi patris*; the god of wine and religious ecstasy</note>, or (as is found in **Acusilaus**) because they showed contempt for the statue of **Juno**. After they began to rage, they first wandered through the whole territory of the Argives...

<summary>This page features a scholarly correction of Pliny the Elder’s Natural History, identifying a scribal error where "Daughters of Proetus" was miswritten as "ear-tumors." The author uses botanical and mythological evidence to restore the story of Melampus curing the princesses' madness with hellebore.</summary>
<keywords>Pliny the Elder, Melampus, Daughters of Proetus, hellebore, Dioscorides, Apollodorus, Greek mythology, philology, textual criticism, Medici Library</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological correction of Pliny’s Natural History. The previous page established that the "raging ear-tumors" (parotidas) mentioned in common manuscripts were actually the "Daughters of Proetus" (Proetidas), who were famously driven mad. This page provides the mythological background of that madness and its cure by the healer Melampus.</meta>

...wandering; then indeed, even through Arcadia and the entire Peloponnese, having forgotten their own dignity, they ran out through lonely places. Until **Melampus**—the son of Amythaon and Idothea, daughter of Abas—a seer, to be sure, and the first who had discovered how to heal with medicines and purgings, promised that he would cure the young women if a third part of the kingdom were signed over to him as a price. When **Proetus** <note>The King of Argos</note> did not agree to this, being clearly deterred by so enormous a fee, the madness of those maidens began to spread more and more daily; the contagion even passed to the other women, so that they commonly left their own homes, killed their children, and wandered through the wilderness. 

Therefore, as the calamity continued to propagate itself further and further, Proetus struck a bargain that he would give the requested fee. But now Melampus said he would not perform the cure until an equal portion of land was also awarded to his brother, **Bias**. At this point, Proetus, fearing that if he rejected the current condition they might demand even more thereafter, agreed to that price. 

And then finally Melampus, having enlisted the strongest of the young men as his companions, drove them from the mountains as far as Sicyon with shouting and a certain divinely-inspired <note>original: *enthea*; possessed by a god</note> (thus he says, for so it is) dance. However, during the pursuit, the eldest, **Iphinoe**, perished; the others, having made use of the purging, recovered their senses. Then Proetus gave them in marriage to Melampus and Bias, and he himself afterwards fathered a son, **Megapenthes**. 

Thus far, essentially, says **Apollodorus** <note>A 2nd-century BC scholar known for compiling Greek myths</note>. **Aelian** <note>original: *Helianus*; a Roman author of 'Various History'</note>, however, in his *Various History*, names the daughters of Proetus as Elega and Celene. Furthermore, **Sextus Empiricus** <note>A skeptical philosopher and physician</note>, in the fourth book of his *Pyrrhonian Outlines*, writes that **Polyanthus of Cyrene**, in his book *On the Lineage of the Asclepiads*, says the daughters of Proetus were freed from their insanity by **Asclepius** <note>The god of medicine</note>; they had fallen into this madness because **Juno** <note>original: *Iunone*; the goddess Hera</note> was angry. 

**Ovid** adds that Bias cast the purgings of their minds into the **Clitorian spring**, the drinking of which makes one a teetotaler. But indeed, he who is commonly thought to be **Probus** <note>A Roman grammarian</note>—a by no means improper commentator on **Virgil** <note>original: *Maronis*; referring to Publius Vergilius Maro</note>—also cites **Hesiod** as a witness, and touches very briefly upon a story almost like this one of Apollodorus. 

However, that there was such an error of mind and spirit in them that they believed themselves to be cows—which Virgil also indicates in his *Bucolics*—should by no means seem like a mere fable, seeing that **Paul of Aegina**, the physician...

### Chapter 51.
# Why the honors in the temple of Ephesian Diana are called "soft" by Martial.

Regarding why **Martial** <note>The Roman epigrammatist, active 1st century AD</note> called the honors in the temple of **Trivia** <note>Another name for Diana/Artemis, "the goddess of the crossroads"</note>—that is, in the temple of **Ephesian Diana**—"soft" <note>original: *molles*</note>, these points can also be brought forward: quite clever (if I am not mistaken) and shrewd enough, but which the commentators have nevertheless omitted. 

And first, indeed, we can think the term was applied concerning the "many-breasted" statue. For as **Saint Jerome** <note>original: *divus Hieronymus*; a 4th-century Father of the Church</note> writes in his commentary on the Epistle of Paul to the **Ephesians**: there was in Ephesus a temple of Diana, and in it an image of the same "multi-mammia"—that is, an effigy of many breasts—by which her worshippers, being deceived, thought she was the nurse of all living things. 

We can likewise refer this to the procession, about which **Xenophon of Ephesus** writes in the first book of the *Ephesiaca* <note>An ancient Greek novel titled "The Ephesian Tale"</note>, to this effect: "A solemn festival of Diana," he says, "was being held in those places, from the city to the temple, which is a distance of exactly seven stades <note>About 0.8 miles</note>. It was necessary for all the local virgins to celebrate the procession, especially splendidly adorned. Besides them were the youths, the peers of **Abrocomas**, who was then about seventeen years old; and he was present with the youths, as if leading the way in that procession." 

A great multitude of people was present for the spectacle, whether locals or guests; clearly, the custom held that in that celebration, both husbands were found for the virgins and wives for the youths. The procession, therefore, proceeded in an orderly fashion: first, of course, the sacred objects—torches, baskets, and incense; then horses, dogs, and hunting weapons; and also the war-gear of some. But most things were peaceful; each of the women had composed herself as if for the eyes of a lover, and **Anthia** led the rank of the virgins. 

Thus indeed Xenophon writes—not that famous Athenian one <note>Xenophon of Athens, the famous historian and student of Socrates</note>, but another no less delightful: the Ephesian; who a little later also adds this: "When, therefore, the sacred..."

...procession: the entire multitude would come into the temple to sacrifice; and there all the finery of the procession was taken off. And men and women, youths and maidens, gathered in the same place. It is not absurd to hear the "soft honors" <note>original: *molles honores*; referring back to Martial's description of the temple rites, likely suggesting effeminacy or luxury</note> because of the priest of Diana as well; about which **Domitius** <note>Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century scholar and commentator</note> also mentions something from **Strabo** <note>A Greek geographer of the 1st century AD</note>. Furthermore, there exists a letter from **Heraclitus of Ephesus** <note>A famous pre-Socratic philosopher known for his misanthropy and the doctrine that "everything flows"</note>, that supreme philosopher—namely, the one who was called *skotinos* <note>original: *scotinos*; Greek for "dark" or "obscure"</note>, that is, "the shadowy one," because of the obscurity of his books—and who used to weep over all human actions. In this letter to **Hermodorus**, he mocks the Ephesians in roughly this way: "How much better are wolves and lions than the Ephesians! They do not enslave one another, nor does an eagle prey upon an eagle <note>original: *empritat*; likely a corruption of a word meaning to seize or prey upon</note>, nor does a lion serve wine to a lion! But neither does a dog castrate a dog, as you have castrated the priest of the goddess." He refers to the **Megabyzus** <note>A title for the high priest of Artemis at Ephesus, who was required to be a eunuch</note>, out of fear that a man might be consecrated to a virgin goddess; and he notes in what way you are impious toward nature so that you might be pious toward a statue. For from the start, you arrange it so that the first priest to pray to the gods has lost his virility. Indeed, you even accuse the goddess of unchastity, as if fearing lest she be served by a man.

# Chapter 52.
## What the "Altar frequented by Horns" is in the works of Martial.

In the aforementioned epigram <note>The author is analyzing the first epigram of Martial’s *Liber Spectaculorum*</note>, there is this other passage, which, as far as I can see, has not been understood by Domitius nor by others.
->And let the altar frequented by horns disguise the god.<-
For the fact that Domitius interprets this as the **Temple of Ammon** <note>The temple of Zeus-Ammon in the Libyan desert, where the god was depicted with the horns of a ram</note> in Libya does not work in any way. For since the most magnificent buildings in the world are being commemorated here—those which are counted among the Wonders—so that by comparison with them the praise of the **Amphitheater** <note>The Colosseum in Rome</note> may grow, it would surely be ridiculous to hear here of the temple of Ammon, which is indeed "very poor" <note>original: *pauperrimo*</note>, as **Lucan** <note>A Roman poet of the 1st century AD</note> says in these verses:
> They had come to the temple, which for the Libyan tribes
> The uncultivated **Garamantes** <note>A powerful tribal confederacy in the ancient Sahara</note> alone possess. There stands the Horned 
> Jupiter, as they tell it; but not brandishing thunderbolts, 
> Or like our Jupiter; but **Ammon** with twisted horns.

> ->There, the Libyan tribes did not build wealthy<-
> ->temples, nor do offerings shine with Eastern gems:<-
> ->though for the Ethiopian peoples, the blessed<-
> ->Arabs, and the Indians, Jupiter Hammon is the only god.<-
> ->The god is still poor: his shrines, unviolated through<-

<note>original: Lucan, *Pharsalia* 9.511–517. The poet Lucan describes the Temple of Jupiter Hammon in the Libyan desert as a place of ancient simplicity, contrasting it with the decadent wealth of Rome.</note>

Neither from the words of **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author and natural philosopher</note> nor from those of **Virgil** <note>original: *Maronis*; referring to Publius Vergilius Maro</note> will you conclude that the temple of **Hammon** among the **Nasamonians** <note>A nomadic Berber tribe of ancient Libya</note> was notable for its buildings or its wealth, if you carefully weigh the words of each. And indeed, I am mistaken unless it is rather the **Altar of Apollo** in **Delos** that should be understood—the one they call the *Ceratinon bomon* <note>original: *Ceratinon bomon*; Greek for "The Horned Altar"</note>—which **Plutarch** <note>A Greek-Roman biographer and philosopher</note> says was celebrated as one of the seven wonders of the world. He notes that it was joined and compacted only from right-side horns, without any glue or binding. Plutarch writes this in the short work where he discusses "Whether land or sea animals are more clever." **Ovid** also mentions this same altar in the letter from **Cydippe** to **Acontius** with this verse:

> ->I marvel at the altar built from countless horns.<-
<note>original: *Miror & innumeris structam de cornibus aram*; Ovid, *Heroides* 21.99</note>

Plutarch also writes about this altar in Delos in his *Life of Theseus*, stating not that it was made from right-side horns, but rather compacted only from left-side horns, which he calls the *keratonabomon* <note>original: *κερατωναβωμον*; another Greek term for "Horn-Altar"</note>, unless there is a error in the manuscripts. **Diogenes Laertius** also makes some mention of the *Ceratine* altar <note>original: *Ceratinæ arę*; "The Horned Altar"</note>—that is, the one made of horns—in his *Life of Pythagoras*. Moreover, the poet **Callimachus**, in his *Hymn to Apollo*, writes that **Apollo**, while only four years old, fashioned an altar from the horns of Cynthian goats <note>Goats from Mount Cynthus on the island of Delos</note> which his sister **Diana** <note>The Roman name for Artemis</note> had killed in the hunt. He likewise built the foundations and the wall from horns alone. These are his very words:

> ->At four years old, Apollo first laid the foundations<-
> ->in beautiful Ortygia, inside the circular lake.<-
> ->Artemis would continually bring the heads of<-
> ->Cynthian goats from the hunt, and Apollo wove the altar.<-
> ->He built the base with horns, and fixed the altar<-
> ->from horns, and placed walls of horn all around it.<-
<note>original Greek: "Τετραέτης Τα πρῶτα θεμείλια, φοῖβος ἔπηξε / καλῆ ἐν ὀρτυγίη περιηγέος ἔνδοθι λίμνης / ἄρτεμις ἀγρώσουσα καρήατα συνεχὲς αἰγῶν / κυνθιάδων φορέεσκεν, ὁ δ’ ἔπλεκε βωμον ἀπόλλων / Δείματο μεν κεράεσσιν ἐθέθλια, πῆξε δὲ βωμον / ἐκ κεράων κεραοὺς Δὲ πέριξ ὑπεβάλλετο Τοίχους." From Callimachus, *Hymn to Apollo* 58-63. Ortygia is an ancient name for Delos.</note>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into classical errors, moving from the description of the Altar of Horns to a critique of Martial's epigrams and an error found in Cicero's writings.</meta>

Therefore, for this reason, **Martial** <note>A Roman poet known for his *Epigrams*</note> says, "He conceals the god" <note>original: *Dissimulat deum*; from Martial’s *Spectacles* 1. Martial suggests the Flavian Amphitheater is so magnificent it rivals divine works</note>: as if that work could no longer appear to be the work of a god, since this other thing—the **amphitheater** <note>The Colosseum in Rome</note> made by a mortal hand—is far superior. Certainly, through the entire epigram, this most clever poet has used carefully chosen words. He commands **Memphis** <note>A city in Egypt, famous for the Pyramids</note> to be silent, because it is "barbaric"; and he does not want **Babylon** to be boasted of for its "ceaseless labor," because that very boasting implies the burden of the work. And he says the honors of **Trivia** <note>An epithet for the goddess Diana, referring here to the Temple of Artemis at Ephesus</note> are not to be praised because they are "soft" <note>original: *molles*; referring to the perceived effeminacy or luxury of the Ionians</note>, and praise is not owed to soft things. He also says that the **Mausoleums** <note>The tomb of Mausolus, one of the Seven Wonders</note> which "hang in the air" ought not to be raised "above the air itself"—that is, all the way to the stars—so that in this epigram nothing is left unweighed.

->Chapter 53<-

That the name of **Agamemnon** is placed in **Marcus Tullius Cicero’s** *On Divination* <note>original: *De Divinatione*</note> in place of **Ulysses**; also, in passing, a passage in the *Letters to Atticus* concerning "vermilion-tinted wax" <note>original: *miniatula cera*; referring to the red wax used by critics to mark errors in a manuscript</note> is corrected and explained.

<margin>a</margin> **Aulus Gellius**, in his *Attic Nights* <note>original: *Noctes Atticae*; a collection of notes on grammar, philosophy, and history</note>—specifically in those most brilliant passages—notes a very obvious error of Cicero's. It is not a matter of great importance, nor is it surprising that he erred, but rather that the error was not noticed afterward, either by Cicero himself or by his secretary **Tiro**. For when Cicero translated certain verses of **Homer** into Latin, he writes that **Ajax** says them in that poet’s work, when in truth they are spoken by **Hector**. This frankness of Aulus Gellius makes it so that I myself do not fear to report in these books a small matter comparable to his, and almost a close relative or twin to that previous one—unless perhaps (which I would more eagerly believe, given such a long span of time) it was a flaw in the manuscript copies. However, even in the most ancient books, we find it written the same way; nor is it truly a slip into one name or the other caused by the similarity of the letters. These are the words of Cicero in the second book of *On Divination*: "For I would be surprised if I believed what you said **Calchas** <note>The primary seer of the Greeks during the Trojan War</note> predicted about the years of the Trojan War from the number of sparrows; concerning whose prophecy **Agamemnon** speaks in Homer (as I have translated it in my leisure)."

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into classical errors, specifically addressing a mistake in Cicero's "On Divination" where he misattributes a speech from Homer's Iliad to Agamemnon instead of Ulysses.</meta>

"Bear up, O men, and endure hard labors in your hearts,
So that we may be able to know the prophecies of our seer Calchas,
Whether they have been held as false or as true beginnings of the breast."
<note>These verses are a Latin translation of Homer’s Iliad (Book 2, 299-300) made by Cicero himself.</note>

And then he sets forth the other verses of Homer likewise translated by him. Therefore, because **Cicero** relates that **Agamemnon** speaks these things in **Homer**—if indeed Cicero left it written in that way—this is proved to be in no way true; since in Homer himself, in the second book <note>original: "Rhapsodia"</note> of the *Iliad*, not Agamemnon but **Ulysses** pronounces the verses of that sentiment. Nor is there any reason why anyone should be annoyed or wonder that these things are also found in Cicero, since it appears from his own letters that sometimes errors of memory of this kind occurred in Cicero’s books, which were noticed and corrected either by **Atticus** or by **Brutus**. 

Just like that error in the *Speech for Ligarius* <note>original: *pro Ligariana*</note> concerning **Lucius Curfidius**, which he confesses is his own error? But, as they say, he calls it a <term>memory error</term> <note>original Greek: μνημονικον ἁμάρτημα; a slip of the mind</note>. Just as I believe occurred in the *Orator*: Cicero had put **Eupolis** <note>An Athenian poet of Old Comedy</note> in place of **Aristophanes**. Then Atticus put Aristophanes back in place of Eupolis. Therefore, Cicero even says sometimes that he fears lest his <term>compositions</term> <note>original Greek: syntaxıs; referring to his literary arrangements or treatises</note> must be marked in many places by the <note>original: "miniata illius cerula"</note> little vermilion wax seals of that man [Atticus]. 

But since it happened that we mentioned the vermilion wax, a passage here from the second-to-last book of the *Letters to Atticus* must be corrected and interpreted by us in passing, in which I found it incorrectly written thus: "With these letters written, I gave myself to my <term>compositions</term>; which indeed I fear, lest your excessive [marks] must be noted in many places; so distracted <note>original Greek: "meteoros"; literally 'lofty' but used here to mean 'anxious' or 'in suspense'</note> am I and hindered by great thoughts." I think it should be read: "Lest they be marked in many places by your **little vermilion wax marks**," <note>original: "miniatula cera"</note> so that the meaning is: [he gave himself] to the composing of books—for Cicero said he gave himself to his <term>arrangement of work</term> <note>original Greek: "syntaxıs"</note>! But he feared nonetheless that they would be marked in many places by the little vermilion wax of Atticus himself, to whom he writes—whom he often respects as an editor—because Cicero was "on edge" <note>original Greek: "meteoros"</note>, that is, his mind was in suspense and (as he says) hindered by great thoughts. 

I believe Atticus was accustomed, if any passage was treated by Cicero with insufficient perfection, to affix little red wax marks to that place, which we also are accustomed to do now. Moreover, in the manuscript which they say belonged first to **Francesco Petrarch**, and certainly later to **Coluccio Salutati**: and...

<summary>This page discusses a factual error in Cicero's "On Divination" regarding the Iliad and explains the historical practice of literary editing using "vermilion wax" marks (miniatula cera) by Cicero's editor, Atticus.</summary>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of manuscript errors and corrections in the works of Cicero, specifically focusing on a philological correction involving a "red mark" used by his editor, Atticus, before transitioning into a new chapter on logical fallacies.</meta>

...after this, in the work of **Leonardo Bruni** <note>original: *Leonardi Arretini*; a leading humanist and historian of the 15th century</note>, and soon after in that of **Donato Acciaiuoli** <note>A 15th-century scholar known for his commentaries on Aristotle</note>—men who were the most learned of their respective ages—the text still stands as: "lest your <term>red lead</term> <note>original: *miniata cerusa*; *cerusa* refers to white lead used in pigments, which is an error in this context</note>." But if you extend the second-to-last letter of the second-to-last word just a little from the top—that is, if you make an **l** out of the letter **i**—you will undoubtedly remove every error! Indeed, in the common manuscripts of those same letters, it is read as follows at the very end of the volume: for I dreaded those **red wax marks** <note>original: *miniata cerula*; *cerula* means a small wax tablet or mark, here referring to a proofreader's mark in red wax</note> of yours. Thus, by this evidence, all suspicion and ambiguity may now be abolished.

### Chapter 54.
### Who the "Horned Ones" are in Quintilian.

<margin>c</margin> You will not easily find someone to explain to you who the <term>Horned Ones</term> <note>original: *Ceratinae*; a type of logical paradox or sophism</note> are in the first book of **Quintilian**. However, those who wish to consult the Greek sources and the Latin commentaries will clearly understand that it is a type of <term>insoluble reasoning</term> <note>original: *ratiocinationis inexplicabilis*</note> which introduces certain "handles" and "hooks," as it were, of dialectical perplexity. I believe it was named thus from the very subject matter of the words they used, just as it appears in **Seneca’s** fifth book of *Letters to Lucilius*. "Sit," he says, "as a man of leisure, and pose little questions of this kind: 'What you have not lost, you possess. You have not lost horns. Therefore, you possess horns.'" There are other examples of this sharp madness that have been composed. But you may also find this discussed in the *Attic Nights* <note>A famous work by Aulus Gellius (c. 180 AD) containing various snippets of lore and logic</note>. For who does not know that horns are called *kerata* in Greek? **Lucian** also mentions this in the *Philosophers' Symposium*, as he does regarding the <term>Heap</term> <note>original: *Sorite*; the Sorites paradox</note> and the <term>Reaper</term> <note>original: *Therizonte*; another logical puzzle</note>! Moreover, in the *Dialogue of the Dead* between Castor and Pollux, you will find him saying much the same: "And they produce horns among themselves, and they make <term>Crocodiles</term> <note>original: *Crocodilos*; refers to the 'Crocodile Paradox,' a classic dilemma in logic</note>, and they teach how to ask such perplexing questions." Thus, from these "horns," you should understand this "Horned Reasoning" <note>original: *Ceratinen ratiocinationē*</note> that we mentioned—though it is more truly a ridiculous reasoning than a logical one. Furthermore, in another dialogue titled either *The Dream* or *The Cock*, he says: "And teaching that from two negations, one affirmation is constructed; and that it is not night, if it be day. Sometimes he even told me that I had horns."

<summary>This page concludes a philological correction of a typo in Cicero's letters (changing 'cerusa' to 'cerula') and explains the "Horned Paradox" (Ceratinae), a logical fallacy mentioned by Quintilian, Seneca, and Lucian.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo Bruni, Donato Acciaiuoli, Cicero, philology, Quintilian, Seneca, Lucian, Ceratinae, logic, paradoxes, syllogisms, Attic Nights</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of classical logical puzzles, moving from the "Horned Paradox" discussed on the previous page to other famous dialectical "snares" used by ancient philosophers to test the limits of reason. It transitions into Chapter 4, which defines the "Crocodile Paradox."</meta>

...and others of that kind. **Eubulides of Miletus** <note>A 4th-century BC philosopher of the Megarian school, famous for inventing several classic paradoxes</note> also made extensive use of these sorts of little dialectical questions in inquiry—the kind frequently devised to sharpen the intellect. These are laid out by **Diogenes Laertius** <note>A biographer of the Greek philosophers</note> in his *Life of Euclid of Megara*; let us attempt to translate them: the **Liar**, the **Deceiver**, the **Veiled Electra** <note>A paradox concerning knowledge: if you see your brother veiled, do you know him or not?</note>, the **Heap** <note>original: *acerualis*; also known as the *Sorites* paradox, which asks at what point a collection of grains becomes a "heap"</note>, the **Horned Paradox** (or the **Cornuted**), and likewise the **Bald Man**. Regarding this, there is a passage in some comic poet or other, as the same Laertius reports: "But Eubulides, questioning with his insulting **Horned Paradoxes** and confounding the orators with lying and boastful speeches, departed with the fluency of **Demosthenes** <note>The most famous of the Athenian orators</note>." The **Suda** <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world</note> also cited this passage.

But even the great **Zeno of Citium** <note>The founder of Stoicism</note> numbered the **Horned Paradoxes** among the other quibbles of the dialecticians, along with the **Nobody** paradox <note>original: *utidas*; from the Greek *outis*, referring to a wordplay paradox</note>. Diogenes Laertius recorded this in the seventh book of his *Lives of the Philosophers*. Finally, **Marcianus Capella** <note>A late antique author of an influential encyclopedia of the seven liberal arts</note> suggests this same thing to us in his fourth book when he says: "Lest they ever bear lost horns upon their brow."

### Chapter IV.
#### Who the "Crocodiles" are in the same author.

In the same **Quintilian** <note>The foremost Roman educator and rhetorician</note>, also in the first book, mention is made of the **Crocodile Paradox** <note>original: *Crocodiline*</note>, a term that is obscure and unknown to most people. However, we found information about this in **Doxapatres**, the Greek commentator on **Aphthonius** <note>A 4th-century teacher of rhetoric</note>; although in his work, the term is **Crocodilites** <note>The Crocodile-ist</note> rather than *Crocodiline*. I believe this is more accurate. We translate his words as follows:

"The **Saw**," he says, "and the **Crocodile Paradox**, as it is found in an Egyptian fable. A certain woman was walking along the banks of a river with her son. A crocodile snatched the son away, saying he would return him if the woman answered truly <note>i.e., if she correctly predicted what the crocodile was going to do</note>. She replied that he would not return the boy; and because of this, she argued it was only fair that he be returned." Thus far his account. Soon after, he says they call this proposition the **Crocodile Paradox** because of this crocodile; he indicates that the Greeks also call it the **Saw** <note>original: <term>πρίωνα</term> (*priona*); so named because the logic "cuts" back and forth like a saw</note>. **Lucian** <note>A 2nd-century satirist known for mocking philosophers</note> assists in this in his dialogue titled *The Sale of Lives*, depicting **Chrysippus** <note>A major Stoic philosopher who refined the study of logic</note> speaking in roughly this way, with someone responding to him who claims to be a buyer.

<summary>This page catalogs ancient logical fallacies and paradoxes, such as the "Horned One" and the "Crocodile," citing various classical and Byzantine authorities to explain their origins and mechanics.</summary>
<keywords>Eubulides of Miletus, Diogenes Laertius, logical paradoxes, Ceratinae, Crocodile Paradox, Chrysippus, Quintilian, dialectic, Greek philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general categorization of logical fallacies and "snares" mentioned by Diogenes Laertius to a specific example: the Crocodile Paradox. It then shifts topics to natural history and classical poetry, specifically debating the physical characteristics of the rhinoceros as described by the poet Martial and various ancient scholars.</meta>

...then he said: "Do you have a son?" "What is the point of this question?" "Suppose a **crocodile** should find him wandering near a river and seize him, and then promise to return him if you tell the truth about whether he intends to return the boy or not. What would you say he should do?" "You are asking a truly complicated thing." From these examples, I believe it is clear that the **Crocodile Paradox** <note>original: *Crocodiliten*</note>, just like the **Horned Paradox** <note>original: *Ceratinen*</note> discussed above, is a type of logical puzzle <note>original: *sophismatos*</note> that is difficult to untangle, which the ancient logicians and especially the **Stoics** <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy that emphasized logic as one of the three branches of knowledge</note> used. Quintilian elegantly called this kind of thing "exquisite ambiguities."

## Chapter 56.
### Why Martial, in his book *On the Spectacles*, spoke of a "double horn": where he discusses the Rhinoceros and Ethiopian bulls.

There is an epigram by **Martial** <note>A Roman poet (c. 40–104 AD) famous for his short, witty poems about Roman life</note> in his work *On the Spectacles* concerning the rhinoceros, of which these are the final lines:

> For he tossed the heavy bear with his double horn,
> Just as a bull tosses straw dummies into the stars. <note>original: *Namque gravem cornu gemino sic extulit ursum / Iactat ut impositas taurus in astra pilas.* Martial compares the rhinoceros's power in the arena to a bull tossing practice targets.</note>

**Domitius** <note>likely Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century commentator on classical texts</note> explains it this way: "By 'double,' he means a horn that is strong and powerful." He cites **Virgil**: "The plow-share with a double back." <note>original: *Gemino dentalia dorso* from the *Georgics*</note>. Or perhaps because the rhinoceros actually has two horns, as I have read only in **Pausanias** <note>A 2nd-century AD Greek traveler whose writings provide a bridge between natural history and geography</note>: of which one stands out from the nostrils with remarkable size, and the other breaks out higher up, small but very strong. 

I must admit that Domitius claims to have read in Pausanias alone that the rhinoceros has two horns. But what are we to believe about **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, whose *Natural History* was the standard reference for the Middle Ages and Renaissance</note>, or **Solinus** <note>A 3rd-century compiler of marvels</note>, or others who attribute only one horn to the rhinoceros? Were they all ignorant of what only Pausanias noticed? Furthermore, how shall we respond to **Septimius Florens Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD)</note>? Among all the Christians whose Latin works survive, he is the oldest, and I would dare say the most diligent in almost every branch of literature. In his third book *Against Praxeas*, explaining those words from the **Old Testament** <note>original: *vetere instrumento*</note>: "His beauty is like a bull's; his horns are the horns of a **unicorn**; with them he shall push the nations even to the ends of the earth." <note>A reference to the blessing of Joseph in Deuteronomy 33:17</note>. He says, "Certainly the rhinoceros was not intended by the 'one-horned' [unicorn], nor the **Minotaur** by the 'two-horned' [bicorn]," and so forth.

<summary>The author explains the "Crocodile Paradox" as an unsolvable logical puzzle and then investigates whether the rhinoceros has one or two horns based on classical sources like Martial, Pausanias, and Pliny.</summary>
<keywords>Crocodile Paradox, logic, Stoics, Rhinoceros, Martial, Pausanias, Tertullian, natural history, unicorn, biblical interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the debate over Martial's description of the rhinoceros, specifically addressing the "double horn" mentioned in his poetry. It moves from citing the geographer Pausanias to a grammatical analysis of Martial's verse, arguing that the rhinoceros actually had only one horn and that the "double horn" refers to a bull. It then transitions to Chapter 57, which identifies exotic birds mentioned by Suetonius and corrects errors in the manuscripts of Pliny the Elder.</meta>

Furthermore, concerning this matter, **Pausanias** <note>A Greek traveler and geographer of the 2nd century AD</note> mentions in his *Description of Boeotia* that not all rhinoceroses have two horns—for this could be clearly refuted. Rather, he says there are bulls in **Ethiopia** <note>In antiquity, "Ethiopia" often referred generally to sub-Saharan Africa</note> which they call Rhinoceroses "because for them, at the tip of the nose," (for these are his words) "there is a horn, and another above it which is not large; but on the head, they have no horns at all." <note>original Greek: ὅτι σφίσιν ἐπ' 擦κρᾳ τῇ ῥινί... ἐν ἑκάστῳ κέρασ' καὶ ἄλλο ὑπὲρ αὐτὸ οὐ μέγα ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς κεφαλῆς οὐδ' ἀρχὴν κέρατά ἐστιν.</note> Since, he says, there is a single horn on the very end of the nose for each of them, and then another above it, not a large one. But truly, on the head, there are not even the beginnings of horns. 

Therefore, Pausanias does not say that one horn is of remarkable size, or that the other is the strongest. **Domitius** <note>likely Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century scholar</note> perhaps recalls this so that the bear might be more easily lifted up by these horns in the poem. Nor does Pausanias mean the rhinoceros itself, but as we said, the Ethiopian bull, which by some resemblance was itself called a "Rhinoceros" by certain people. For this reason, we think **Martial** <note>A Roman poet (c. 40–104 AD) famous for his epigrams about Roman life and the Colosseum</note> should be interpreted thus: that he means the bear was lifted and tossed by that single horn of the Rhinoceros, [even though the bear was] "heavy for a double horn"—that is, it would be heavy even for a bull, which has twin horns. 

In this reading, "horn" would be in the dative case <note>indicating the recipient of the "heaviness"</note>, not the ablative case <note>indicating the instrument of lifting</note>. For while bulls toss straw dummies <note>original: *pilas*; these were effigies used to provoke animals in the arena</note> to the stars with their horns, they still could not lift a bear, "heavy for a double horn"—and for that reason, the Rhinoceros lifted it more miraculously with its single horn.

## Chapter 57.

What the *tetraones* are in Suetonius; and the correction of an error in the copies of Pliny.

The nature of the birds called *tetraones* <note>Likely referring to the black grouse or a similar game bird</note> mentioned in **Suetonius's** life of **Caligula** <note>The third Roman Emperor, known for his extravagance</note> is entirely unknown. His own words are these: "The sacrificial victims were *flamingos, peacocks, bustards, Numidian tetraones, guinea-fowls, and pheasants,* which were sacrificed by species every single day." Furthermore, the matter would not have been difficult to discover, had we not possessed such uncorrected copies of **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the *Natural History*</note>. For what we read in him in the tenth book of his *Natural History*, where he discusses geese: "The *Erythrotaonas* <note>A corrupted Greek term in Pliny's text likely referring to 'red grouse'</note> are distinguished by their own brilliance and absolute blackness." This, in that most ancient copy of Pliny...

<meta>This page continues the philological discussion of animal names in classical texts, moving from the rhinoceros to the identification of the grouse (tetraon) in the works of Pliny the Elder. It then transitions into a new chapter regarding the history and rituals of the Secular Games, a major Roman religious festival.</meta>

The public library of the **Medici family** <note>original: "Medicæ gentis." Referring to the Laurentian Library in Florence, founded by the Medici</note> is read almost correctly, namely with only one letter changed; such words are often interpolated <note>altered or inserted into a text</note> in all ancient volumes. For since these volumes are also extremely faulty in many places, they still preserve traces, by no means obscure, of the true reading to be investigated—traces which are completely erased from new manuscripts by dishonest scribes. In that library, therefore, the text stands as follows: *It is fitting also for trabonas* <note>original: "Decet & trabonas." A corruption of the correct "tetraonas" (grouse)</note>; so that if you swap the order of the first two letters, it would sound like *Terrahonas*. For this reason, following the authority of **Poliziano** <note>Angelo Poliziano (1454–1494), a brilliant Italian humanist scholar and poet</note>, you should read: *Grouse possess their own splendor and absolute blackness. On their eyebrows is a scarlet redness.* <note>Quoting Pliny the Elder, Natural History 10.56</note> Another kind of them exceeds the size of vultures. He also describes their color: no bird, with the exception of the **Ostrich** <note>original: "Struthochamelo"</note>, fills a greater weight of body, having grown so much that it may be caught even on the ground while motionless. The Alps and the northern regions produce them. Thus far he has written concerning the grouse.

## Chapter LVIII.

### The origin and rites of the Secular Games: and besides these, other related matters: and the oracle of the Sibyl cited therein: and presently, certain things explained in passing: and again, other things refuted with some utility.

I believe it will be worthwhile to collect here also the origin of the **Secular Games** <note>original: "ludorum secularium." A Roman religious celebration involving sacrifices and theatrical performances, supposedly held once every "saeculum" or century</note> and the reason for the name, and likewise after how many years they were held, how often they occurred, by what rite, and for which gods they were celebrated. For the matter itself is not unworthy of knowing for anyone who has any passion for antiquity, and it will bring great assistance to understanding several passages of the poets. When, therefore, the Romans and the **Albans** <note>People of Alba Longa, the ancient city that was a rival and precursor to Rome</note> were waging war against each other, and both battle lines were already standing in readiness, there suddenly appeared a certain figure of monstrous appearance, clothed in a dark skin, shouting and commanding that an offering be made to **Father Dis** <note>The Roman god of the underworld, also known as Pluto</note> and the goddess **Proserpina** before they joined in battle. Terrified by this sight, the Romans first built an altar under the earth, and immediately after the sacrifice was performed, they covered it with a mound twenty feet high, so that it would be unknown to all except the Romans themselves.

<summary>This page corrects a textual error in Pliny the Elder regarding the grouse (tetraon) using a Medici manuscript and begins a historical inquiry into the Secular Games, starting with a legend about an underground altar dedicated to the gods of the underworld.</summary>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the discussion of the grouse (tetraon) to the origins of the Secular Games, specifically recounting the legendary story of Valesus Valesius and the discovery of the altar to Dis and Proserpina in the Campus Martius.</meta>

It happened that **Valesus Valesius**—from whom the name and origin of the **Valerian family** <note>original: "Valeriæ familiæ." A prominent patrician family in ancient Rome</note> is derived—a most distinguished man among the **Sabine people** <note>An ancient Italic tribe known for their religious devotion</note> and a wealthy man of rural life, discovered this altar of which we spoke through divine intervention. He possessed a grove before his villa with very tall trees, which, having been struck by lightning, immediately caught fire. Not long after, his two sons and his daughter were seized by a plague-like disease and were suffering to the point that the doctors despaired.

Therefore, while the father, throwing himself before the **household gods** <note>original: "laribus familiaribus." Protective spirits of the Roman home and family</note>, offered his own life and that of the children's mother for the safety of their children, a voice was immediately heard from the grove that had been struck from heaven. It promised they would be safe if, having traveled down the **Tiber** to **Tarentum** <note>Valesius mistakes this for the city in southern Italy, but it refers to a specific volcanic site in Rome</note>, they drank water from the river itself, heated at the hearth of **Dis and Proserpina** <note>The King and Queen of the Underworld; Dis is the Roman equivalent of Pluto</note>.

But because he knew that **Tarentum** was very far away, specifically in the furthest part of **Iapygia** <note>An ancient name for the region of Apulia, the "heel" of Italy</note>, and since he knew that no river called the Tiber was found near that city, he began to form the gloomiest expectations. He was also terrified because he remembered that the voice had come specifically from the powers of the Underworld. Nevertheless, having placed his children in a small boat, he brought them first to **Ostia** <note>original: "Hostia." Rome's primary port city at the mouth of the Tiber</note> and soon after down to the region of the **Campus Martius** <note>The "Field of Mars," a large public area in Rome used for military exercises and religious rites</note>.

Desiring to refresh the children there, as they were thirsty and suffering from heat and fever, he set the sick children on the bank where the river flows most calmly. While he was preparing a makeshift little hut for himself and his small fire had gone out, he was advised by the pilot that they should rather seek **Tarentum** (for that was the name of a nearby place), as he saw smoke rising there. Then, indeed, Valesius was more joyful upon hearing the name Tarentum. Praying to the gods and now almost promising himself the health of his children in good faith, he ordered them to move forward and the vessel to be steered specifically toward that spot.

When he had arrived there, he hastily drew water from the river. Relying on the omen he had firmly grasped, he coaxed the smoking ground there into a flame with his breath. Soon, having heated the water, he offered it in a cup to the children. Sleep followed the drink. In their rest, they saw their disease being wiped away by someone with a sponge. Then he <note>The divine voice or a vision</note> commanded that **dark-colored victims** <note>original: "furvæ hostiæ." It was traditional to sacrifice dark animals to the gods of the Underworld</note> should be sacrificed there to Father **Dis** and **Proserpina**, and that a continuous three-night festival should be celebrated with dances and songs.

Therefore, they rose with recovered health and reported the vision to their father. This wealthy man ordered the ground to be dug deep for the laying of foundations. Thus, the altar of which we spoke was discovered, with an inscription to **Dis and Proserpina**. He sacrificed (what...

The story explains the discovery of an ancient underground altar dedicated to the gods of the Underworld, Dis and Proserpina.</summary>
