<meta>This page continues the legend of Valesus Valesius, describing how his children were healed and the subsequent naming of his son. It then transitions into a scholarly discussion of the Secular Games, citing various Roman authorities such as Varro, Livy, and Horace to debate the proper timing and origin of the festival.</meta>

(he was commanded) to immediately offer **dark-colored victims** <note>original: "furvas uictimas." Black animals were the standard sacrifice for deities of the underworld</note> and to attend the sacred rites for three nights; for he had received exactly that many sons back from the brink of death. Because of this, he was named **Manius Valerius Tarentinus** <note>The name Manius was traditionally associated by Romans with the "Manes" or spirits of the dead</note>: because he had obtained the health of his children from the **spirits of the dead** <note>original: "dīs manibus"</note> at **Tarentum** <note>A site in the Campus Martius in Rome, named after the city in southern Italy, believed to have a volcanic entrance to the underworld</note>.

In this manner, therefore, some have handed down the tradition. But **Marcus Varro** <note>A famous Roman scholar (116–27 BCE) known for his vast writings on history and religion</note> says in the first book of *On the Origins of the Stage*: "When many portents occurred, and the wall and tower located between the **Colline** and **Esquiline gates** were struck by lightning from heaven, and because of this the **Fifteen Men** <note>original: "Quindecimviri." The college of priests responsible for the Sibylline Books</note> consulted the **Sibylline Books**, they reported that the **Tarentine Games** should be held for **Father Dis** and **Proserpina** in the **Campus Martius** for three nights, and that dark-colored victims should be sacrificed."

Thus far concerning the origin. However, the **Secular Games** <note>original: "Seculares [ludi]"</note> are named not because they are held every hundredth year, or even every hundred and tenth; for while both views have their supporters, the facts themselves thoroughly disprove them. Rather, it is more credible that they are called this because they generally occur once in a man's lifetime; just as common speech uses the phrase "after an age" <note>original: "post seculum." A *saeculum* or "age" was considered the longest span of a single human life, after which a new generation took over</note> to describe many other things that are rare. They are called **Tarentine** either from the place itself, which is called **Tarentus** for this reason, or because the altar of **Father Dis** (as we said above) was hidden in the earth there, or because the bank of the **Tiber** river was worn away at that spot. For this reason, the river was also called **Rumon** by the ancients, and named **serra** <note>The Saw</note> in sacred rites, because it "ruminated"—that is, it ate away and cut the banks. From this, **Maro** <note>The poet Virgil</note> says:

> Grazing the banks and cutting through the rich cultivated lands. <note>original: "Stringentem ripas / & pinguia culta secantem." (Aeneid 8.63)</note>

---

Furthermore, concerning the intervals of time in which these games are repeated, the evidence is ambiguous. Indeed, in the **Secular Hymn**, **Horace** declares that they take place every 110 years, which is confirmed by the records of the **Fifteen Men**, the edicts of the **Divine Augustus**, and finally the oracle of the **Sibyl** itself, which still exists. On the other hand, **Valerius Antias**, **Titus Livius** <note>Livy</note>, and likewise **Marcus Varro** are recorded as testifying that they return after a hundred years. As for what we read in **Herodian**—that they were accustomed to be restored after a span of three generations—I fear that the reading there may be corrupted, and for that reason, I think my interpretation from the Greek should be read with indulgence. Nor (if you count the dates) can you entirely determine either how great the intervals were in the past or how great they ought to be. Now, the first Secular Games were established...

<meta>This page continues the scholarly discussion regarding the timeline and history of the Secular Games. It moves from the mythical origins of Valesius to the recorded historical instances of the games, beginning with the first consul of the Roman Republic, P. Valerius Publicola, and moving through the accounts of historians like Zosimus and Censorinus up to the imperial restorations by Augustus and Claudius.</meta>

They believe that the **games** <note>original: "ludi"</note> were held after the expulsion of the kings by **P. Valerius Publicola** <note>Publius Valerius Publicola was one of the four aristocrats who led the overthrow of the Roman monarchy in 509 BCE</note>, who was the first consul. Indeed, when the citizens were suffering from a plague, he made public vows at this very altar we have mentioned and sacrificed a **black bull** to **Father Dis** <note>The Roman god of the underworld, also known as Pluto</note> for the men and a heifer of the same color to **Proserpina** for the women. He held games and **sacred banquets** <note>original: "lectisternia." A ritual where images of the gods were placed on couches and offered food as if they were guests at a feast</note> for three nights, and he then hid the altar by piling up earth as it had been before. It was inscribed with this title: *I, P. Valerius Publicola, consecrated this grain-field to Dis and Proserpina, and held games to Dis and Proserpina for the sake of the safety of the Roman people.*

**Zosimus** <note>A Greek historian of the 5th-6th century CE who wrote a history of Rome</note>, writing in **Greek**, records that the second games were held in the four hundred and fifty-second year after the founding of Rome <note>original: "secundo & quinquagesimo poſt Romam conditam... anno." Though the Latin literally says 52nd, the historical context usually refers to the 452nd year of the city</note>, following the warning of the **Sibylline verses**. Because the city was occupied by disease, men were chosen specifically to consult the books; following their response, the earth was again dug up at this very altar at the edge of the **Campus Martius**, the sacred rites and games were performed according to custom, and safety was once more restored to the Romans. However, this writer [Zosimus] named only one consul—perhaps due to a mistake in his manuscript—calling him **M. Publius Quintius**, whereas **Censorinus** <note>A 3rd-century CE Roman grammarian who wrote "On Birthdays," a key source for the Secular Games</note> records **M. Valerius** and **Spurius Verginius** as the consuls. 

The same author proves that the third and fourth games were held during the times of consuls whom he cites by name, using the authority of **Valerius Antias**, **Titus Livy**, **Varro**, **Piso**, **Gellius**, and **Hemina** <note>A list of prominent Roman historians and annalists</note>; he even summons the records of the **Fifteen Men for Performing Sacred Rites** <note>original: "Quindecemvirum sacris faciundis." The elite college of priests who guarded the Sibylline Books</note> as testimony. But because the slip of scribes is slippery when it comes to proper names and numerical notations, you will find these details corrupted—if I am not mistaken—in the commentary of Censorinus. 

Furthermore, the **Divine Augustus** restored these games after they had gradually fallen into disuse (as some believe) and been interrupted. As Livy says, he did so with great preparation during the consulship of **L. Censorinus** and **M. Manlius Puppius** <note>This refers to the famous celebration of 17 BCE</note>. **Ateius Capito** <note>A famous Roman jurist and expert on pontifical law</note> suggested the timing and duration of the cycle, while the **Sibylline books** dictated the ritual for the sacred rites. 

Furthermore, the emperor **Claudius** celebrated the sixth games in his own fourth consulship and that of **L. Vitellius**. It was as if these had been anticipated by Augustus and not reserved for the legitimate time; although Claudius himself mentions in his own histories that Augustus restored them long after they had been interrupted, having very carefully calculated the account of the years. For this reason, the tradition says that even the voice of the **herald** <note>A public official who announced the upcoming festival</note> was laughed at when he invited the people in the traditional manner...

It highlights the transition from Republic-era traditions to the grand imperial restorations by Augustus and the controversial timing of Claudius's games.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the timing of the Secular Games, moving from the Republican era to the Imperial period. The author is particularly concerned with the discrepancies between the celebrations held by the emperors Augustus, Claudius, and Domitian, using evidence from ancient poetry and numismatics to correct the errors of contemporary commentators.</meta>

...with the solemnity of the **games** <note>original: "ludi"</note>—games which no one living had ever seen before, nor would anyone see again—even though there were still survivors who had witnessed them in the past. It even happened that certain actors, who had been brought on stage long ago, were brought out once more. For we have learned that **Stephanio** <note>A famous Roman comic actor (pantomime) of the 1st century CE</note>, who was the first to institute dancing by request, danced at both of the Secular Games: namely, those of **Augustus** and those of **Claudius**. Between these two celebrations, no more than eighty-three years <note>original: "lxxxiii." Historians usually calculate 64 years between Augustus's games in 17 BCE and Claudius's in 47 CE; the "83" here may be a textual error in the source or a different chronological tradition</note> had passed, although Stephanio lived for a long time afterward.

**Domitian** held the seventh games during his fourteenth consulship and that of **L. Minutius Rufus**, calculating the schedule not from the year when Claudius had most recently held them, but from the year when Augustus had originally celebrated them. Finally, on various coins in the collection of our friend **Lorenzo de' Medici** <note>original: "Laurentium noſtrū Medicem." Referring to Lorenzo the Magnificent, the author's patron in Florence</note>, we have seen this very inscription <term>Stelographia<gloss>An inscription on a monument or coin</gloss></term>: **HE CELEBRATED THE SECULAR GAMES IN HIS FOURTEENTH CONSULSHIP** <note>original: "LVD. SAEC. FEC. COS. XIIII."</note>. We were already pointing out to our students that **Papinius** <note>The poet P. Papinius Statius (c. 45–96 CE)</note> shared this view in a short poem addressed to the city prefect **Gallicus**, titled *Soterias*, back when we were publicly interpreting that volume nine years ago. The words are as follows:

> Nor would the new ages have brought so great a crime upon the fates,
> Or the altar of restored **Tarentum** <note>A specific site in the Campus Martius in Rome where the Secular Games were celebrated; not to be confused with the city of Tarentum in Southern Italy</note> have sinned.

In this passage, our friend **Domitius** <note>Likely Domizio Calderini, a contemporary Renaissance scholar and rival of the author</note>, although he mentions the altar of Tarentum to my students, suspects nothing at all regarding these Secular Games. Indeed, in the works of **Martial** <note>A Roman poet famous for his epigrams</note>, how nearly he forgets his own literary learning, not to mention his own talent! We clearly refuted him (if I am not mistaken) both at that time and again a year later when we were explaining the **Fasti** <note>The Roman calendar and the poem by Ovid explaining it</note>. There is an epigram by Martial about a matron who, I believe, had been present at two games—those of Claudius and of Domitian—and in it is this very verse:

> Twice has my life witnessed the Roman **Tarentum**.

In this passage, this none-too-wise fellow <note>Referring again to the commentator Domitius</note> claims that the sacred rites at Tarentum were accustomed to be celebrated every thirty years—something that no one has ever heard of even by rumor. Furthermore, the same man, when handling those verses from the fourth book of the same poet, writes:

> Let him cherish the ages returning in a great cycle <note>original: "lustro." Usually a five-year period, but here used poetically for a longer era</note>,
> And the sacred rites which Romulean Tarentum holds.

He specifically targets the commentator Domitius for misinterpreting the frequency of the games and the location of the Tarentum altar.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the chronological account of the Secular Games, transitioning from the Early Empire to the later Roman emperors and the thousand-year anniversary of Rome. It then shifts from history to a description of the specific rituals and preparations involved in the festival.</meta>

[The author] even separates the latter from the former, and believes that just as the Secular Games were suggested in that instance, so too were those fabricated thirty-year rites <note>original: "tricenaria sacra." This refers to a shorter 30-year cycle occasionally proposed by ancient writers but dismissed by our author as "commenticia" or made-up</note> suggested in this one. Moreover, **Varro** mentions this **Tarentum** <note>original: "Tarenti." Not the city in southern Italy, but a specific site in the northern Campus Martius in Rome where the games were traditionally held</note> in his commentary on the Latin language, as does **Ovid**, once or even twice, in his *Fasti* <note>Ovid’s poetic treatment of the Roman calendar</note>.

But I return to where I left off. In the one hundred and tenth year after the games of **Domitian**, the Emperor **Septimius Severus** restored them as well—his very beautiful triumphal arch can still be seen in Rome today—along with his sons **Antoninus** <note>Commonly known as Caracalla</note> and **Geta**, during the consulship of **Chilo** and **Vibo**. **Herodian** <note>A Greek historian of the 3rd century CE</note> records that he saw these games himself, and **Dio** [Cassius] recorded for posterity that they took place in his own time.

**Zosimus** <note>A 5th-century Byzantine historian</note> denies that they were restored after Severus. This is because this cycle of one hundred and ten years, which had been accepted by authority, would have fallen during the third consulship of **Constantine**, the Christian prince, and **Licinius** (whom Constantine later defeated in war). However, we read in the chronicles of **Eusebius** and in the ninth book of **Eutropius's** history that during the reign of **Philip**—although he is believed to have been the first Christian emperor—the thousandth year of the city of **Rome** was celebrated with a massive array of games and spectacles. Countless beasts were killed in the circus, and theatrical games were celebrated in the **Campus Martius** for three days and three nights while the people kept watch. It is said that at these secular games, **Philip the Younger**—the one who is believed never to have laughed—noted his father **Philip the Elder** laughing somewhat too boisterously and turned his face away in disapproval.

It remains for us to briefly touch upon the ritual in detail. As the games approached, heralds were sent throughout all Italy to summon people to games "which they had never seen before and would never see again." Then, in the summer time, a few days before the spectacles were held, the <term>Quindecimviri<gloss>The "Fifteen Men for Performing Sacred Rites," a priestly college in charge of the Sibylline Books and the Secular Games</gloss></term>, sitting before a platform in the temples on the **Capitoline** and the **Palatine**, distributed purifying agents to the people. These consisted of torches <note>original: "teda"</note>, sulfur, and bitumen; however, slaves were not permitted to receive them. The people gathered not only in the places mentioned above, but also in the **Temple of Diana** on the **Aventine Hill**, and it was the custom for wheat, beans, and barley to be given to each person. Then, in the manner of the mysteries of **Ceres** <note>The Roman goddess of agriculture, whose festivals often involved night vigils</note>, vigils were held. When the festival day finally arrived...

<summary>This page concludes the historical timeline of the Secular Games, highlighting the celebrations under Septimius Severus and Philip the Arab, and begins a detailed description of the purification rituals and public distributions preceding the festival.</summary>
<keywords>Septimius Severus, Philip the Arab, Millennium of Rome, Zosimus, Quindecimviri, purification rites, Tarentum, Roman rituals</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the rituals of the Secular Games (Ludi Saeculares), moving from the historical timeline discussed on the previous page to the specific sequence of sacrifices, hymns, and public distributions that occurred over the three-day festival.</meta>

<sig>i i</sig>

For three days and three nights, devoted to the sacred rites, they occupied themselves mostly on the very banks of the **Tiber**. Sacrifices were undertaken to **Jupiter**, **Juno**, **Apollo**, **Latona**, and **Diana**; furthermore, to the **Fates** and those known as the **Ilithyiae** <note>Goddesses of childbirth</note>; then to **Ceres**, **Dis** <note>The god of the underworld</note>, and **Proserpina**. Thus, at the second hour of the first night, the Emperor himself, at three altars built upon the riverbank, would sacrifice as many lambs, and together with him the **Fifteen Men** <note>original: "Quindecemuiri," the college of priests in charge of the Sibylline Books and these games</note> would perform the immolation. Once the altars were stained with blood, they would burn the bodies of the victims. A stage was constructed in the manner of a theater; lights and fires were lit, and hymns—composed specifically for this purpose—were sung in harmony. In addition, the spectacles usually performed on such solemn occasions were presented. Those taking part in the celebrations were given a reward of wheat, beans, and barley—the same items we showed earlier were divided among the entire population.

In the morning, it was the custom to ascend the **Capitoline Hill**, perform the traditional rites there, and then gather in the theater for the games held in honor of **Apollo** and **Diana**. On the following day, at the hour prescribed by the oracle, it was the custom for noble matrons to gather on the Capitoline, offer prayers to the deity, attend the *lectisternia* <term>lectisternia: a ritual banquet offered to the gods, where their statues were placed on couches</term>, and sing hymns according to the ritual. Finally, on the third day, at the **Temple of Palatine Apollo**, twenty-seven boys <note>original: "ter noueni," or three times nine</note> wearing the *toga praetexta* <note>the purple-bordered toga worn by children and magistrates</note> and an equal number of girls—all of whom had both parents still living—sang hymns and songs of praise in both Greek and Latin. Through these, they commended their empire and the safety of the people to the immortal gods.

Should anyone wonder what kind of games or sacrifices were performed at that time, let them know that games of every variety were celebrated in all the theaters, and sacrifices were held in all the temples, by night as well as by day. There is no doubt about the theatrical performances and the chariot races. We recall that actors participated in the games of **Domitian**, and furthermore, that on the day of the circus games—in order to complete one hundred races more easily—we read that the number of laps for each race was reduced from seven to five. One detail regarding **Augustus** is particularly noteworthy: during these games, he forbade young people of both sexes from attending any nighttime spectacle unless accompanied by an older relative. Indeed, now that these matters have been set forth as they came to mind, that little verse by the poet **Ausonius** <note>A 4th-century Roman poet from Gaul</note> will no longer remain ambiguous.

It also highlights logistical changes made by Domitian to the chariot races and moral regulations imposed by Augustus.</summary>

<sig>ki</sig>

Three nights were celebrated with the Tarentine games <note>The *Ludi Tarentini* were the precursor to the Secular Games, held at a site called the Tarentum on the edge of the Campus Martius</note>. Now the reasoning found in **Horace** will no longer be obscure: why he mentioned the **Fates**, **Ilithyia** <note>The goddess of childbirth</note>, and **Diana** from the **Aventine Hill**; why also he recorded the prayers of the children, and the **Fifteen Men** <note>original: "Quindecéuiros," the priestly college in charge of the Sibylline Books</note>, and likewise the **Sibylline verses**. These verses are still found today in the works of the same **Zosimus** <note>A 5th-century Greek historian who recorded the oracle's text</note>, preserved in very ancient manuscripts without any accents <note>original: "citra ulla fastigia," referring to the lack of diacritical marks in early Greek uncial manuscripts</note>, just as we have intentionally presented them below.

But when the longest span of time comes for men,
Passing through a cycle of one hundred and ten years of life,
Remember, O Roman—even if you should utterly forget—
Remember all these things: to the immortal gods
Perform sacrifices in the plain by the vast water of the **Tiber**,
Where it is narrowest, when night comes over the earth
After the sun has hidden its light; there perform
Sacred rites to the **Fates**, the givers of all birth: lambs and black goats.
And after these, appease the **Ilithyiae**,
Who help in childbirth, with sacrifices as is lawful. And there, furthermore,
Let a pregnant black sow be sacrificed to the **Earth** <note>The Greek text here uses a corrupted form likely meaning Gaia or the Earth</note>.
But let all-white bulls be led to the altar of **Jupiter**
By day, not by night; for to the heavenly gods
The manner of sacrifice is by day. So also for her:
Sacrifice a heifer, the beautiful body of a cow, to **Hera** <note>The Roman Juno</note>;
Let it be received near her temple. And let **Phoebus Apollo**,
Who is also called the **Sun**, receive equal
Sacrifices, O son of Latona; and may you **Latins** sing
Paeans <note>Hymns of praise or triumph</note>; let the boys and girls hold the temple
Of the immortals; but let the girls hold their own chorus separately,
And separately from the boys, the male youth; but let their parents
All be living, those for whom the family is still flourishing on both sides <note>This refers to the *patrimi et matrimi*, children whose both parents were still alive, required for certain religious rituals</note>.
And let those women bound by the yokes of marriage on that day
Kneel by the famous altar of **Hera**, sitting there
To entreat the deity to grant deliverance from troubles
To men and to women, but especially to the female sex.
And let all bring from their homes such things as are proper to bring...

To provide propitiations to the gentle spirits and the blessed

So that they may be established for both women and men.
Then, let him remember to provide for the days,
And in the latest nights, at the god-befitting seats,
Let the vast gathering and their zeal be softened with laughter.
Keep these things always in mind within your heart,
And then all the Italian and Latin land
Shall always hold its neck under the yoke of your scepter.

Why should we not also try to render these into Latin? Even if it is not done elegantly, as it is an extemporaneous effort, it is nonetheless done faithfully; we have preserved, as far as possible, the color—or perhaps you would call it the ruggedness—of that unpolished but venerable antiquity. Therefore, let whoever wishes assign these verses to those still rustic times.

But when the longest span of human life,

Bring solemn sacred rites to the gods in the plain <note>The Campus Martius, specifically the area known as the Tarentum</note>
When dark night presses upon the lands and the sun is hidden.
Let a dark victim from the flock of lambs and goats
Fall to each of the powers of birth; then appease

Let the fertile **Earth** receive a pig and a black sow for herself;
But before the altar of **Jupiter**, sacrifice bulls with white bodies
In the daylight; for light is most pleasing to the gods above.
Let a heifer with a shining neck soak the temple of **Juno**;
And likewise let **Phoebus Apollo** be appeased,
Whom they also call the Sun, the offspring of **Latona**.

With both father and mother living <term>patrima et matrima</term> <note>Children whose parents were both still alive were considered ritually "whole" and were required for the performance of the Secular Hymn</note>.
And let the married women on their knees at the altar of **Juno**

Let the unblemished ones pray, and let them call the goddess into their vows. Furthermore, let every man and woman—though the woman especially—receive the **Februa** <note>original: "Februa"; these were instruments of purification or offerings used during Roman festivals, particularly associated with cleansing rites</note>. Then, from their homes, let all bring the first-fruits of life as offerings to the gentle gods (as is right) and to the blessed heavenly ones. Before that, however, be mindful to keep those things stored at home, which may soon be granted as need demands: to the men and to the assembly of women. Then, let the vast multitude fill the seats worthy of the gods through the days and likewise the nights; and let them mix their holy days with charming games. If all these things are stored within your steadfast mind, the entire land of Italy and all the Latins shall place their necks under your yoke and shall fear the scepter of the victor.

### Chapter 59.

That Ovid, and not Virgil, appears to be the author of the *Priapeia* <note>A collection of poems dedicated to the fertility god Priapus</note>. Then, that well-known little verse found in Martial, "Laugh, if you are wise, O girl, laugh," is undoubtedly by **Naso** <note>original: "Nasonis"; refers to Publius Ovidius Naso, known as Ovid</note>; but it seems to be cited from his epigrams rather than from either his *Art of Love* or his tragedy *Medea*. Finally, something new and marvelous regarding Ovid's *Medea* is added.

¶ The epigrams called the **Priapeia** are attributed by most to Virgil; among these, I believe, is **Servius** <note>A famous 4th-century commentator on Virgil</note>. However, in those books of **Seneca** <note>referring to Seneca the Elder’s works on rhetoric</note>—which had long been hidden and have only recently come to light (though they appeared in a state not entirely pure), and which contain the opinions, divisions, and rhetorical "colors" of orators and rhetoricians—I have discovered that they are spurious and belong not to Virgil, but to Ovid (unless, perhaps, we believe the only surviving copy to be faulty). Indeed, Ovid's own words in the second volume [of Seneca] are as follows:

"I remember a certain man of praetorian rank expressing a sentiment of this kind when he was declaiming a legal controversy concerning a woman who sued her husband for ill-treatment because she remained a virgin despite being married. Later, she sought a priesthood. 'We know,' he said, 'that well-known abstinence of husbands: who, even if they grant the first night to fearful virgins, nevertheless play in the neighboring regions.' **Scaurus** <note>Mamercus Aemilius Scaurus, a Roman orator and politician known for his wit</note>—who was not only a most eloquent man but also a most witty one, and who never allowed anyone’s foolishness to pass unpunished—immediately heard that Ovidian line..."

<summary>This page concludes the Latin verse translation of the Sibylline Oracle regarding the Secular Games and begins a scholarly debate on whether the Priapeia poems were written by Ovid instead of Virgil, citing Seneca the Elder.</summary>
<keywords>Sibylline Oracle, Secular Games, ritual purification, Ovid (Naso), Virgil, Priapeia, Seneca the Elder, Scaurus, Roman rhetoric, authorship debates</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological argument regarding the authorship of the Priapeia. The author, likely Angelo Poliziano, argues that the stylistic "lasciviousness" of these poems aligns more closely with Ovid (Naso) than with the modest Virgil.</meta>

While the foolish [organ] of the other place fears a wound <note>original: "Dum timet alterius uulnus inepta loci"; this is a line from a poem in the *Priapeia* collection</note>.
And he ceased; nor did he say more. Thus far Seneca <note>original: "Huc ufq; Seneca"; referring to a quotation from Seneca the Elder</note>. We know that this little verse is found in a Priapeic epigram, which begins:
->*I could have spoken to you obscurely.*<-
But both the style itself (unless I am mistaken) and even more so a certain genuine Ovidian playfulness <note>original: "lasciuia"</note> in those verses point to him. However, to what extent this authority is to be trusted should be left to the judgment of the readers. Just as I have not written this as a certainty, so I have not written it as a groundless thing. Certainly, such things suit Ovid, who is more obscene in his other works; conversely, they do not suit Virgil, who—because of his virginal modesty—was even called **Parthenias** <note>original: "Partheniã"; a Greek-derived nickname meaning "The Maiden," referring to Virgil’s legendary shyness and moral reputation</note>. The very title of that work is such that even for an unsworn and suspect witness, one would surely be ashamed not to believe it [to be Ovid's]. Indeed, it is admitted that **Naso** <note>Ovid's cognomen</note> wrote other epigrams as well, from which **Priscian** <note>A famous 6th-century Latin grammarian</note> cites this little verse:
->*Happily fierce, with the Cossus slain, he bore the grand spoils.*<-
And **Quintilian** <note>A celebrated Roman rhetorician</note> had surely read this from the same playful poet:
->*Why should I not call you, a fury, a fury?*<-
Nor would I believe that the phalaecian hendecasyllabic verse <note>original: "hendecasyllabum phalerium"; a specific meter of eleven syllables common in light or playful Roman poetry</note> cited by **Martial** was found anywhere else than in those epigrams:
->*Laugh, if you are wise, O girl, laugh.*<-
For as to what **Domitius** <note>Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century humanist commentator</note> thinks—that this refers to some precept from the *Art of Love*—that is refuted by the authority of **Martianus Capella**, who clearly recognizes it in his satire as a verse of Naso, and not as one of Martial's own. I have even heard some who believed it came from the tragedy **Medea** <note>A lost tragedy written by Ovid</note>, which **Tacitus** and **Quintilian** praise, and which—to my surprise—the author **Tertullian** claims was almost adorned in Virgilian robes: and we ourselves have noted this in our *Nutricia*! But this does not seem likely to me. For who has ever read phalaecian verses in either Greek or Latin tragedy? And what is there at all tragic about this verse, which recoils and shrinks entirely from that rhetorical gravity? Indeed, does not Martianus himself almost clearly deny that this is from a tragedy, if his words are faithfully recited and carefully weighed? For did he not, in his satire—because it was austere and censorious, and because he had chastised the playfulness of excessive tragic arrogance—as if striking back and inviting one to "smooth the brow" (as the expression goes), say: "Therefore, look back at the verse of the **Paelignian youth** <note>original: "Peligni... iuuenis"; Ovid was born in Sulmo, in the territory of the Paelignians</note>: and (unless you wrinkle your nose at tragedy)..."

<summary>The author argues that the *Priapeia* and other playful verses belong to Ovid (Naso) rather than Virgil, citing Virgil's reputation for modesty ("Parthenias") and the specific poetic meters used. He also challenges the idea that certain verses originated from Ovid's lost tragedy, *Medea*.</summary>
<keywords>Ovid, Virgil, Priapeia, Medea, authorship, Martial, Quintilian, Martianus Capella, Parthenias, Latin poetry, philology</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of Ovidian authorship (the Priapeia) to philological examinations of specific words and rituals, specifically the sacrificial term 'Agon' and its usage in poetry and rhetoric.</meta>

"Laugh (if you are wise), O girl, laugh." <note>original: "Ride(si sapis) o puella ride"; a quote from Martial’s Epigrams, 2.41.1</note>. 
Unless, he says, you are "wrinkled tragically," <note>original: "Ni tragicum... corrugaris"</note> that is, unless you knit your brow with tragic severity. This signifies the roughness caused by the sadness and squalor of wrinkles. Therefore, if that little verse itself belongs to tragedy, by what means does the poet here strive most of all to recall his satire from that tragic austerity? Truly, this joke is (as I judge) a "cold" one: *He will spit upon the wintry Alps with white snow.* <note>original: "Frigidus hibernas cana niue cõspuet alpes"; a line by Furius Bibaculus famously ridiculed by Horace for its bombastic style</note>. Now, if anyone doubts the accuracy of this reading, I would wish them to inspect the most ancient books of **Marcianus** <note>Likely referring to the humanist Michele Marullo or a specific manuscript tradition</note> from the public **Medicean library** in Florence <note>The Laurentian Library founded by the Medici family, a center for Renaissance scholarship</note>; for I believe his diligence will surely support our good faith.

### Chapter 60.

The word **Agon**: which the **Victimarius** <term>Victimarius: A minor official in Roman religion who performed the actual slaughter of the sacrificial animal</term> was accustomed to use: about which **Ovid** also writes in his *Fasti* <note>A poem explaining the Roman calendar and its rituals</note>.

> **Ovid in the first book of the Fasti**
> A tucked-up minister can be the cause of the name,
> By whose blow the victim falls for the heavenly gods.
> Who, about to stain the drawn knives with warm blood,
> Always asks "Should I act?" [**Agat-ne**] and acts only when commanded.

Regarding this rite of slaying the victim, and likewise the word which the priest's assistant used: I found mention made through these words in only one author, **Seneca** <note>Seneca the Elder, the rhetorician</note>, in the same book of oratory and rhetoric. "Then, with the greatest uproar of the students: 'And you, whoever you are, executioner, when you have raised the drawn axe, before you strike, look back at the father.'" **Asinius Pollio** <note>A Roman statesman, orator, and historian known for his sharp wit</note> mocked this beautifully. He says: "Let the son stretch forth his neck. Let the executioner stretch forth his hand, then look back at the father and say: **'Agon?'** [Shall I act?]" Which is what usually happens with sacrificial victims! But even with the joke set aside, he spoke a most true thing: that the executioner could not come except at that time when the father could no longer be moved by prayer. Thus says Seneca. From which it is gathered that the word which the **Victimarius** used was indeed **Agon**: that is, "Should I act?" [**ago ne**]. Wherefore Ovid says beautifully:

> Always asks "Should I act?" [**Agatne**] and acts only when commanded.

<vocab>Victimarius, Agon, Ovidius, Seneca, Asinius Pollio, Fasti, sacrificium, Agonale, hostia</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into ancient texts, moving from the definition of the sacrificial term 'Agon' to correcting errors in the manuscripts of Pliny the Elder and Macrobius regarding the properties of wine and hemlock.</meta>

### Chapter 61.
## An error in the manuscripts of Pliny regarding hemlock and wine; and a mistake in Macrobius where Aconite is used instead of Hemlock.

The words from the letter of **Androcydes** <note>A physician to Alexander the Great</note> to **Alexander the Great**—a man famous for his wisdom—are found incorrectly recorded in these Plinian manuscripts in this manner: *King, when you are about to drink wine, remember that you drink the blood of the earth. Just as hemlock is poison to man, so too is wine.* This is certainly a trivial sentiment, too forced and strained; I would even add it is insipid and false to say that wine is poison to man just as hemlock is. 

But indeed, in that most ancient Plinian manuscript of the **Medicean family** <note>The Laurentian Library collection in Florence</note>, which we have already cited often, you will find it written thus: *Hemlock is poison to man, and wine is poison to hemlock.* <note>original: "Cicuta homini uenenū est/ cicutae uinū"</note> For just as hemlock kills a man, so the potency of hemlock is extinguished by the drinking of pure wine. From this, Androcydes strives to persuade Alexander that he should use wine more sparingly, as it is a most powerful substance, being "the poison of poison." For this reason, he had taught in a similar sense that it was "the blood of the earth." Concerning these words, **Pliny** concludes: *Nothing is more useful for the strength of the body, nor more ruinous to pleasures, if moderation be lacking.* 

Similarly, the same author in his twenty-fifth book, speaking of hemlock as deadly to man, says: *The warming nature of wine is a remedy before the poison reaches the vital organs.* **Dioscorides** <note>An ancient Greek physician and pharmacologist</note> recorded the same thing. The **Divine Ambrose** <note>Saint Ambrose of Milan</note>, therefore, in the book he composed on the utility of fasting, said: *Indeed, the power of wine is greater than that of poison. Finally, poison is excluded by wine, not wine by poison.* Moreover, **Plutarch** in his *Symposiacs* <note>Also known as "Table Talk," a collection of dinner-table discussions</note> says almost the same things as Pliny regarding hemlock. 

Then, **Macrobius** in his *Saturnalia* attempted to express that passage—just as he did many others—almost word-for-word. Although in all the Macrobian manuscripts I have seen thus far, "**Aconite**" <note>A highly toxic plant, also known as monkshood</note> is found instead of "**hemlock**" <note>original: "cicura" (likely a typo for cicuta)</note>; this is even the case in the very ancient manuscript belonging to **Bernardo Michelozzi**, a well-lettered man and a friend of ours. But indeed, I believe that to be a fault of the copies; for I cannot be persuaded that Macrobius was so unlearned that he would translate the Greek **Conion** <note>original: "Conion"; from the Greek κώνειον (kṓneion), meaning hemlock</note> as "Aconite." I would more readily believe he used the Greek word, just as Ambrose also did...

<summary>This page identifies and corrects a philological error in the manuscripts of Pliny and Macrobius, clarifying that ancient wisdom held wine to be an antidote to hemlock poison rather than a poison itself. The author uses a "most ancient" Medicean manuscript to restore the original meaning of a warning given to Alexander the Great.</summary>

<keywords>Pliny the Elder, Macrobius, Hemlock, Wine, Androcydes, Alexander the Great, Medicean Library, Philology, Medical Botany, Saint Ambrose, Plutarch</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the properties of hemlock before transitioning into a philological analysis of a passage in Cicero’s "Ad Herennium" concerning memory techniques and anatomy.</meta>

...in the *Hexahemeron* <note>A treatise on the six days of creation</note>, he said that **Rufinus** <note>Rufinus of Aquileia, a 4th-century translator</note> translated the word "hemlock" from **Basil’s** <note>St. Basil the Great</note> book of the same argument and title. However, it was a slippery slip indeed to use "Aconite" <note>A different genus of poisonous plant, Wolfsbane</note> instead of "Hemlock."

# Chapter 62.
## On what "Rams' Testicles" are, and which one is the "Medical Finger" in the works of Cicero.

**Cicero** <note>The text refers to him as Tullius</note>, in the third book of *To Herennius*, when treating the art of memory, describes a certain mental image in this way: *And we shall place the defendant at his bed: in his right hand a cup, in his left hand tablets, holding rams' testicles with his medical finger.* At this passage, I see that even some scholars of no small reputation hesitate as to what we are reading: "holding rams' testicles with his medical finger." The "Medical" finger is the name of one of the five fingers; it is, I believe, the one next to the little finger <note>The ring finger</note>. **Porphyrio**, commenting on that line in **Horace’s** final sermon:

> Who, if by chance anything were hidden,
> Would point it out with his index finger.

He says this: "This is because each individual finger was called by a certain name; and these are the names: **Thumb** (*Pollex*), **Index**, **Infamous** (*Famosus* <note>The middle finger, often called 'infamous' due to its use in obscene gestures</note>), **Medical** (*Medicus*), and **Little** (*Minimus*)." Some of the Greeks—of the more recent era, I believe—write the names of the fingers in the Roman fashion as follows: *anticheir* (thumb), *deiktikos* (pointer), *iatros* (healer/medical), *daktyliotes* (ring-bearer), and *otites* (ear-finger). This would be to say: Thumb, Index, Medical, Ring finger, and Ear finger <note>The little finger was used to clean the ear</note>. I neither affirm nor refute this. It must only be noted that the "Medical" finger is said by some to be the middle or "infamous" one, rather than the one next to the little finger. Among the Greeks themselves, however, they read these names: *anticheir* (thumb), *lichanos* (forefinger), *sphakelos* (middle), *epibates* (ring-finger), and *konops* (little finger). 

As for the "rams' testicles," you read in the entry for *Scortes* in the abridgment of **Festus**: *Scortes* are the skins of rams' testicles, and they are named after those same skins. Thus says Festus. But **Pedianus** <note>Asconius Pedianus, a Roman commentator</note> says that *sportas*, *sportulas*, and *sportellas* <note>various types of baskets</note> were receptacles for money, as were sacks, small bags, purses, leather pouches (*scorreas*), and knapsacks. Thus, there can be no doubt that Cicero intended for us to imagine leather money-purses <note>original: "testiculos arietinos"</note> suspended from that finger which is next to the little finger.

<summary>This page identifies the "medical finger" as the ring finger and explains that Cicero's reference to "rams' testicles" is a metaphor for leather money-purses used in mnemonic visualization.</summary>
<keywords>Cicero, Ad Herennium, medical finger, anatomy, mnemonics, Festus, Greek finger names, Rams' testicles, money purses, Porphyrio</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Medical Finger" and rams' testicles, concluding a philological investigation into a passage from Cicero’s "To Herennius" before moving on to discrepancies in Varro’s citations of Terence and the nature of "limping" poetic meters.</meta>

Scorreas <note>original: *Scorreas*</note>—that is, leather pouches made from the skin of rams' testicles.

### Chapter 63.

That the words of Terence are cited differently by Varro than they are found in the manuscripts or as Donatus recognized them.

**Marcus Varro**, in his books *On the Latin Language*, says this: "In **Terence**, he whacks <note>original: *scortatur*, from *scortum* (hide/prostitute), meaning to frequent prostitutes</note>, drinks, reeks, and I bring perfumes." "To whack" means to visit a prostitute frequently. Thus he says, and then continues with other remarks to that effect. However, all the manuscripts of Terence, without exception, do not have "he whacks" as Varro read it, but "he buys provisions" <note>original: *obsonat*</note>. Moreover, the passage is in the *Adelphoe* <note>"The Brothers," a play by Terence</note>: *He buys provisions, drinks, reeks, and I bring perfumes.* This reading is also recognized by **Donatus** <note>A famous 4th-century grammarian and commentator</note>. What shall we say then? Is there a mistake in all the manuscripts of Terence, or (which I would scarcely dare to believe) did the memory of Varro himself waver? Varro was called the "most prolific writer" <note>original Greek: *polygraphōtaton*</note> by **Cicero**, just as **Aulus Gellius** caught Cicero himself in a similar slip. Let us admit that even the greatest minds are sometimes hindered by carelessness, while diligence is beneficial everywhere.

### Chapter 64.

An explanation of the verses of **Ausonius**, and also of **Ovid**, regarding the Choliambic or Scazon meter.

These are the iambic trimeter verses of the poet **Ausonius**:
To whom shall I yoke, from the turnings of the millstones,
Those who turn the rocks with mechanical weight,
Three-legged nags, their backs broken by the whip?
What should we think he means here? Indeed, as I believe, he refers to those verses which are called **Choliambs** or **Scazons**—as if you were to say "limping" verses <note>from the Greek *chōlos* (lame) and *skazōn* (limping)</note>. Such are found at the beginning of the work of **Persius**, and likewise in **Martial**, who cites the term by name:
*Go, my Scazon, and meet my friend Apollinaris.*
For a Scazon is an iambic trimeter, also consisting of six feet:

<summary>This page explores philological errors in Marcus Varro's citations of Terence and introduces the technical definitions of "limping" (Scazon) poetic meters in Latin literature.</summary>
<keywords>Marcus Varro, Terence, Donatus, Cicero, Aulus Gellius, philology, textual criticism, Ausonius, Scazon, Choliambus, poetic meter, iambic trimeter</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of poetic meters, specifically focusing on the structure and history of the "limping" or Choliambic meter, building on the previous mention of Ausonius's verses.</meta>

...just like the standard iambic! But it differs in this one respect, as **Hephaestion** <note>A 2nd-century Greek grammarian of Alexandria known for his manual on poetic meters</note> also writes: that the standard meter occupies the final position with an iamb or a pyrrhic <note>A metrical "foot" consisting of two short syllables</note>. But in the third-to-last position, it accepts all feet of which that meter is capable, being indifferent regarding the quantities of the syllables. However, this "limping" iambic <note>Also known as the *choliambic* or *scazon*</note> requires a spondaic <note>A foot consisting of two long syllables</note> or a completely trochaic <note>A foot consisting of one long and one short syllable</note> ending. The second-to-last foot, moreover, is not only disyllabic, but clearly an iamb or a spondee. 

Truly, as for what **Hephaestion** says—that a spondee is received in the fifth seat of the choliambic—I personally do not remember ever finding this among Latin authors. But he himself cites this Greek verse:

-> "Dragging it to the tip, cooling it like a sausage." <note>original Greek: *ep’ akron helkōn hōsper allanta psychōn*</note> <-

And **Quintilianus Aristides** <note>A Greek author on music from the 3rd century AD</note>, a Greek author, speaking about the iambic trimeter in his book *On Music*, says it becomes "limping" when it receives a spondee at the very end. Finally, **Ovid** also writes thus about both types of iambic in the first book of the *Remedies for Love*:

> -> Let the iambic be drawn like a sword against hostile enemies, <-
> -> Whether it be swift at the end, or whether it drags its foot. <-

Both types of meter are made for insults and license: they are fierce and bloodthirsty. For this reason, **Ovid** says they should even be "drawn" like weapons against hostile enemies. 

**Dionysius of Halicarnassus** <note>A Greek historian and teacher of rhetoric in Rome during the reign of Augustus</note>, likewise, in his book *On Interpretation* <note>Referring to his work *On the Arrangement of Words*</note>, says that Choliambics are a vehement or sharp meter. He says that soon after **Hipponax** <note>A 6th-century BC Greek poet known for his sharp, satirical wit</note>, when he wished to provoke his enemies with insults, he broke the meter on purpose and made it limping and out of rhythm instead of standard, which is suited to bitterness and abuse. For just as a disjointed figure prepares the way for bitterness, so (as he says) does a disjointed composition. 

Therefore, **Ausonius** <note>A 4th-century Roman poet and statesman</note> calls these Choliambics "Nags" <note>original: *Caballos*, meaning work-horses or old nags</note>, drawing a metaphor from a chariot or carriage to which nags are hitched; and to show they are slow, he says they are taken from the windings of millstones <note>Likening the repetitive, "dragging" rhythm to horses walking in circles to turn a heavy mill</note>. Furthermore, he called them "backs broken by the whip," and then "three-footed," because in Greek they are called *trimeters* <note>A meter consisting of three metrical units</note>, although they have six feet; for in iambic verse, every two feet are seen as a single unit <note>In iambic meter, the unit of measurement is the *dipody*, or double-foot</note>. He said "those who roll stones with mechanical weight" either to describe the millstones he had indicated they turn, or to show they are violent and fierce allegorically, as if throwing stones...

<summary>This page provides a technical and historical analysis of the Choliambic (or "limping") meter, citing Greek and Roman authorities like Hephaestion, Ovid, and Dionysius of Halicarnassus to explain its aggressive and satirical nature.</summary>
<keywords>choliambic meter, scazon, iambic trimeter, Hephaestion, Ovid, Dionysius of Halicarnassus, Hipponax, Ausonius, poetic feet, satire</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of poetic meters, specifically focusing on the "limping" iambic (scazon) and its use in insults and satire, before transitioning into an analysis of classical sculpture and gestures described in poetry.</meta>

...they twist and roll great weights against the enemy. Likewise, concerning both types of trimeter <note>A verse line consisting of three metrical units, or "measures"</note>, the same **Ausonius** <note>A 4th-century Roman poet and teacher</note> says elsewhere in a short Greek verse:

-> "The 'limping-footed' trimeter, which both limps and does not limp." <note>original Greek: *heilipodēn skazonta kai ou skazonta trimetron*</note> <-

Which you might turn into Latin as: "The bandy-legged trimeter, both limping and not limping." Concerning these same "limping" verses, the younger **Pliny** <note>A lawyer and author in ancient Rome, famous for his letters</note> writes thus in a certain letter from his fifth book: "Therefore, either break off your delays, or take care lest those very same little books—which my hendecasyllables <note>A light, eleven-syllable meter often used for playful or erotic poetry</note> could not entice from you with flatteries—are extorted from you by the insults of 'limping' verses."

# Chapter 65.
## What the "peacemaker" pose is in statues, and that Statius refers to it in the first book of the *Silvae*.

**Statius** <note>A Roman poet of the 1st century AD</note> writes in his first *Little Forest* [Silva], which was written about the Great Horse <note>Referring to the massive equestrian statue of the Emperor Domitian in the Roman Forum</note>:
> "It is a joy to behold the face,
> Where signs of war are mixed with the bearing of a calm peace."

Then the same author says:
> "The right hand forbids battles."

**Domitius** <note>Domizio Calderini, a 15th-century Renaissance scholar and commentator on classical texts</note> explains this passage as follows: "'The right hand forbids battles'—this means either he is unarmed in the statue, or that the Temple of Peace is located to his right." This interpretation is not pleasing. However, if anyone likes this sense—that the right hand forbids battles because it is unarmed, or likewise what is handed down regarding the Temple of Peace itself—even though it does not please its own author, I shall certainly not take it badly if my own opinion is not approved by him.

For I believe that a specific pose is being noted: the one called the **peacemaker** <note>term: *pacificator*</note> in statues. Regarding this, we read the following in **Quintilian** <note>A famous Roman educator and rhetorician</note>: "There is also that posture which is common in statues of a 'peacemaker': with the head tilted toward the right shoulder, and the arm extended from the ear, he stretches out his hand with the thumb turned down <note>original: *infesto pollice*. While *infesto* usually means 'hostile,' in the context of Roman oratorical gesture, it refers to a specific position of the thumb used to command attention or silence</note>. This pose is especially pleasing to those who boast of speaking with an uplifted hand." Thus far Quintilian.

Moreover, there still exists (unless I am mistaken) a statue in Rome in that "peacemaker" pose, which is visited as one of the primary sights of the city in front of the **Lateran Church** <note>The author refers to the famous bronze equestrian statue of Marcus Aurelius, which stood near the Lateran Palace until the mid-16th century, when it was moved to the Capitoline Hill. In the Middle Ages, it was mistakenly believed to represent the Emperor Constantine</note>.

# Chapter 66.
## A passage in Plautus’s *Mostellaria*, and likewise another in the *Braggart Soldier*, rescued from corruption.

In the *Mostellaria* <note>A comedy by the Roman playwright Plautus, often called *The Haunted House*</note> of **Plautus**, there is a passage written erroneously in most manuscripts in roughly this manner: "Do you see the ornaments on the doors? I see them. Look with what skill they sleep. They sleep. Indeed, he wanted to say what was fitting." <note>original Latin: *Viden ornamenta in foribus: uideo. Specta qua arte dormiunt. Dormiunt. Ille quidem ut conuenit uolui dicere*</note> But since no sense can be drawn from these words, and a witty remark of Plautus is perishing through the fault of the scribes, I shall (I believe) perform a worthwhile service if I restore the text to its healthy state from a Plautine manuscript cited by us before. The text is actually exactly this: "Do you see the **joints** in the doors? I see them. Look with what skill they 'blink.' They 'blink'? I meant to say 'blink' in the sense of how they fit together." <note>The author is correcting *ornamenta* (ornaments) to *coagmenta* (joints/seams) and *dormiunt* (they sleep) to *conniuent* (they blink or close together)</note> For because they say those who are drowsy "blink" <note>original Latin: *conniuere*</note>, as in this little verse read in the works of **Cinna** <note>Helvius Cinna, a Roman poet and contemporary of Catullus</note>:

-> "Now the pupil, heavy with immense sleep, begins to blink." <note>original Latin: *Iam grauis ingenti conniuere pupula somno*</note> <-

Therefore, when the slave **Tranio** <note>The clever lead character of the *Mostellaria*</note> was showing that the joints in the doors were not at all loose—for that is what "joints" <note>original: *coagmenta*</note> are—he says jokingly that they "sleep" first, and then that they "blink," using a metaphor drawn from the eyes of those who are falling asleep.

In a similar way, I think another passage from Plautus’s *Braggart Soldier* <note>Original title: *Miles Gloriosus*</note> should be corrected, which is erroneously read as follows: "It is a wonder that you live on oil, when wheat is so cheap. Why so? Because you are dim-sighted." <note>original Latin: *Mirus oleo uictitare te tam uili tritico. Quid iam? quia lusciosus*</note> Since no meaning can be elicited from this, I believe it should be read thus: "It is a wonder that you live on **darnel**, when wheat is so cheap." <note>original Latin: *Mirust lolio uictitare te*</note> Indeed, you will find this same *mirust* everywhere in Plautus as a contraction for *mirum est* ("it is a wonder"). Furthermore, **Ovid** says in the first book of the *Fasti* <note>A poetic calendar of Roman religious festivals</note> that the eyes are damaged by darnel:

-> "And may the fields be free of darnel that ruins the eyes." <note>original Latin: *Et careant loliis oculos uitiantibus agri*</note> <-

**Fulgentius** <note>A Latin grammarian of the late 5th or early 6th century</note>, a grammarian not to be despised, also notes this. Therefore, since the slave **Sceledrus** wants to suggest that his fellow slave **Palaestrio** is dim-sighted and has damaged eyes, he says it is a wonder that he lives on darnel <note>a weed, also known as tares, which was anciently believed to cause blindness or dizziness if consumed in bread</note> when wheat itself is so cheap. He mockingly asserts that he concludes the man lives on darnel <margin>What dim-sighted people are.</margin> from the fact that he is "dim-sighted" <term>lusciosus: a term for someone who suffers from a specific vision impairment</term>. Furthermore, those are called "dim-sighted" <note>original: *lusciosi* or *lusciciti*</note> who have such a defect of the eyes that they see more clearly in the evening than at midday.

<summary>This page features a philological correction of two corrupted passages in the plays of Plautus, using botanical and medical lore to restore the original puns regarding door joints and dim-sightedness.</summary>
<keywords>Plautus, philology, Mostellaria, Miles Gloriosus, textual criticism, darnel, lolium, Cinna, Ovid, Fulgentius, ancient medicine, Latin comedy</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from a discussion of the playwright Plautus and the restoration of a specific comedic passage to a broader investigation of classical symbols and the correction of errors in the works of Juvenal, Callimachus, and Catullus.</meta>

### Chapter 67.

->The Crow was seen among the ancients as a symbol of concord; it was not the Stork, as everyone assumes when reading Juvenal.<-

It is now taken as an established fact that it is the **Stork** which represented the image of concord among the ancients; and so everyone interprets that little verse of **Juvenal** <note>Decimus Iunius Iuvenalis, a Roman poet active in the late 1st and early 2nd centuries AD, famous for his biting *Satires*</note>:

> "And Concord, which rattles when its nest is greeted." <note>original Latin: *Quaeque salutato crepitat Concordia nido*. This refers to the clattering sound storks make with their bills.</note>

However, they offer no testimony at all for this that is supported by the weight of authority. I, on the other hand, accept the **Crow** <term>Cornix: The hooded crow or similar corvid, often associated with fidelity in antiquity</term> instead, led by the testimony of the Greek writer **Aelian** <note>Claudius Aelianus, a Roman author and teacher of rhetoric who wrote in Greek; his work *On the Nature of Animals* is a collection of anecdotes about animal behavior</note>, who in his third book on animals writes roughly as follows regarding this bird: "Crows," he says, "are most faithful to one another; once they have entered into a partnership, they love each other with the greatest devotion. Nor would anyone see these kinds of animals mixing together licentiously or at random. Furthermore, those who are skilled in such matters often say this: if one is killed, the mate lives out its life in widowhood." Moreover, I also hear that the ancients, during weddings, would invoke the Crow after the hymn to **Hymen** <note>Hymenaeus, the Greek god of marriage ceremonies</note>, and they were accustomed to give it as a sign of concord to those who had come together for the sake of seeking children. Furthermore, those who observe the perches and flights of birds say that for those seeking auguries, the obedience of only a single Crow is an unfavorable omen. Thus says Aelian. But also, on two gold coins of **Faustina Augusta** <note>The name used for the wives of Roman Emperors, here likely referring to Faustina the Elder or her daughter Faustina the Younger</note>, **Lorenzo de' Medici** <note>The ruler of Florence and a massive patron of Renaissance scholarship, known as 'the Magnificent'</note> recently showed me a very clear little image with the actual title of **Concordia**.

### Chapter 68.

->Verses from Callimachus regarding the hair of Queen Berenice; and from there, a mistake removed from a verse of Catullus.<-

The noble poet **Catullus** <note>Gaius Valerius Catullus, an influential Roman poet of the 1st century BC</note> translated the elegy of **Callimachus** <note>Callimachus of Cyrene, a celebrated Greek scholar and poet at the Library of Alexandria</note> regarding the hair of **Berenice** <note>Berenice II of Egypt; legend says she cut off her hair as a votive offering, and it was said to have been transformed into the constellation Coma Berenices</note> received among the stars with wonderful elegance into the Latin language; however, many parts in it are corrupted, full of errors, and written recklessly through the ignorance of the scribes. But the argument itself is partly found in...

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philological investigation into classical poetry, specifically moving from a discussion of Juvenal and Aelian to the textual restoration of Callimachus and Catullus, focusing on how Greek forms should be preserved in Latin translations.</meta>

You may find the Latin author **Hyginus** <note>Gaius Julius Hyginus, a scholar and friend of Ovid, attributed with works on astronomy and mythology</note> partly in **Theon** and also in **Achilles**, the Greek commentators on **Aratus** <note>Aratus was a Greek poet famous for the *Phaenomena*, a poem on astronomy</note>. Truly, as far as I have discovered, the Greek elegy of **Callimachus** <note>One of the most influential Greek poets of the Hellenistic age, known for his refined and scholarly style</note> itself is no longer extant for us! Rather, it has perished through the passage of time. However, in the works of Theon, the interpreter of that same Aratus, I find the beginning of it to be as follows: 

<note>original Greek: Ἤ με κόνων ἔβλεψεν ἐν ἠέρι τὸν Βερενίκης βόστρυχον. ἡ κείνη πᾶσιν ἔθηκε θεοῖσ.</note> "Conon saw me, the Lock of Berenice, in the sky; she dedicated it to all the gods." <note>This refers to the *Coma Berenices*, an elegy celebrating the constellation named after Queen Berenice II of Egypt’s hair</note>

Furthermore, in the commentaries on **Apollonius of Rhodes** <note>Author of the *Argonautica*</note>—collected from Lucilius Tarrheus, Sophocleus, and Theon—where the **Chalybes** <term>Chalybes: An ancient people of the Black Sea region, legendary for their invention of iron-working</term> are discussed in the second book, one may find this: 

<note>original Greek: μέμνηται αὐτῶν καὶ καλλίμαχοσ χαλύβων ὡσ ἀπόλοιτο γένοσ τηλόθεν ἀντέλλοντα κακὸν φυτὸν οἰιμιν ἔφησαν.</note> "Callimachus also mentions the Chalybes, saying: 'May the race of the Chalybes perish, for they brought forth an evil plant [iron] rising from afar.'"

Based on this, who would doubt that the verse should be read as: "O Jupiter, may the whole race of the **Chalybes** perish"? This uses the Greek genitive form *Chalybōn*. Some read this ignorantly as "of the Telorum"; the most common manuscripts retain "of the heavenly ones" <note>original Latin: *caelitum*</note>, which even goes against the requirements of the poetic meter. But I know that this observation of ours has long since been made public; however, that it originated from us, he who published it knows well—and that most learned man from everywhere, **Pico** <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, the famous Renaissance philosopher and humanist</note>, would also freely admit it.

### Chapter 69.

That "**Oarion**" is the correct reading in **Catullus**; whereas those who violate good books read it as "**Aorion**."

->In that same elegy of Catullus translated from Callimachus, "Oarion" is read in place of what<-
->would be "Orion." Since some have already begun to rashly attack this word,<-

which is still whole and unviolated, I must stand against this sinister audacity of the unlearned with "every foot" (as the saying goes) <note>a Latin idiom meaning to use all one's resources or strength</note>; or rather, by the authority of Callimachus himself, who in his extant hymn to **Diana** says:

<note>original Greek: οὐδὲ γὰρ ὦτοσ οὐδὲ μεν ὠαρίων ἀγαθὸν γάμον ἐμνηστεύσαν.</note> "For neither Otus nor indeed Oarion sought a good marriage."

But also **Nicander** in his book *Theriaca* writes similarly: <note>original Greek: βοιωτῶ τεύχουσα κακὸν μόρον ὠαρίωνι.</note> "Fashioning an evil fate for the Boeotian Oarion." And **Pindar** in the *Isthmian Odes*: <note>original Greek: οὐ γαρ φύσιν ὠαριωνείαν ἔλαχεν.</note> "For he did not receive the nature of Oarion." And elsewhere: <note>original Greek: ὀρειᾶν πελειάδων μὴ τηλόθι ὀαρίωνα νείσθαι.</note> "That Oarion does not walk far from the mountain Pleiades." For this reason, **Eustathius** <note>A 12th-century Byzantine scholar and archbishop known for his massive commentaries on Homer</note> thinks in his commentary on the fifth book of the *Odyssey* that he was named from the word <note>original Greek: ὀαρίζειν</note> *oarizein* [to converse or dally]. Therefore, the true reading is not "Aorion," but "**Oarion**."

### Chapter 70.

Of what things the cap and daggers are a symbol on the coin of **Brutus**.

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the author's previous focus on Greek fragments and their Latin translations to numismatics (the study of coins) and textual criticism of Virgil’s *Aeneid*. The author continues to use ancient sources and physical evidence, such as old manuscripts and coins, to correct common errors in literature.</meta>

<sig>i l</sig>

If anyone should find a coin of Brutus <note>Marcus Junius Brutus, the most famous of Julius Caesar's assassins</note> depicting his own hidden image, and furthermore a cap <note>The *pileus*, a felt cap given to emancipated slaves, symbolizing liberty</note> and two small daggers, let him not labor too long in explaining the reason and the cause: let him read the forty-seventh book of the histories of Dio <note>Dio Cassius, a Roman historian who wrote in Greek; his work is a major source for the late Republic</note>. For from these, he says, Brutus signified that the fatherland had been liberated by himself and Cassius. Indeed, it was more common and manifest that the cap was traditionally worn by those who had attained liberty, as is confirmed by many authorities.

### Chapter lxxi.

Certain passages are still commonly read in the works of Maro <note>Publius Vergilius Maro, better known as Virgil</note> which go against the rules and the logic of meter.

I am ashamed to report it, as it is so manifest; yet, so far as I know, no one until we pointed it out has noticed the error that has grown into the manuscripts of Virgil's *Aeneid*, in the eighth book:

> <note>original: "Quod fieri ferro / liquido ue poteſtur electro."</note> Which can be made of iron or liquid amber <note>electrum: a term that could refer to amber or a naturally occurring alloy of gold and silver</note>.

However, in that volume which is kept in the innermost part of the Vatican Library—miraculously old and written in large characters <note>Referring to "uncial" or "capital" script, typical of late antique prestige manuscripts</note>—you will not find *potestur* <note>an archaic passive form of "to be able"</note> but rather the more common word *potest* ("it is possible"). That this is the truer reading is proven by the fact that the first syllable of *electrum* is always placed as long by both the Greeks and by our own poets. Nor do the interpreters say anything regarding the word *potestur*, which is otherwise new and unusual. Nevertheless, it does not escape me that the ancients also produced this form, as did Pacuvius <note>Marcus Pacuvius, an early Roman tragic poet</note> in his *Chryses*, and likewise Lucretius <note>Author of *De Rerum Natura*</note>; just as *poterat* was read by Nonius Marcellus <note>A 4th-century grammarian known for preserving fragments of lost Latin works</note> in the annals of Caecilius. But another passage suffers similarly from a related fault in the same poet:

> <note>original: "Sceptra Palatini / ſedemq; petiuit Euandri."</note> The scepters of the Palatine and the seat of Evander he sought.

In that same old volume, one finds written not the three-syllable *petivit* ("he sought"), but the two-syllable *petit*. The force of the meter compels this; otherwise, the first syllable of *Evandri* would be short, contrary to all logic or custom. Truly, in both verses, there is a spondee <term>spondee: a metrical foot consisting of two long syllables</term> in the fifth position. In the word *petit*, however, that last syllable is rightly lengthened, either because it is at the end of a word or because it has merged from two syllables into one. Martianus Capella <note>A late antique author of an influential encyclopedia on the liberal arts</note> teaches that a syllable is common <term>common syllable: in poetry, a syllable that can be treated as either long or short</term> whenever it ends in the letter "T," just as we see in that line of Virgil:

> <note>original: "Nam tibi Thymbre caput Euandrius abſtulit enſis."</note> For the sword of Evander took your head, O Thymber.

<summary>The author explains the symbolism of Brutus's "assassination" coins and then argues for specific textual corrections in Virgil's Aeneid based on a very old manuscript in the Vatican Library.</summary>
<keywords>Brutus, Dio Cassius, Virgil, Aeneid, Vatican Library, paleography, Latin meter, electrum, Evander, philology</keywords>

# Chapter lxxii.
## Regarding the philyra and ribboned crowns.

**I** see that the nature of the *philyra* <note>The inner bark or "bast" of the linden tree, used in antiquity for making ribbons and writing material</note> is unknown to many, and is even incorrectly understood by several interpreters. For Horace uses this word in the first book of his *Odes*:
> Garlands woven with *philyra* displease me. <note>original: "Displicent nexæ philyra coronę." — Horace, Odes 1.38</note>

And likewise Ovid in the fifth book of the *Fasti*:
> The drunken guest, his hair bound with *philyra*, 
> Dances: <note>original: "Ebrius incinctus philyra conuiua capillos / Saltat" — Ovid, Fasti 5.337</note>

From which it is clear that its use among the ancients in making garlands was very famous. Now, first of all, if you compare what Theophrastus <note>A Greek philosopher and the "father of botany"</note> writes about the *philyra* in the third book of *On Plants*, or what Pliny relates in the sixteenth book of his *Natural History*, you will easily recognize that what we call the Linden <note><term>Tilia</term>: the linden or lime tree</note> is the same as what the Greeks call the *philyra*. And indeed, Theodorus Gaza <note>A 15th-century Greek scholar who translated Aristotle and Theophrastus into Latin</note>—as learned and diligent a man as you could wish—translates it in this way. Furthermore, there are certain thin membranes from the inner bark <note><term>liber</term>: the inner bark of a tree, which also gives us the Latin word for "book"</note> of the Linden tree, or made in imitation of it, from which either ribbons <note><term>lemnisci</term>: ornamental ribbons or streamers hanging from a crown or garland</note>—that is, small bandages hanging from crowns—were made, or small books; these are specifically called *philyræ* in the Greek tongue. Pliny confirms part of this in the same book with these lines:
> Between the bark and the wood are thin layers, a manifold membrane, from which the cords of the Linden are drawn out: the thinnest of these are the *philyræ*, famous for the honor of the ribboned crowns of the ancients. <note>original: "Inter corticẽ & lignũ tenues tunicas... tenuissimę earũ philyrę, coronarũ lẽnisci celebres atiquorũ honore." — Pliny, Natural History 16.25</note>

And again, in the twenty-first book, he mentions ribbons and *philyræ* thus:
> Crassus the Rich <note>Marcus Licinius Crassus, a member of the First Triumvirate, known for his immense wealth</note> was the first to give crowns at his games with leaves imitating silver and gold. Then ribbons were also added, which it was an honor to add to the crowns themselves; this was because of the Etruscans, for whom none but golden ribbons ought to be joined. These remained plain for a long time: Gaius Caesar <note>Julius Caesar</note> first instituted them. Publius Claudius Pulcher even added gold leaf to the *philyra*. <note>original: "Crassus diues primus argẽto auroq; folia imitatus... P. Claudius Pulcher: bracteafq; ẽt philyrę dedit." — Pliny, Natural History 21.4-6</note>

Furthermore, we see ribboned crowns <note>lemniscatas coronas</note> (if I am not mistaken) repeatedly on the coins of Lorenzo de' Medici <note>The de facto ruler of Florence and patron of the author</note>. Mention of these is also found in Suetonius <note>Roman biographer of the Caesars</note>, just as the word for ribbons is carefully explained by Pompeius Festus <note>A 2nd-century Roman grammarian</note>. Tertullian <note>An early Christian author</note> also says something regarding ribbons in his book *On the Soldier's Crown*.

<summary>The author identifies the "philyra" mentioned by Roman poets as the inner bark of the linden tree, explaining its historical use in creating decorative ribbons (lemnisci) for honorary crowns.</summary>
<keywords>philyra, linden tree, tilia, lemnisci, ribbons, crowns, Horace, Ovid, Pliny the Elder, Lorenzo de' Medici, botany, classical antiquity</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on the linden tree (Tilia), the author continues to define the term "philyra" by citing various ancient authorities to establish its meaning as both a botanical layer and a writing material.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<warning>The page exhibits significant foxing and staining throughout the margins and text area.</warning>

Nicas <note>likely referring to Nicander of Colophon, a Greek poet and physician</note>, however, in his commentary—which he arranged in alphabetical order—though he wrote in Greek, interprets the **philyra** in roughly this sense: he says that the **philyra** is a plant having an inner bark <note>original: "librum"</note> similar to papyrus, from which they even twist ropes. 

Moreover, that the **philyra** is pliable like wool-work, or rather can be spun, is suggested by Tertullian <note>An early Christian author from Carthage</note> in that little book titled *On the Cloak* <note>original: "De pallio"</note>. Furthermore, Herodian—the Greek author whom I formerly translated into Latin and dedicated to Pope Innocent VIII—mentions in the first book of his *History* a small book made of very thin **philyra**, which could be unrolled in either direction. 

But also Martianus Capella, in the second book of *The Marriage of Philology*, says: 
> "One could see what books, how many volumes, and works in what languages flowed from the virgin's mouth. Some appeared to be made of papyrus, coated with cedar oil <note>Cedar oil was used to protect papyrus from insects and decay</note>. Other books were wrapped in linen scrolls; many also were made from the skins of sheep; but rare were those written upon the bark of the **philyra**." 

Then you may also find these words of Ulpian <note>A prominent Roman jurist whose work makes up a large part of the Digest</note> in the title of the *Digest* regarding legacies and trusts: 
> "Under the name of 'books' are included all volumes, whether they are on paper <note>original: "charta" – here meaning papyrus</note>, or on parchment, or on any other material. But even if they are made on **philyra**, or linden <note><term>tilia</term>: the linden or lime tree</note>—as some are made—or on any other skin, the same must be said." 

Finally, Eustathius <note>A 12th-century Byzantine scholar and archbishop</note> in his second commentary on Homer’s *Odyssey*, having first mentioned nautical ropes, says the bark of the Linden—which he calls **philyra** in Greek—provides a great deal of material for that kind of rope. 

It was accepted from this that individual skins, or layers, or thin membranes—even if made of a material different from the Linden—should be called **philyras** or **philuras** (with the Greek *y* changed to *u*, according to the old and common custom of the Latins). Just as Pliny, writing about papyrus in his thirteenth book, says: 
> "From it are prepared papers divided by a needle into very thin, but as wide as possible, **philurae** <note>original: "philuras" – here referring to the strips of the papyrus plant</note>." 

For that other thing which Pliny says in his fourteenth book certainly does not pertain to these explanations: that **philyra** berries and powder remove excess salt from foods.

### Chapter lxxiii.
## That the word "expernata" should be restored to the copies of 
## Catullus.
