<meta>The author continues his philological investigation, moving from the physical materials of ancient books (discussed on the previous page) to the recovery of lost texts through fragmentary manuscript evidence.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>l iii</sig>

What we are now about to relate will perhaps be mocked and hissed at by some as uncertain, wavering, and insufficiently explored. But I thought I ought to speak of it, lest this trace of antiquity, though nearly destroyed, be entirely extinguished, whatever its worth may be. 

Four years ago in Rome, Manilius Rallus <note>A Greek scholar and poet active in Rome during the Renaissance</note>—a Greek man, but highly cultivated in Latin letters—showed me a certain fragment of Sextus Pompeius Festus <note>A 2nd-century Roman grammarian whose work is a vital source for ancient Latin</note> (for so it was titled); it was truly ancient, but for the most part mutilated and gnawed away by mice. This held my attention greatly, since these remains, such as they were, survived from the original volume itself which the author Festus had composed, and not from this current abridgment <note>Referring to the epitome made by Paul the Deacon in the 8th century, which the author considers an inferior, "ignoble" summary</note> which is now in our hands, violently compressed and shortened—evidently by some ignoble and unlearned person who deserves little credit in the field of literature. 

Pomponius Laetus <note>Julius Pomponius Laetus (1428–1498), a famous Italian humanist and founder of the Roman Academy</note>, a man most learned in antiquity and fine literature, had also kept for himself some pages from that same fragment; these he likewise gave to me to read and transcribe. 

From that reading, I certainly suspected that I would restore a word of Catullus <note>Gaius Valerius Catullus, the Roman lyric poet</note> to its proper place, as if by "right of return" <term>postliminio</term> <note>A legal term meaning the restoration of rights to someone returning from exile or captivity; here used metaphorically for restoring a text</note>. For where the word **suppernati** <note>original: "suppernati"</note> is explained in this current abridgment, it shows it signifies those whose thighs are cut in the manner of pig hams <note>original: "pernarum"</note>, and a verse from Ennius <note>Quintus Ennius, the "father" of Roman poetry</note> is cited:

> The unjust pride of the Carthaginian <note>original: "poeni"</note> cut through their hams.

At this very place, the old manuscript has an explanation clearly gnawed around the edges; yet it is such that it appears from the poorly connected remains of letters that Catullus was also cited after Ennius, and that little verse was quoted which is now erroneously read as follows:

> Buried in the Ligurian [earth], it lies, severed <note>original: "separata"</note> by the axe.

Now, while I would not rashly claim anything regarding the rest of the poem, I seemed then, by sniffing out every syllable, to be able to offer as a certainty that **suppernata** <note>meaning "hamstrung" or "undercut"</note> should be read in it, not **separata** <note>meaning "severed"</note>. I gathered this as best I could from those few scraps <note>original: "minutalibus"</note> or shavings, divining what I could even from the nearly obliterated traces. But if the credibility of the truth still seems to hang in the balance for anyone, I would rather it hang there than fall or lie flat. We certainly place some firm support in this (and I do not think unfairly).

<meta>The author continues his exploration of classical anecdotes and textual criticism, moving from the physical recovery of ancient fragments discussed on the previous page to specific stories about ancient artists and a critique of contemporary scholars who misinterpret classical texts.</meta>

### Chapter 74.
#### A rather charming story about the painter Zeuxis and Helen.

**Zeuxis**, that most noble painter from Heraclea, once painted a portrait of Helen for the people of Croton. This work was excessively praised, and Cicero—in his writings on rhetoric <note>original: "in rhetoricis"; likely referring to De Inventione</note> against Hermagoras—wrote about it most brilliantly. However, some years ago in Rome, while I was visiting Giovanni Lorenzo of Venice—a man of great authority in the papal court and no less learned in literature—I read something in passing in Aelian’s books *On Varied History* <note>original: "Heliani libris omnifarie historiae"</note>. Lorenzo was a man of "refined nose" <note>a Latin idiom for sharp wit or keen judgment</note>, and it seemed to me that this story was not at all unworthy of being read here. Aelian says that Zeuxis would admit no one to view the painting unless they had paid an entry fee <note>original: "manupretium"</note>. Because of this, even back then, the portrait of Helen was commonly nicknamed "the harlot," because she was a source of profit.

---

### Chapter 75.
#### A corrected word in the *Ibis*: together with an explanation of a meaning hitherto unknown.

<gloss>He</gloss> recounted Domitius's <note>Likely referring to Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century scholar known for his controversial commentaries</note> little book on Naso’s <note>The poet Ovid</note> *Ibis*; he prefaced it by claiming to write things he had received from Apollodorus, Lycophron, Pausanias, Strabo, Apollonius, and other Greeks and Latins. In that commentary, he invents and devises many vain and ridiculous things on the fly for his own convenience. By doing so, he proves either that he has completely lost his "forehead" <note>original: "frontem"; in Latin, the forehead was the seat of shame, so to lose it meant to become shameless</note> or that there was such a wide gap between his forehead and his tongue—as someone once said—that his forehead was unable to restrain his tongue. It is madness that some lovers of this man are still found who are so blind that they have not yet opened their eyes, such as they are, to these things. It is madness indeed, truly madness, to give an opportunity for shamelessness to others by fostering the errors of someone simply because he occasionally held an opinion that was not regrettable. But what can you do with these masters of "literacy" <term>litterationis</term> rather than "literature" <term>litteraturae</term> <note>The author distinguishes between basic schooling (litteratio) and deep, cultured scholarship (litteratura)</note>? They raise their eyebrows at such trifles more than anything else—like the horns of a snail—and the more their leaden wits are cultivated, the more they grow dull.

<summary>This page contains a lighthearted anecdote about the painter Zeuxis charging admission to see his portrait of Helen, followed by a sharp polemic against the scholar Domitius for his unreliable commentary on Ovid's Ibis.</summary>
<keywords>Zeuxis, Helen of Troy, Aelian, Cicero, Giovanni Lorenzo, Domitius Calderinus, Ovid, Ibis, textual criticism, Renaissance scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>The author continues his scathing critique of the scholar Domitius Calderinus, specifically targeting his commentary on Ovid's Ibis. On the previous page, he accused Domitius of fabricating sources; here, he focuses on a specific textual corruption regarding the Trojan Horse.</meta>

But indeed, lest we completely waste our effort by ignoring those who, by their own worthlessness, deserve to be held in contempt, we shall proceed with the rest of our purpose. Therefore, there is one particular passage among the others that is enough to turn my stomach, as it is currently read so erroneously:

> And as talkative Agenor, his throat crushed, is in the horse;
> So let the path of your voice be closed by a thumb.
> <note>original: "Vtque loquax in equo est elisus guttur Agenor / Sic tibi claudatur pollice uocis iter." These lines are from Ovid's Ibis, a poem of curses.</note>

On this passage, Domitius says: "Agenor was strangled after a fall from a horse, having thrust his hand into his mouth." Is that really so, most learned man? But I beg you, my dear Domitius, produce—if you can—either a Greek or Latin author of this story, provided they are reliable, and I will no longer refuse to count myself among your companions or admirers. Otherwise, let even the literary men who sweat over this labor be condemned as most unlearned and even most impudent—and indeed by unanimous vote—simply because I disagree with you.

To me, the writing seems correct and emended if free conjecture is applied to reason. I believe the word is not "Agenor," but "Acerno" <note>meaning "of maple wood," referring to the material of the Trojan Horse</note>, so that we may take the passage to be spoken of **Anticlus**. Mention of him is found in **Homer**, in the fourth book of the *Odyssey*. 

For when **Helen**—driven by a divine impulse—approached that Trojan horse, which was woven from beams and heavy with soldiers, she began to imitate the voices of each man’s wife. She did this to call forth the longings of those hiding within, to rub their memories raw, and to try to draw out a response. But while the others maintained their patience and silence, Anticlus alone was on that slippery edge, at the very point of responding. He was immediately prevented by **Ulysses**, who pressed his hands over him and suffocated him. Thus, by intercepting—or rather, blocking—the evidence, the danger was warded off. 

Furthermore, **Tryphiodorus**, a Greek author, in the poem he wrote about the capture of Troy <note>original: "de Ilio capta"</note>, calls this man "Anticlus, son of Ortyges" and names his wife **Laodamia**, saying:

> And Anticlus, son of Ortyges, whom the Achaeans buried right there where he died, their eyes full of tears.
> <note>original Greek: "ὀρτυγίδης δ' ἄντικλος ὃν αὐτόθι τεθνειῶτα / ἴσσε δακρύσαντες ἐπεκτέρῑξαν ἀχαιοὶ."</note>

And the same author says elsewhere:

> And Anticlus, when he received the sting of Laodamia's voice, alone opened his mouth and was about to give a reply, but Odysseus held him down.
> <note>original Greek: "ἄντικλος δ' ὅτε κέντρον ἐδέξατο λαοδαμείης / μοῦνος ἀμοιβαίην ἀνεβάλλετο γῆρυν ἀνοίξας / ἀλλ' ὀδυσεὺς κατέϣαλτο."</note>

<vocab>Agenor, Domitius, Homer, Odyssey, Anticlus, Tryphiodorus, Troy, Laodamia, Ulysses</vocab>

<summary>The author corrects a perceived error in Ovid's Ibis, arguing that a reference to "Agenor" is actually a corruption of "Acerno" (maple) and refers to the story of Anticlus. Anticlus was a Greek soldier nearly tricked by Helen into speaking from inside the Trojan Horse, only to be silenced by Odysseus.</summary>

<meta>The author continues his critique of the scholar Domitius Calderinus. And he adds other things thereafter, by which the whole story is explained in many verses, just as it is by Homer. But we also touched upon this in our *Ambra* <note>original: "Ambra"; this refers to Politian's own poetic work, a "silva" or occasional poem dedicated to the study of Homer</note> in this way:

> Anticlus, son of Ortyges, his throat shut off by a thumb for fear of betrayal.
> <note>original: "Indiciiq; metu praeclusum pollice fauces Anticlon ortygiden." This refers to the myth where Odysseus held Anticlus’s mouth shut inside the Trojan Horse to prevent him from crying out and revealing the Greeks' hiding place.</note>

Truly, there are many things similar to these in the works of Domitius, which clearly show how far his license <note>referring to his "philological license" or tendency to invent facts</note> would have progressed—especially since it was encouraged by the favor of the unlearned—had he not ceased to dwell among those above while still a young man. <note>Domitius Calderinus died at the age of 30, a fact Politian uses here to suggest his errors were the result of a career cut short before he could reach maturity.</note>

->Chapter LXXVI.<-
->That it should not be "Antilochus by Hector," but that it seems it should be read otherwise in the first book of the *Heroides*.<-

In the first epistle of Ovid’s *Heroides* <note>Naso refers to the poet Publius Ovidius Naso</note>, it is read thus:

> "Whether someone reported that Antilochus was conquered by Hector;"
> <note>original: "Siue quis Antilochum narrabat ab Hectore uictum." This line is from Penelope’s letter to Ulysses, where she recounts the news of the war.</note>

But Antilochus will certainly by no means be found to have been conquered or killed by Hector. Therefore, I believe either "Amphimachus" should be read, or instead of "by Hector," it should say "by Memnon." For it is established from the thirteenth book <note>referring to the *Iliad* XIII, 185</note> of Homer’s epic that Amphimachus, the son of Cteatus, was killed by Hector; he is even counted as one of the Greek leaders in the Catalogue of Ships. It is likewise established that Antilochus, the son of Nestor, was indeed slain by Memnon. Therefore, just as a manifest error is detected in the text that has prevailed until now, so too with these corrections supplied by us for the occasion—which I hope you, the reader, will take in good part—they are supported, if I am not mistaken, by some degree of merit; if not of absolute truth, then certainly of probability.

---

->Chapter LXXVII.<-
->By what argument one should say "Vergilius" and not "Virgilius."<-

Whether one should say "Vergilius" or "Virgilius," as is now commonly spoken—that is, with the second vowel <note>the letter 'e'</note> or rather the third <note>the letter 'i'</note> in the first syllable—I see is still a matter of debate among the learned. However, as...

<summary>Politian concludes his critique of Domitius Calderinus, argues for a textual correction in Ovid's Heroides regarding the death of Antilochus, and begins a philological debate on the correct spelling of Virgil's name.</summary>
<keywords>Politian, Ovid, Heroides, Antilochus, Hector, Memnon, textual criticism, philology, Virgil, Vergilius, Domitius Calderinus</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>The author, having finished his correction of Ovid’s *Heroides* on the previous page, now transitions into a famous philological debate regarding the spelling of the poet Virgil's name. He defends the value of precise scholarship against critics who view such "minute" details as a waste of time.</meta>

I shall say *Vergilius* <note>original: "Vergilium"</note> with an "e" rather than *Virgilius* with an "i," a practice which, through our efforts, is now beginning to find favor with certain learned men, whereas the version with "i" has commonally prevailed far too much. The reason for this lies in several very ancient monuments <note>The author refers to "monumenta," which in this context means ancient stone inscriptions or primary archaeological evidence.</note> that support our observation. These I will gladly submit on account of certain shameful men—I know not who—full of rust and decay, who are most worthy of all hatred and disdain. Although these men are always stuck at the elementary level, they nevertheless dare to claim for themselves the censorship of letters among their unlearned supporters.

They do this so that they might not only mock this diligence and zeal of ours—whatever it may be—in discovering and restoring antiquity (if it be permitted), but also criticize it so harshly as if they thought it a crime that we amuse ourselves with this leisure <note>original: "otium"; in the Renaissance, this referred to productive, scholarly "leisure" or time spent in study.</note>. Or, as they say even more contemptuously, they claim we begrudge ourselves our own leisure for these things, while we spend our effort on such frivolous and light matters, as if we were wasting time—that most precious thing—on superfluities.

Yet if these critics had ever read that the greatest men composed entire volumes about single letters, and that not even Caesar himself <note>Julius Caesar wrote a lost grammatical treatise titled *De Analogia*, which Politian uses here to justify his own interest in spelling.</note> feared to descend into such subtleties, they would perhaps grant me pardon—I who am not exactly the busiest of men—if I occasionally treated such matters among my higher studies. These are small matters, indeed, but they are matters by which even great things are assisted.

For I do not think I am rightly criticized if I pursue these things also, but only if I pursued these things *alone*. I turn to these studies not as one goes from leisure to study, but as one goes from study to leisure. I do not stumble upon them by profession so much as by chance, just as if, walking conveniently along the seashore, I were to safely collect shells in the meantime.

Yet I do not raise my eyebrow in arrogance over this, nor would I easily allow myself to be judged by it, as I admit these are not just "trifles and toys" <note>original: "apinas... et tricas"; a Latin proverbial expression for worthless things, originating from the names of two vanished towns in Apulia.</note> as the humorous proverb goes, but truly almost nothing—I confess it! But yet it is better to do this very "nothing" than to drag out long yawns (perhaps following their own example) and to grunt shamefully in the mud, or than to do nothing at all; for by doing absolutely nothing, we learn to do evil <note>Politian is paraphrasing a common Latin proverb: "nihil agendo, male agere discimus."</note>.

Therefore, we must present our evidence, lest our credibility falter, and so that nothing remains difficult for those wishing to learn. You will find, therefore, at Volsinii <note>Modern Orvieto or Bolsena; an ancient Etruscan and Roman site.</note> a certain marble tablet with very ancient and almost obsolete characters...

<summary>Politian defends his preference for spelling "Vergilius" (with an 'e') over "Virgilius" and justifies the value of philological minutiae as a worthy use of a scholar's leisure time.</summary>
<keywords>orthography, Vergilius, Julius Caesar, philology, Volsinii, academic leisure, Latin spelling</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>The text continues the philological argument from the previous page, where the author began defending the spelling "Vergilius" (with an "e") over the common "Virgilius" (with an "i"). He shifts from theoretical defense to citing specific physical evidence—inscriptions and manuscripts—found in Italy.</meta>

...inside the church of the virgin Christina <note>The Basilica of Santa Cristina in Bolsena.</note>, on the stone that serves as the altar of the apostle Peter, where the name is read as **VERGILI**. You will also find this name of "Vergilius" noted in the same way in Sutri <note>An ancient town in Lazio, Italy.</note> on another stone table, which they likewise use as an altar inside the church of the virgin. We inspected both of these closely, accompanied by several witnesses; for we desired to be not only "ear-witnesses" <note>original: "auriti testes"</note> of ancient investigations, but "eye-witnesses" <note>original: "oculati"</note> as well.

Indeed, in these very Pandects <term>The Pandects, or Digest, is a massive 6th-century compendium of Roman law compiled under Emperor Justinian; the Florentine manuscript (the *Littera Florentina*) was considered the most authoritative archetype in the Renaissance.</term> which are now publicly preserved in Florence—the primary manuscript of Prince Justinian himself—the name is written in no other way than with an "e." It is the same in the "Maronian" volume <note>A manuscript of Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro).</note> inscribed in capital letters, which is shown as a wonderful antiquity in the inner Vatican Library in Rome. 

Furthermore, Landinus <note>Cristoforo Landino (1424–1498), a central figure of the Florentine Renaissance and Poliziano's former teacher.</note>—a man both eloquent and learned, and for a long time now a most celebrated teacher of fine letters in Florence—now has in his hands the commentary of Tiberius Donatus. I owe much to Landinus as the teacher who guided the first lessons of my youth. Today, though he has already secured his own fame while we are still struggling in this most laborious field of study <gloss>a</gloss>, he favors us as a colleague (if I may call myself that) in our profession. He supports us so much that whatever praise we acquire, he seems to embrace and recognize it as if it were his own. 

He, therefore (as we said), has in his hands the commentary of Tiberius Donatus, which is written in large, ancient characters; its authority would leave the matter of this spelling beyond doubt. The same is found in the codex of Saint Augustine’s *The City of God* from the public library of the Medici family, and also in a volume of Columella <note>A Roman writer on agriculture.</note> from the private collection of the same family—both written in Lombardic script <term>Poliziano uses "Lombardic" to describe various early medieval scripts (like Beneventan or Caroline minuscule) that appeared ancient and "Gothic" to Renaissance humanists.</term>. 

Then there is a very old book of Seneca’s letters, which was made available to me by Niccolò Michelozzi—secretary to Lorenzo de' Medici and a man of elegant intellect—as well as many other volumes venerable for their antiquity! We have shown this evidence repeatedly: now to Jacopo Modesto of Prato—our close friend and helper in our studies—and now to others among our students, as the situation allows or requires. 

Moreover, in the collections which Jucundus <note>Giovanni Giocondo (c. 1433–1515), a famous architect, engineer, and scholar who collected ancient inscriptions.</note> recently sent to Lorenzo de' Medici—a man who, in my opinion, is not only the most diligent but also beyond dispute the most skilled in ancient titles and monuments above all other mortals—it is recorded...

<summary>Poliziano lists numerous primary sources—including the Florentine Pandects, Vatican manuscripts, and inscriptions in Bolsena and Sutri—to prove that Virgil's name was historically spelled "Vergilius." He also pays tribute to his teacher Cristoforo Landino and mentions his access to the prestigious Medici libraries.</summary>

<keywords>Vergilius, orthography, philology, Cristoforo Landino, Lorenzo de' Medici, Pandects, Justinian, Tiberius Donatus, manuscripts, epigraphy</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s philological argument regarding the correct spelling of the name "Vergilius" (with an "e"). He moves from citing ancient manuscript evidence (like the Vatican Virgil) to physical stone inscriptions found in Rome. He then transitions to a new chapter correcting a significant error in the transmission of Roman Law found in the Digest.</meta>

I also find two inscriptions which (as he indicates) are found on marbles in Rome: **TIBERIUS VERGILIUS DONATUS**. And again: **GAIUS PAPIRIUS CESTUS TO HIS WELL-DESERVING WIFE, VERGILIA OPTATA.** <note>original Latin: "TI. VERGILIVS DONATVS... C. PAPIRIVS CESTVS VERGILIAE OPTATAE VXORI SVAE BENEMERENTI DE SE."</note> Although these monuments, strengthened by the great age of centuries, are sufficient defense for me, yet the facts themselves and reason also agree. For just as the **Vergiliae** stars <note>The Pleiades; Poliziano argues they are named from the Latin "ver," meaning spring.</note> are correctly named from "spring," so I would believe this proper name was derived from the *Vergiliae* stars themselves, or likewise from "spring" <note>original: "a uere"</note>; rather than—by Hercules!—from a "wand" <note>Latin: "virga." This refers to a popular medieval folk-etymology that linked Virgil’s name to a magic wand or the laurel branch.</note>, as some triflers suggest. 

For that idea is not only found in less reliable authors, but it is refuted by this very fact: that many people were called by this same name before this famous poet was ever born. As long as no weightier testimony or stronger reasoning is provided, we must stand by ancient custom rather than by modern ignorance—the source from which this jumbled way of speaking entered our languages. Indeed, a great many of us younger people are blinded when we try to look upon the truth. 

Moreover (to complete the rest of my evidence), we noticed in Rome that the name **Verginius** too—a name closely related—and not **Virginius**, was recently carved on a marble basin in front of the church of Saint Mary Major <note>The Basilica of Santa Maria Maggiore in Rome.</note>.

### Chapter lxxviii.

Concerning a negation falsely added in the first volume of the Digest, in the chapter dealing with the office of the proconsul and the legate.

Aulus the legal expert <note>Aulus Cascellius, a Roman jurist.</note> writes as follows in the second book of his *Commentary on the Edict*: **"A legate, when jurisdiction has been delegated to him, has the right to appoint a judge."** <note>original: "Legatus mãdata sibi iurisdictione iudicis dãdi ius habet."</note> 

But you may find these words in the first volume of the **Digest** <term>The Digest (or Pandects) is the massive 6th-century compilation of Roman law ordered by Emperor Justinian.</term>—in the chapter where the office of proconsul and legate is discussed—with a negation wrongly added in most manuscripts. In those, it reads: **"A legate... does NOT have the right to appoint a judge."** <note>original: "Legatus... ius nō habet."</note> 

However, in those Florentine Pandects <note>The *Littera Florentina*, a famous 6th-century manuscript of the Digest brought to Florence from Pisa in 1406; Poliziano considered it the ultimate authority.</note> which we believe to be the original archetype, there is absolutely no negation. Consequently, the Florentine legal commentator **Accursius** <note>Accursius (c. 1182–1263), author of the "Great Gloss" which was the standard interpretation of Roman law in the Middle Ages.</note>, having himself come upon a faulty manuscript, twists himself in knots—I might almost say, pitiably—trying to explain the error.

<summary>Poliziano uses Roman inscriptions to prove "Vergilius" (not "Virgilius") is the correct spelling and identifies a major transcription error in standard legal texts by comparing them to the authoritative Florentine Pandects.</summary>
<keywords>Vergilius vs Virginius, etymology, Roman inscriptions, Santa Maria Maggiore, Pandects, Florentine Digest, Accursius, legal philology, manuscript errors</keywords>

### Chapter 79.
### Why the face of King Seleucus is horned on coins.

The face of **King Seleucus** <note>Seleucus I Nicator (c. 358–281 BC), one of Alexander the Great’s generals and founder of the Seleucid Empire.</note> appears with horns on coins. The reason for this is found in **Appian’s** *Syrian Wars*. For he says that a wild bull, intended for the sacrifice of **Alexander the Great**, escaped its bonds; Seleucus restrained it with his bare hands and killed it. And because of this, Appian says, "they add growing horns to his statues" <note>original Greek: "προστιθέασιν ἐπὶ τοὺς ἀνδριάντας ἐπιφὺε τὰ κέρατα"</note>. This signifies that, for this reason, horns were eventually placed upon his statues.

---

### Chapter 80.
### A lesser-known myth concerning Tiresias and Pallas: through which a passage in Propertius is clarified.

**Propertius**, in the fourth book of his *Elegies*, says the following:

> The prophet Tiresias beheld great Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom and war.</note>,
> while she washed her strong limbs, her Gorgon shield set aside.

There still exists a hymn by the poet **Callimachus**, titled *On the Baths of Pallas* <note>original Greek: "εἰς λουτρὰ τῆς παλλάδος"</note>. In this work, the myth of **Tiresias** is treated in many elegiac verses, following roughly this account: There was a certain nymph in Thebes named **Chariclo**, who was dear to **Pallas** above all others. She was the mother of Tiresias. But once, while Pallas and Chariclo were washing themselves naked at the **Hippocrene** spring on Mount **Helicon**, the hunter Tiresias happened to approach the same spring to quench his thirst. Thus, he unintentionally caught sight of **Minerva** <note>The Roman name for Pallas Athena.</note> naked, and he was immediately deprived of his sight. 

For he says it was so cautioned by the **Saturnian laws** <note>Divine laws established by Saturn/Cronus, the father of the gods.</note>: that whoever looks upon a god against the god's will, looks at a great price. **Abammon the Egyptian** <note>A pseudonym used in the Neoplatonic work *On the Mysteries*.</note> writes something similar regarding the sight of deities in his letter to the philosopher **Porphyry**; though **Proclus** believes this work belongs to **Iamblichus** rather than Abammon. From this comes the line in **Virgil**:

> Throw it over your head; do not look back. <note>original Latin: "Transq; caput iace: ne respexeris"</note>

The **Homeric Leucothea** also warned **Ulysses** <note>Odysseus</note> of this. But to return to where I left off: when Tiresias had fallen into this calamity, Pallas was moved by the tears of the nymph Chariclo. She made him a prophet, granted him a long life, and even after his death, decreed that he would remain prudent and wise...

<summary>Poliziano explains the iconography of horned coins belonging to Seleucus I and clarifies a mythological reference in Propertius using Callimachus's hymns, detailing how Tiresias was blinded after accidentally seeing Athena bathing.</summary>
<keywords>Seleucus I Nicator, numismatics, Appian, Tiresias, Pallas Athena, Propertius, Callimachus, Chariclo, divine law, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the myth of Tiresias and Pallas Athena, picking up mid-sentence from the previous page's account of how the goddess blinded the young hunter for seeing her bathing, but then compensated him with the gift of prophecy.</meta>

...she granted; and furthermore gave him a staff, by which he might walk with steady steps as if with a guide. We also touched upon this in our poem *Ambra* <note>original: "Ambra," one of Poliziano's Latin poems dedicated to Lorenzo de' Medici</note> when we were speaking of the poet **Homer**:

> He then gives a powerful staff
> to great **Tiresias**, who once beheld **Pallas** naked,
> and with this gift he compensated for the light that was snatched away,
> accustomed to direct his steady steps with the staff as a guide.

And likewise in our poem *The Nurse* <note>original: "Nutrix," another of Poliziano's Silvae</note>, we introduced Tiresias through a periphrasis <note>A roundabout way of describing something; a "kenning" or descriptive phrase used instead of a name.</note>:

> What of him to whom, after seeing the naked limbs of Pallas,
> it was permitted to behold nothing else?

Moreover, in the fifth book of the *Dionysiaca* <note>An epic poem by Nonnus of Panopolis (5th century AD) concerning the life of Dionysus.</note> by **Nonnus**, he introduces **Actaeon** <note>In Greek myth, Actaeon was a hunter turned into a stag and killed by his own dogs after seeing the goddess Diana bathing.</note> exclaiming that Tiresias was fortunate because he beheld **Minerva** <note>The Roman name for Pallas Athena.</note> naked without dying, having only lost his eyes; and she even transferred that lost light into his very soul. And thus he says:

> Happy Tiresias! You looked upon the naked form 
> of merciful, though denying, **Athena**, without perishing. <note>original Greek: "ὄλβιε Τειρεσία . σὺ μὲν ἔδρακες ἐκτὸς ὀλέθρου γυμνὸν ἀναινομένης οἰκτείρμονος εἶδος ἀθήνης"</note>

And a little later:

> You live, though you have lost the light of your eyelids;
> Athena has moved the brightness of your eyes into your mind. <note>original Greek: "ζώεις σῶν βλεφάρων ὀλέσας φάος ὑμετέρων δὲ ὀφθαλμῶν ἀμάρυγμα νόῳ μετέθηκεν ἀθήνη"</note>

I had written this much on this passage of the poet when our friend **Pico della Mirandola** <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous Renaissance philosopher and close friend of Poliziano.</note>—acting like a second Cynthius <note>An epithet for Apollo, the god of poetry, who in classical literature famously "pulls the ear" of a poet to give advice or a warning.</note>—pulled my ear at the very moment these pages were to be printed. He advised that we should also include the elegy of **Callimachus** <note>A Greek poet and scholar of the Library of Alexandria (c. 310–240 BC).</note> itself, which is the only one of its kind extant among the Greeks (so far as I know), but is very rare to find. He suggested that, even though I was excluded by the unfair constraints of time, I should nonetheless attempt to translate it into Latin. 

Now, I am someone who does not usually enjoy writing against my own inclination, nor do I trust enough in extemporaneous work; yet I did not delay in undertaking this burden by whatever means. For when Pico demands something of me, I cannot allow myself to be ignorant of what I do not know, nor unable to do what I cannot—either because I feel myself so dear to the man, or because I am persuaded that he sees more in my own affairs than I see myself. We have translated it, therefore, almost word-for-word, or what is called in Greek <term>para poda</term> <note>Literally "at the foot"; a translation style that follows the source text very closely, line by line.</note>, not only in sense but even in its poetic meter—which is most difficult—while preserving the stylistic colors. I only warn the reader now that a few things still seem faulty to me in the Greek manuscripts; it would not have been right to hide this.

<summary>Poliziano discusses the myth of Tiresias's blinding and prophetic compensation, citing his own poetry and the Greek epic poet Nonnus. He reveals that his friend Pico della Mirandola pressured him at the last minute to include a rare Greek elegy by Callimachus, which Poliziano translated word-for-word despite time constraints.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s defense of his philological work and his restoration of ancient Greek texts, specifically the hymns of Callimachus, which he had previously discussed in his own poetic works, the Silvae.</meta>

...let our critics correct! <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing a thought about textual restoration.</note> But I should also mention that the seventh verse from the end had faded away, leaving only a single word. This we have restored in Latin through our own conjecture. Know also that this interpretation is our own work; it flowed from us before it ever bubbled up in anyone else's commentaries. The *Silvae* <note>Poliziano’s "Silvae" were a series of influential Latin poems used as introductions to his university lectures.</note> themselves, which we published so many years ago and cited earlier, will prove this; one of these we once interpreted publicly before a great gathering with many students taking notes. 

Let what was our task, therefore, remain our right; let us not be thought (as the proverb goes) to have been "born on the fourth moon" <note>An ancient Greek proverb (*tetradi gegonenai*) referring to those who perform hard labor from which only others benefit, much like Hercules.</note>, laboring only for others after the example of **Hercules**. Now I also wonder why **Domitius** <note>Domizio Calderini, a rival humanist scholar often criticized by Poliziano.</note> and certain others after him, at every moment and on every occasion, dare to write that this or 그 that was said by **Propertius** <note>The Latin text reads "Proprio," likely a transcription error for "Propertio," the Roman elegiac poet.</note> in imitation of **Callimachus** <note>A scholar-poet of Alexandria (3rd century BC). Roman poets like Propertius and Catullus claimed him as their primary influence.</note>. They say this even though, besides a few hymns, absolutely nothing of that poet has come down to us—certainly nothing containing themes of love. But let us now prick up our ears for the verses of Callimachus written below in the ancient manner, without any of those modern little accent marks. <note>Poliziano preferred printing Greek without accents, believing this closer to the "original" ancient style.</note>

->CALLIMACHUS ON THE BATHS OF PALLAS<-

All you who are pourers of the bath for **Pallas**, come forth,
come forth! I heard the whinnying of the sacred horses just now,
and the goddess is ready to come.
Hasten now, O golden-haired Pelasgian women, hasten!
**Athena** never washed her great arms
before driving the dust of the horses from her flanks,
nor even when, her whole body spattered with gore, she brought back
the weapons of the wicked Earth-born giants <note>A reference to the Gigantomachy, the battle between the Olympian gods and the Giants.</note>.
But first of all, unyoking the horses' necks from the chariot,
she washed them in the springs of **Ocean**
from sweat and foam; and she cleansed the dried
froth from their bit-champing mouths.
O Achaean women, bring no perfumes or alabaster jars—
I hear the sound of the axles' whistling—
Bring no perfumes, bath-pourers, for Pallas, nor alabaster jars,
for Athena does not love mixed ointments.
Bring no mirror either; her eye is always beautiful.

Not even on Mount Ida did the Phrygian <note>The "Phrygian" refers to Paris, the prince of Troy, who judged the famous beauty contest between Hera, Athena, and Aphrodite.</note> judge such a strife.
Nor did the great Goddess look into a mirror of mountain-bronze, nor into the translucent
eddies of the river Simois <note>A river near Troy; the goddess Athena is contrasted here with Aphrodite, who used mirrors and water to admire her reflection.</note>;
nor did Hera. But Cypris <note>A common title for Aphrodite, the goddess of love, who was born on Cyprus.</note>, taking up the gleaming bronze,
often rearranged the same lock of hair twice.
But she [Athena], after running twice sixty double-courses <note>The *diaulos* was a traditional Greek footrace where the runner went to the end of the stadium and back.</note>,
just as the Lacedaemonian Stars <note>The "Stars" refers to the Dioscuri, Castor and Pollux, the twin sons of Zeus associated with Sparta (Lacedaemon).</note> do beside the river Eurotas,

such as Castor and Heracles use for their anointing;
bring also a comb all of gold, so that she may 
comb her hair, after she has smoothed her sleek locks.

the virgin daughters of the great sons of Achestor <note>The "Achestoridae" were likely the daughters of the noble families of Argos, perhaps those involved in the healing arts.</note>.

for this is an ancient custom of the people of Argos.
Eumedes, a priest who found favor with you, taught this; 
he, once realizing a death was being plotted against him, 
fled with your holy image <note>original: "ἄγαλμα" (agalma). This refers to the Palladium, the ancient wooden statue of Athena believed to protect the city.</note>
and went away, settling on the Creion hill. 
On the Creion hill, he placed you on the jagged 
cliffs, which they now call the "Pallatides" <note>The "Pallatides" or "Palladium-rocks," named after the goddess Pallas Athena.</note>.
Come forth, Athena, sacker of cities, wearer of the golden helmet, 
you who delight in the clatter of horses and shields!
Today, water-carriers, do not dip your vessels; today, Argos,
drink from the springs and not from the rivers.
Today, servants, carry your pitchers either to Physadeia 
or to the excellent spring of Amymone, daughter of Danaus <note>Physadeia and Amymone were famous springs in the region of Argos.</note>.
For truly, mixing his waters with gold and flowers, 
Inachus <note>The river-god of Argos.</note> shall come from the mountains of the sheep-pastures.

"O companion, do not grieve for him <note>original: "ω εταρα". Athena is addressing Chariclo, Teiresias's mother.</note>. For because of me, many other rewards shall be his in exchange for his lost sight. For I shall make him a prophet celebrated by those yet to be born, far greater than any other. He shall know the birds—which are auspicious, and which fly in vain, or whose wings portend no good omen. He shall utter many oracles to the Boeotians, many to Cadmus, and to the great descendants of Labdacus <note>The royal house of Thebes; Teiresias becomes the central prophet in the myths of Oedipus and Antigone.</note> hereafter. I will also give him a great staff which shall guide his feet as needed, and I will grant him a long-lived end to his life. So she spoke and nodded her head, making it certain; for to Pallas alone among his daughters has Zeus granted that she may wield all her father’s attributes. O bath-pourers! No mother gave birth to the goddess, but rather the head of Zeus. The head of Zeus! For already...

### Poliziano’s [Translation] From Callimachus’ *On the Bath of Pallas*.

We hear the rumble of the sacred horses; Go now, O golden-haired Pelasgian <note>The Pelasgians were the mythical earliest inhabitants of Greece; here used as a poetic name for the women of Argos.</note> women, Go.

<meta>This page continues the translation of Callimachus’ *Hymn 5*, "The Bath of Pallas," though the source text has switched from the original Greek to a Latin verse translation. It picks up at the ritual description of Athena's (Pallas's) preferences for her bath, contrasting her rugged, virginal nature with the more vanity-driven grooming of Venus and Juno.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>

<note>This Latin text is a verse translation of the Greek poem by Callimachus. It describes the ritual preparations for the washing of a cult statue of Athena in the city of Argos.</note>

The goddess did not first wash her mighty arms
Until she wiped the dust from the flanks of her horses <note>original: "cornipedum" — literally "horn-footed," a poetic way of describing hooves.</note>.
Nor even then, when she came with her armor bloody from much slaughter,
Returning terrible from the unjust earth-born giants:
Even then, having unyoked the smoking necks of her horses,
She washed them in the copious springs of the Ocean,
Until the sweat and the foul drops were gone, and all
The foam was cleared from their mouths, where it had stiffened around the chewed bits.

Go, O Achaean women! <note>The "Achaeans" here refers to the women of Argos participating in the festival.</note> But bring no perfumes or alabaster jars;
I hear for certain the creaking sound of the axle itself <note>The "creaking axle" signals the arrival of the goddess's carriage or the cart bearing her statue.</note>.
Washers of Pallas, bring no perfumes or alabasters;
For no mixed ointments please this goddess.
Nor bring her a mirror, for her face is always most beautiful:
For not even on Mount Ida long ago, under the Phrygian judge <note>The "Phrygian judge" is Paris, who famously judged the beauty contest between Hera (Juno), Athena (Pallas), and Aphrodite (Venus).</note>,
Did this great goddess look at herself in polished brass or in the waters of the Simois <note>A river near Troy.</note>,
Even though its surface was crystal clear.
Nor did Juno look; but Venus alone looked at herself in the shining bronze
And rearranged the same lock of hair again and again.
But Pallas, having run her course twice sixty times <note>The Latin "bis sexaginta spatiis" refers to the long laps or "stades" run during athletic training.</note>,
Just like the Spartan stars <note>term: "Stellę... lacedęmoniæ" (Spartan stars). This refers to the Dioscuri, the twin brothers Castor and Pollux, who were associated with athletic excellence and the constellation Gemini.</note> by the Eurotas river,
The goddess merely rubbed herself with the rich oil
Which her own tree <note>The olive tree, which Athena famously gifted to the city of Athens.</note> had produced from its berries.
O girls, a morning blush flashed out on her,
Like the color of a rose or the seeds of a pomegranate.
Therefore, now bring only that "masculine" oil
With which Castor and the son of Amphitryon <note>term: "Amphitryoniades." This is a patronymic for Hercules (Heracles), the son of Alcmene and Zeus, though raised by Amphitryon.</note> anoint themselves.
Bring also a comb made of solid gold, with which she may
Smooth her hair and part her thick locks.

Come forth now, O Pallas! The virgin flock is ready for you,
The dear daughters of the great Acestoridae <note>The noble families of Argos who held the hereditary right to perform these rites.</note>.
O Pallas, for now even the shield of Diomedes <note>Diomedes was a hero of the Trojan War; his shield was kept as a sacred relic in Argos and paraded alongside Athena’s statue.</note> is being
Carried out, as the ancient custom of the Argives has maintained.

To these women; this is not my own speech, but another's <note>A traditional poetic device where the narrator claims to be retelling an ancient myth rather than inventing it.</note>.
Once in Thebes, O maidens, the goddess Pallas loved
One nymph above all the companions she had—
The mother of Tiresias <note>Chariclo.</note>; and they were never apart!
Whether toward the walls of ancient Thespiae,
Or Coronea, or whether she made for Haliartus,
Riding through the heights to visit the peoples of Boeotia;
Or to Coronea, where the beautiful grove breathes with scents,
Where her altar stands by the river Curalius.
Often the goddess carried her in her own chariot;
No conversations of nymphs or ritual dances <note>original: "Thyasi" — referring to the *thiasus*, a religious procession or group of worshippers.</note>
Were pleasing enough, unless Chariclo herself was present;
Truly, many tears were also destined for her.

Although she <note>Chariclo, the mother of Tiresias</note> was ever a dear companion to great Minerva <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Athena</note>,

While, therefore, both are bathing! And it is the time of midday light!
And while there is the greatest stillness upon the whole mountain!
Tiresias, alone, with his dogs accompanying him, was walking
Through the sacred places, his cheeks bright with light down <note>original: "leui flore genas nitidus"; this indicates he was a youth reaching manhood</note>:

He stood still, he fell silent; as if grief had bound
His knees with glue; his helpless mind held back the sound of his voice.
But the Nymph <note>Chariclo</note> cries out: "O Goddess, what has been done to my son?
Is such the friendship of you goddesses?
You have snatched away my boy's eyes. My son,
Unhappy one, you have seen the breast and flanks of Pallas <note>Athena</note>.
But you shall not see the sun again. Alas, wretched me! O mountain,
O Helicon, never to be traversed by me again!
You exchange too much for too little: you who take the eyes of my son
In exchange for a few stags and gazelles."
Thus, having embraced the dear boy with both arms,
The mother repeats the mournful song of the nightingales <note>original: "Aedonidon"; in Greek myth, the nightingale is a symbol of eternal maternal mourning</note>,
Groaning sadly! But indeed, Minerva, having pitied
Her companion then, addresses her with these words:
"Divine woman," she said, "take back these things which anger brought forth:
It is not through my will that the boy has been deprived of his sight.

### Chapter 81.
## Concerning Ocnus and the Little Donkey: and that in Propertius it is erroneously read as "Ornus."

In the same Propertius, in his fourth book of elegies, two little verses are read thus:

> One more worthy than *Ornus* <note>original: "Orno"</note> to twist the rope sideways:
> and may he feed your eternal hunger, little donkey.

For a long time, I confess, a certain doubt, like a pebble in my shoe <note>original: "scrupulus"</note>, had pricked and goaded my mind: until an ancient manuscript plucked it out, which Bernardinus Valla—a celebrated legal expert and man of the highest nobility in Rome—lent to me about five years ago. There, I read not "Orno," but "Ocno," and I immediately showed what I had read to two very learned young men who had conveniently come to greet me at my home. It is clear, however, that the subject of that Socratic painting <note>Referring to a famous painting by Polygnotus depicting the underworld</note> is being suggested by Propertius: of which Pliny tells thus in the thirty-fifth book of his *Natural History*: "And he is a lazy man, called Ocnus, twisting a rope of *spartum* <note>a type of tough grass used for cordage</note>, which a little donkey gnaws away." But indeed, this lazy Ocnus (as I see) by the allurements of many has suddenly become a wanderer and a "jumper" <note>original: "desultor"; someone who moves rapidly from one thing to another</note>: daily he now changes his seat. Wherefore, so that he may finally rest securely in his wooden seat <note>original: "siliquastro"</note>, trust must be sought especially from that manuscript which we cited: that is (to speak truly) from the root from which the opportunity for this correction sprouted. For thus anyone will understand that I do not claim the works of others for myself, but only my own: and that in my books nothing comes before professional integrity <note>original: "fide"</note>.

### Chapter 82.
## That in the *Digesta*, under the title "On Producing Documents," where it is read "to another," it is more fitting to read "to the white tablet."

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page transitions from a discussion on textual corrections in Propertius to a legal philology note regarding the Roman Digest, followed by a detailed exploration of the Egyptian deity Harpocrates as referenced in the poetry of Catullus and the writings of Plutarch.</meta>

<margin>i</margin>In the title "On Publishing" <note>original: *de edendo*</note>, which is read in the second volume of the *Digests* <note>a collection of Roman legal rulings</note>, the words of Ulpian from the fourth book on the Edict are as follows: "Labeo says that he also 'publishes' <note>presents legal documents</note> who leads his adversary to the white board <note>the *album*, the wooden board where the Praetor's edicts and court schedules were posted</note> and demonstrates what he is about to declare." Thus it is also written in the Florentine Pandects <note>the *Littera Florentina*, a famous 6th-century manuscript of Roman law</note> themselves. Yet all the copies currently in circulation have "to another" <note>original: *ad alium*</note> instead of "to the white board" <note>original: *ad album*</note>. From this, clearly, arose much hesitation for Accursius <note>a 13th-century legal scholar whose glosses were standard in law schools</note>. You should understand it, however, as referring to the Praetor’s white board.

## Chapter 83.
## Concerning Harpocrates.

<margin>e</margin>There is an epigram by Catullus:
> Gelius had heard that his uncle was accustomed to scolding,
> If anyone spoke of or engaged in illicit pleasures.
> To prevent this happening to himself, he thoroughly seduced his uncle’s
> own wife, and thus rendered his uncle a Harpocrates <note>the god of silence</note>.
> He did what he wanted; for though someone now "services" <note>original: *irrumer*, a vulgar term for a sexual act used here as a threat of humiliation to ensure silence</note>
> the uncle, the uncle will not utter a word.

It has long been asked who this Harpocrates might be, whom Catullus mentions both in this place and in another epigram, saying:
> If anything has been committed to a silent and faithful friend,
> Whose deep loyalty of soul is well known:
<margin>m</margin>> You will find me, Cornelius, to be one of those consecrated by that law,
> And consider me to have become a Harpocrates.

However, both in Venice and likewise in Verona (as we have said), nearly eight years ago, I publicly responded to several scholars about him. I now call upon their faith and conscience to witness this, lest I seem to anyone to be claiming the work of others as my own. Plutarch, therefore, in the book he wrote *On Isis and Osiris*, writes that Harpocrates was born from Isis herself and Osiris, who had relations with her after he was already dead. But he was born by a premature birth, and with weak legs. Furthermore, he was brought forth around the time of the winter solstice, incomplete and unformed, while the offspring of the earth were just beginning to flower—or rather, just starting to turn green. For this reason, the first-fruits of the harvest are offered to him. Yet Plutarch says he should not be thought of as an imperfect god, or an infant, or finally as some kind of legume; rather, he is the presiding deity and governor of unrefined and incomplete speech, and of the silence of the young. For this reason, he presses his finger to his lips as a sign...

<summary>This page corrects a common scribal error in Roman legal texts regarding the Praetor's notice board and begins a mythological study of Harpocrates, the Greco-Roman interpretation of the Egyptian god Horus the Child.</summary>
<keywords>Harpocrates, Catullus, Plutarch, Roman Law, Digest, Ulpian, Isis and Osiris, silence, philology</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Egyptian deity Harpocrates, transitioning from his role in Latin poetry to his broader religious significance in Egyptian tradition and his reception by early Christian and late antique scholars.</meta>

...of taciturnity and silence. Indeed, in the month they call Mesore <note>the last month of the ancient Egyptian calendar</note>, while offering the firstfruits of legumes, they are known to say: "The tongue is fortune; the tongue is a demon." Regarding Egyptian trees, they say the peach tree <note>original: *persica*</note> is especially sacred to him, because the fruit is shaped like a heart and the leaf is very much like a tongue.

Thus far from Plutarch <note>a Greek philosopher and biographer</note> concerning Harpocrates, gathered here and there and in intervals. Lucilius, the Greek poet, also mentions him in an epigram to Dionysius, warning that if one has an enemy, he should not pray for Isis or Harpocrates to strike them, nor for the "blind god" <note>likely referring to Plutus, the god of wealth, often depicted as blind and associated with divine anger</note> who makes people angry; rather, the enemy will learn what God and Simo <note>likely a reference to a character known for sternness</note> are capable of. 

But Tertullian <note>an early Christian author from Carthage</note> also says in his *Apology*: "Serapis, Isis, and Harpocrates, with his dog-headed companion <note>original: *cynocephalo*, referring to the god Anubis or Thoth</note>, were forbidden from entering the Capitol—that is, they were expelled from the council of the gods. The consuls Piso and Gabinius, who were certainly not Christians, abolished them and even overturned their altars to restrain the vices of these shameful and idle superstitions. Yet you have restored them and granted them the highest majesty." 

Moreover, Marcus Varro <note>a prolific Roman scholar and contemporary of Caesar</note> writes in his book *On the Latin Language*: "Heaven and earth are the same gods as the Egyptian Serapis and Isis; and if Harpocrates signals with his finger, it is so that you may remain silent." Ovid also understood this in the ninth book of his *Metamorphoses*:

> And holy Bubastis <note>the cat-headed goddess Bastet</note>, and Apis <note>the sacred bull</note> of varied colors,
> And he who suppresses his voice and urges silence with a finger <note>referring to Harpocrates</note>.

Likewise, Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo</note> says in the nineteenth book <note>Though the text says 19, this discussion typically appears in Book 18, Chapter 5 of *The City of God*</note> of *The City of God*: "And since (he says) in almost all temples where Isis and Serapis were worshipped, there was also a statue that seemed to urge silence by pressing a finger to its lips, Varro thinks this signifies that people should keep quiet about the fact that these gods were once mere humans." 

Thus far Augustine. But in the *Letter of Abammon the Egyptian to Porphyry*—which the Platonist Iamblichus is thought to have either translated into Greek or perhaps composed himself—there is mention of a certain "second god." His name in the Egyptian tongue is given as Icheon, and the text shows that he ought to be worshipped with silence. Porphyry <note>a Neoplatonist philosopher</note> also writes at length about this same practice regarding the worship of the highest god in his work *On Abstinence from Animal Food* <note>original Greek: *en tō peri zōōn apochēs*</note>. Indeed, David the prophet similarly begins a certain psalm in this way...

<summary>This page explores the religious and symbolic significance of Harpocrates, the god of silence, through various classical and early Christian perspectives including Plutarch, Varro, and Augustine. It focuses on the iconography of the finger to the lips and the philosophical necessity of silence in divine worship.</summary>

<keywords>Harpocrates, silence, Egyptian mythology, Isis, Serapis, Tertullian, Varro, Saint Augustine, Porphyry, symbolism, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues from a discussion of the Egyptian deity Harpocrates and his associations with silence. It transitions from these religious and literary reflections to a legal commentary on the status of freedmen in Roman law, specifically referencing the works of the Emperor Justinian.</meta>

*Lecha Dumiggia theilla elohim be ziggion* <note>original: "Lecha Dumiggia theilla elohim be ziggion," a transliteration of the Hebrew text of Psalm 65:1: "Praise awaits you, our God, in Zion."</note>

In Latin this means: "To you silence is praise, O God in Zion." However, the Septuagint translators <note>the 70 scholars who produced the standard Greek version of the Hebrew Bible</note> translated it differently, as:
> "A hymn becomes you, O God, in Zion."

For this reason, I believe the Egyptians included Harpocrates in their sacred rites: to show that the highest of the gods should be worshipped in silence. Pliny also mentions Harpocrates in the 33rd book of his *Natural History* as follows: "By now, even men are beginning to wear Harpocrates and statues of Egyptian deities on their fingers" <note>referring to the fashion of wearing signet rings featuring the god of silence</note>. To return to the Lelius of Catullus <note>The author likely means "Gellius" from the poems of Catullus, as the following anecdote matches Catullus's poems regarding Gellius and his uncle</note>: that man acted cleverly so that he would not be scolded by his uncle; having set aside the uncle's wife, with whom he was having an affair, he began to sexually humiliate the uncle himself. By this means, he forced the uncle to be silent, since a person performing such an act <note>original: *fellator*</note> cannot speak.

## Chapter 84.
### Concerning freedmen who are called *dediticii*.

The *Institutes* <term>Institutes: a foundational legal textbook for students of Roman law</term>, which are cited in civil law, were published under the name of the Emperor Justinian, though they were actually compiled by Tribonian and other learned men. They are also found written in Greek with the exact same meaning. The only difference is that certain ancient Roman rituals and customs are reported somewhat more abundantly and broadly in the Greek text than in the Latin version, so that the matter might be fully understood by foreigners who were strangers to Roman ways and customs. But because the description of those freedmen <note>original: *libertinis*</note> who were called *dediticii* <term>dediticii: the lowest class of freed slaves; literally "those who have surrendered"</term> is handled rather strictly and briefly in our Latin *Institutes*, it seemed appropriate to explain it here more extensively and clearly, following the sense of the Greek volume.

Among the ancients, those who were manumitted <note>formally released from slavery</note> attained either a greater and "just" liberty, becoming Roman citizens; or a lesser degree, becoming Junian Latins <term>Junian Latins: a class of freedmen who lived as free people but died as slaves in the eyes of the law, with their property reverting to their former masters</term> under the *Lex Junia Norbana*, which was the originator and authority of that class. Or they attained an even lower status and became *dediticii* under the *Lex Aelia Sentia*. For if anyone, while they were still serving in slavery, had undergone punishment for a crime, such as being branded <note>original: *inscriptus*, referring to a brand or tattoo placed on a slave's forehead as punishment</note>...

<summary>This page concludes a theological and literary discussion on Harpocrates, the god of silence, before transitioning into a legal commentary on the various classes of freed slaves in Roman law according to the Institutes of Justinian.</summary>
<keywords>Harpocrates, silence, Psalm 65, Catullus, Pliny the Elder, Justinian, Tribonian, Roman Law, Institutes, freedmen, dediticii, Lex Junia Norbana, Lex Aelia Sentia</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Roman legal categories from the previous page, specifically concluding the definition of the lowest class of freedmen as established in Justinian's Institutes. It then transitions to a literary and historical commentary on the Roman games dedicated to the goddess Ceres.</meta>

<warning>Bleed-through from the reverse page visible throughout the text.</warning>
n ii

...that is, if they had received marks or brands burned into their foreheads; or if they had been thrown into a public prison; or if they had been whipped for a crime and had confessed to their offense; and then, having later found favor with their Master, were set free, they were called "surrendered freedmen" <note>original: *libertinus dediticius*</note>. This was modeled, clearly, after the "surrendered foreigners" <note>original: *peregrini dedititii*</note>. For when, at some point, certain foreigners who were tributaries of the Romans became arrogant and suddenly took up war against them, they were then conquered by force. Having thrown down their weapons, they surrendered themselves to the victors. The Romans treated them humanely enough that they obtained their lives, but they were marked with this brand of disgrace, so that they were thereafter called "surrendered" because they had surrendered themselves. Therefore, this class of freedmen are also called "surrendered ones," so named by the Aelian-Sentian Law <note>The *Lex Aelia Sentia* of 4 AD, which restricted the manumission of slaves who had committed crimes</note>. Just as they were like those foreigners in having committed a crime, they were called by the same name.

## Chapter 85.
### In what manner the games of Ceres are understood in Juvenal: and concerning Memmius, the founder of the Cerialia.

Juvenal, in his fourteenth satire, says:

> Therefore, you may leave behind all the curtains
> Of Flora, Ceres, and Cybele.

None of the interpreters of this poet have said anything regarding these games of Ceres: neither the name by which they were called, nor what happened in them, nor even by whom they were first celebrated, nor where mention was made of them. Yet each of these points was not foreign to the duty of a good and learned interpreter. For this reason, we must repair this entire oversight, lest the scrupulousness of our concern be found wanting even in small matters. The games of Ceres, then, which are remembered here, were called the *Cerialia*. In these games, horse races were celebrated in the Circus. Gaius Memmius, a Curule Aedile <term>Curule Aedile: a high-ranking Roman magistrate responsible for public buildings, markets, and the organization of festivals</term>, was the first to establish them. Cornelius Tacitus, in the sixteenth book of his *Histories* <note>The author likely refers to the *Annals*, as the surviving books of the *Histories* do not reach sixteen; the event described appears in *Annals* 15.53</note>, speaking of the Emperor Nero, says: "At last they decided to carry out their plans on the day of the Circus games, which are celebrated for Ceres, because the Emperor <note>original: *Caesar*</note>, having rarely left his home or gardens, usually went to the spectacles of the Circus..."

<vocab>Juvenal, Games of Ceres, Cerialia, Memmius, Tacitus, Nero, surrendered, freedman, Aelian-Sentian Law</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the legal origins of "surrendered" freedmen (dediticii) who were previously punished for crimes, and begins a scholarly commentary on the Roman festival of Ceres (Cerialia) mentioned by the satirist Juvenal.</summary>
<keywords>Roman Law, freedmen, Lex Aelia Sentia, Juvenal, Cerialia, goddess Ceres, Gaius Memmius, Tacitus, Nero, Circus games</keywords>

    *(Self-Correction during drafting)*: The Latin phrase "ad Cyathum & uinũ Nicomedi stetiſse" refers to a famous slur against Julius Caesar, suggesting he served as a cupbearer/catamite to King Nicomedes of Bithynia. I should handle this delicately but clearly in the "museum label" style.

    *(Refining the Ovid quote)*:
    "Circus erit pompa celeber/numeroq; deorum/ Primaq; uentosis palma petetur equis."
    -> "The Circus will be crowded with the procession and the assembly of gods, and the first palm of victory will be sought by horses swift as the wind."

    *(Refining the Lorenzo de' Medici mention)*:

    <meta>This page continues the commentary on the Roman games of Ceres (the Cerialia), transitioning from literary analysis of Juvenal to historical and numismatic evidence. It discusses the roles of various Roman officials and the patronage of Lorenzo de' Medici in identifying historical figures.</meta>

    ...frequented them. <note>The text continues from a discussion of commentators who missed details about these games.</note> And a little later: *Until the Cerialian games of the Circus should be celebrated with numerous races of horsemen.* Ovid, in the fourth book of the *Fasti* <note>Ovid’s *Fasti* is a poetic calendar of Roman festivals</note>:

    The Circus will be crowded with the procession and the assembly of gods,
    And the first palm of victory will be sought by horses swift as the wind.

    Regarding these games of Ceres, there is no need for a guide to explain the cause.
    Dio <note>Dio Cassius (c. 155–235 AD), a Greek-language historian of Rome</note>, the writer of history, also narrates in his forty-second book that when Caesar had gained control of affairs, two aediles <note>Roman magistrates responsible for public buildings and festivals</note> were then for the first time created from the patricians, and four from the plebeians. But he writes that those two higher-ranking ones were named after Ceres, and that this practice, from that beginning, has remained up until his own age. The same Dio also says in his forty-seventh book that before the defeat of Brutus <note>Marcus Junius Brutus, the assassin of Caesar, defeated at Philippi in 42 BC</note>, it happened that the plebeian aediles presented gladiatorial contests to Ceres instead of horse races.

    Furthermore, Lorenzo de' Medici <note>The famous ruler of Renaissance Florence and patron of the arts (1449–1492)</note>, a man excelling in every praise, showed us several coins which bear the face of a bearded and unshorn man, with a double title: *Quirinus* and *C. Memmius*. On the reverse side, however, is a small image of Ceres being carried by dragons <note>Ceres was often depicted in a chariot drawn by winged serpents or dragons</note>, with a title of this sort: *Memmius, Curule Aedile, first celebrated the Cerialia.* Here we observed that *Preimus* <note>original: *Preimus*</note> was noted with a diphthong in the first syllable, rather than the standard spelling *primus* [first].

    We believe this man was either Gaius Memmius himself, who was praetor with Lucius Domitius, and who accused Caesar of having stood as a cupbearer at the wine of Nicomedes <note>A reference to the scandalous rumor that Julius Caesar had a sexual relationship with King Nicomedes IV of Bithynia</note> along with the other catamites; he also tore the same Caesar apart with the harshest orations. Later reconciled, he even had Caesar as a supporter in his petition for the consulship. Regarding him, Cicero writes thus in the fourth book of the *Letters to Atticus*: *O what a man, what a citizen Memmius is! All the resources of Caesar support him; they have joined Domitius with him as consul; as to the nature of that pact, I do not dare to commit it to a letter.* And to him, I think—or certainly to his son—the poet Lucretius dedicated his book *On the Nature of Things* <note>original: *de rerum natura*</note>. Indeed, both the dignity of the man and the dates align, unless we prefer to think it was rather the son of that same Gaius Memmius, named after his father, who was then made Curule Aedile by Caesar.

    ...frequented [them]. And a little later: *Until the Cerialian games of the Circus should be celebrated with numerous races of horsemen.* Ovid, in the fourth book of the *Fasti* <note>Ovid's poetic calendar of Roman religious festivals</note>:
    The Circus will be crowded with the procession / and the assembly of gods /
    And the first palm of victory will be sought by horses swift as the wind.
    Regarding these games of Ceres, there is no need for a guide to indicate the cause.

    Dio <note>Dio Cassius, a Roman historian who wrote in Greek</note>, the writer of history, also narrates in his forty-second book / that once Caesar had gained power: two aediles were then for the first time created from the patricians / and four from the plebeians. But those two higher-ranking ones / he writes were named after Ceres / and he writes that this practice has remained from that beginning up to his own age. The same Dio also says in his forty-seventh book / that before the defeat of Brutus <note>The assassin of Julius Caesar, defeated at Philippi in 42 BC</note>, it happened that the plebeian aediles exhibited gladiatorial contests to Ceres instead of horse races. Furthermore, Lorenzo de' Medici / a man excelling in every praise / showed us several coins / which bear the face of a bearded and unshorn man / with a double title: *Quirinus* and *Gaius Memmius*. On the reverse side, however, is a small image of Ceres being carried by dragons <note>Ceres's chariot was traditionally pulled by winged serpents</note> / with a title of this sort: *Memmius, Curule Aedile, first celebrated the Cerialia.* Where we also observed that *Preimus* <note>original: "Preimus"</note> was noted with a diphthong in the first syllable, rather than the standard *primus* [first].

    We think this man / was either Gaius Memmius himself: who was praetor with Lucius Domitius: and who accused Caesar of having stood at the wine-cup of Nicomedes <note>A famous slur against Julius Caesar's youth in the court of King Nicomedes of Bithynia</note> with the rest of the catamites: and who tore the same Caesar apart with the harshest orations: later reconciled / he even had Caesar as a supporter in his petition for the consulship: regarding whom Cicero writes thus in the fourth book of his letters to Atticus: *O what a man, what a citizen Memmius is! All the resources of Caesar support him / they have joined Domitius with him as consul; by what pact / I do not dare to commit to a letter.* And to him, I think: or certainly to his son, the book of the poet Lucretius *On the Nature of Things* <note>original: "de rerum natura"</note> was written. Indeed, both the dignity of the man / and the dates align / unless however we prefer to think / it was rather the son of that same Gaius Memmius, named after his father / who was then made Curule Aedile by Caesar.

    ### Chapter 86.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Roman games of Ceres (the Cerialia), transitioning from literary analysis of Juvenal to historical and numismatic evidence. It highlights the role of various Roman officials and cites evidence from the private collection of Lorenzo de' Medici to identify historical figures, notably Gaius Memmius.</meta>

<warning>Bleed-through from the reverse page visible throughout the text.</warning>
n ii

...frequented [them]. And a little after: *Until the Cerialian games of the Circus / should be celebrated with numerous races of horsemen.* Ovid, in the fourth book of the *Fasti* <note>Ovid's poetic calendar of Roman religious festivals</note>:
*The Circus will be crowded with the procession / and the assembly of gods /*
*And the first palm of victory will be sought by horses swift as the wind.*
Regarding these games of Ceres, there is no need for an indicator of the cause.
Dio <note>Dio Cassius (c. 155–235 AD), a Roman historian who wrote in Greek</note> also, the writer of history, narrates in his forty-second book / now that Caesar had gained control of affairs: two aediles <note>Roman magistrates responsible for public buildings and festivals</note> were then for the first time created from the patri- / cians / and four from the plebeians. But those two higher-ranking ones / he writes were named after Cere- / s, and he writes that from that beginning it has remained up until his own a- / ge: The same Dio also in the forty-seventh book / says that before the defeat of Brutus <note>Marcus Junius Brutus, the assassin of Julius Caesar, defeated at Philippi in 42 BC</note> it happened / that the plebeian aediles exhibited contests of gladia- / tors to Ceres instead of horse races. Furthermore, Loren- / zo de' Medici / a man excelling in every praise / showed several coins / to us which bear the face of a bearded / and unshorn man / with a double title: *Quirinus* and *Gaius Memmius*. On the other side, however, is a small image of Ceres being carried by dragons <note>Ceres's chariot was traditionally pulled by winged serpents or dragons</note> / with a title of this sort: *Memmius, Curule Aedile, first celebrated the Cerialia.* <note>original: "Memmius. Aed. Cur. Cerialia Preimus fecit."</note> Where we also observed that *First* <note>original: "Preimus"</note> was noted with a diphthong in the first syllable, rather than *first* [primus]. We think this man / was either Gaius Memmius himself: who was praetor with Lucius Domitius: and who accused Caesar of even having stood at the wine-cup of Nicomedes <note>A famous slur against Julius Caesar's youth in the court of King Nicomedes of Bithynia</note> / with the rest of the catamites: and who tore the same Caesar apart / with the harshest orations: Later reconciled / he even had Caesar as a suppor- / ter in his petition for the consulship: Regarding whom Cicero writes thus in the fourth book / of his letters to Atticus: *O what a man, what a citizen Memmius is! All the resources of Caesar support him / they have joined Domitius with him as consul; / by what pact / I do not dare to commit to a letter.* And to him, I think: or / certainly to his son, the book of the poet Lucretius, written *On the Nature of Things* <note>original: "de rerum natura"</note>. Since both the dignity of the man / and the dates align / unless however we prefer / to think / it was rather the son of that same Gaius Memmius, named after his / father / who was then made Curule Aedile by Caesar.

<meta>This page transitions from general Roman terminology to a specific philological study of the "tenth wave." It follows a discussion on Roman social and religious customs, now moving into the realm of literary tropes and maritime folklore shared among classical poets.</meta>

# On the tenth or "decuman" wave: which very many poets have mentioned.

The poet <note>Ovid</note> has these verses in the first book of his *Tristia* <note>original: "Tristium," a collection of poems written by Ovid in exile</note>:
> The wave that comes from here towers over all other waves:
> It follows the ninth, and comes before the eleventh.
> <note>original: "Qui uenit hinc fluctus, fluctus supereminet omnes: / Posterior nono est: undecimoq; prior." (Ovid, Tristia 1.2.49-50)</note>

By this, he understands the <term>decuman</term> <note>From the Latin "decumanus," meaning related to the number ten; it was used to describe things of extraordinary size.</note> wave, which is said to be the largest. In the same way, <term>decuman eggs</term> are spoken of, which are the largest eggs; for it is believed that the tenth egg laid is born larger than the rest. From this, the <term>decuman shields</term>—which were of the greatest size—received their name. So too the <term>decuman gate</term> in a military camp, which is the gate turned away from the enemy. Similarly, the <term>decuman boundary</term> <note>original: "decumanus i agro limes"</note> in a field, running from east to west, does not seem to me to be named (as Marcus Junius Nypsus <note>A 2nd-century Roman land surveyor</note> opines) as if it were "two-handed" <note>original: "duocimanus"</note>; for that is far too harsh and forced an explanation. Rather, it is so called because it is the longest and largest. For the "cardo" <note>the north-south axis</note> itself is considered to be halved when the decuman line intercepts it. But concerning this tenth or decuman wave, the same poet <note>Ovid</note> also makes mention in the eleventh book of the *Metamorphoses* with these words:
> Rising up more vastly, the force of the tenth wave rushes on.
> <note>original: "Vastius insurgens decimæ ruit impetus undæ" (Ovid, Metamorphoses 11.530)</note>

And Silius Italicus in his fourteenth book:
> Not otherwise than the north wind rushing headlong from the peak of Mt. Rhodope: 
> When it throws itself in, it drives the sea onto the lands 
> with a tenth surge.
> <note>original: "Non aliter Rhodopes boreas a uertite preceps: / Cum sese imisit decimoq; uolumine pontum / Expulit in terras." (Silius Italicus, Punica 14.121-124)</note>

And also Seneca in the tragedy *Agamemnon*:
> This ship sinks under the weight; that one exposes its shattered side 
> to the waters: the tenth wave rules over this one.
> <note>original: "Hæc onere sidit: illa conuulsum latus / Summittit undis: Fluctus hanc decimus regit." (Seneca, Agamemnon 501-502)</note>

Valerius Flaccus also says this in the second book of the *Argonautica*:
> How great and how often, even with the skill of Pallas <note>The goddess Athena</note>, 
> has the towering swell of the tenth wave crashed down in vain.
> <note>original: "Quanta quoties & Palladis arte / Incassum decimæ cecidit tumor arduus undæ." (Valerius Flaccus, Argonautica 2.52-53)</note>

Lucan as well, in the fifth book of the *Pharsalia*:
> This tenth wave—wondrous to tell—lifts up 
> [his] fate along with the fragile boat.
> <note>original: "Hæc fatum decimus dictu mirabile fluctus / Inualida cum puppe leuat." (Lucan, Pharsalia 5.670-671). This refers to Julius Caesar attempting to cross the sea in a storm.</note>

Thus, even this fact itself is notable and remarkable: that this tenth wave is celebrated by such a "choir" of conspiring poets.

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of classical philology and textual correction.
