## Chapter LXXXVII.
### How a word in Cicero’s letter was corrupted, and then shamelessly changed into the name "Philoctetes" instead of "philotheoros."

In Cicero's letter to Trebatius <note>Gaius Trebatius Testa, a prominent Roman jurist and friend of Cicero</note>—which begins "In the Trojan horse" <note>original: "In equo troiano"</note>—you know that at the very end he writes, "they are wise too late." In almost all copies, the following is found: "Then, as to the fact that you showed yourself no less a **Philoctetes** <note>A Greek hero of the Trojan War, known for being marooned on an island due to a foul-smelling wound</note> in Britain, I certainly do not blame you." For a long time, I had tortured and exhausted myself trying to elicit sense from these words, but nothing came of it. For why would Cicero speak of Philoctetes here? 

But later, through the efforts of Lorenzo de' Medici <note>The famous ruler of Florence and patron of scholars</note>, I obtained that most ancient manuscript which I also cited above; it was much less deformed, defiled, distorted, and confused than the others. In that volume, it was written thus: "Then, as to the fact that you showed yourself no less a **philotheoros** <note>original: "philotheorum," from the Greek "philotheoros," meaning a lover of spectacles or a spectator</note> in Britain, I certainly do not blame you." 

For a <term>philotheoros</term> is one who is fond of sightseeing. This is exactly how Trebatius had behaved in Britain: while avoiding battle, he devoted himself to leisure in the midst of arms. This provided Cicero with material for a joke and a taunt <note>Trebatius was famously unenthusiastic about the military aspects of Caesar's British expedition, preferring to stay in his tent or avoid the front lines</note>.

---

## Chapter LXXXVIII.
### From where the expression "We were Trojans" was drawn; and what is found in literature regarding the Lacedaemonian chorus and song.

"We were Trojans" <note>original: "Fuimus troes," a famous line from Virgil’s Aeneid (2.325) used to describe a greatness that has completely vanished</note>: this figure of speech is so effective because it was drawn from the custom of that Lacedaemonian <note>Spartan</note> chorus and adopted, as it were, into a proverb. For as it is written in Plutarch’s book titled *How a man may praise himself without envy*, the chorus of the Lacedaemonians was threefold: of the Old Men, the Boys, and the Young Men. 

The Old Men sang: 
"We were once brave youths." <note>original: "ἄμμες ποτ' ἦμεν ἄλκιμοι νεανίαι"</note> 
By this, they meant that they had once been robust young men. 

The Boys sang: 
"We <insert>^</insert> indeed shall be much better than you." <note>original: "ἄμμες δέ γ' ἐσόμεθα πολλῷ κάρρονες"</note> 
By which they declared they would be far better than the elders. 

But the song of the Young Men was this: 
"We indeed are [the ones now]; if you wish, look upon us." <note>original: "ἄμμες δὲ γ' εἰμέν· αἰ δέ λῇς· ἀυγάσδεο"</note> 
Indicating that they were already that which...

<meta>The catchword "quod" at the bottom of the page indicates the next page will continue explaining what the young men "already were" in the context of the Spartan song.</meta>

<summary>This page contains two philological corrections: first, restoring the word "philotheoros" (spectator) in a letter of Cicero that had been corrupted to "Philoctetes," and second, tracing the proverb "We were Trojans" to a traditional three-part Spartan choral song.</summary>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philological commentary. After discussing the correction of Cicero's letters and the use of the term "philotheoros," the author concludes his thoughts on the decline of the Milesians and moves into a detailed critique of the grammarian Servius regarding a passage in Virgil’s Bucolics.</meta>

...whether they were such, or whether they hoped these would be so; and they were ready to put the matter to the test. This is written in the collections of Diogenianus <note>A Greek grammarian of the 2nd century AD known for his collection of proverbs</note>. Aristophanes places a similar proverb about the Milesians <note>Inhabitants of Miletus, a Greek city-state famous for its wealth and eventual decline</note> in his comedy *Plutus*: "Once upon a time the Milesians were brave" <note>original: πάλαι ποτ' ἦσαν ἄλκιμοι μιλήσιοι</note>. By this, we are to understand that they are no longer brave men, though we say they once were. With this understanding, Cicero, when dealing with the conspirators, said "they have lived" <note>original: "vixerunt"</note>; by using this word, he declared their death more significantly, and somehow with much less ill-will than if he had plainly said they were "killed."

### Chapter LXXXIX.

->That Servius the grammarian reads the bucolic verses of Maro <note>Virgil, whose full name was Publius Vergilius Maro</note> as incorrectly as he explains them falsely. Certain matters are noted and related there regarding the initiation of boys, and regarding several gods of the ancients, especially Genii and Junos.<-

In the *Bucolics* <note>Virgil's *Eclogues*, a collection of pastoral poems</note> of Virgil, these two short verses are read as follows:
> Begin, little boy: those on whom your parents have not smiled,
> No god has deemed worthy of his table, nor goddess of her bed.
Explaining these, Servius <note>A famous 4th-century commentator on Virgil</note>, a grammarian of no small fame, contaminates—if I may say so with his permission—the true reading and fabricates a story for himself suited to his own convenience, against all authority of the ancients. For it should not be read as "to whom" <note>original: "cui," the dative singular</note>, but "who" <note>original: "qui," the nominative plural</note>; that is, not in the dative case and singular number, but in the nominative plural. Although among the ancients, the same word was written with the same letters in both instances, as the attentive reader will find in the first book of Quintilian’s *On the Education of the Orator* <note>original: "Institutio Oratoria," a foundational Roman work on rhetoric</note>. From this, even now a verse in the seventh book of Valerius Flaccus’s *Argonautica* reads:
> And the care of whom <note>original: "cui," which the author argues should be read as the plural "qui"</note> perhaps another ship holds in flight.
Even now, I say, that verse retains "to whom" <note>cui</note> and not "who" <note>qui</note>, both in the most ancient manuscript from which the rest derived, and in these printed editions. However, regarding our claim that we should read "who" <note>qui</note> and not "to whom" <note>cui</note>, the same Quintilian teaches in his ninth book, declaring it to be a "figure of speech" regarding number, such that a singular is joined to a plural. For regarding those, he says: "Who not..."

<summary>The author concludes a discussion on proverbs regarding past glory and begins a detailed grammatical correction of Virgil's fourth Eclogue, arguing that Servius misinterpreted a plural "who" as a singular "to whom."</summary>
<keywords>Virgil, Servius, Quintilian, Milesians, Cicero, Latin grammar, philology, Eclogues, Valerius Flaccus</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s rigorous critique of the grammarian Servius. On the previous page, the author began questioning Servius's interpretation of a famous couplet from Virgil's fourth Eclogue concerning a child who does not smile at his parents. The author argues that Servius’s reading is philologically and mythologically unsound, preferring the authority of the Roman rhetorician Quintilian.</meta>

...smiled, they have not deemed him worthy. Who would doubt that regarding the integrity of Virgil’s text, Quintilian should be believed rather than Servius? This is especially true since Quintilian sometimes cites the actual handwriting of Virgil and Cicero. Therefore, it is clear, as we have said, that the passage is cited erroneously by Servius. Moreover, who does not see that Servius invented a story for himself out of his own purpose and convenience? For nowhere do we read that Vulcan was cast down <note>Referring to the myth of Vulcan being thrown from Mount Olympus</note> for this reason: because he was born deformed, or because Juno did not smile upon him.

For the poet Homer says he was cast down because he brought help to his mother against his father Jupiter. Furthermore, according to Homer, Vulcan was born from Jupiter and Juno, though Hesiod in his *Theogony* <note>A poem describing the origins and genealogies of the Greek gods</note> feigns that he was born from Juno alone, just as Minerva is said to have come from Jupiter alone. This is why he is called "fatherless" <note>original: ἀπάτωρ (apator)</note> by Theocritus in a riddle titled "The Altar" <note>original: βωμος (bomos)</note>. Wherefore, parents will hardly be referred even properly enough to Jupiter and Juno. 

But it is also quite weak how that same Servius says that Vulcan was neither admitted to the banquets of the gods nor deserved divine honors, even though Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, an ancient Roman scholar</note> counts him as the seventh among the "select gods." Then, the matter would not have proceeded even by the logic that Servius attempts to establish, since he does not say Vulcan was cast down because he did not smile, but because his parent did not smile on him! Let us see if he himself might soon suspect more credible things; certainly, Servius is now refuted by the truest arguments.

And indeed, this is how I would read it and how I would explain it: "Parents of the boy Saloninus <note>Traditionally identified as the son of Virgil's patron Asinius Pollio</note>, of whom we are now treating—let 'parents' be in the vocative case—him, those who did not smile, neither did a god deem worthy of his table nor a goddess of her bed." This means that neither the Genius nor the Juno <note>In Roman religion, every man had a protective spirit called a *Genius*, and every woman had a *Juno*</note> thought this boy worthy of the vital breezes—referring to those who did not smile. According to the testimony of Quintilian, this is a figurative way of saying "this boy who did not smile," or "he who is from the number of those who did not smile." For it was believed that each person had his own god and his own goddess, that is, his own *Genius* and his own *Juno*, the guardians of life. These, therefore, Maro <note>Virgil</note> indicated pre-eminently <note>original: κατ' ἐξοχήν (kat' exochen)</note>. For the table belongs to the Genius, as in:

-> Pour the wine for the Genius: <-

And the bed to Juno. This is also what Junius Philargyrius <note>An early commentator on Virgil</note> teaches, interpreting this very passage thus: "When noble boys are born," he says, "a bed for Juno Lucina <note>The goddess of childbirth</note> is placed in the atrium, along with a table <note>original: mēsura; likely a corruption of *mensa* (table)</note> for Hercules." But also in the works of Varro...

<summary>The author refutes the grammarian Servius’s interpretation of Virgil's Eclogues, arguing that the passage refers to Roman birth rituals involving the Genius and Juno rather than a fabricated myth about Vulcan.</summary>
<keywords>Virgil, Servius, Quintilian, Vulcan, Homer, Hesiod, Genius, Juno Lucina, Philargyrius, Roman birth customs, Saloninus</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s philological debate against the commentator Servius regarding Virgil’s fourth Eclogue. The author is examining the specific Roman deities associated with infancy and birth to prove that Servius’s interpretation of "god and goddess" is incorrect.</meta>

We read that boys are initiated into the gods of eating, drinking, and sleeping—Educa, Potina, and Cuba <note>These are "indigitamenta," minor Roman deities who presided over specific actions of physical development</note>—at the moment they are first moved from milk and the cradle. Donatus <note>A famous 4th-century grammarian</note>, bringing this authority of Varro into his commentary on Terence's *Phormio*, also cited this very verse of Maro <note>Virgil</note> here; he also reported that Probus <note>Marcus Valerius Probus, a Roman grammarian</note> had noted the same. Therefore, the table and the bed are signs of vitality, because they were employed at these very beginnings of life. 

Moreover, man has his own unique property of laughing. But for one who does not laugh, how can life be "worth living" <note>original: "uita uitalis," a phrase famously coined by the early Roman poet Ennius</note>, as Ennius says? However, you cannot refer "god and goddess" <note>Referring to Virgil's line: "On whom his parents have not smiled, no god deems worthy of his table, no goddess of his bed"</note> to these deities of Varro—Educa, Potina, and Cuba—because these are all female, as Augustine also declared in the sixth book of *The City of God*. 

Nor, by the same reasoning, can they be referred to Pilumnus and Pitumnus <note>Also known as Picumnus, these were brothers or partners in Roman myth who protected the household during childbirth</note>, the gods of marriage. Regarding them, Marcianus <note>Martianus Capella, a late antique author</note> says Varro writes in the second book of *The Life of the Ancestors* that if a newborn was healthy [vital], he was immediately lifted up by the midwife and laid on the ground so that he might be omened to stand upright; they were also accustomed to spread a bed in the house for these gods. 

It remains, therefore, that what we have said concerning the Genius <note>A tutelary spirit or "guardian angel" assigned to every man at birth</note> and Juno <note>The female equivalent of the Genius, assigned to every woman</note> should also be supported by the "props" of authorities. Thus Pliny says in the first book of his *Natural History*: "Since individuals make as many gods out of themselves, adopting Geniuses and Junos for themselves." Seneca, in his letter to Lucilius, speaks thus: "I want you to set this aside, however, so that you remember how much our ancestors believed they were Stoics; for they gave both a Genius and a Juno to individuals." From which, I believe, Juvenal says: 

> "And with the master's servant," he says, "swearing by his Juno." 

For the satiric poet criticizes this: that the master's servant swore not by the Genius [the male spirit], but by Juno—evidently in the manner of a woman. Wherefore Quartilla speaks thus in Petronius Arbiter: 

> "May I have my Juno angry at me if I ever remember being a virgin. For as an infant I was defiled <note>original: "inquinata"</note> with my peers, and as the years went by, I applied myself to older boys, until I reached this age." 

From this also I think that proverb arose: it is said that "he who has carried the calf can carry the bull" <note>A reference to Milo of Croton, used here to describe the progression of her experiences from childhood</note>. Otherwise, if it be granted, it is clear that our critique is not unfair, and that the commentary of Servius is now failing—which, before we put forth such things, had been held as beyond doubt.

<summary>The author uses classical authorities like Varro, Augustine, and Pliny to argue that newborns were assigned personal spirits (Genius for men, Juno for women). This evidence is used to dismantle Servius’s interpretation of Virgil, suggesting Servius misunderstood the divine roles in early Roman life.</summary>
<keywords>Educa, Potina, Cuba, Genius, Juno, Varro, Roman childbirth customs, Servius critique, Roman religion, vitality</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from a discussion of Roman household deities and Virgilian commentary to a new chapter where Poliziano addresses his critics and introduces the illustrious circle of scholars who support his work. He specifically sets the stage to critique a translation by the famous scholar Theodore Gaza.</meta>

The remaining matters we will leave in the middle—lest we flatter ourselves—to those who wish to form an independent opinion.

## Chapter 90.
### **What kind of man Theodore Gaza was: and yet how much he slipped in translating a problem of Aristotle.**

I do not refuse to come under judgment or the roll of the dice; whatever this may be that I write, provided that in this list <note>original: "albo," referring to the white tablet used for public notices in Rome</note> two literary plagues I most suspect are not present: ignorance and envy. I hope that no judge happens upon me who is "more unmusical" <note>original: "amusoteros," a Greek term used by Symmachus to describe someone lacking in culture or education</note>, or "Opican" <note>original: "opicus," a term used by the ancients to describe someone unrefined or barbarous</note>, or harsh, stubborn, and sworn to a man’s words rather than to the faith of truth. 

If judges are to be named, I do not want Tarentines, Cosentines, or Sicilians to read these works of mine—as Lucilius <note>Gaius Lucilius, the Roman satirist, who famously said he wrote for the moderately learned rather than the most or least educated</note> said of his own—but Latin men, not ignorant of Greek literature. I do not want a Junius Congius <note>A legal and historical writer mentioned by Lucilius as a mediocre reader</note>, but a Scipio, or the most learned of all, a Persius or a Rutilius. That is to say, I seek men who are their equals: men lean in body but rich in wisdom, talent, and learning, for whose judgment one should rightly toil. 

Such a man, if I may say so, is he himself who supplies me with so many tools for study and deep leisure, descendant of grandfathers and great-grandfathers <note>Poliziano here adapts a famous line from Horace’s first Ode to praise his patron</note>: Lorenzo de' Medici, the pillar of the Florentine Republic. Either states or princes experience his most circumspect judgment and natural depth of mind in the action of great affairs; likewise, those who have not looked as deeply within as those close to him do not fail to admire him in letters and the studies of humanity and wisdom. 

Such also is my friend Giovanni Pico, of the Princes of Mirandola, a most perfect work of nature; from whom philosophy, now meditating on speaking in Latin, will, I believe, reach its highest peak. Such, moreover, are those two bound by a single bond of love: the Venetian patricians, specifically Hermolaus Barbarus <note>Ermolao Barbaro (1454–1493), a Venetian scholar famous for his "Castigationes Plinianae" and for purifying Latin translations of Aristotle</note>, the fiercest enemy of "barbarism," who has provided the arms and tools of words for Latin philosophy with a most diligent ear...

<summary>Poliziano defines the ideal, learned audience for his work—including Lorenzo de' Medici and Pico della Mirandola—while preparing to critique a translation error by Theodore Gaza.</summary>
<keywords>Theodore Gaza, Aristotle, Lorenzo de' Medici, Pico della Mirandola, Ermolao Barbaro, humanism, philology, literary criticism</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s defense of his scholarship. He has just finished listing the elite circle of scholars whose approval he seeks—men of deep learning who "polish or fashion anew" the tools of Latin letters to match the Greeks. He now introduces Girolamo Donato and begins a detailed philological critique of the famous translator Theodore Gaza regarding Aristotle’s theories on melancholy and genius.</meta>

...polishes or fashions anew on the anvil; so that through his industry, we now live almost on equal terms with the Greeks, whether in polish or in abundance of this kind. 

And Girolamo Donato <note>Girolamo Donato (c. 1457–1511) was a Venetian nobleman, diplomat, and humanist known for his Greek scholarship</note>: a man I know not whether to call more grave, or more learned, or even more refined; certainly overflowing with every charm and elegance. Yet I fear his judgment might seem too lenient to some, because he has praised me wonderfully in a most beautiful poem, as well as in one or two letters. For though it might be shameful to believe so much good of myself—I who am far from tolerable, let alone perfect—it is even more shameful to disbelieve such a man when he affirms such things about me, as he clearly does, from the heart. 

Therefore, as long as these few men, or those like them (if any exist), look with favor upon these works of ours, whatever they may be, I now consider as naught <term>floccipendo: a Latin verb meaning to value something at the price of a tuft of wool; to care nothing for</term> those mere images and ghostly shadows whose nature it is—to use the words of a holy man—to terrify children and to chatter in dark corners. 

Theodore Gaza <note>A pioneering Greek scholar (c. 1398–1475) who fled the Fall of Constantinople and became a central figure in the Italian Renaissance, particularly for his translations of Aristotle</note>, then, a Greek man and, as even the learned believe, most erudite, translated Aristotle's *Problems* into Latin. But in that problem where it is asked why we see that all men who have excelled in talent—whether in the study of philosophy, or in the administration of the state, or in the composition of poetry, or in the practice of the arts—were melancholics, some to such an extent that they were even troubled by the afflictions of black bile <term>atra bilis: literally "black bile," the humor thought to cause both melancholy and extraordinary genius</term>; in that very problem, there appeared (unless I am mistaken) an error by the translator so manifest and notable that it can no longer be excused or ignored. 

For when Aristotle offers examples there of heroes who were said to have suffered from black bile, and deals first with Hercules, he concluded that Hercules was indeed of such a temperament by these sorts of signs and proofs: that the "falling sickness" <term>comitialis morbus: epilepsy, so named because its occurrence could dissolve a Roman public assembly, or *comitia*</term> was named "sacred" after him; that he killed his sons in a fit of madness, as is treated in Seneca’s tragedy; and that before he died, he suffered from an eruption of teeming ulcers. From this, I believe, the story of that "poisoned tunic" <note>original: "nexea tunica," the Tunic of Nessus which, according to myth, clung to Hercules' skin and burned him</note> originated in myth, for this too is an affliction of black bile. From this same cause, he says, that kind of ulcer also broke out on Lysander the Spartan <note>A famous Spartan admiral and general who defeated the Athenians at Aegospotami</note> shortly before his death. 

Theodore, therefore, concerning the children and the ulcers of Hercules, translates it thus: "The *folly* of the boys," <note>original: "Puerorum... morio mentis," where Gaza seems to have mistranslated a Greek word for 'madness' as 'folly' or 'buffoonery'</note> he says, "expresses this same thing, as does the eruption of ulcers which sometimes precedes death." Whereas in the Greek it is written...

<summary>Poliziano praises the scholar Girolamo Donato and begins a critique of Theodore Gaza’s translation of Aristotle’s "Problem 30.1." He argues Gaza failed to accurately capture Aristotle's description of Hercules' madness and physical ailments as symptoms of melancholy.</summary>

<meta>The previous page introduced Girolamo Donato and began a critique of Theodore Gaza’s translation of Aristotle’s *Problemata*. Poliziano is specifically focusing on Aristotle’s discussion of melancholy (black bile) in heroes, using the example of Hercules.</meta>

*“and the frenzy regarding his children, and the eruption of sores that occurred before his disappearance.”* <note>original: "καὶ ἡ περὶ τοὺς παῖδας ἔκστασις καὶ ἡ πρὸ τῆς ἀφανίσεως αὐτοῦ ἐπὶ τῇ ἑλκῶν ἔκφυσις γενομένη"</note> Nor indeed would I doubt that it is useful, before we translate the passage itself, to weigh more diligently what that phrase *on the* <note>original: "ἐπὶ τῇ"—Poliziano is questioning a specific Greek prepositional phrase in the text</note> intends, which Gaza ignores, or what meaning can be drawn from that preposition and article if they are separated. And I believe it should be read and corrected as *on Mount Oeta* <note>original: "ἐν οἴτῃ"—a brilliant philological correction by Poliziano, suggesting the original text referred to the location of Hercules' death</note>, so that the literal sense is: "And in his children, terror—or if you prefer, a displacement of fear—and before his own death, an eruption of sores on Mount Oeta." 

As for why we preferred to translate *Ecstasis* <note>original: "ἔκστασις"—literally a "standing outside" of oneself; a state of frenzy or madness</note> as "terror" or "displacement of the mind," rather than as Gaza did, "motion of the mind," we follow authorities in the former: Hilary of Poitiers <note>A 4th-century bishop and theologian</note>, an esteemed interpreter of the Psalms; and in the latter, Jerome <note>St. Jerome, the translator of the Vulgate Bible</note>, who nevertheless in some places also rendered that word *Ecstasis* simply as "displacement" in Latin. For Cicero also defines terror as a fear that moves the mind from its place. 

But regarding the name, we shall speak another time. Now to the matter at hand. Aristotle brings forward examples through which it becomes clear that Hercules was also tormented by <term>black bile: "atra bile," the humor associated with melancholy and, in excess, with both madness and genius</term>, just as he later shows of Ajax, Bellerophon, and the others. Thus, the sense, the order, the logic, and moreover the unassailable and invincible truth, completely support our interpretation. For why would one discuss the mental displacement of children here in the midst of discussing heroes? Or what is further removed from this black bile, which we call madness, than the nature of children? Why does Gaza take this eruption of sores before death as a general occurrence, which neither Aristotle’s words signify, nor matches his clearly stated purpose? Indeed, he strives to prove by certain arguments and examples that not children, nor just anyone, are troubled by black bile, but heroes and the greatest of men. 

Furthermore, one must consider even more carefully: whether only the disease "Hercules" is being named, as Aristotle says, or whether even Hercules himself is called by it, as Theodore Gaza adds of his own accord. And this same passage was poorly translated by Pietro d'Abano <note>A famous 13th-century physician and philosopher (c. 1250–1316)</note> as well—to whom the surname "The Conciliator" <note>From his famous work *Conciliator Differentiarum Philosophorum et Medicorum*</note> was given because of his great skill in the nature of things and medicine! But as those were the times, I am less indignant toward a man who was insufficiently skilled in both languages <note>Poliziano is critiquing the medieval lack of Greek scholarship compared to the new Renaissance standards</note>. Yet he betrays his own error by his own evidence in a certain commentary composed on these very *Problems*. For he had cast himself into such difficulties...

...so that he is able to explain nothing: and I am forced to say that "stupor" (or what he himself calls the "freezing" <note>original: "congelationem"—Gaza's choice of word for a medical or psychological state</note> that happens to children) is named from that source; that is (as I believe) from Hercules. Which I would grant him now, if any word of that naming were pronounced from that same origin. But indeed, anyone may now know that Theodore’s <note>Theodore Gaza (c. 1398–1475), a leading Greek scholar whose translations Poliziano is rigorously correcting</note> case is easily pushed aside, prostrated, and bound, even if we add nothing further. 

Nay, I believe that if he were to come back to life and be warned of this, he would in no way defend it as correct—if indeed he gave more to truth than to his own bias. Rather, as is customary in the game of "Twelve Lines" <term>Twelve Lines: *duodecim scriptis*, an ancient Roman board game similar to backgammon</term>, since he would repent of the move once it was made, he would ask that it be granted to him to "pull back his stone" <note>An idiom meaning to retract a statement or undo a mistake, like taking back a move in a game</note>. 

Nor would we note these things about him if we did not value him highly. For we are not so possessed by a mad leisure that, while our own dignity is intact, we would wish to abuse it against the recent and already too abundant crop of trivial trifles—and against those (I might almost say) beggarly rags and shameful things of chatterers. I know not whether they should be exploded with laughter or ignored in silence, so that these forever ignoble men may hide among their own follies. 

However, we have brought this forward only—not indeed for the sake of "caviling" <note>original: "obstrigillandi"—to find fault or obstruct</note>, as they say—but rather to warn scholars who patiently endure and have undertaken this most laborious and most envious burden of writing. Let them never relax their focus, nor spare any industry or labor; nor should they indulge themselves or yield to the weight of the task. They should look around on all sides, balance, weigh, and demand perfection in every detail; they should sniff out everything, shake it out, examine it, and correct it again and again, and bring it back more often to the anvil <note>A reference to Horace’s *Ars Poetica*, suggesting that writing must be forged and hammered into shape</note>. 

And meanwhile, let them consult even the less learned, and let them not despise the "small strengths" (if I may say so) of the more humble. For it is almost always the case that we are "Lynx-eyed" <note>original: "lyncei"—referring to Lynceus, the Argonaut famous for his keen sight</note> regarding the errors of others, but bleary-eyed and blind regarding our own; and we do not see, as Catullus says:

> That part of the knapsack which is on our back. <note>original: "Manticae quod in tergo est"—a reference to Catullus 22.21 and the fable that we carry our own faults in a bag behind us where we cannot see them</note>

But above all, let those who write beware of this: lest, while they hasten too much toward the desire for praise, they propel their still-raw studies into the public...

<meta>Catchword: propel</meta>

...they call. I confess that I wonder at this regarding Theodore: why he wrote as he did in the preface to his books on Aristotle’s *History of Animals*—the very works for which he is most highly praised—claiming that he was helped by no one and that he did not even need to compete with other translators, whom he says are no trouble at all to surpass. He claims this despite the fact that George of Trebizond <note>George of Trebizond (1395–1486) was a Greek philosopher and rival to Gaza; their feud was legendary in Renaissance intellectual circles</note> had already translated those same books so brilliantly before him. By either restoring terms he found among the ancients or by coining and devising new words himself that reflect a truly Latin character <note>original: "Latiam" (OCR reads "lariam") — referring to the Latin language or spirit</note>, George was, I believe, the first of the modern scholars to teach us that it is our own fault, rather than a lack in the language, that we have fewer names for things than the Greeks do. 

Therefore, if anyone reads George’s books diligently, he will certainly have less praise for Gaza, who follows almost exactly in George’s footsteps. Indeed, if a reader is a man of integrity, I believe he will be annoyed that Theodore so thoroughly concealed his debt to the very man from whom he profited most—treating him with almost contempt and mockery, even though it was primarily George's labors that he was plundering. 

Perhaps George will even be grateful to us for striving to restore these works to their rightful authors. For George’s own pride in that work is clear from the words in his own preface, where he says he is giving these books to the Latins in a form no less elegant or corrected than they are found among the Greeks. 

Yet Theodore even tries to claim for himself the things George produced in Greek regarding the months, which were largely gathered from the very preface of the translation we are discussing. But there were (as is apparent) the most bitter enmities between these Greeks. It reached such a point that even in his grammatical commentary, Theodore calls George "Pornoboscos" <note>original: *Pornoboscon* — Greek for "brothel-keeper"</note>, which means someone who keeps harlots. He was alluding, I think, to the ancient Greek grammarian George, whom the Greeks nicknamed "Choeroboscos" <term>Choeroboscon: Greek for "pig-feeder"; Gaza is turning a traditional academic nickname into a crude insult</term>. 

As for the other things Theodore published here: provided he is not to be accused of literary theft <note>original: *repetundarum* — a legal term for extortion, here applied to plagiarism</note>, just as they do not lack erudition and diligence, so they are (it seems to me) in need of a stricter and more careful examination. In this place, we do not wish to tarnish the bright names of learned men in the judgments of the unskillful, nor yet do we wish to hide the truth. For what...

...whatever we might miss in him, or wish to pass over without honor: let truth itself be his advocate. However, just as we want our style to be as noble and prepared as possible, we also want it to be as little accusatory, licentious, or harmful as possible. For we do not profess a talent for cunning so much as we display our industry. What we once thought of the learning and talent of Theodore <note>Theodore Gaza (c. 1398–1475), a leading Greek scholar of the Renaissance who fled to Italy</note>, we have testified in several Greek and Latin epigrams; of these, we will now subjoin just one, and a Greek one at that—provided that someone still reads these things, captured by a love for learning. It is as follows:

->Great Theodore Gaza once lay here, between<-
->the Ausonian <note>Ausonian: a poetic term for Italian or Latin</note> and Heliconian <note>Heliconian: referring to Mount Helicon, the home of the Greek Muses</note> Muses.<-
->For to the latter he owed his birth, and to the former his upbringing;<-

->He excelled equally in the wisdom and the tongue of both.<-
->Neither in life nor in death did he choose one over the other;<-
->instead, he chose to lie in great Italy upon Greece,<-
->so that the glory of both might be shared.<-
<note>original: "κειτο μέγας ποτ’ἄγων γαζη θεοδώροιο ἀμφί..." — This epigram celebrates Gaza’s unique status as a bridge between the Greek and Latin intellectual worlds.</note>

### Chapter 91.

->How the same man translated into Greek a passage found in Cicero,<-
->“The Marrow of Persuasion,” and the polished verses of Eupolis about<-
->Pericles: and by what Latin names the goddess Peitho is designated.<-

Theodore Gaza himself translated Cicero’s golden little book *On Old Age* <note>Latin: *De Senectute*</note> into the Greek language, certainly not unsuitably or unsuccessfully. But in this work too (to avoid saying anything more severe) he is occasionally a little bit less diligent. Let this meanwhile serve as proof: where Cicero mentions briefly that Marcus Cethegus <note>Marcus Cornelius Cethegus (died 196 BC), a Roman consul famous for his eloquence</note> was called by Ennius “The Marrow of Persuasion,” <note>original: "Suadae medulla"</note> Theodore interpreted that phrase “Marrow of Persuasion” as *sweet-speaking* <note>original: "μελίγηρυν" (meligerin)</note>, as if you were to say he was simply pleasant in speech. Yet Cicero himself, in his *Brutus*, explains this passage by Ennius and the words quite fully. Persuasion...

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Theodore Gaza’s translation work. Having previously praised Gaza’s general mastery of both Greek and Latin, the author now focuses on a specific error in Gaza's translation of a passage from Cicero’s *Brutus*, where Gaza failed to capture the nuances of "the marrow of Persuasion."</meta>

...Pericles.

"The marrow," he says, which the Greeks call **Peitho** <note>original: Pitho; the personification of Persuasion in Greek mythology</note>, and of which the orator is the producer—this, **Ennius** <note>Quintus Ennius (c. 239–169 BCE), an early Roman poet often called the father of Roman poetry</note> called **Suada** <note>Suada: the Roman goddess of persuasion, the equivalent of the Greek Peitho</note>. He wishes **Cethegus** <note>Marcus Cornelius Cethegus, a Roman consul and orator praised by Cicero for his charm</note> to have been the "marrow" of this goddess; just as **Eupolis** <note>Eupolis (c. 446–411 BCE), a famous writer of Old Comedy in Athens</note> wrote that a goddess sat upon the lips of **Pericles**, so our orator [Cicero] says that this man [Cethegus] was the "marrow" of Persuasion. Thus far Cicero in the *Brutus*. From this, it is evident that the passage was certainly not understood by **Theodore** [Gaza]. For he should not have interpreted it as *honey-voiced* <note>original: μελίγηριν (meligeryn)</note>, but rather as the *marrow of persuasion* <note>original: πειθοῦς μυελὸν (peithous muelon)</note>. Yet we shall not argue this at greater length, for it is not a matter for a dispute over words, since **Marcus Tullius [Cicero]** is himself its judge and witness.

Since, however, Marcus Tullius also cites Eupolis—noting that this goddess of persuasion "sat upon the lips of Pericles"—and since he himself calls her *Peitho*, while Ennius calls her *Suada*: come now (if you please), let us set down the very verses from Eupolis’s *Demes* <note>original: Δήμοις (Demoi); a lost play by Eupolis where famous Athenian leaders were brought back from the dead</note> (for that is the name of the comedy). I believe it will be pleasing to students if we perform a sort of "gleaning" <term>spicilegium; literally the gathering of fallen grain after a harvest</term> or "grape-gathering" <term>racematio; the picking of small clusters of grapes left by the harvesters</term>; or if we restore to a single body these tablets, as it were, collected from this shipwreck of literature. These are the verses we find:

->He was the mightiest of men at speaking,<-
->whenever he came forward. Like the best runners,<-
->he overtook the other rhetors by sixteen feet.<-
->Do you call him "swift"? Besides his speed,<-
->a certain Persuasion sat upon his lips.<-
->So did he cast a spell; and alone among the orators,<-
->he left his sting remaining in his listeners.<-

However, we did not drink these verses of the poet Eupolis directly from his own springs—seeing as his works have been lost to time—but rather we received some of them from a certain very accurate commentator on **Aristides the Rhetor**, and others from a letter of **Pliny the Younger**. Indeed, Aristides himself, in the oration entitled *For Pericles*, invokes the testimony of that same comic poet, speaking thus: "and a certain Persuasion sat alone upon his lips." 

That which Ennius called *Suada*, I believe **Horace** called the same as *Suadela* <note>Suadela: a synonymous name for the goddess of Persuasion</note>. As he says, "Fair-Speech [*Suadela*] and Venus grace the well-monied man." Nor do I agree with **Porphyrio** <note>Pomponius Porphyrio, a 3rd-century scholar who wrote commentaries on Horace</note>, who thinks this is merely an epithet of Venus. For Cicero, in the third book of *On the Orator*...

<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion of how ancient authors personified the power of eloquence. Having previously discussed Theodore Gaza’s translation of "the marrow of persuasion," the author now explores the different Latin terms used to describe this divine quality, specifically "Charm" (Lepos) versus "Persuasion" (Suada).</meta>

...when speaking of Pericles, he preferred to name it **Charm** <note>original: Leporem; referring to wit or grace of speech</note> rather than **Persuasion** <note>original: Suadam</note>, saying: "The ancient comic poets—even when they were speaking ill of him, which was permitted in Athens at that time—said that **Charm** dwelt upon his lips, and that such power was in him that he left a kind of sting in the minds of those who heard him." On the other hand, **Quintilian** <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus (c. 35–100 CE), a Roman rhetorician whose *Institutes of Oratory* became a standard textbook</note>, not even daring to utter the Latin name of that goddess [Suada], writes thus in the tenth book of his *Institutes of Oratory*: "And that testimony of ancient comedy concerning Pericles could most justly be transferred to this man [Cicero]: that a certain goddess of persuasion sat upon his lips." Furthermore, **Martianus** <note>Martianus Capella (5th century CE), author of a famous allegorical encyclopedia of the seven liberal arts</note> calls her sometimes **Persuasion** and sometimes **Peitho** <note>The Greek name for the goddess of persuasion</note>. We ourselves, in our poems *Manto* and *Ambra*, have called her sometimes **Persuasion** and sometimes **Charm**, evidently following the examples of Ennius and Cicero.

### Chapter 82.

**Who the *Causarii* are in the works of Livy.**

**Livy** <note>Titus Livius (64 BCE – 17 CE), the author of a monumental history of Rome</note> says in the seventh book of *From the Founding of the City*: "Let a third army be enrolled by **Titus Quintius** from the **causarii** <note>soldiers discharged for medical reasons; "invalidated" soldiers</note> and the elders, to serve as a guard for the city walls." You will not easily find someone who can readily explain to you who the *causarii* are; but you may easily gather it from the words of the jurist **Ulpian** <note>Domitius Ulpianus (c. 170–223 CE), one of the most cited legal authorities in Roman law</note> in the sixth book of his *Commentary on the Edict*. These words are written in the third volume of the **Digest** <note>The *Pandects* or *Digest*, a massive compilation of Roman legal opinions commissioned by Emperor Justinian I</note>, under the title concerning those who are "marked with infamy." The words are as follows: "Dismissed for reason of ignominy—this was added because there are many kinds of discharges. There is the **honorable discharge** <note>original: honesta [missio]</note>, which is granted when the term of service is complete or earlier by the emperor’s favor; and the **medical discharge** <note>original: Causaria</note>, which releases a man from the labors of military service because of ill health." From these words, we can reason that *causarii* is the proper name for soldiers dismissed for reasons of health.

**A certain preface of Emperor Justinian vindicated from some faults and errors.**

<meta>This page transitions from the author's previous discussion of legal and rhetorical terminology to a critique of textual corruptions in the works of Justinian. It also introduces a new chapter regarding biblical commentary, featuring the author's collaboration with the philosopher Pico della Mirandola. Note: the chapter numbering jumps from 82 on the previous page to 84 here, suggesting a missing section or a numbering error in the original manuscript.</meta>

I am sometimes ashamed of this age in which I was born—an age both too unlearned and too arrogant—as I repeatedly consider what monsters of things and words have occupied even the very law books by which we are governed. For instance, I recently noted, laughing yet perhaps also annoyed, in the letter of **Justinian** <note>Justinian I (482–565 CE), the Byzantine Emperor who codified Roman law</note>—which is placed before the books of the **Digest** <note>The *Digest* or *Pandects* is a massive compilation of juristic writings forming part of the *Corpus Juris Civilis*</note> and addressed to the illustrious men and professors **Theophilus, Dorotheus, Theodorus, Isidore, Anatolius, Thallelaeus, Cratinus,** and the most eloquent **Salaminius**. In that letter, the Emperor teaches that fourth-year students were called by the Greek term **lyras** <note>original: *lyras*; a term for students who had completed certain core requirements</note>, and likewise those of the fifth year **prolytas** <note>original: *prolytas*</note>, as it stands in those original archetypes <note>The most authoritative or earliest manuscript copies</note> of the **Pandects**. Yet, all current copies now have **goats** <note>original: *hircos*</note> in the first place and **colored ones** <note>original: *coloritas*</note> in the second—words of absolutely no meaning! Yet, these are nonetheless explained by **Accursius** <note>Accursius (c. 1182–1263), a medieval jurist famous for his legal commentaries</note> as if they were perfectly suitable. Furthermore, the first-year students should be called not **dupondii** <note>original: *dupondii*; literally "two-pounders," a mocking nickname for freshmen</note> as is common, but *dupondii* as I find in the archetypes—for they called them by that frivolous and ridiculous nickname, as he also says. But also in that same letter (to point this out in passing as well), the words from the sixth rhapsody of the **Iliad** are missing where the Emperor mentions **Glaucus** and **Diomedes** exchanging unequal things. For immediately after that word, this verse of **Homer** ought to follow, if we trust the archetype:

->Gold for bronze, a hundred oxen's worth for nine.<- <note>original Greek: χρύσεα χαλκείων εκατόμβοια ἐννεαβοίων; referring to *Iliad* 6.236</note>

### Chapter 84.
A charming commentary by **Severian** <note>Severian of Gabala (c. 380–408 CE), a popular preacher and biblical scholar</note> concerning the making of the moon and the sun, and regarding the monthly days and years.

Pico, at any rate, compared all their opinions, as well as those of the Latins, most diligently with the views of the **Jewish** and **Chaldean** interpreters...

<meta>This page continues the author's scholarly investigations, shifting from a critique of legal transcriptions to a theological and astronomical discussion regarding the creation of the celestial bodies. It maintains the collaborative spirit mentioned on the previous page, likely involving insights shared with Pico della Mirandola.</meta>

...there is nothing he does not examine and investigate, from which truth might be drawn out, or obscurity removed, or faith strengthened, or impiety refuted. But what I remember us reading a few days ago in the works of **Severianus** <note>Severian of Gabala (d. after 408 CE), a popular preacher and bishop known for his six sermons on the creation of the world (*Cosmology*)</note>—a most brilliant author—seemed not at all unworthy of being included in this collection. The **Hebrew lawgiver** <note>original: *Hebreus*; referring to Moses, traditionally considered the author of Genesis</note> records that the **Sun** and **Moon** were made by God at the beginning; specifically, on the fourth day after He had commenced the creation of the world. However, since He appointed the one over the day and the other over the night, it can be believed that He placed the former [the Sun] at the boundary of the east and the latter [the Moon] at the boundary of the west. 

Moreover, God ought not (as this author indeed opines) to have fashioned the Moon from the very beginning in such a way that it was diminished, plucked away, slender, divided, empty, washed out, or exhausted of light—such as we see it now when it is new, or between months, or waning, or in conjunction, or even in an actual eclipse. Rather, it was created with a full orb and a complete circle: not **gibbous** <note>original: *gibbosa*; the phase where more than half but less than all of the moon is illuminated</note>, not swollen, not curved, not crescent-shaped, not divided, and not "silent" <note>original: *silens*; a term used in ancient astronomy for the New Moon, when it is invisible</note>. For that subsequent multiformity of seasons, nights, and days was to serve as a marker of intervals and distinctions. 

Therefore, when the Sun was newly brought forth and entering its morning gates, the Moon, directly opposite, held the very boundary of the west—a gift-bearer of the orb for the first time, and in its, so to speak, "apprenticeship." But as the Sun itself circled toward the west, the Moon in turn was carried by its own course to the border of the east. Thus, obedient to the command, they presided equally over day and night. 

But why did the face of a "fourth-day moon" <note>The crescent shape the moon usually has four days after its new phase</note> not show itself then, since that was the day on which it was brought forth? Because, he says, a fourth-day moon would have been unable to hold the furthest limit of the west. Thus, with a rounded appearance and a completely full face, displaying a fifteen-day-old moon instead of a four-day-old one, it had, as it were, claimed eleven days for itself—and the Moon is naturally outstripped by the Sun by exactly that many days. From this it follows that the Moon, as if in good faith, rightfully restores those eleven days to the Sun. For since the monthly calculation of the Moon is twenty-nine and a half days, the annual total for twelve months results from exactly 354 days. This total, adding to itself another eleven days—which the Moon at the beginning, like a surplus...

<summary>The author cites Severian of Gabala to argue that the Moon was created full on the fourth day of Creation to perfectly balance the Sun and establish the 11-day difference between lunar and solar years.</summary>
<keywords>Severian of Gabala, Creation, Sun, Moon, astronomy, lunar cycle, Genesis, epact, 354 days</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the astronomical discussion from the previous page regarding the state of the Moon at the moment of its creation. The author moves from this theological-scientific debate into philological corrections of legal and poetic texts, specifically addressing the "Digest" of Roman law and the epigrams of Martial.</meta>

...had presumed, he established this solar year of 365 days. Thus far **Severianus** <note>Severian of Gabala, whose work on the creation was introduced on the previous page</note>—with a commentary I find more charming than true—by which that legendary exaggeration about the antiquity of the **Arcadians** <note>A people of the central Peloponnese in Greece who claimed to be older than the moon</note> could grow immeasurably. They are called **proselenae** <note>original Greek: *proselenoi*</note>, which means "before the moon." If it were admitted that the moon was already eleven days old—not just by the age of the world itself, but also older than its own initial day—it would support their claim of being "pre-lunar."

### Chapter 95.
## An elegant Greek adage restored in the copies of the *Digest* concerning guest-gifts.

I am free, for the sake of legal scholars, to restore a Greek proverb that has been completely removed from their books but remains intact in the archetype. It is found in a certain letter of the Divine **Severus** <note>Emperor Septimius Severus (r. 193–211 CE)</note> and the Emperor **Antoninus** <note>Emperor Caracalla (r. 198–217 CE)</note>. This letter is cited by **Ulpian** <note>One of the great Roman jurists whose writings form a large part of Justinian's legal code</note> in the first book of *On the Office of the Proconsul*, and it is placed under the same title in the first book of the **Digest** <term>Digest: A massive compilation of Roman legal opinions commissioned by Emperor Justinian in the 6th century</term>. These are the words of the letter, which, since they are most elegant and full of useful wisdom, I believe should be learned by heart by those who are to govern the Republic: "As far as **xenia** <term>xenia: Formal gifts given to guests or provincial officials, which were strictly regulated to prevent bribery</term> are concerned, hear what we think. There is a Greek proverb: 'Neither everything, nor always, nor from everyone' <note>original Greek: ὄυτε πάντα . ὄυτε πάντοτε . ὄυτε παρὰ πάντων (*oute panta, oute pantote, oute para panton*)</note>. For it is very inhumane to accept from no one, but to accept indiscriminately is most base, and to accept everything is most greedy." Thus they wrote. Furthermore, you could translate the Greek words in this manner: "Not everything, not at all times, and not from everyone."

### Chapter 96.
## On the great lustfulness of mice, and an interpretation of the words of Aelian, and therein a passage in Martial corrected and explained.

In the seventh book of the **Epigrams** <note>A collection of short, witty poems by the Roman poet Martial (c. 38–103 CE)</note>, it appears this way in the common manuscripts:
> For when you call me "life," when you call me your "eyes"!
<note>original Latin: *Nam cum me vita: cum me tua lumina dicis!*</note>

But indeed, all the oldest copies have it thus:

<summary>The author concludes a discussion on the Moon's creation before turning to philological corrections of legal texts (the Digest) and classical poetry (Martial), focusing on a Greek proverb about the ethics of gift-giving for officials.</summary>
<keywords>Severianus, Arcadians, proselenae, Ulpian, Digest, xenia, gift-giving, Roman Law, Martial, epigrams, philology</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philological corrections of classical texts. Following the previous page's discussion of Greek adages in legal codes, the author now addresses a specific term of endearment in Latin poetry—the word "mouse"—before moving on to correct a corruption in the text of Suetonius's "Life of Claudius."</meta>

"For when you call me 'mouse,' and when you call me 'my light.'" <note>original: "Nam cum me murem: tu cum mea lumina dicis." This is a quote from Martial, Epigrams 11.29.3-4.</note>

This is a point which, until now, no one has noticed or corrected. One might truly ask: in what sense does a girl, when flattering her lover, call him a "mouse"? Is it in the same sense that she is also accustomed to calling him her "little sparrow"? Perhaps it is because mice are also held to be extremely lustful <term>salacious: from the Latin 'salax,' meaning lustful or prone to lechery</term>, just as I find written in the twelfth book of **Aelian** <note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175–235 CE), a Roman author and teacher of rhetoric who wrote in Greek</note> *On the Nature of Animals*, with words to this effect:

"They say that mice are most lustful," he says, "and they bring forth **Cratinus** <note>An Athenian comic poet of the 5th century BCE</note> as a witness in his play *The Fugitives*: 'Come now, I shall strike you like lightning with the softness of a mouse,'—referring to the softness of **Xenophon** <note>Presumably a contemporary of the poet noted for his delicate nature</note>. But indeed, they say that the female mouse is also quite frantic regarding matters of Venus <note>The goddess of love; here a metaphor for sexual desire</note>." 

And again, in **Epicrates** <note>A 4th-century BCE Greek comic poet</note> in the *Chorus* (for this is a play by Epicrates): "Finally, a detestable madam approached me, swearing by the girl, by **Diana**, and by **Persephone** <note>original: "Phersephattam"</note> that she was a young heifer, a virgin, an unbroken foal. But in truth, it was a 'mouse-hole' <note>original: "Cauus murinus." A vulgar slang term for a woman's anatomy</note>." By this exaggeration, he wanted to say she was most lustful, since he called it a "mouse-hole" rather than a "mutinum" <term>mutinum: an ancient Latin term for the phallus or a specific sexual organ</term>. And **Philemon** <note>A poet of the New Comedy in Athens</note> says: "A white mouse, when one sees her." But it shames me to speak of it. That detestable madam cried out so immediately that it is often impossible for it to remain hidden. Thus far Aelian, who is supported by the authorities of comic poets of great name.

# Chapter 97.
## The word "Automaton" should be read in Suetonius, which is now commonly read as "either decorated" [aut ornatum]; and what is signified by that word.

A passage in **Suetonius** <note>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus, a Roman historian famous for his biographies of the twelve Caesars</note> in his *Life of Claudius* is read so incorrectly in most volumes: "If either the decoration [aut ornatum], or the stage-machine [pegma], or any other such thing failed to work." However, the true and uncorrupted manuscripts have it thus: "If the **automaton** <term>automaton: A self-moving mechanical device, often used in Roman theaters for special effects</term>, or the stage-machine [pegma]." 

Check either the book in **Bologna** <note>original: "Bononiae"</note> from the library of Saint Dominic, or the other one in **Florence** from the library of Saint Mark (which the **Medici family** <note>original: "gens Medica"</note> opened to the public); both are ancient. But there is one even older than both, which we now possess in our own household—to say nothing of many others—lest I perhaps be caught being too ambitious and excessive in citing evidence; everywhere you will find this latter reading [automaton] without a doubt.

<summary>The author explains the erotic subtext of being called a "mouse" in classical literature and corrects a textual error in Suetonius's biography of Claudius, restoring the technical term for a mechanical device.</summary>
<keywords>Martial, Aelian, Suetonius, Claudius, automaton, philology, Latin poetry, Greek comedy, Medici family, manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s philological exploration of classical terminology and customs. Having previously discussed poetic terms of endearment and the nature of desire in Greek comedy, the author now pivots to the technical definition of "automata" in engineering and the legal rituals of Roman slave manumission.</meta>

It appears, therefore, that they were commonly called **Automata**: which mechanics constructed in such a way that they seemed to perform some action of their own accord, as if by an unknown cause. Such devices are now found in clocks, or in certain revolving machines in which we marvel at little figures running about and playing by some hidden force. Others include fountains or even fire displays which, by some skillful arrangement and novel appearance, shoot out water or flames at fixed intervals. For we can translate the word *Automaton* into Latin as "acting of its own will" or "spontaneous." 

Thus **Homer**, in the second book of his epic <note>original: "secunda Rhapsodia," referring to the Iliad</note>, says that **Menelaus** came to the feast "automatically" <note>original: "Automaton"</note>, meaning he came of his own accord and not at anyone’s summons, as if moved by his own impulse. **Eustathius** <note>A 12th-century Byzantine scholar and commentator on Homer</note> also explains this; he then notes that more recent writers use the term *automata* for things that happen by chance, as if "in vain" <note>original: "μάτην" (*matēn*)</note>, that is, rashly and "as it happened" <note>original: "καὶ ὡς ἔτυχεν" (*kai hōs etychen*)</note>. And a little later he adds something very similar. 

Furthermore, the philosopher **Hero** <note>Hero of Alexandria (c. 10–70 CE), a mathematician and engineer famous for his inventions</note> in his *Pneumatics*, says that water is pushed upward "automatically"—that is, spontaneously—from a hollow sphere. He describes this device just as he also fashions a crow that turns toward other little birds (which are also imitation) "automatically" <note>original: "αὐτομάτως" (*automatōs*)</note>. Indeed, he even teaches how to construct a small shrine <term>ediculum: a small, decorative temple or niche</term> whose doors likewise open "automatically"—that is, of their own accord—when a fire is lit, and close when it is extinguished; all of which should be understood in the sense we have described.

### Chapter 98.

->A passage from Persius: and likewise from Seneca explained: concerning the freeing of<-
->slaves with a rod and the ritual turning.<-

**Persius**, in his sixth satire, speaks thus:
> Not that man whom the foolish officer strikes with a rod; <note>original: "Non in festuca lictor quem iactat ineptus"</note>

In this line, the most diligent poet signifies a man being set free according to ancient custom. For it is recorded by **Plutarch** <note>A Greek-Roman historian and philosopher (c. 46–119 CE)</note> in his treatise *On the Delay of Divine Vengeance* <note>original: "de his qui sero puniuntur a Deo"</note> that it was the Roman custom, when someone was freeing a slave, to throw a slender rod <term>festuca: literally a stalk or straw; in Roman law, it was the rod used by a lictor to touch a slave during the ceremony of manumission to signify their new status as a free person</term> upon his body.

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the technical mechanics of "automata" discussed previously to legal and cultural rituals in the Roman and Greek worlds. It specifically addresses the Roman ceremony of manumission (freeing a slave) and the Greek custom of welcoming victors of the sacred games by breaching city walls.</meta>

From this comes that line in **Plautus**’s play *The Braggart Soldier* <note>original: "i milite," referring to the play *Miles Gloriosus*</note>: "What, was she born free, or made free by the rod <note>original: "festuca," a stalk or rod used by the lictor to touch a slave during the manumission ceremony to symbolize their liberation</note>? Is she a slave or a free woman?" Since they were also "spun around" <note>original: "circumagebantur"</note> when receiving their freedom, perhaps it was to show that they were now permitted to move in whatever direction they wished, according to their own judgment. This is what **Persius** <note>A Roman satirist (34–62 CE)</note> also said earlier:

> The master whirls this man around; in the moment of a spinning top,
> out comes **Marcus Dama** <note>Dama was a common slave name; once freed, he took the Roman name Marcus to show his new status as a citizen</note>:

And they were often freed by the magistrate <note>original: "pretore"</note> using the "rod of freedom" <note>original: "uindicta"</note>; for this reason, he mentions both "he tosses" and "the lictor." For the lictors <note>civil servants who attended high-ranking Roman magistrates</note> used to wait upon the praetors. **Seneca** also teaches that they were usually spun around when they were being manumitted <note>The legal act of releasing a slave from *manus* (the master's hand)</note> in the eighth letter of his first book to **Lucilius**: "You must serve philosophy," he says, "so that true liberty may befall you. He who has subjected and surrendered himself to her is not delayed for a day: he is spun around immediately. For this very service to philosophy is freedom." "He is spun around" here means he is manumitted and set free.

## Chapter 99.
## Why they were accustomed to tear down part of the walls, as Suetonius says the victors of the sacred games did.

**Suetonius**, in his life of **Nero**, says: "Returning from Greece to Naples—because he had first displayed his art there—he entered with white horses through a broken part of the wall, as is the custom of the *hieronicae* <note>original: "hieronicarum," from the Greek *hieros* (sacred) and *nike* (victory), referring to victors in the prestigious Panhellenic games like the Olympics</note>." So he says. No one, however, doubts that the victors of the sacred contests are called *hieronicae*. But thus far, I find the reason for this custom only in **Plutarch**, in the second book of his *Table Talk* <note>original: "Symposiacon"</note>, expressed in this sentiment: "As for the fact," he says, "that victors riding in a chariot are permitted to divide and cast down part of the wall, it surely has this meaning: that a city has no great need for a wall when it possesses men who are able to fight and to conquer."

## Chapter 100.
## What the "silences of the Moon" are in Virgil; or what the "silent virgin" is in Horace; and the refutation of the opinions of Servius and Acron regarding them.

<summary>This page explains the Roman legal ritual of spinning a slave around during manumission and the Greek tradition of breaching city walls for athletic victors to symbolize that a city's strength lies in its men rather than fortifications.</summary>
<keywords>manumission, Roman law, Plautus, Seneca, Nero, Suetonius, hieronicae, Plutarch, city walls, Marcus Dama, lictor, vindicta</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's scholarly commentary on classical literature and natural philosophy. Having previously discussed Roman legal rituals, the author now turns to a literary and scientific analysis of a specific line from Virgil's Aeneid, using it as a springboard to discuss ancient Greek music theory (harmonics) and the "music of the spheres"—the Pythagorean belief that the movements of celestial bodies create a divine harmony.</meta>

**Virgil**, in the second book of the *Aeneid* <note>original: "Aeneidos secundo"</note>, says:
->“Through the friendly silences of the quiet moon.” <note>original: "Tacitæ... per amica silentia lunę"</note><-

**Servius** <note>A famous 4th-century commentator on Virgil</note> therefore says: "It should be known that Troy was captured on the seventh [day] of the moon; an image of which was established at Argos." From this comes what he says: "Met by the moon" and elsewhere, "Through the dark night." By "the quiet moon," he either signifies the night in the poetic fashion, or he spoke according to physical reasoning. For there are seven circles: **Saturn**, **Jupiter**, **Mars**, the **Sun**, **Venus**, **Mercury**, and the **Moon**. And the first, which is Saturn’s, sounds loudly; the rest sound less so according to their order. Just as we have heard in the **cithara** <note>a professional version of the lyre</note>, whose last string sounds less.

Thus far **Servius**. I would gladly ask him: why is the moon "friendly" to those attempting to hide and deceive? Or whether he refers the "silent moon" found in **Marcus Cato** and likewise in **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of Natural History</note> to that same harmony of the planets.

As for his claim that the last string of the cithara sounds less: this will only follow if "last" is understood not as the first, but as the "highest" <note>original: "summa"</note>, which in Greek is called the ***hypate*** <note>the string physically highest on the instrument, but producing the lowest pitch</note>. From this string, a deeper sound is produced. Just as from the outermost second [string] and the thinnest—which I would more gladly call the "last"—a sharp <note>high-pitched</note> voice is brought forth. This high voice seems to sound more because it is stretched forward, as **Porphyry** says in those commentaries he left on **Ptolemy**’s *Harmonics*, citing **Theophrastus** in his second book *On Music*. As he says, a deep sound is heard less because it is diffused all around.

Furthermore, we read in **Macrobius** <note>A 5th-century Roman philosopher</note> and others that the orb of Saturn—whose revolution is more rapid—produces the sharpest sound. I think this will by no means be approved by **Plato**, either in the *Timaeus* or the *Republic*, for he writes that the orb of Saturn is the slowest; although perhaps this is referred to the longer circuit of its path. But **Aristotle**, certainly, in his second book *On the Heavens* <note>original: "de çelo"</note>, says the higher orbs are slower because they are more retarded by the force of the eighth stellar orb <note>the sphere of the fixed stars</note>.

Therefore, setting aside the opinions of **Alexander**, **Simplicius**, or the more recent scholars on this matter: if you wish to gather the clearly true meaning of **Macrobius**, you should refer it to the daily motion; which without doubt will be found to be the fastest in the highest spheres and most violently stirred. But as for us, having dismissed this fictitious (as I think) and least [plausible] commentary of **Servius**...

<summary>The author critiques Servius’s commentary on Virgil’s "silent moon," transitioning into a technical debate on ancient harmonics and the relative speeds and sounds of planetary spheres according to Plato, Aristotle, and Macrobius.</summary>
<keywords>Virgil, Servius, music of the spheres, planetary harmony, hypate, Saturn, astronomy, harmonics, Aristotle, Plato, Macrobius, ancient music</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the commentator Servius. While the previous page discussed the Pythagorean "music of the spheres," the author now rejects that mystical interpretation of Virgil’s "silent moon." Instead, he argues for a more practical, literary, and scientific understanding of the phrase based on ancient Roman agricultural and religious texts.</meta>

...with the necessary subtlety: rather, we understand the "silences of the quiet moon" <note>original: "tacitę lunę silẽtia"</note> to refer to the moon itself, which they call "silent"—that is, the time when it is shining the least. This was so that the Greek ambushes could be better hidden; it is surely a beautiful and elegant metaphor <note>original: "tralatatione"</note> transferred from the sense of hearing to the sense of sight. For just as nothing is heard in silence, so nothing is seen in darkness. 

**Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist</note> therefore says in the sixteenth book of his *Natural History*: 
->“The day of the moon’s conjunction <note>original: "coitus lunæ," the phase where the moon is invisible from Earth</note> is called the 'silent moon' by some, and the 'inter-lunar period' <note>original: "iterlunium"</note> by others.”<-

Then the same author says in his eighteenth book: 
->“And they order this to be done when the moon is silent.”<-

**Marcus Cato** <note>An influential Roman statesman and author of the oldest surviving Latin prose work, *On Agriculture*</note> also writes in his book *On Agriculture*: 
->“Now let manure be carried out when the moon is silent; now likewise when the moon is silent, let figs, olives, apples, and pears be grafted.”<-

Finally, he even orders that meadows be manured in early spring when the same moon is silent. It would be ridiculous to refer these passages of **Cato** and **Pliny** to the "music of the stars" <note>a reference to the previous page's discussion of planetary harmony</note>, or to seek such roundabout interpretations in a very ancient expression that is so widely accepted by common usage.

Indeed, I would take the "silent moon" here, as I have said, to mean "shining very little at that time," which was the specific time the Greeks had to observe. Yet I would not interpret it so strictly as to suggest he meant the exact moment of the moon's conjunction <note>the New Moon</note> when the entire night is pitch black. That idea is surely refuted because the poet [Virgil] says shortly after: "Met by the moon" <note>original: "Oblati per lunam"</note>. Was the moon not yet shining, then, when they were sailing from the island of **Tenedos** towards evening? It began to shine only when they had already occupied the city of **Troy**. Therefore, I do not think the moon was either late or shining all night, nor was it the exact moment of the conjunction, but rather I believe it refers to the period of time during which she did not shine. For a word in poetry often seems to embrace more than one meaning. It is for this reason we see that line in our poem on *Country Life* <note>original: "noſtra Ruſtico," likely referring to Virgil's *Georgics*</note>:

> “And what the silent one warns, and what the moon between months <note>original: "intermenstrua Phoebe"</note> warns.”

Nor, however, would it be absurd—beyond the temporary nature of the description—to understand the moon's "friendly silences" in the way **Cicero** writes that "silence" is used in the context of **auspices** <note>the Roman practice of reading omens from birds or nature</note>: it refers to that which is free from every flaw. Note also how beautiful and ingenious the explanation is in **Acron** <note>a commentator on the poet Horace</note>, who, while explaining a passage of **Horace** in the second book of his *Odes*, writes:

> “While the priest climbs the Capitol
> With the silent virgin.”

<summary>The author rejects a mystical "celestial music" interpretation of Virgil's "silent moon," citing Pliny and Cato to prove it is a common agricultural and astronomical term for the dark phase of the moon. <meta>This page continues the author's critique of the commentator Servius, shifting from natural phenomena (the silent moon) to Roman religious mythology. The author then transitions into a personal defense, addressing rumors that his scholarly insights were stolen from the late Niccolò Perotti.</meta>

He interprets the "Silent Maiden" <note>original: "Tacitam uirginem," referring to the goddess Tacita or Muta</note> as if she were "illustrious through the nobility of her priesthood." To persuade the foolish of this, he even misuses the authority of **Virgil** <note>original: "Maronis"</note>, who said: "Through the friendly silences of the silent moon" <note>original: "Tacitæ per amica silentia lunæ"</note>. As if "silent moon" in this context should be taken to mean "illustrious." Clearly, he had not read in **Dionysius** <note>Dionysius of Halicarnassus, a Greek historian of Rome</note> (at least in his Roman history), nor in **Plutarch**, or many others, that it was entirely unknown to all people which sacred objects or statues the **Vestal Virgins** <note>the priestesses of Vesta who guarded Rome's sacred fire</note> were in charge of protecting. It was for this very reason—because they maintained silence—that the maiden was called "silent," just as the Trojan **Minerva** <note>the Palladium, a sacred statue of Athena/Minerva rescued from Troy</note> was only permitted to be seen by the one who was authorized.

But so that this book—although it is already too long as it is—may nonetheless be crowned with a sort of finishing flourish <term>Coronide</term> <note>original: "Coronide," a mark or flourish drawn at the end of a book to signal completion</note> and so that nothing remains entirely unaddressed, we want the reader to know: after we had adorned this single "century" <note>a collection of 100 scholarly essays or "Miscellanies"</note> just as it is now—except for a few rare blossoms scattered here and there last year—and had shown it publicly to several friends, a story began to spread among the common people. Its author is not certain, but it has filled many ears with whispers of this kind. They say it is no wonder if **Poliziano** <note>the author, Angelo Poliziano, speaking of himself</note> has dictated certain new and remarkable things before, or is now beginning to report them in writing. They claim that all these things were long ago most diligently written out by **Niccolò Perotti**, the Bishop of Siponto <note>a prominent humanist who died in 1480</note>, especially in that book to which he gave the title *The Horn of Plenty* <note>original: "Copiæ cornu," a massive commentary on the poet Martial</note>. For since (at least according to those tellers of tales) his patron **Lorenzo de' Medici** <note>the ruler of Florence and Poliziano’s protector</note> possesses great influence and favor with the **Duke of Urbino** (to whom that book was dedicated), it was through Lorenzo that the original manuscript <term>archetypus</term> of that work was made available to Poliziano. Therefore, they say, he is now a plunderer: having once hooked this book, like a sort of "divine wand" <note>original: "uirgulæ diuinæ," likely a reference to a dowsing rod or a magic wand</note>, he makes public material into his own private right. Like **Aesop’s jackdaw** <note>a reference to the fable of the bird who disguised himself in the beautiful feathers of other birds</note>, he preens himself in borrowed plumage. When our friend **Roberto Salviati** <note>a Florentine diplomat and close friend of Poliziano</note>—who is a most dutiful man and a great lover not only of literature but of all literary men—reported something of these rumors (which have been hallowed and aired in various social circles) to me, I immediately recognized...
