--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his work against critics. Having addressed specific scholarly disputes on the previous page, he now describes the emotional toll of envy and lists the legendary teachers and peers—such as Ficino and Pico della Mirandola—who shaped his intellectual development.</meta>

...contemptible ways of envy, which, while it strives to harm someone, transforms itself into all things; and just like that Proteus described by Licentius:
> The boar foams / the water flows / the lion roars / the snake hisses.
<note>original: "Spumat aper / fluit unda / fremit leo / sibilat anguis." This verse, likely referencing a poem by Augustine's pupil Licentius, uses the shape-shifting sea god Proteus to symbolize how envious critics constantly change their arguments to find a way to strike.</note>
But because I had grown callous to its blows, I am not for that reason angry at my own studies, even though they repay such great labor with such a [bitter] reward. Nor have I condemned myself for failing to appease this most foul plague <note>referring to envy</note> with idleness; nor—as some perhaps hoped—did I tear up the book!

Instead, I set it aside for a short while. Letting go of the desire to publish for the moment, I merely watered the book from time to time like a garden, using little irrigation channels. I then moved it toward a fuller style of thought—moving, as they say, "from husks to bread" <note>a metaphor for moving from superficial or "thin" writing to substantial, nourishing scholarship</note>—that is, from playful trifles to the serious matters of philosophy. Thus, having turned my mind toward higher studies, I began works of a more serious nature. These too will appear in their own time for the public good (if the subject is handled with sufficient skill), so that the eyes of those petty critics might sting all the more.

Indeed, while I was still at a tender age, I studied under two most excellent men. One was **Marsilio Ficino** of Florence, whose lyre was far more successful than that of Orpheus the Thracian; for Ficino, unless I am mistaken, called back the "true Eurydice"—by which I mean the **Platonic wisdom** <note>Ficino was the primary figure responsible for translating Plato into Latin and reviving his philosophy in the West</note> of the most profound judgment—from the depths of the underworld. The other was **John Argyropoulos** of Byzantium, by far the most famous of the **Peripatetics** <note>the "Peripatetics" were the followers of Aristotle’s school of philosophy</note> of his time. I devoted effort to both branches of philosophy, though not with total consistency. By nature and age, I was more inclined toward the charms of the poet **Homer**, whom I was then translating into Latin verse with a young man's remarkable passion and dedication.

Later, however, as other affairs and business pressed upon me, I occasionally "drank" from philosophy only as **dogs drink from the Nile** <note>an ancient proverb used by Pliny the Elder to describe doing something in a hurried, glancing way—legend says dogs at the Nile drink while running to avoid being caught by crocodiles</note>—lapping a little and then fleeing. This continued until this most noble prince, **Giovanni Pico della Mirandola**, returned to this city. He was drawn here as much by the kindness of **Lorenzo de' Medici** as by their shared virtue and genius. He is a man—or rather, a hero—endowed most abundantly with every gift of fortune, body, and soul. A youth of almost divine beauty and imposing physical presence, he possesses a most piercing intellect, a singular memory, and tireless devotion to study. Furthermore, he has a clear and rich eloquence; indeed, it is hard to say whether he is more remarkable for his judgment or his character! Already this same man...

<sig>p ii</sig>

<summary>Poliziano defends his persistence in scholarship despite envious critics and recounts his education under Marsilio Ficino and John Argyropoulos, culminating in his admiration for the brilliant Pico della Mirandola.</summary>
<keywords>Envy, Proteus, Marsilio Ficino, Platonic philosophy, John Argyropoulos, Aristotle, Homer, Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance Humanism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s autobiographical account of his intellectual development, following the mention of Marsilio Ficino. The "he" described at the start of this page likely refers to Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, Poliziano's close friend and another titan of the Renaissance, whom the author credits with pushing him toward more serious philosophical study.</meta>

<sig>u q</sig>
<warning>Some foxing and light stains throughout the page.</warning>

...most expert in philosophy, and likewise equipped and instructed beyond belief in the literature of various languages and in all the honorable arts. Finally, to sum it up once and for all, he is greater than any possible praise. He, therefore, has constantly urged me—with whom he is accustomed to share his sweetest cares and sometimes to trifle pleasantly, and whom he has chosen as an almost constant companion of his studies (which is the highest honor)—to explore philosophy. He insisted I do so not with sleepy eyes as before, but with vigorous and wakeful ones, as if to animate me with the trumpet-blast <note>original: "classico" refers to a trumpet call used in the Roman army to signal the troops</note> of his own voice. Therefore, having found such and so great a sponsor or leader, I was by no means lacking to the opportunity; rather, I met it and seized it. Through an almost constant handling of the greatest authors—as if by frequently rubbing flint— I seem to have struck out at least a few sparks of truth. I have caught so much flame in the kindling that, in this dark and moonless night, some brightness finally begins to shine for me.

But behold, meanwhile, while I was totally immersed in this, that "Horn" <note>The author refers to the *Cornu Copiae* ("Horn of Plenty") by Niccolò Perotti, a massive scholarly work published posthumously in 1489 that competed with the author’s own project</note> was suddenly released to the public. There is a great rush. It is in everyone’s hands. It is poured out. It is spread about. Why say more? Calumny <note>slander or false accusations</note> sets me free. You would have seen immediately the faces of some who were silently mourning—and then, by some means I know not how (just like Thrasymachus in Plato’s writing), they were blushing in a way they never used to do. <note>In Plato's *Republic*, the aggressive sophist Thrasymachus famously blushes when Socrates defeats him in an argument.</note> But as for that "Horn"—though it is certainly not empty—what I might see inside it is not something to be revealed at this time. 

Let it suffice for the present to say that I did not aim for the same goal as the author of that volume, nor was the same height intended or achieved by us both. For if we both happened to treat the same passages in our work (which was bound to happen in some places), there is nonetheless a thicker wall between us than there was between Pyramus and Thisbe. <note>A reference to the mythological lovers who could only speak through a thin crack in a wall; Poliziano argues that even when he and Perotti discuss the same topics, their methods and quality are worlds apart.</note> As proof, consider that "Cotytto" of Juvenal <note>A Thracian goddess; Poliziano is likely correcting a manuscript error or a previous commentator's mistake</note>—which in his work is "Cocytus"—about which he fights with Domitio <note>Domitio Calderini, a rival scholar</note> as if over some impoverished kingdom; or likewise the altar of Martial frequent with "horns," or the linden tree, or anything else of that sort which I entrusted to the labor of my assistants and attendants. Indeed, it even happened that during the very interval in which my *Miscellanea* <note>The author's own work, "The Miscellanies"</note> were paused, some hundred annotations were produced, learnedly, by Hercules! (For who...

<summary>The author describes how his mentor (likely Pico della Mirandola) inspired him to move from "trifles" to serious philosophy. He also defends his scholarly work, the Miscellanea, against a rival publication (Perotti's Cornu Copiae), arguing his methods are superior.</summary>
<keywords>Pico della Mirandola, philosophy, scholarship, Miscellanea, Cornu Copiae, Thrasymachus, Juvenal, Martial, Domitio Calderini, Renaissance rivalry</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface or introductory letter. The author (Angelo Poliziano) is addressing the publication of a rival work—Niccolò Perotti’s "Cornu Copiae"—which contains scholarly discoveries Poliziano claims to have taught in his own public lectures years earlier. He navigates the delicate balance between defending his intellectual priority and maintaining his friendship with the rival party.</meta>

...denies) and laboriously produced by a man very friendly to us, who is not ignorant of our purpose and is also well-lettered. I believe it was not without some chance that he produced one or two works of almost the same argument as ours—certainly quite a few things that are nearly identical to those which we dictated to a crowded public auditorium nearly nine years ago, either when we were interpreting <term>Ovid's Fasti</term><note>original: "Fasti Nasonis." Ovid’s poetic calendar of Roman myths and festivals.</note> or later, <term>Horace's Satires</term><note>original: "Flacci satyras." The satirical poems of Quintus Horatius Flaccus.</note>.

I swear most solemnly (if there be need), holding the worn altar: I thought immediately that I should concede entirely to a friend, if not by my own right, then certainly by the common right of friendship. I intended not to claim any of those findings as my own, since he had claimed the title for himself—and, I think, not without doing me an injury. For I am not so swollen with pride, nor so fond of myself, that I would wish to be seen as the teacher of so learned a man in any matter, lest that proverb be thrown in my face: <term>A pig teaching Minerva</term><note>original: "Sus Minervam." A classical Greek and Latin proverb used when an ignorant person attempts to instruct an expert.</note>. 

But since he spoke as if he had finally discovered these things as the first to do so—asserting they were altogether unknown (so I believe he thought) and untold until that day—I confess I am compelled to do what he himself, by that <term>humanity</term><note>original: "humanitas." Refers to the Renaissance ideal of refined, civilized, and scholarly conduct.</note> he professes, will easily endure: to simply and candidly absolve my profession from those suspicions of common ignorance. For just as I might rightly be held either uncivil or arrogant if I did not think that both these and even better things could be born at home for one who is so diligent and learned—since "God has placed all things in the middle of the field"<note>A classical idiom suggesting that knowledge and opportunity are open to everyone through effort.</note>—so on the other hand, to desert the <term>defense of one's reputation</term><note>original: "patrocinium famae." A legalistic term for protecting one's good name.</note> might seem not only negligent but also (if I am not mistaken) almost cruel toward oneself. 

<term>Donatus</term><note>Aelius Donatus, a famous 4th-century Roman grammarian.</note> used to say, "Perish those who said our things before us!" We, however, pray for and desire prosperous and happy things for him who either found or certainly said our things *after* us. We only ask that he not be reluctant to admit us, as if a partner, into the fellowship of those things which so many students received under our instruction so long ago, and in which we labored to the best of our ability; lest it be that "all things are not common among friends," as <term>Euripides</term><note>The famous Greek playwright; the phrase "friends have all things in common" was a staple of classical ethics.</note> says.

<summary>In this concluding passage, Poliziano defends his intellectual priority against a friend's rival publication, asserting that he taught the same material years earlier while attempting to remain gracious. He famously flips a quote by Donatus to suggest that scholars should welcome those who discover their ideas independently rather than resenting them.</summary>

->declares that he is responsible neither for the spelling<-
->nor for any errors committed by others.<-
<note>Poliziano is distancing himself from any typographical errors made by the printer or the "friends" who proofread the work—a common precaution for authors in the early days of the printing press.</note>
->At Florence, in the Year of Salvation 1489.<-
->On the nineteenth of September.<-
<note>original: "Decimotertio kalendas octobris." Using the Roman calendar, the 13th day before the Kalends (the 1st) of October is September 19th.</note>
